--- Page 2 ---
“An act of real creation is always a pro-
jection of the self into the future.”
           —Albert William Levi, Rector,
                     Black Mountain College




Black Mountain College was just such a
projection. This experimental school,
according to founder John Andrew Rice,
sought to develop “individuals rather than
individualists,” men and women who could
creatively participate in society. Free from
conventional academic apparatus, students
would have a tabula rasa on which they
could learn to see the world afresh, guided
by the perceptual techniques of such avant-
garde artists and teachers as Josef Albers,
M. C. Richards, Merce Cunningham, John
Cage, and Charles Olson.
    In a remote lakeside retreat fifteen miles
east of Asheville,    North Carolina, this dream
turned into one of the most innovative and
influential learning communities in the
United States. From its founding in 1933 to
its closing in 1956, Black Mountain College
attracted faculty and students seeking a
cooperative, experiential approach to
learning outside traditional academia.
     Mervin Lane—a former student at the
College—has compiled the first book to
show the school and its community through

xviii                      Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 23 ---
Those years have profoundly affected for us, the nature
of whatever has happened since. Memories still serve as
fertile seeds for further thought and action.
     The Black Mountain set-up was supposed to be
designed to encourage positive action and significant
participation in whatever was going on, because we
thought that gave a solid basis for enjoyment of life.
And we did enjoy it, didn’t we, on many levels?
                                                 —Ted Dreier,
                                letter to Ati Gropius Johansen,
                                                           1988

--- Page 24 ---


--- Page 25 ---
Introduction



  A life lived long ago affects one in myriad and sometimes magical
  ways. In part, that is what the “sprouted seeds,” the people in this
  collection, speak of in their inimitable voices. But present states of
  mind and life attitudes have also influenced what they have chosen
  to remember and record as well as the way they have gone about
  recounting it.
       T. S. Eliot’s essay “Tradition and the Individual Talent” noted
  the mutual influence of the present and the past. As Eliot put it,
  there is a sensibility that “will not find it preposterous that the past
  should be altered by the present as much as the present is directed
  by the past.”
       The experiences of thirty, forty, and fifty years ago recalled
  here are so affected by present thought and attitude that it should
  not be surprising that a past experience may be misremembered,
  magnified, and touted as causal simply because its effect and
  significance are proved to be a first step and momentous by token
  of present value and importance. These shifting and changing as-
_ sessments—with variations—are the effects of time, of our present
  sense of the past, and of what we recall happened in our lives in a
  very unusual place: Black Mountain College. That place and time
  provide the occasion for this exploration. It is at once a celebration,
  a reunion, a gathering of the clan, a reassessment to carry forward
  into the future, a summing up anew.
       The history of Black Mountain is well-documented in Martin
  Duberman’s    Black Mountain: An Exploration in Community, and in
  Mary Emma Harris’s The Arts at Black Mountain College. The College
  persisted from 1933 through 1956. Its founder, John Andrew Rice,
  had been teaching at Rollins College in Florida and was dissatisfied
  with the overly academic approach to education that he had wit-
  nessed there and at other American colleges. Conflicts between
  Rice, a divided faculty, and the administration at Rollins reached a
  peak of irreconcilability. He and a small group of sympathetic col-
_ leagues and students left. These were seekers sensitive to the con-
  strictions of entrenched, hierarchical, bureaucratic inflexibility.


 An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                      ]

--- Page 26 ---
They felt the need for a more cooperative, co-evolving, independ-
ent, experiential approach to learning, liberated from external pres-
sures and the preoccupation with the apparatus of credentialing.
They rallied in support of Rice’s plan to found a small college
where the curriculum would be oriented towards the uniqueness of
individuals; where no unwieldy administrative, trustee-controlled
system would hamper the free interchange between instructor and
student; where open and honest dialogue would be pivotal not
only to pedagogy but to living and working together in commu-
nity; and where faculty and student representatives would       make
the decisions.
    After enlisting support from a number of influential people
and exploring several likely sites, Rice and his small band ended up
in North Carolina. The dream was embodied at a retreat called the
Blue Ridge Assembly, a set of buildings fifteen miles east of
Asheville, near the town of Black Mountain. The main building was
Lee Hall; the “Lee Hall period” refers to the first seven years of the
College’s history.
     In 1937, an opportunity arose to acquire land and some rustic
buildings across the valley, a summer resort and camp of 676 acres,
with some 50 adjoining acres. The site, designated as “Lake Eden,”
was purchased. In 1940, despite difficulties in raising money for
construction of a Gropius-designed building which would provide
classrooms and individual studies, the College made the move to
the new location. The construction of the Studies Building, along
with providing for all the College’s essentials, became part of the
curriculum itself, and BMC continued its educational and.commu-
nal odyssey until 1956. Insolvency forced the sale of the entire plant
that lamentable year.
     Actually there was never a period in its history when the
school didn’t struggle financially to remain open and viable. For
years, less was more. Then less became too much. At the end there
were fewer students, scant funds, scarce food, lack of essential ma-
terials (like coal for the furnace’), anomie, entropy. But the loyalty
and enthusiasms of the remaining teachers and students held.
     Rice resigned in 1940 but, as far as I can see, his egalitarian
educational principles remained as a force that pervaded the Col-
lege. Though attenuated at times, though one of the factors contrib-



2                     Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 27 ---
uting to factions and conflicts within the community, and though
 modified or differently emphasized by admirers and detractors
 among his successors, his vision remained vital and generative for
 most—if not all—of the twenty-three years of Black Mountain
 College’s life.
      The books written on BMC have different emphases. They all,
 however, delve into history, that is, make some attempt to give an
 accurate account of what went on at the school. Fielding Dawson’s
 Black Mountain Book—the first book to be written on the College—is
 involved with Dawson's own personal and vibrant recollections of
 activities and people, especially teachers, in particular Charles
 Olson.
     Martin Duberman’s book is a history of BMC as a learning
 community    and covers    most of its life. Duberman,   who was   not
 there as a student or teacher, attempts, interestingly enough to
 place himself imaginatively in the role of a participant. He projects
 himself, for instance, into the recorded minutes of a faculty meet-
 ing. The difference between Duberman’s approach and this collec-
 tion is that he has constructed imaginatively an experience he did
 not actually have whereas many of the authors here have imagina-
 tively reconstructed their actual experiences.
      Mary Emma Harris’s book is the third one on the school (she
 also was neither a teacher nor a student), and is preoccupied with
the role the arts played in the educational philosophy and commu-
 nal life of the College. The book also attempts—though not as ex-
 haustively as Duberman—to chronicle the activities and events that
 occurred between the school’s founding and its demise.
      The text at hand presents reminiscences and recollections from
 a cross section of BMC     students and teachers, conveying a wide
 range of impressions of the life they remember. Some have thought
 fit to tell stories or write personal accounts. There are also plays
 and poems, a college transcript and a graduating testimonial, taped
 interviews, numerous      assessments of the College, memoirs of the
 founder, a school-year-opening address by the new rector; and
 some pictorial material. In short, the variety of materials, styles,
 and approaches should interest every reader who attended the
 school and their children who seek out family history. But there is
 also scope and depth enough for someone totally unfamiliar with



 An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                    3

--- Page 28 ---
the College to gain a strong sense of the special educational quality
and spirit in the life of this innovative school nestled amongst the
Blue Ridge and Craggy mountains of western North Carolina.
    I was interested in how John Andrew       Rice’s seeds—planted
back in 1933—sprouted,    how   they blossomed,   and what   was   re-
membered of the nurturing process. What is recalled by people
who had the experience of the school decades ago is not necessarily
validated by objective fact. Memories can, of course, be confirmed
through documentation or consensus, but one cannot expect literal
accuracy of recollections after such lengthy intervals. Distortion
too, will tell us something about ourselves. It would be more perti-
nent to expect a kind of constrained fiction lurking in the memory
and emerging in unexpected ways.
    Former students and teachers may remark in reading some of
the material: “That’s not the way it was at all!” But what would be
gained in disputing any of the accounts at the expense of taking
delight in the versions here? Is it not enough to know that, what-
ever the contours of the experience remembered, it was important
enough to have been re-evoked, re-presented? Doesn’t this say
something, after all, about the legend-inducing magic of the place?
That an experience is recalled must be truth for our purposes here.
And in the sum total of accounts perhaps we may catch a glimpse
of the elusive actuality of the place. Every year was different at
BMC, and all who contributed to its life defined that life while they
were there and gave to it the shape and style that it had during that
time. There were crucial roles enough to go around. We valued and
were valued for our fantasies and competencies.
     In preparing this book, week after week as I opened my mail, |
the various manuscripts seemed to me to reflect the range of atti-
tudes that were always so diverse at BMC. One was not forced to
take part in any event; one did not have to go along with the major-
ity opinion—or with the minority, for that matter; one could press
on or turn about; one could join a formal program of the school or
an informal program organized by a peer; or one could initiate a
movement of one’s own and discover its significance for others. In
such matters the school was completely—and sometimes mad-
deningly—democratic. This book likewise reflects a participatory
process. From the former students and teachers who were asked to



4                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 29 ---
contribute, there were varying responses, no responses; some sent
 in bits and pieces, and as their memories opened up, kept sending
 them; some offered material written decades ago; some wanted to
 submit    reflections   but   couldn’t,   due   to more   pressing   commit-
 ments. Some who did contribute withdrew their pieces after further
 consideration; others contributed published articles.
      The contributions are not preoccupied with misfortunes, mis-
 calculations, frailties, wounds, and weaknesses.          For some this will
 seem an unnatural kind of omission since there is obviously a dark
 side to any community of people living, learning, and working to-
 gether. But for an enterprise that accounts success in terms of hu-
 man development, growth, and the generating of energies, notes
 from underground are not necessarily enlightening. And people
 who are moved to write about others’ shortcomings are not so can-
 did when it comes to their own.
      In this regard, the editor himself came to an early decision with
 respect to a few pieces that ‘named names’ and sometimes cast as-
 persions. Where I judged that what was written would be gratui-
 tously hurtful to someone, I proposed that the author omit that por-
 tion of the writing. Sometimes it was necessary to omit only a last
 name.    I have tried, however,      to keep the experience intact while
  omitting any malicious animus. With more ambivalence, I chose to
_ downplay sex. It may be that sexuality—especially during youthful
  college years—is crucial to the working out of a sense of identity.
 But it is, after all, only one aspect of what occurred at BMC           (and
 what indeed occurs on every college campus) in the questioning
 and working out of “Who and what am I?” Some sexual accounts
 might have raised the entertainment quotient. But I was more inter-
 ested in the form our aspirations took, how we gave ourselves to
 the life lying before and beyond us, and to the common work and
  hours at hand.
       Ken Noland says in his interview that much art nowadays ap-
  peals to an appetite for “consumption” entertainment. But there is
  another kind of art that is not looking to feed this side of the human
  psyche, and is not consumable. He is referring to art that has a
  “long-range resonating effect,” is timeless, is to be seen over and
  over again, and which doesn’t pall the sensibility; it doesn’t wear
_ out. Analogously, Black Mountain College has had a “long-range



 An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                          5

--- Page 30 ---
resonating effect.” There were enduring qualities and interactions
that involved the people who were there. We seek to capture the
spirit of their enterprise, their struggle with their identities, their
efforts to grow through the problems they faced—whether physi-
cal, psychological, social, educational, or spiritual. We wish to see
their gradual immersion into the complexities of human values and
choices, the interplay between communal and individual life. For at
BMC there were disagreements and tensions between individuals
on various issues that involved the commonality. Antagonistic
stances were taken, for instance, with regard to whether and when
to begin accepting black students into the school. (BMC was the
first “biracial” college in the South—a token gesture, but one made
early, in 1945.) There were sharp differences as to whether the Col-
lege as a whole should affiliate with a southern civil-rights organi-
zation, what the emphases       should be in the curricula, and how
much faculty—married, unmarried, with and without seniority—
should be paid. Many of the students, through the student modera-
tor who represented them at faculty meetings, opposed faculty de-
cisions. There was space for responsible opposition beyond power
plays   or adolescent   rebellion.   I think,   for instance,   of student
stances taken with respect to faculty retention and firing, or to the
punitive action taken by faculty when some women students found
themselves in a jam while hitchhiking away from the college. The
list goes on.
      This anthology is a communal book comprising unique indi-
vidual statements and expressions, chronologically arranged ac-
cording to the time of the writer’s attendance. The result is a com-
posite, giving off a tone that appears to resonate in a way that could
not have been predictable. Thus the book hopefully has the syner-
gistic effect that, as Bucky Fuller showed,        the universe runs on.
John Cage, who greatly admired and learned from Bucky, says in
one of his books that “Synergy is an energy greater than the sum of
the several energies had they not been brought together.” This is
true for many situations. It was especially true at BMC.
     Individuals at the College were never lost in the massive num-
bers that attend a large university, and therefore each person was
more singular, more noticeable, seen if not heard by everyone.
Thus there occurred a pointedness and distinctness in the separate



6                       Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 31 ---
energies that were brought together in the community. Many of us,
 of course, didn’t know for sure what was going on all the time, and
 there were even people in our small world with whom we were not
 familiar. But we recognized natural affinities and aversions, and
 because the number of individuals in the community never ex-
 ceeded   150, we did have an intuitive sense about what was not
 openly apparent. We had a small enough field to at least sense most
 of the interactions going on at any one time. And because we lived,
 ate, studied, talked, listened, made love, bickered, worked, cried,
 and laughed together day and night, the areas where the energy
 was given and taken were more proximate to each other, more sus-
 tained; therefore there was an intensity of sharing, a heightened in-
 teraction. This was one of the problems for many at the school:
 though you had all the privacy you could put to use, still nothing
 could be entirely hidden.
      One question posed by the solicitation and selection process I
 used is whether it produced a wide and deep enough cross section
 of students and teachers who attended the school in its twenty-
 three years of existence. My own answer is yes. Pondering the ma-
 terials that came in, I gradually came to the conclusion there could
 easily be any number    of books written, but this one was indeed
 representative. A more specific problem was engaging the view
_and cooperation of the individuals who were at the College in its
 final years. I found that most of these people either did not respond
 at all or, if they did, they begged off. I wondered why. But if the
 essence of BMC was a generating experience, one of growth and
 forwarding development, who really wishes to recount the experi-
 ences of dissolution? Ike Nakata reminded me that the “flame” or
 spirit of BMC was and may even now be there, but the body has to
 be fed. Although there were many heroic struggles to stay alive in
 its twenty-three years, the College as an embodied community died
 in 1956. If the College didn’t work out in the end, or if it died for
 want of resources—and for other reasons too—anyone willing to
 contribute a chronicle of its downfall would be an unusual person,
 if, that is, s/he had been a member of the community at this time.
And for many who were part of its ending, dissolution and disillu-
sion could have served as a kind of proof—since ends are fated in
beginnings—that perhaps there was not enough there to begin



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                   7

--- Page 32 ---
with, and not very much there possibly at the end to save, or even
to talk, much less to write, about. Nevertheless, I am sorry that
there were not more tales of learning from the experience of the
bleak years, because the most creative episodes often arise in times
of shutting up shop and bidding farewell. It is those sunset epi-
sodes that will be missed from what is offered here.
     Nonetheless, the spirit of the College does remain. This book is
a testament to that spirit. And in March 1989, there was even an at-
tempt made to inaugurate a “new” Black Mountain College at Lake
Eden on North Fork Road. Though it failed to take hold, the effort
to continue—thirty-three years later—on the same site must indi-
cate that the land still holds some of the traces of the school’s elu-
sive spirit. Indeed, the materials of our memories include the build-
ings, the millstone, the train-rail dinner gong, the mountains, the
dirt roads, the dogwood in bloom off the porch of Lee Hall, the pas-
ture, Lake Eden itself—and a host of other tangibles, all of which
stimulate Proustlike associations, sounding retrospective depths.
     A bit on how the book came to be. When I went to Carmel in
November 1986 to take in M. C. Richard’s pottery show (which was
stunning!), my wife and I took the opportunity to visit the Nakatas
in Monterey. I hadn’t seen Ike and Alexa for forty years. Ike gave
me some stories he had written, and, to reciprocate, I promised to
send him my book (Going to Town). which I told him contained a
section of anecdotes about BMC. When       I mentioned   this, he sug-
gested gathering personal reminiscences from former BMC stu-
dents and teachers. He wasn’t in a position to do this himself (nor
was | at the time), but he put the notion in my head. After returning
home and thinking it over, I realized there was a need for a many-
faceted collection of personalized and intimate accounts to fill a gap
left by the three books on the College already published. In January
1988 I sent a letter to a few people asking for recollections, materi-
als, and BMC names and addresses. The number of people inter-
ested in the idea of contributing to such a book increased, and ina
year’s time, through correspondence and word of mouth, I had ac-
quired enough material to begin contacting publishers. Ike and I
have now exchanged many letters and ideas. It is to him that credit
should be given for the origin of the concept of the book.
     The most painful task I faced was in reducing the size of the



8                     Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 33 ---
offerings. All of the material was important enough to include, but
 because of space restrictions I was forced to leave out a great deal. I
 have tried to keep the central feeling, the main pulse and substance
 in what I have retained. If     have failed to do so, I can only hope my
 old and new friends will understand and forgive me.

John Andrew Rice
                            (Founder; Rector and Faculty 1933-40)


                 Black Mountain College Memoirs



When Black Mountain College was founded in the mountains of
North Carolina in 1933, it boasted three distinctive features: com-
plete democratic self-rule, extensive work in the creative arts, and
interdisciplinary academic study. At the time it was the only col-
lege in America to offer such a combination. Before Black Moun-
tain, artistically inclined young   Americans      had at best to choose
between traditional colleges, where the creative arts were absent, or
traditional music and fine arts conservatories, where academic sub-
jects were neglected. This ultimately meant that most chose college
and never learned the discipline of the creative arts. Thanks to early
favorable coverage by the national media, and especially by the
Czech-American writer Louis Adamic in Harper’s, talented students
and   teachers   came   to Black   Mountain   to test the unorthodox,
broad-ranging approach to college life and the curriculum, an ap-
proach that would, in a few decades, influence higher education
across the country.
     Although the arts today occupy a larger space in American
higher learning, Black Mountain’s experiment in independent gov-
ernment, with absolutely no outside control, has not been imitated
anywhere that I am aware of. But it was probably this feature
which drew out the greatest strengths and weaknesses of the insti- |
tution and its students and teachers... .
      John Andrew Rice was born in 1888 in Lynchburg, South Caro-
lina, and spent his childhood on family plantations there and in the
Low Country. He attended the Webb School in Bell Buckle, Tennes-
see, where the formal curriculum       consisted    of Greek, Latin, and
Mathematics. There he met John Webb, who was by all accounts a

This piece is taken from an article by John A. Rice’s grandson, William
C. Rice, in The Southern Review, Summer 1989. I excerpt from his intro-
duction, then reprint a selection from the memoirs themselves.



10                      Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 35 ---
teacher of great humanity and learning, and his [Webb's] crusty
older brother Sawney. . . . At Tulane University, he won a Rhodes
Scholarship to Queen’s College, Oxford, where he studied Jurispru-
dence and came to admire the tradition of extensive reading with
tutors. He returned to the Webb School to teach from 1914-16, then
studied English literature at the University of Chicago. He served
briefly in Army intelligence after the Armistice. Starting in 1920, he
taught Classics for thirteen years, first at the Univ. of Nebraska,
next at Douglass College (Rutgers), and, after a stay in England on
a Guggenheim fellowship, at Rollins College. His abrupt firing
from Rollins in the spring of 1933 and the dismissal and res-
ignations of faculty and student sympathizers brought national
publicity and an investigation by the American Association of Uni-
versity Professors. Ultimately it also led to the founding of Black
Mountain College.
     Through his academic career, John Rice dissented from the
orthodoxies of both the educational “Right” and “Left,” to use a
rather crude distinction. He thought that traditional educators
were generally a lifeless lot, authoritarian and unconcerned      with
the student; but he was equally repelled by the lack of liberal learn-
ing and the scientific posing of the Progressives. Never one to
mince words, he used to remark that “in the schools of education
they cast fake pearls before real swine.” He believed that American
 colleges wrongly concentrated on either social or intellectual devel-
 opment and failed to address the “whole person.” But if he was an
 admirer and friend of John Dewey and, in print, an opponent of
 Robert Hutchins, his own philosophy of education was realized
 mainly in everyday academic life rather than professional dis-
course.   His writings on education   were   provocative,   and rich in
anecdote and intuition, but they were also brief and unsystematic.
He was more a conversationalist than an author. He was vital, char-
ismatic, and disarmingly frank; but he was also at times cruel and
destructive. “Too much Socrates, not enough Jesus,” ran the usual
complaint.
     Due mainly to his failings, he was forced to sever ties to Black
Mountain College in 1940. Thereafter he devoted himself to writ-
ing. His autobiography, I Came Out of the Eighteenth Century, was
published in 1942, and the ten years that followed, his short stories



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                    i

--- Page 36 ---
about blacks and whites in the South appeared in the New Yorker,
Collier's, and The Saturday Evening Post, to be collected in 1955 in
Local Color. In his last decade, he wrote very little and lived in Bar-
nesville, Maryland, where I knew him and helped tend his large
vegetable garden. (His planter blood survives in me.) He died in
1963 seer
    The only papers that I knew deserved special attention were
two legal pads of memoirs, in holograph, of Black Mountain Col-
lege, written between 1964 and 1966. Although he was in his late
seventies and failing health, he accepted an invitation of a pub-
lisher to write an account of his time at the College, an invitation
made the more attractive by a generous advance. Unfortunately,
due to worsening illness and a lifelong tendency to put things
off... he never came close to assembling a whole usable manu-
script. ...In these memoirs ... we may hear the storyteller, teacher,
classicist,   conversationalist,   humorist,   and   too-severe   critic   all
given voice ... His attention, as a writer, to nonverbal elements of
communication may be seen to mirror his commitment, as an edu-
cator, to forms of learning that do not derive from the written word
but from sight, hearing, and everyday human experience.— William
GP Rice


On Artists
|
We were born artists, all of us. We were born alike, free and equal;
free to create the world in which we chose to live, equal in our right
to that freedom; and there our likeness ended. From that moment
diversity became the rule, as each of us little artists fumbled—the
artist always fumbles—never quite sure—and the artist is never
quite sure—what that world would be like, that unique private
creation. Nor, as we looked back in wonder, would we ever know
how or why we had made our choices, accepted this color, form,
sound, why we rejected that. But we had soon discovered that there
was also a public world, ready-made, stern, demanding. Some of us
gave up at once; not quite, of course, a few small desires we kept
secret or painted in acceptable colors; but the artist in us was dead
or dying. Some did not give up...only we learned now what



12                       Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 37 ---
could not find its way alone, what [we] needed: how. There were
  then full-grown artists, whatever the calendar said.
      But between the vincible, who had surrendered to the public
  world, and the invincible, who would        not, there were those who
  began to ask why they had given up, unable to take their submis-
  sion as final and yet not knowing how, or whether indeed, they
  might become artists once again. It would not mean creation, they
  sadly knew, but was it just barely possible that there could be such
  a thing as having another try, this time at re-creation?
       It was for these that Black Mountain was founded. The invin-
  cible would of course be welcome too, but all they needed was free-
  dom. They would also, we hoped but with justified doubt, serve as
  examples. But to the faltering or even lapsed artists we meant to
  give—I borrow a phrase from Henry Allen Moe, the Guggenheim
  Moe—a “leg-up.”
      I had seen it work when I was a boy at Webb School in Tennes-
  see. Old Sawney would say to a newcomer, “A boy, any boy de-
  serves another chance. I don’t know anything about you, son, and |
  don’t want to know.” The context was of course different, but I saw
  wonderful things happen to some of the unscreened. (Where can
  they go now?)




  No artist can explain his art, because his art is himself. Here the
  Heisenberg principle holds: when he tries to look at writers and art-
  ists as himself what he sees is a self that doesn’t exist as artist. The
  artist is a lover and his art is his girl. But, doggone it, he is himself
  his girl. The pointed finger says, “Ah-ha, he is self-centered, you
  mean. He worships himself.” Of course, and he wouldn’t be worth
  two cents if he didn’t. But no artist is all artist, always or com-
  pletely. He is also like you and me, subject to headache and leg-
  ache and other minor pains, and minor pleasures. He has eyes to
  see with and he sees, ears to hear with and he hears, even though
  he be deaf; he also has a mouth and it’s his mouth that gets him in
  trouble. Not content with using it for the ingress of food or for talk-
  ing about things that we lapsed artists talk about, he foolishly tries
  to talk about his love, himself as lover. Ask him how it feels to be a




_ An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                      13

--- Page 38 ---
lover and all you'll get is a catalogue of symptoms. “Odi et amo,”
says Catullus [“I love and I hate’—opening of the poem to Lesbia,
his beloved], and Oh brother is he right. “How shall I compare thee
to a summer’s day?” How indeed, forgetting swelter and rain? “My
love is like a red red rose.” Yeah, not omitting the thorns. Symp-
toms, never the thing itself. One of the boys at Black Mountain told
me he was in love with one of the girls. I said, “How            do you
know?” He said, “When I see her I get sick to my stomach.”
     No, the artist is never   quite sure what is happening,       and is
quite incapable of knowing what will happen. This is why the foun-
dations, with the exception of the Guggenheim,          shy away    from
him. Nor can he even know what has happened; he will, whatever
his wish, impose the self now upon the self then. Again the Heisen-
berg principle is at work: the self now changes the self then, dis-
torts, twists, and sometimes inflates it, to a likeness of the self now.
For he, as the rest of us, hates to admit that he doesn’t know what
he did, or why.
     That is the job of the critic, who is by nature a scientist, and the
competent critic, as scientist, can tell us, and the artist, what hap-
pened, not how....


Josef Albers
While the gold diggers were splashing and dribbling their way to
fortune, Albers was content with little. He never pushed his wares.
When   he was asked to show his paintings to a visitor, he did so
willingly. But he never made the offer, nor ever hinted that an invi-
tation would be welcome.        He was, unprodded,       singularly un-
worldly. ...
     We were told little about his stay at the Bauhaus, and, once
told, seldom heard again. With these exceptions: how no one had
noticed him there until he had scavenged pieces of glass—the bot-
tom of a green blown bottle I remember well—and made a leaded
pouch, which he still had and was always willing to exhibit; his
other boast—ominous to our ears—that during the whole time
there he had not allowed his wife to unpack all their belongings—
they might be moving any day....
     Albers’ early drawings were representational, but even at that



14                     Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 39 ---
time not personal. The same sense of line was in those early works.
 but otherwise they might have been done by another competent
 artist.I saw them not by design on his part, but because, for some
 strange reason, he sent theminto Asheville by invitation, to an ex-
 hibit by local painters. There was little of what I had become accus-
 tomed     to in his work, the work which will some day perhaps be
 called his middle period.
      At that time I saw only that, conventional art. Later I had rea-
 son to suspect that his articulated ideas about art in general came
 from the same period; for, as expressed, they hardly forecast the
 modern. The evidence was a paper he wrote... at the College. I
 read it with amazement. It was quite unrelated to what he was
 doing at the time, almost alienated from his and other contempo-
 rary art. A critic, not knowing the writer, would have placed him,
 in fact, in the nineties. He wrote it with the intention that it be the
 foreword to a new catalog for the College.
     But some of us, knowing that it was hardly a statement of what
 the College was trying to do, played a trick on him. We told him,
 we treacherous friends, that it was much too good to be buried in a
 college, that a national magazine was its appropriate place. It
 worked; for us at least. It was never published. I am not sure that it
 was even offered to our editors; but, whether or no, one thing was
 to be said of it: that it was literate, and quite clear, which is more
than can be said of most writing by the moderns. They write
 drivel....
      He had brought with him many drawings, and these he was
 willing to show. Most of them were pure abstractions, concerned
 with the interplay of black and white. There was, in contrast to con-
 ventional   drawing, none   of what   the usual draughtsman     would
 think of as background. The white was as active as the black. This
 was his conscious intention, for he was always actively aware of
 what he called “rest-forms.” (He often complained, to the covert
 amusement     of the listener, of the inadequacy of the English lan-
 guage.) Instructed in the importance of rest-forms, I was able at last
 to see what bothered me ever since. But while most of his drawings
 were impersonal, with nothing whatsoever in them of humanity,
 some of them were startling in what I can only call their assault on
 the viewer. I told Albers that I found one in particular very disturb-



 An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                   15

--- Page 40 ---
ing. He said, with a glint of pleasant malice, “Don’t you think, Herr
Rice, you had better find out why?”
    When I was teaching in Webb School, a new boy was found to
be about half blind. He was taken to an oculist in Nashville and
fitted with glasses. When he got back he could not stay in a room,
he must be outside, seeing for the first time leaves on a tree, and
birds, and the stars at night. Albers was our glasses. When he came
to Black Mountain his love affair with color was already in full
spate; and color, I think, was his only love. He liked people, was
generous with his affections, but love was another matter. Try as he
would, he could not excuse it. For, while he could be serenely de-
tached almost, when he was looking at and talking about a draw-
ing—the black, the white, the “rest-forms’”—in the presence of
color his passion was to the onlooker almost embarrassing.
     But when it came to painting, he put colors on the canvas, not
himself. He would not, if he could, have used color, slyly or boldly,
to depict his own condition. Unlike his contemporaries, he was in-
capable of splashing and dribbling himself into public view. Like
the true lover of the poets, his concern was to display, and thereby
portray, the infinite variety of the beloved.
     But color was not all: color required form. His studio was evi-
dence. Most studios are messy places, globs of paint on the wall,
floor, and chair, palettes like relief maps, dirty smocks, and
blotched rags everywhere. Not Albers’: it was like a monk’s cell in
austerity. There was no invitation to mooning. There was work to
be done, and he was a tireless worker. There was little talk with the
intruder, and that was small. Talk was interference with his search
for perfect unity of color and form, and he was always searching.
He was therefore impatient with us gabbers. He did not maintain
that thought also requires form, and we were searching.
     An acute visitor, having seen Albers’ drawings in black and
white, said to me, “Wonderful studies in optics.” The paintings
were that, too, and more. Many viewers found them cold, untinged
with anything human. So they were, and so intended. They were
celebrations of color.
     He was self-taught, and fortunate in his teacher. But every
good teacher, even the great, has his tastes, to which he himself is
blind. Albers knew, and apparently felt the need to know, little of



16                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 41 ---
the past. I can recall, from the years I was his pupil, only one direct
 glance behind him: he referred to, and quickly disapproved,
 Goethe’s theory of color. But the past may beget blindings—I cite
 the academic mores as proof—and Albers may well have been right
 in his disregard, because of the peculiar form his art eventually
 took. (I use the word in its exact sense, as being his and his alone,
 uniquely without historical origin.) He had seeing eyes, the will to
 use them, and a hand to record what he saw. No one can ever for-
 get the delicacy and sureness of his touch.
      But the present can also dim one’s sight. Albers, and his wife,
 Anni, would ask, when in doubt, “Oh es modern ist?” and, if it was
 not modern, they turned away. . . . Even so, his sharpest disparage-
 ment was reserved for some of his German colleagues. We soon
 learned not to mention certain names, unless we felt in need of a
 row. Plagiarist was the mildest epithet.
     The Bauhaus had evidently not been paradise and Albers
 showed no inclination to building another on the foundation of
 memory.    Nor, whatever may have been a secret desire, did he so
 much as suggest, while I was there, that the College become an art
 school. His disapproval of me, which increased with the years, was
 not the certainty of my veto; it was more general, and frustratingly
 dual: the German    in him thought I acted with too little authority;
_ the artist, too much. I was grateful to the stasis, for in between the
 two was a man of great charm. He also had a great fund of affec-
 tion, which he spent freely. Not love. I doubt that he ever loved
 anyone,   and   there were   limits   even   to his affection.   He   could
 broach no revealing of colleagues in his profession; nor, I regret to
 say, of a student who began to grow his own wings. Odd.
      Maybe it was not odd. The artists I have known were most
 of them spiritual porcupines, quills up at the slightest threat; and
 the young area threat. In another age it may have been different.
 I do not know. I do know that nowadays the artist, any artist,
 thinks of himself alone. Agnes de Mille [choreographer who taught
 at BMC in 1944] says of dancers that they do not look at one
 another, they look off into space. The young have at that moment a
 choice: they may return—this was the hope at Black Mountain, to
 call them back—or they may retreat to loneliness. Some did return,
 haltingly at first, and did begin to re-create the world in which they



 An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                        te

--- Page 42 ---
chose to live; and others did not, and found ready models among
the lonely.
       Albers was not one of them, nor with them. He was not en-
gaged, as novelists and critics say many are today, in the “search
for identity”: he had never lost it. That was what I liked most about
him. He was tough, annoyingly tough at times, especially when my
haphazard ways were the target; conscious of himself, with noth-
ing in him of puling self-consciousness. Iwas tough, too, but with a
difference, I think, that led us away from a happy companion-
ship—for at first and for long we delighted in each other—a com-
panionship that was not only personal, but grew from our mutual
concern for the College. In that we were, and remained, one. But I
could not enter with all my heart his world, nor he mine. For the
world which I had begun in childhood to create for myself, and was
still creating, was peopled.
      I was born in the South and am a born Southerner. In the South
of my childhood and youth life was intensely personal. Ideas in-
duced a yawn, and things, excepting on occasion their extreme
scarcity; but let a name be mentioned, my tribe was off. I listened,
to words, tone, gesture, and I learned, as other small boys and girls
were listening and learning. (The Southern writers bear witness.)
What we learned was that, of all the world had to offer, people
were    the most interesting; so that when, years later, I heard So-
phocles say, “Of all the wonderful things, none is more wonderful
than man,” I said yes, yes.
     But the small boy creating his world was rejecting as well as
accepting, by what principle, if any, I do not know. I rejected
things; Albers people. Not entirely, of course, but in the order of
importance. I suspect that, confronted with that old chestnut of a
dilemma, “baby or priceless painting,” Albers would have endured
a moment of hesitation before rushing from the burning building
with one or the other tucked under his arm. I do him injustice. It
would of course have been’the brat—regrettably.
       Not that he was a closet-dweller, as any one would know who
ever saw him, as I did, gently persuading a timid young woman to
open her eyes and see. He believed deeply, that art was a sure way
to freedom,    the sunset.   Other ways   were   suspect.   “What   is the
lure,” he asked, “for any one to study economics?”          and his tone
answered the question. What indeed?

18                     Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 43 ---
When I left Black Mountain, Albers and I parted in silence,
loud silence. How could he say—for he always spoke the truth—
how could he say to me, “I am sorry, Herr Rice. I am also glad”?
And how could I have answered? But I see him now as I saw him
that first morning on the mountain path, and all that happened af-
terwards has found a quiet place in memory.


Love and Justice
Fortunate were those whose outside and inside were one; or be-
came one. It happened, at times slowly, quietly, or it might be in a
moment of crisis. I well remember a boy sitting with lifeless hands
dangling between his knees. I said, “Well, you know, this is it.” I
can also again see the dropped jaw of the boy, who had just told me
that, yes, he loved the girl, but she had insanity in the family. I
laughed. “What family hasn’t?” I asked him. “Mine has, an old
maid aunt and a daffy great uncle, and probably others.” Or the
blight in a girl’s question, “You don’t mean he’s stupid?”
     But a co-educational college is not necessarily a match box, as
Swarthmore was called . . . ; rather, it is a sample room. The trouble
with a women’s college is that there are too many men there, in the
thick imagination,   so it becomes   necessary   to [vacate] on week-
ave Leeper
     Nobody left Black Mountain on weekends. On Saturday night
there was dancing, to live music; very live music, bouncing, gay,
once in a while swooning, pounded out by John Evarts on the pi-
ano. He was no martyr, though by midnight he might be limp with
fatigue and was always decorated with sweat; he loved music, not
for itself, but for what it did to people. He was a Wagnerite until he
became aware that some noises tended to induce causticness.
     Love and sister hate. But, while hate concealed thrives on pri-
vacy, hate revealed requires support, the more the more burning;
else it becomes mere peevishness.
     And yet, for the commendation, love is not enough. Love is a
thing too frail to hold society together, or even a small community.
When I was a boy in South Carolina, our guest at a dinner was a
bishop whose wife we thought to be capable of all the sins one
could commit alone. (Unfaithfulness was beyond her reach.) I was
not listening to the grown-up talk until he said loudly, “Love is a

<

           duty. We are commanded to love.” My silent answer was a thun-
           dering “No.” If he could not, why should I even try? Years later a
           student at Rollins asked, “Is it true that you believe in free love?” I
           asked, “Is there any other kind?” and was reproved by a friend for
           my ambiguous answer. There is no other kind. Love is free and
           cannot be bound. Love bound is a weak and puny thing, the more



           20                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 45 ---
so the stronger the bonds. Love, being free, can work some mir-
acles, can even transfer hate into itself. But what about contempt?
For contempt is also free.
     No, something stronger is needed, something that is not free,
can be explained, made comprehensible to others: justice. For love
obeys no command, in spite of what the bishop said, but justice
does, justice must obey the command of thought. Without thought
there can be no justice. Thought is at the center of justice, the hard
core; and, although it can spread outward toward thoughtfulness,
compassion, mercy, pity, thought is dominant. | can tell, explain,
make clear what I think; but not love. That I can only describe,
warm with vague words, and hope that my hearer will know what
I mean. But I cannot demand a response to love, while to thought I
can. If any one had told me that I must love Senator [Joseph R.]
McCarthy, I would have been deaf. But if one had said, “Be just to
the man,” I would have felt obliged to try. To my feeling he was
inspissated evil; to my mind he was an artist, a great artist who had
created an amazing world for himself. How had he done that? I
would have to ask myself; how, out of the myriad offerings, had he
chosen evil so sanely? Love rejected him entirely; thought said not.
But it was only when thought had done its best that feeling should
have its say. The result would have been a mutual softening.
     When he was in New York [Louis] Adamic couldn’t wait until
morning for the paper, so we, he and [his wife] Stella and I, regu-
larly went to the nearest news stand at about eleven o’clock. We
also quite regularly had some kind of argument, with vehemence
on all sides. One night it was unusually harsh. It was during the
abdication crisis. Louis said, grinding his teeth, forcing the words
out—he was a stutterer who refused to stutter—”the damned fool.”
I said, mildly, “I don’t think so.” He said, “You don’t?” and the
guilty noise meant I was no different from Edward. So, in for it, I
said, “In fact, Ienvy him,” and before Louis could utter his outrage,
Stella said, “So do I.” Louis, now confronted with two fools, said
with elaborate courtesy, bitten off word by word, “Maybe you can
tell me why. Please do.” “Yes,” I said, “I can. I envy any one who
can love that much.” I still do, even when I see the haggard man
limping into the Waldorf luggage and all.
    This is concentration of love, personal, one to one; more com-




An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                  2]

--- Page 46 ---
mon, I suspect, than we know, for happy love is content to have no
history. But dispersive love, impersonal, of mankind in general, is
rare, so rare that no community can rely upon its being present.
Love between two may be enough; love among many is not.
     All for love, and well spent, we hear when a king is the lover.
But there are others, many, who are content, prefer to remain ob-
scure. Love for all is a different matter, vastly more rare. Those who
loudly profess to love mankind seem unable to see the trees for the
forest. We had some at Black Mountain, of various degrees of com-
mitment, on the staff—not among      the students; they were not so
foolish, at least not yet.


The Fireplace
It was around this fireplace we sat one winter night and heard So-
phocles tell the meaning of the death of Oedipus. When the last
word was spoken, we sat and then, one by one, moved away, silent.
On other occasions, many, warmed by the sight of its blaze, sat, not
around but away in another semi-circle, around a visitor and lis-
tened to his talk. On these evenings the nature of the speaker pre-
determined the nature of the fire. If he promised enlightenment, the
fire responded with silent light and warmth; but if the speaker was
a prospective bore, the fire was ready with another response.
     In the woods on the mountain side there were dead trees in
plenty, and members of the College who liked to, cut and sawed
and hauled and piled quarters around the fireplace, oak, and, for
special occasions, chestnut. For blight had travelled the length of
the chain and killed every chestnut tree. But with one exception,
the burning of chestnut was not preferred. On the afternoon before
a visitor would be speaking, I was asked, “Oak, or chestnut?” and’
the fire laid accordingly. Then, at night, while the visitor was being
introduced, some one lit the fire. Well, usually a sentence or two
were audible, until the fire.got going about its business. The noise
was sudden and loud, pop and crackle, splendid increasing noise,
as if a small boy had been given an unlimited supply of fire-crack-
ers. It was accounted a great evening when nobody could recall
having heard a word that was said.




22                      Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 47 ---
Equality
I came   out of childhood—well,    as far as I have got—loaded    with
prejudices. Some of them, on examination, I have suppressed: that
is to say, when they do revive, they have slipped by the guard. I no
longer believe the South won the war, really or unreally. I no longer
believe that white is better than black. . . . I no longer believe that
good manners are a sign of virtue, though I am pretty sure that bad
manners prove a lack. I no longer have any tolerance for the cult of
equality, which abridges liberty; and here I speak for every single
Southerner, whatever the color of his skin.
    The cult of equality got its beginnings, I think, in the nineties.
Until then, until about 1895, the common assertive saying was, “I’m
as good as you are.” Fair enough, for it put the burden of proof
where it belonged. But then came a change, and by 1930 we began
to hear, “You're no better than I am,” and the burden of proof was
where it did not belong.
     . . . [Jn education, during my childhood and youth, the cult
had not got a footing. Education, public and private, had still the
grace of inequality. In Charleston the Negroes were taught by the
ladies from the Aristocracy, and in Virginia they remembered that
Robert E. Lee, officer and gentleman, had ended his life the presi-
dent of a college.
      In Oxford there was of course no nonsensical talk about equal-
ity. My scout made it clear to me quite early that he and I were not
equals, and I had better not forget it. The don in turn also knew
how to rap a knuckle.
      It was not until I went to the Middle West that I met the cult
head on. I can still recall the churning laughter within me when a
professor of history boasted that he had got his first appointment at
Nebraska when the other candidate was Samuel E. Morison. He
also invited me to join his church, on whose platform—that was his
word—anybody could stand. It was also at Nebraska that I met my
first professor of education. In the intervening years I have not
changed my opinion.
    The cult is to be seen at its greenest in the teachers college, the
rotten spot in American education. But in that seed bed it should
flourish, for where else is nobody better than anybody else? From
here it spread a bland surface on all the public schools, while be-


An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                   23

--- Page 48 ---
neath that surface there rages a fierce conflict for status, based upon
the reality of inequality; but, being unacknowledged, it is savagely
undisciplined. In the classroom the cult robs alike the rich- and the
poor-minded.


Black Mountain’s Borrowings
: from Communists,      “From each, etc.”; from the Quakers, consen-
sus; from   Oxford,   teachers   not taskmasters,    outside    examiners;
from New England, the town meeting, everybody having his say;
from   Greece,   Socratic   following   of the   argument;     from   Webb
School, another chance; from Harvard, hospitality in ideas, how-
ever strange; from the South, its inheritance; from New           College,
“Manners Maketh Man.”




                                          Theodore              Dreier
                                                     (Faculty 1933-49)


                                             Early Close Calls



The College opened in September 1933. In July 1933 there had been
no financial planning. Mr. Rice had been given personally $1,000 by
the father of a student at Rollins who wanted to come with us if we
succeeded in starting a new college, to enable him and his family to
live over the summer and try to get the College organized. He had
persuaded an anonymous couple (friends of his and of mine who
had been on the Rollins faculty with us until a year before) to put
up an “underwriting” of $10,000 which we said we hoped not to
have to use because we intended to be self-supporting... . But there
was no plan or budget, and Rice and I met at the house of President
Aydelotte’s at Swarthmore (Rice’s brother-in-law) to try to plan
how to proceed. I was the most concerned at the lack of any finan-



24                      Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 49 ---
cial plan, and Aydelotte suggested to Rice that they had better
make me treasurer, which I thereafter never got out of for more
than a dozen years. Anyway, I stayed up all night making a budget,
and the next day, although Rice wanted to announce the new col-
lege immediately, he was persuaded that that was not a responsible
way to proceed, that we somehow had to be able to see our way
through at least one year before we could tell people to definitely
come. So we said that we could run for one year for $30,000 if we
had fifteen students who would pay $15,000 and had $15,000 addi-
tional in cash or underwriting. We agreed that we had to meet
these minimum conditions before we would publicly announce the
new college. This did not allow anything for faculty salaries, which
would only exist if more that fifteen students came. At the moment
there were a lot of students who said they wanted to come but actu-
ally only three or four who were willing to commit themselves. We
were expecting Josiah Spurr, a famous retired geologist who had
been on the Board of Overseers at Harvard, and then a professor at
Rollins along with us, to be our head (first called “president,” but
then his title changed to “rector”) because since the fight at Rollins,
it was thought better not to have Rice, even though he was obvi-
ously our real leader, as head. I was to be treasurer and I realized
that somehow I had to make sure of the basic ten-thousand-dollar
underwriting from our friends, and find $5,000 more at a mini-
mum, as well as to make sure of the fifteen or more students who in
aggregate would pay at least $15,000.
    This was the bottom of the Depression. We had all suffered 30
percent cuts our last year at Rollins because the president had been
unable to find enough money to pay full salaries. Money was al-
most impossible to find, but John and I set out independently,
though coming together from time to time, touring New York and
New England visiting mostly the families and friends of Rollins
students who wanted to come with us, and following up any leads
we could find for nailing down commitments of either students or
money and, equally important, additional teachers who would join
us if we started and enable us to round out our curriculum.
     The middle of August came, and Rice and I met again at
Swarthmore, this time alone. We had some very good news. Our
minimum conditions had just been met. Moreover, we had quite a



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                   20

--- Page 50 ---
number of very promising new teachers who were ready to join us,
receiving only room and board for pay, if we once gave the word
that we would go. Especially exciting was contact with Josef Al-
bers,   a remarkable   German   teacher from the Bauhaus    in Berlin,
which Hitler had just closed, who wanted to come to America and
who seemed to be just the kind of man we wanted in the arts. More-
over we had lined up an advisory council (one of our ideas was to
avoid having an outside governing board, and to organize with a
minimum of administration) of some distinction, whose names
were very reassuring to many parents of prospective students. But
there was bad news too: it seemed almost too late to announce a
new college to start next month! Most of the faculty who were
going to join us from Rollins had dropped out, and Mr. Spurr, who
was going to be our head, had a heart attack at just this time, and so
also had to drop out. Furthermore, before we could telephone the
good news to our underwriters that we were ready to start, they
telephoned us to say that they felt we should drop the whole proj-
ect because it obviously had no realistic chance of success. They
were not withdrawing their support—they wanted us to make the
decision—but they wanted there to be no mistake of how they felt.
Incidentally, they were members of our newly formed advisory
council. I told them that I would consult with Rice and let them
know within twenty-four hours. I did so. There were now only five
of us left from the original Rollins group of a dozen or so faculty
members    whom    we hoped would be with us. Of these five, two
were with us because they-had been on our side and stood by us in
the battle at Rollins rather than because they shared our educa-
tional ideas; and the other had not been heard from since the begin-
ning of the summer.
    The next two days were pretty wild. If a certain telegram had
come an hour earlier we would not have started. If I had not suc-
ceeded in nailing down a reporter on the Herald Tribune in New
York (on my way up to Lake George to get my family to drive
down to North Carolina) who wrote up a good story that got in the
morning paper, we would not have started. But we telephoned our
underwriters that we were starting, and although it still all but roi
lapsed twice, start we did. I drove my family down to Black Moun-
tain, essentially to stay for nearly sixteen years.



26                     Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 51 ---
How and why we moved to Lake Eden from Blue Ridge is an-
 other story. World War II brought on one serious crisis after an-
 other for the College, and several times it looked as though we
 would be unable to keep going in spite of our best efforts. Until the
 war   started   at the end of the summer        of 1939, the College had
 gradually been getting stronger and stronger in spite of some inter-
 nal dissension that culminated in the departure of Mr. Rice. The
 faculty had been greatly strengthened, and the student body too
 had grown almost as much as we wanted it to (about 75 to 80). We
 wanted to keep it under 100. The uncertainty of our tenancy at Blue
 Ridge (the YMCA, who owned the buildings, had never been will-
 ing to write any lease for more than one year at a time—and for
 only nine months at that) had led to our purchase of the 700-acre
 summer hotel property across the valley, locally known as Lake
 Eden, and we had invited the new architectural firm of Gropius
 and Breuer to design the ideal college campus for us at the new
 place. (We were good friends of both: Gropius, founder of the origi-
 nal Bauhaus in Germany was now head of the Architectural School
 at Harvard,     and   Breuer,   also   from   the   Bauhaus,   was   one   of
  Gropius’s new team of professors there.) Architectural plans were
_ made and a model built. Time Magazine published a story on the
  College showing a photomontage picture of the model in the land-
  scape, from which one could see how the new buildings would ac-
  tually look when completed. The model itself was displayed at the
  Museum of Modern Art in New York and there served as a feature
  for opening a money-raising campaign for a couple of million dol-
  lars (a huge sum in those post-Depression days) to build the new
  campus. But by this time World War II had already broken out. Af-
  ter a promising opening, the campaign fizzled, and it quickly be-
  came evident that it was not time to try to raise money. I had or-
  ganized the campaign while working on sabbatical leave at MIT,
  where I continued until the spring of 1940. But the fall of France
  really shook everybody at that time. Moreover, the YMCA had in-
  explicably refused to renew our lease for the following year. I left
  MIT and rushed back to Black Mountain to find that the YMCA was
  planning to start a new girls’ school and use the Blue Ridge build-
  ings for themselves. So suddenly the College had no place to go af-
  ter May 1940! The summer buildings at Lake Eden were far too



 An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                          27

--- Page 52 ---
small even if they had been heatable. The situation looked as bad as
having to start the College all over again, and with the threat of war
in the air, that would have been impossible.
     The YMCA    had difficulties in starting their new school so
quickly and agreed to let us have the buildings for one more year,
which we used to organize a new building program designed to
transform the Lake Eden property so we could use it. We planned
to do the building with our own hands, spending the $3,000 that we
figured we could spare beyond running expenses if we continued
to suspend faculty salaries (small payments were made to the fac-
ulty individually, based on their actual pressing needs) for a Far-
mall tractor ($1,000) and a freightcar-load of cement ($2,000), to
start the foundations of a large new building for student and fac-
ulty studies on the shore of Lake Eden. We would then raise money
to build on those foundations as well as to adapt for winter use the
buildings already there: a dining hall, a couple of lodges for dormi-
tories, and a few cottages in which some of the faculty could live,
by installing heating plants and insulating the buildings (the previ-
ous winter we had had a spell of sub-zero temperatures that lasted
two weeks).
    At first everyone thought the idea impossible and ridiculous.
But time was so short that we realized that unless we assumed it
could work and started immediately it would be the end of the Col-
lege. To let our wonderful College end without an effort to save it,
however,   was even more    ridiculous, so we started organizing.    It
was May, and we had one month to come up with a plausible pro-
gram. The first requirement obviously was to get together a team of
key people who believed it couldbe done, and to let them take it
from there. They would have to find and put in place the necessary
human and financial resources. We found a remarkable “modern”
architect named Lawrence Kocher (who had been head of the De-
partment of Architecture at the University of Virginia, and then
editor of The Architectural Record) who offered to come and make
[the] designing of the buildings we needed a part of an architec-
tural course that he would teach if we could find a special mini-
mum salary for him. (We persuaded the Carnegie Corporation to
supply it under their “Visiting Artist” program.) We also found a
remarkable German     laborer, Richard Gothe, who     had received       a



28                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 53 ---
Ph.D. in economics and [had] then run summer work camps for the
 American Friends Service Committee. He visited the College and
 spoke very convincingly to the community of what student labor
 could do, so that we hired him to teach economics and to direct our
 own program (he later turned out to be too autocratic to work ef-
 fectively with American    students, but in fact got us started, and
 then we were    able to take over ourselves).Finally, we found an
 unusual local contractor named Charlie Godfrey, who had built the
 reputation of working with his men, and had just done a very good
 job of building a large hotel in nearby Montreat, a southern Presby-
 terian resort. He had not had any work (as most other contractors
 at that time had not) for a long time, but his reputation was excel-
  lent, and he was intrigued at the idea of trying to teach students
  how to build. Charlie could direct work in most of the building
  trades, including stone and concrete masonry, welding, carpentry,
  plumbing, electrical wiring, and a good many other areas. He said
  that if he was allowed to have one professionally skilled helper,
  they could lay out work every morning so that when the student
  crews came to work in the afternoon, he and his helper would be
  able to direct the work.
       We couldn’t find anyone to undertake the money-raising nec-
  essary to buy materials, so I proposed that we start building the
  foundations, take pictures of the work accomplished as it pro-
 ceeded, and travel around to possible prospects showing them
  what we had done and asking for funds to continue. At that time
  everybody was wondering whether the great democracies could
  compete effectively with the dictatorships under Mussolini and
  Hitler, who were overrunning Europe. We claimed that we were a
  small democracy that was bound to show that we could mobilize
  ourselves to meet a crisis, and we did! (As the program actually got
 underway the following fall, I was invited to the White House to
 dinner with Mr. and Mrs. Roosevelt—and about twenty others—
and then after dinner to speak and show pictures of the students
 and faculty of BMC at work, to those who had not retired to work
 with the president. That did not in itself lead directly to any money,
 but it didn’t do any harm, and it certainly raised our spirits and
 helped us in that way to get the job done.) We had a summer pro-
 gram at Lake Eden almost right away, running a “work camp” with

           a number   of summer    courses, including one in architecture.   We
           were able to give Larry Kocher and his family a cottage, as well as
           to a number of other faculty members. By the end of the summer
           we had a large Studies Building designed, work begun on insulat-
           ing the summer buildings so that they could be used in winter, had
           the farm going in a good way (supplying all the dairy products and



           30                     Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 55 ---
most of the meat needed by the College) and were all set, except for
    money, to start construction of the new Studies Building.
         A crisis arose in the building program in September 1940, when
    it was unexpectedly found that mud pockets under the foundation
    trenches made it necessary to drive piles to support the new build-
    ing. We could perhaps cut piles in our own forest, but there was no
    way that we could afford a pile driver. We barely had the means to
    pay running expenses for food. The discovery on a Friday after-
    noon—just when the land had been drained and all the foundation
    trenches dug and ready to receive concrete the following Mon-
    day—faced me with about the worst dilemma I had ever had to
    face. The whole College was counting on starting to pour founda-
    tions right after the weekend—then we could begin to hope to take
    pictures of something that would show aboveground. After I
    brought Charlie over to the building site, I left him pondering while
    driving back to Blue Ridge trying to decide in my own mind what
    to do. It was a gloomy Friday evening and Saturday morning. What
    could we tell the students who were all so full of hope and rarin’ to
    go?
         But Saturday night Charlie called me up, and when I went
    down to his house he showed me a sketch of a pile-driving machine
    that we could build ourselves and power with our Farmall tractor.
   _It was made of a twenty-seven-foot-high double tower (made of
    two-by-twelves) built with vertical guiding tracks to stand astride
    our foundation trenches on wooden skids. He proposed to cut a
    500-pound black-gum log that wouldn’t split, haul it to the top of
    the twenty-seven-foot tower with a steel cable that could be wound
    up on a wide-flanged steel pulley that he had designed to fit on the
    tractor’s power drive, throw out the tractor clutch when the log got
    to the top of the tower with its guides, which would release the
    pulley, allowing the 500-pound log a free-fall of twenty-seven feet,
    which Charlie was sure would be enough to drive any piles that we
    had to drive. Being an engineer myself, I could see that Charlie
    knew what he was talking about. We revised our program for Mon-
    day so as to send all the students and faculty who were scheduled
    to work Monday afternoon up into the forest to cut piles instead of
    pouring concrete as they had expected.
        They were all pretty exhilarated when they heard what had



? 7 Bn Anthology of Personal Accounts                                 3]

--- Page 56 ---
happened, and how we were going to be able to go ahead anyway
with maybe only two weeks delay while Charlie built the pile
driver and started driving the piles that they were cutting. We
drove all the piles, poured all the foundations, started putting up
the wooden framing real fast so as much would show as possible,
took the pictures up north to the White House to show to people
we thought might help us, brought back the money, bought more
materials, and continued the cycle. Meanwhile     Larry and Charlie
directed the work and implicitly did some of the best teaching ever
done at the College, where practical and theoretical considerations
were both appropriately emphasized and put and kept in propor-
tion, so that one might indeed think that not only the buildings, but
the design and experience of constructing them were in a very fun-
damental sense works of art.
     There are dozens of items about Black Mountain that I could
bring to mind—such as the time we surprised Bimbus with a sec-
ond grand piano in her room at the end of the Studies Building, and
later when she and Kurt Apelbaum played together on them and
finally began to improvise together, half the College sitting outside
their door in the hallway, listening. What wonderful times we did
all have together there! And everyone so different and yet so real.
     Bobbie and I go ahead with each day and make the most of
whatever life at eighty-six has to offer, which seems to be opening
out into more and more. Any extra effort I spend now helping
Maharishi Mahesh Yogi to develop the potential silence and peace
latent in every individual. This really means that every one of us
has to learn how to be an artist.
