Josef Albers
                                                       (Faculty 1933-49)


                                        March       1965 Interview




  Interviewer: [Many think] that the students from BMC have been far
      more successful that the students from the Bauhaus, which is a
       true test of the school.
  Albers: Well that’s hard to say which ones are better, you know.
       Whether an art student develops now or later—in most cases
       the development shows later. And since at BMC weren’t only
       art students . . . to compare them with those from the Bauhaus
      [as to] which were more successful, I feel obliged to be careful
      not   to make   promises.   And   I’ve been   asked   several   times,
      “What about your successful students?”—though there are
      several names which have success. But this is not from me a
      measure. What I’m worth or not worth has to be decided later.
      And    as it is with me, so it is with all the younger ones who
      came to me. Let’s wait. To make a conclusion now already,
      whether the students here were more successful, I am inclined
      not to make a statement on it. Because I am not a prophet. Let’s
      see what will be. Maybe it takes time, longer than we both live,
      to find out where is a successful influence—and that is again a
      new question. ... Whom do we thank that Cezanne was some-
      time alive and did something important?
            You see, from my Bauhaus benefit (you know I was there
      longer than anyone else: thirteen years—three years a student)
      I would say I’m not a Bauhaus product. When it comes to an
      honest conceitedness, I would say I have done more to the
      Bauhaus than the Bauhaus to me. I have taken a course only—
      Itten—which was obligatory, but I have not studied with more
      closer people, people I admire much more: Klee and
      Kandinsky. I have not studied with them. I was thirty-two


_ Interview courtesy of the Josef Albers Foundation, Inc.



 An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                       33

--- Page 58 ---
years old when I went to the Bauhaus. I had already quite some
     years of the academy and art school behind me. But I was tired,
     and went to that place to-start anew with practical work; there-
     fore I went there. But that I am a preserver of the Bauhaus I do
     not believe is true. And I have always made a special effort not
     to follow my successful neighbors or teachers. I knew when I
     came to this part of the world there was a great imitation of all
     the successful ones, as it is now true. You see, at that time was
     it Picasso, Matisse, and Klee, and we called that Picasso-itus,
     Matisse-somnia, and I added Klee-tomania. But that was an in-
     teresting development that stopped suddenly because there
     was still something easier to do—that was self-expression! You
     see the bandwagon of Kandinsky is small, the bandwagon          of
     Matisse is relatively small, and you compare the bandwagon of
     abstract expressionism! You know, when you see these thou-
     sands riding behind the few successful ones, that was such a
     bandwagon affair—it is frightening. You see that [abstract ex-
     pressionism] was easier than Klee or Kandinsky or Matisse or
     Picasso; therefore the bandwagon was so big.
Interviewer: Then, when you reached BMC, what did you try to give
     the people?
Albers: Well, I indicated already that when they asked me to come
     to this country I said on the telegram answer I don’t speak Eng-
     lish, you know. And then they sent a telegram: Come anyway.
     So we were glad to leave. I told you before how that went first
     very hopelessly at the consulate, and then suddenly with a
     great speed. When I came here now some of the students asked
     me what I want to do, what I planned to do to teach art. And I
     said: “To open eyes.” That was probably my first conscious
     answer and connection with my aims, with my job: to BpPe
     eyes. And I think that I have stuck to this..
Interviewer: And when you had a class, how did ites Did you have
     many students or a few’at a time, maybe one or two... ?
Albers: You see, BMC was never a big college. Was always a very
     small one. Experimental. We had sometimes a proportion of
     student and teacher as was nowhere in the world. You see. .
Interviewer: Two to one...
Albers: It was terrific! In the beginning very small, and as one had to



34                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 59 ---
expect,   there were   girls first because   the boys at that time
        thought that’s only for girls—art—for boys that was “sissy.”
        But the interest was so that they came more and more, even the
        faculty joined my classes: So I had always a nice bunch. I made
        it exciting. And I didn’t need tricks: to have just naked girls,
        you know. Because I do not believe in that for drawing, what I
        consider very basic study, very basic. But not as a necessary
        preparation for painting. No, I considered this a marvelous
        general education means, because it makes you really learn to
        observe visually—in order to transfer anything we see outside
        in lines—for drawing means in lines, first, mainly. You see, that
        is abstraction of a very stiff kind to do. And as I say all the time,
        when it comes to nudes, nudes have no lines. You see, the con-
        tours we see around change with every move of us, they go
        with us. The roundness, you see, roundnesses—what            is most
        life-drawing is females. They are the least ones to reveal lines
        because of their fat, or their roundness.     Where are the lines?
        The lines are with the legs. There is a line, you might say. Be-
        tween the lips there is a line. That’s all the lines we have for the
        human figure. To put that into a line is a long, long study and
        needs lots of preparation. But at BMC we did quite some draw-
        ing and there I found out it is not just a matter of aiming with
        the eye. It’s also an aiming with our motor sense. Know what
        that is? It is our muscle sense, our coordination, what keeps us
        upright. The motor sense is very much involved. I demon-
        strated. I remember shooting abroad—it’s always this eye—
        aiming with the eye before you pull the trigger. But in America
        the shooting is with the arm moving through the air without
        aiming. The motor sense aims in the right direction. That’s a
        matter of feeling, not of seeing. You don’t watch what the arm
        does, no. You go by the feeling within the arm, you see. And
        this motor sense business I have developed, particularly here
        [at Yale], still more, and that indicates already that I was not
        just repeating Bauhaus stuff. I tried in a new part of the world
        with another part of youngsters, another mentality, to develop
        something independent. And that was my great fortune: that I
        was exposed to a new mentality, to new conditions, to a very
        different curiosity. And I was often asked which students I pre-


=)
     An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                    35

--- Page 60 ---
ferred—here or abroad. And I said often, most times: here. Be-
     cause their imaginations are very eager, terribly eager. But you
     have to put everyday a different way to them. With my dra-
     matic performance, you know that’s enough to do it: present
     the same problem a thousand times.
          So one of the first experiences I brought into their minds
     was in the beginning drawing in the air. One of the first classes
     I [told them] to write your signature, what you put underneath
     your check, and then write it in the air. And I wrote my name
     in the air. But so that they could read it—not for my reading. I
     did it backwards, and then they didn’t know what I am doing,
     so I went to the blackboard and wrote it. Who got it? Who
     didn’t get it? Be honest. Who got it now? Let’s admit that we
     are visually underdeveloped. Now write your name into the
     air. Now try whether you can do it backwards. Ja? Now you
     see you must know that the motor sense helps us do this. You
     can also do this visually.
          Then I can do this: this is an A in normal position. This is
     an V in another position, upside down. Now I try to write my
     name upside down. Can you do that? All right. That’s not for
     the sake to swindle. Now you see there are already three ways
     of writing our signature. For what? Means to the right, in west-
     ern civilization. Leftward. Now, upside down, please. Now, do
     also the leftward upside down. You see, I can do it in all four
     ways of painting. And then they saw the arm was willing. I
     made them these forms. We repeat the forms, we reverse the
     forms, upside down, downside up, and so we get in training
     not only for the eye, but for the arm too.
          And so I came to another way to make them aware, and to
     experience right away—when I suddenly can visualize with
     closed eyes how my name looks in the mirror. That's imagina-
     tion. Not to claim I can see something else. Most imagination
     doesn’t claim, but really visualize so that you can show it with
     your hands your writing how it will look in the mirror. Ja?
     How it will look in the water. You see, that’s again another mir-
     roring. And so all those little ups and downs, leftside right—
     and these exchanges—they were for me at that time such an
     innovation that I tried to develop that on, independent of any



36                     Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 61 ---
other teaching. And that was not done at the Bauhaus—not
       even by myself. Here, in this new part of the world, I felt
       obliged to start a new life on my own, and a new life that was
       related to the mentalities here, and so I developed drawing.
            That was already the time when self-expression came up,
        very much lubricated by John Dewey. Well, I do not believe in
       him so much. What is this self-expression when you make a pot
       on the table, you make the opening of any horizontal standing
        round   thing? But a horizontal circle, foreshortened, is an el-
       lipse. And I took several weeks to make the eye aware under
       what circumstances does a foreshortened circle look different
       than a horizontal ellipse. Several weeks just to study ellipses. It
       looks for the girls terribly boring, but after a while they say: my
       eye sees now the world a little more intensively than I have
       done before. That was what we at BMC [did]—at least I consid-
       ered it my obligation. It was practical work.
            You know we did our own building. And we had our own
       farming. So the girls and the boys they knew each other sweat-
       ing. And not like here, when you come Saturday evenings to-
       gether with new makeup only. Ja? You see, to get respect for
       the other one when he is not just in the highest makeup (that is
       something else: it’s “Morning Mood”). And the [sweating,
       working] made some people, put them in the right place, on
       their own feet.
            When a dilettante takes a nail to put into that board for
       nailing, a dilettante first tries to make that nail stand vertical on
       that board, and then tries to hammer it. That shows that s/he
       has never observed a carpenter. A carpenter takes the nail
       slanting in the direction of the curve of the hammer. So when
       the hammer comes down on the nail the direction goes—the
       continuation of that direction is—through the nail, and not of
       the nail, what ends on the nail, the finger. So when I told that
       girl, please watch the nail slanting in the curve the hammer is
       doing in the air, then you will see—Ahhh! These little experi-
       ences [are called] “stealing with the eyes.” I tried to develop—
       according to my promise here—to open eyes.
            I might just end with one more [example]. You see, I
       thought carpentry is a very wonderful handicraft—with the



“ ~ An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                    37

--- Page 62 ---
most material considered. When they made bookshelves—the
     boys—they took a vertical board and a horizontal board and
     put the nails through the vertical board into the horizontal
     board. That’s the greatest nonsense when it comes to carpentry,
     because the nails go parallel into the long grain. It falls apart.
     The nail has to go across the direction of the grain. That’s easy
     to understand. I made them “steal” with the eyes.
          We had a carpenter from Black Mountain come up once a
     week to show them what means: thinking in wood. What
     makes you think in wood? So I said to the master, you saw a
     board, and don’t make any comment       on the doing. You do as
     you always saw, as all carpenters do. And he did that. And
     then I said, “Who is willing to do what he did?” The first girl
     was most eager; she took the saw, held the board here, and the
     saw there. “You have not watched,” I said. “Next one.” He has
     not watched. He didn’t see that the thumbnail is the lead for
     the saw. It’s always going along there. ..    . “Oh, oh, oh, why
     didn’t you tell us to do that?” I said, “No, I want you to find
     out yourself. I don’t want you to listen to me and forget what
     goes into the one ear through the other ear.” If you have expe-
     rience and have done wrongly and know why it is wrong, and
     have done it rightly—that sticks with you. And not by reading
     printed matter. You have to watch. .. . All this practical work is
     of thinking in situations. And I made that statement once at a
     faculty meeting; thinking in situations. Two of the professors
     were grinning. I said, “What do you smile around?” “It doesn’t
     exist thinking in situations.” I said, “No, not for bobokworms.”
          This is just a little sample of how I developed here, and
     handling of the youngsters by making them excited to see that
     experience is the most exciting and the most lasting way of
     study. And not spoiling your eyes with constant printed mat-
     tele                      ‘
Interviewer: What factors contributed to the success of the College,
     in general? [Was it] possibly this working relationship between
     the student and faculty?                                     :
Albers: One of the best principles there was that practical work has
     a direct influence on our behavior. That was done at the farm,

     An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                   39

in building, in keeping the roads in shape. I was the oldest pro-
     fessor there, but I was in charge of the roads. . . .
Interviewer: What factors contributed to the failure of the school, the
     failure to continue on?
Albers: | would say that was the result of a basic changing in atti-
     tude. In the beginning was a most exciting affair because most
     of us were refugees. We didn’t have salaries—a little pocket
     money. But later came this fashion of economics, our greatest
     fear in human learning. Everyone has the same rights, every-
     one has the same function. It was in the end so that a young
     teacher with a wife and two children who has never taught got
     double the space we had—the two oldest. Of course, why not?
     But they had also to have double the money. And that was so
     automatic and so done that it didn’t work. There is no equality
     among people, neither mentally or physically. As we look dif-
     ferent, so we are different, you see. This theory of equal posses-
     sion won’t solve any problems in the world. That is my convic-
     tion. That was why a few wisecracks there—they considered
     themselves competent in economics; therefore they were com-
     petent for all human tasks, for everyone. And that led to fric-
     tion. There were particularly a few such guys, who treated the
     colleague of one of the founders so badly—though they had
     promised to keep me alive after a few years. But I quit. That
     was   the end. In 1949. Several   of us left, and   that was   the
     “down” of the College.
          You see, it was a misinterpretation of democracy. In de-
     mocracy everyone has to vote. .. .So we had meetings; endless
     meetings. Today yes, tomorrow no. It was so blown up this so-        .
     called democracy that it just proved impossible. There are
     things that you don’t discuss with babies what they have to eat.
     That’s the same parallel to education. That they have to talk
     about everything—this is just nonsense. Today that’s the rea-
     son why the children rely on the parents. They have no educa-
     tion, unfortunately.
          BMC went the way it deserved. Too many incompetent
     people playing competent, you know. And that is teenager
     style. When that goes into grown-up people, that’s just hell.
     And everyone knows better than everyone else. Learn to admit



40                     Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 65 ---
that sometimes you have to shut up. Why don’t they have juve-
    nile delinquency in the Asiatic people in this country, the Japa-
    nese children, and the Chinese? Why? Because they all have
     respect for their parents’ And we will learn that soon that this
     is the sign of being educated: able to behave in relationship,
     and not like mules.
Interviewer: Do you think that an experiment like BMC could suc-
     ceed now?
Albers: Ja, 1would say. I’m inclined to say yes because I still believe
     in exposure to practical work; and this exposure to art means
     such a broadening compared with only book reading and lis-
     tening to professors, who have only the ambition to teach
     graduate students instead of undergraduates. . . . I believe that
     the babies have to have the best teachers. And the graduates?
     They don’t want teachers at all. They want to do their own
     nonsense. “Me, teaching undergraduates? Never.”
Interviewer: Very short-sighted, to say the least.
Albers: That is the art: to develop the little ones.

                            March 1965 Interview:
                    Teaching Weaving and Design



Anni Albers: I developed a weaving workshop there [at BMC]. I
    tried to develop weaving on a more professional basis so that
    the students would be equipped, possibly, to go on either to
    teaching or as designers. That meant that I tried to develop a
    rather basic approach to the problem of construction of

AStien.



Anni Albers and student in Weaving Room. © 1986 the Estate of

                weaves, emphasizing the structure, rather than what is sO eas-
                ily done in handweaving, the decorative side. Some of my stu-
                dents, especially the boys, turned out to be very inventive in
                the way of construction, and I had several of my students who
                graduated in the field of textiles. And we had really to develop
                our own method of establishing a level of performance, to
                find—as in other fields at BMC—the outside examiners for this

           42                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 67 ---
topher Turner, who then was a young teacher in landscape de-
   sign at Harvard, to come down, and he was one of the ex-
   aminers of Don Page, who you may see in New York. I can’t
   remember now the other examiners. We had two or three,
   probably in each field, and I had at least two or three students
   who graduated in textile design. One of them was Lore Kad-
   den. She was in industry, for several years, making designs.
         My interest in textiles is threefold. I have done designing
   for practical purposes, like drapery materials, upholstery mate-
   rials, and so forth. And I’m still busy doing that to a degree. I
   have made and am making designs for Knoll Fabrics. They
   have some of my materials.
         The second category are my pictorial weavings. I don’t call
   them tapestries because this usually means a specific tech-
   nique. And I use so many mixed techniques that in order to
   avoid confusion I am no longer using the term “tapestries.” I
   have made those which I consider single pieces, that is, art, in
   the direction of art, let’s say, of any of my Bauhaus days. One
   of them, for instance, is at the Busch-Reisinger Museum at Har-
   vard.
       And then a third concern is the teaching of weaving, for
   which I have developed some new ways of developing this tac-
   tile sense, which has been lost to a large degree because of our
   always using ready-made materials: cellophane, wrapped
   bread, instead of the dough—and so on. I am not any more in-
   volved in the process of making, and therefore it is necessary to
   reestablish—perhaps, you can call it artificially—some of this
   involvement in the doing, which I call preweaving, pretextile
   studies. And it is in fact one chapter in my book, of which I told
   you, which is on weaving, and where I refer back to those stud-
   ies which were made at BMC.
        In BMC, really at a time when the then-new buildings at
   Lake Eden were going up, I had to teach a class without any
   roof over my head, or looms—no looms were available. And
   we had to do something that could be done by just sitting
   down together, and we started by selecting grasses and seeds,
   and so on, and putting them together in specific orders, which
   are textile orders, and not orders of the sculptor or the metal

man. And since then, I have been lecturing in many parts of the

          [At BMC] I think one important thing was that we had the

     a community, and they just couldn’t play around on the out-
     side so very much. Also, we believed in really disciplined
     work, and we tried—just as my husband must have told you—
     to go back to the basic concerns, you know.




                                 Peggy Loram Bailey
                                         (Faculty 1933-35; 1938-39)


                                             “Schon, schon”



As a member of the first faculty at Black Mountain, I sometimes feel
that the first two years of its inception were perhaps the most excit-
ing and creative. But I suppose the most important thing that hap-
pened to me was that it was there I met my future husband. But
there were many other things: the extraordinary impact of wonder-
ful teachers like John Rice and Josef Albers, the sense of community
life, the innovative   methods   of study, the influx of personalities
such as John Dewey,      Thornton   Wilder, Marcel   Breuer, the em-
phasis on the arts, and the magnificent scenery of the Blue Ridge 7
Mountains.
    I took an elementary drawing class with Albers. I think I was
the only faculty member among the students, who were all much
more skillful than I. We worked in pencil and charcoal, using draw-
ing boards for easels, and the class met on Saturday mornings for
two hours or maybe three. The first assignment was to draw an
ordinary glass milk-bottle. In this day of the ubiquitous carton, one
doesn’t encounter any more heavy glass milk-bottles, but at that
date they were omnipresent. It is a very hard object to draw—get-



44                     Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 69 ---
ting the exact symmetry for the lines of the bottle’s neck was very
tricky. I had to have several sessions of struggle before I was able to
meet Albers’s demands. He used to come up behind one, and if the
drawing was going well, murmur, “Schén, schén.” That was the
supreme accolade. After the milk-bottle phase was overcome, I was
allowed to go on to sketching folds of drapery and then a large
pitcher. Albers was the best teacher I ever had; he had an almost
mystical feeling about the discipline of line. He would point things
out to the class, crying, “Is it to see?”




                                    Norman          B. Weston
                                                   (Student 1933-38)


                                    “But what did you do
                   with that Kandinsky print... ?”



The Alberses arrived at BMC in the fall of 1933. Someone picked
them up at the station in Black Mountain, probably Ted Dreier, and
drove them up to Lee Hall. Nan and I were in a group standing on
the porch waiting to greet them. I vividly remember their funny-
looking clothes and Juppi’s very odd haircut. A pleasant picture to
have in mind. Of course, they didn’t have a hundred words of Eng-
lish between them, and Anni had most of those. After some study
of the English Language, Juppi made a trip to the village, walked
into a store, and greeted the clerk, “Good-bye.”
    I was not an art student by any stretch of the imagination, but I
learned a lot from Albers about order and precision in ideas, phi-
losphy, and physical things. Working with the printer and Albers
on the annual report was a lesson in precision that I’ll never forget,
and a standard of quality that was entirely new to the printer.
    When BMC opened in 1933 everything had to be decided.




An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                   45

--- Page 70 ---
There was no schedule to follow. What time to have breakfast?
Should there be separate floors for men and women? How many
meals should be served on Sunday? (“What do you mean, how
many meals; three, of course!” But some thought two would be bet-
ter, and it got to the point that the three-mealers wouldn’t speak to
the two-mealers.)
     John Rice was the major force in the early years, while Ted
Dreier raised most of the money and kept the place going from a
practical point of view. I am indebted to both of them for much of
what I learned at BMC, and I think it was a lot. However, as time
goes on, Albers seems to stand out as a truly great teacher.
     I only talked to Albers once after leaving BMC in 1938, and that
was to ask him to come to Detroit to make a speech. It was a couple
of years before his death. His response was, “Norman, I have so
much to do I can’t possibly take the time to come out there. But
what did you do with that Kandinsky print you bought?”
     The print was part of a set I bought for five dollars, forty years
before our conversation. He remembered.




                                    Doughten Cramer
                                                   (Student 1933-37)

                              A Hike to Craggy Dome



The College’s setting was extraordinarily important to me. The
mountains of western North Carolina are beautiful beyond descrip-
tion, and it is as if the atmosphere of the College was consciously a
part of the living beauty. It made one sensitive to everything.
     I took many walks in the mountains while I was at BMC, some-
times overnight, sometimes for a mere thirty minutes. They con-
stantly unfolded scenes of varying loveliness. A few yards from
Robert Lee Hall one could plunge into real wilderness filled with



46                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 71 ---
birds, squirrels, deer, even wildcats and bears. It was     an experi-
ence, especially for a city dweller, to venture into such a rugged
unknown. For my part, I came from an urban area and had never
camped out before. The hike to Craggy Dome was my first real ex-
perience in wilderness, so it became an important adventure for
me. Later on, we took many overnight trips in the mountains, and
these trips, taken alone or with other students and faculty mem-
bers, became an important part of my growing up.
    At nine o’clock one October morning in 1933, Frank Rice and I
decided to take a hike to the top of a mountain called Craggy
Dome, directions to which had been given to us by a farmer named
Allen, one of the the thirty-one people in the initial college commu-
nity. The dome was at one end of a formation called “the Saddle,”
which could be seen from the front porch of Lee Hall. The climb
would be to an altitude of 6,053 feet.
      From the College, the valleys that cut back through the moun-
tains can’t be seen. But these valleys are very beautiful. The one
Frank and I walked through had the Craggies towering on the left
and in front, while on the right was a checkerboard of plowed
fields, which covered some low-lying mountains like a quilt. Frank
said it reminded him a bit of Germany.
     Aiming for an area known as the Asheville Watershed, Allen
had told us to keep right all the way. But there were so many small
roads to the right that we weren’t sure where to turn. After we
were convinced we were lost, we came to a very dirty house with
some very vicious dogs trying to get at us through the fence. The
din of their barking rushed the whole family to the front porch. We
inquired of Pa how to get to the Asheville Watershed, and he gave
us some very complicated directions, something like: “Well, you
miss one mountain and go to the right of the next one, which’ll be
an eye-see away. You'll cross a stream called Sugar Fork three
times while you skirt a cliff. Keep yourselves always to the right.
But the first road you want to take will be on your left.”
     We thanked him and started on again, and sure enough did
find the road to the left. Gradually the road narrowed and turned
into a faint trail, which seemed to go into the very wild of the wilds.
I have never heard such still woods. If we hadn’t seen the marks of
other human feet every once in a while, we would have felt lost



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                   47

--- Page 72 ---
again. The trail seemed to go through an expansive arch of trees,
like a huge tunnel underground. Some places there was no sun and
it was very chilly, and then we’d come out into an open place filled
with black briars where it would be very warm. The trail seemed
easy enough to follow, but after a mile or so it began to steepen. As
the climb became steeper, it also became more beautiful. The trees
appeared against the dark rocks of the cliff; the effect was a joy to
see. We took all of this in as we sat down to eat at one-thirty.
     After lunch we continued until we reached the stream the
mountaineer said we were to follow all the way to the top, and
which was called Sugar Fork. It certainly was a lovely sight, like so
many streams in mountains, rushing merrily along to its distant
and ultimate goal. We crossed Sugar Fork three times within a
short distance, and each time we drank long and appreciatively
from its cool water. This was the best water I have ever drunk, so
much sweeter than other water. It had a soothing quality which I
have never tasted since. I think the water we drank was the only
thing [that] gave us strength to reach the top, for the path by this
time seemed one long stepladder, a continuous forty-five-degree-
angle climb.
    We rested many times, the ascent was so stiff. We’d climb until
we could go no further, and then rest. After about five minutes,
we'd start again, and the first ten steps seemed as easy as at the
beginning of the hike. But soon we'd have to stop again. This went
on. Finally, after what seemed like an endless physical and mental
trial, we reached the top. No matter how much we both endured on
the climb up, the view was incredibly worth it. In almost all di-
rections we could see as far as the atmosphere permitted, overlook-_
ing mile on mile of mountains and valleys until all was swallowed
up by a veil of mist. We felt very small indeed.
     I felt I could have rested there forever, I was so weary. But
what a great feeling to enjoy stretching out, looking over such a
gorgeous panorama. After spotting some local and familiar sights,
including the College building, we realized that it was 5:00 p.m. and
had begun to get cold. And just as the sun began to set we started
down the mountain, as fast as our weary legs would permit. It took
us less that an hour to get down the same distance it had taken us
three hours to climb. Near the place where the trail ran into the



48                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 73 ---
road, we were hailed by two men, one with a warden’s badge on.
We thought we were in trouble because all through the woods “No
Trespassing” signs were posted. But instead of the heavy hand of
the law descending upon us—the gods be praised!—they offered us
food and conviviality. After this unexpected refreshment and con-
versation, plus a future invitation to join them any weekend for a
bear hunt, we said our farewells and took off.
     The last miles in the dark were some of the most torturous
miles I have ever walked. I couldn’t get my legs to do what I
wanted them to do, I was so tired. Every house we went by would
have dogs who'd start barking when we got within half a mile of
them, and then continue barking until we were half a mile beyond.
Thus our slow progress could be followed. I think we aroused ev-
ery dog in the valley.
     I never felt better than when we reached the town of Black
Mountain, where we got a lift for part of the remaining distance,
which seemed like eternity itself. We finally arrived at 9:00 p.m. I
immediately took a hot shower and went to bed. The sound sleep
was marvelous when I awoke the next day refreshed. Frank and |
figured we had covered about twenty-six miles on this trip. Two
days later, I would have been glad to do it again.

     “Good evening, Ladies, Gentlemen.
     Tonight we hear the semifinal lecture
     Of our series on the history of
     Now defunct Black Mountain College.
     Tonight we have as our guest speaker
     A local scholar, often called
     Black Mountain’s Gibbon,
     A student of decline and fall.
     Ladies and Gentlemen:
     Vergil Proudfoot!”

     Vergil Proudfoot bows, arises.
     The audience greets him with applause,
     Clapping with both hands, because
     One hand without the other cannot clap.
     Vergil Proudfoot, he from the lecture stand—
     “Thank you, gentle folk,
     Thank you, Cassius.
     It’s an honor to be here tonight.
     My topic is, as you already know,
     Black Mountain College: The Middle Year.
     Cassius, please help me find my manuscript.
     Could be I left it in the locker room.”
     Cassius, he to Vergil Proudfoot—
     “Just a moment, Ill go back and see.”
     Exit Cassius into the locker room,

Randy for the stour.

     “Upon the stand the noble Rector sits,
     Behind him stand the noble Board of Fellows.
     Prima donnas all, all exit-prone.
     The Student Moderator slumps
     In patient inattention.


     “Trumpets shriek, the troops tramp out in time

     (The music’s good, those Bauhaus carls

     “The Partisans of Order’s troops tramp
     In faultless 4/4 time.
     Like polished andirons gleam their polished helms,

     “The Partisans of Art trip out
     On dancing feet, hand in linkéd hand,
     A linkéd line of little echoes,
     Ranked in a quadrate echelon—
     A homage to the square.

     “Poetry’s Partisans troopers’ feet,
     Shod in cothurni of ancient Greek and Roman tragedy,
     Describe in measured pace a grave and elevated dance. .
     Onlookers look on, awed.


     “The Partisans of Music, concert-pitched,
     Alla tedesca they Andante dance,
     Swinging to the music of the Landler.
     Dodge a crotchet, duck a quaver,
     Fail to clear a double bar,
     Fall down on their fermatas, rise,
     Sit down, take a nice long rest,
     Segue into hemiola.

--- Page 77 ---
“The Partisans of Work Program,
  In sleeveless suits of unchain’d mail bedight,
  In rented greaves, unpressed,
  Fraught with shovels, picks,
  Brushhooks, toothless saws,
  Infrequently a besom,
  More infrequently, a mop,
  Bring up the rear on feet
  Unknown to feet of prosody.

  “Down the unimproved gravel road,
  Out into the unmowed Playing Fields of Eden,
  File the Armies of the Partisans,
  Draw up in skirmish lines,
  Randy for the stour.

  “The noble Rector, he a noted exegete,
  Reads, with running comment, verses from
  The Sermon on the Amount,
  Tots up sundry treasures, singles out
  Endowment, dearest treasure of his heart,
  Passes to the problem of two masters
  (Here BMC has got the Bible beat),
  Dwells on food, with feeling, especially drink,
  Omits the fowls—they’re safe inside the barn,
  Considers the lilies of the field,
  Considers them ill-educated herbage,
  They toil not—slothful plants,
  Useless to the Work Program;
  Neither do they spin—untutored plants,
  Do they think they’re Spiderworts?
  For the morrow takes no thought,
  Let morrows have their thoughts to them reserved,
  Here runs out of exegeses, fishes out
  The Orders of the Day, stands up.
  The noble Rector, he to the Partisans—
  ‘O Partisans! O nobles,
  Trusty housecarls, faithful thegns,




An Anthology of Personal Accounts                     53

--- Page 78 ---
These are your orders:
Clarify, in suasive succinct words,
The Concept of Community!’

“The Partisans, drawn up in glittering strength,
Square off, await the count.
The count, already up, comes down;
‘Tis the signal for the stour.


“Even as the Kilkenny Cats,
Limned in Irish lore,
Closed in battle noisy, fought until
Naught was left to them except their tails,
So the Armies of the Partisans
In hoarse-voiced screams clarify
The Concept of Community.
Scream meets screech, yowl meets squall,
Caterwaul meets counterwaul. Then suddenly,
Laryngitis mutes the raucous throats.
The stour is done,
The battlefield is quiet as a purr.

“But who has won the stour?
Poetry? Work Program?
Music? Order? Art?
Did all five win? Could all five have lost?
If so, let’s' get in touch with Clausewitz.
But stay, three have met defeat, have quit the field;
Two remain, theirs the Fruits of Victory,
Art and Music. They have won
The Battle of the Playing Fields of Eden.

“A pale moon rises, the silent valley fills with mist,
Frogs creak in reeds beside the silent lake.
The weary warriors stack their weary arms,
Their dusty sabatons take off their feet,
Retire to sleep, make up their cots,
And sleep like tops.”



                     Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 79 ---
Louis Adamic
                                                   (Visitor 1935, 1937)


                   Excerpts from “Black Mountain:
                      An Experiment in Education”



The job of a college is to bring young people to intellectual and emo-
tional maturity; to intelligence, by which I mean a subtle balance
between the intellect and the emotions; not merely to an arbitrarily
selected amount of cramming. ... A college should take account of
the whole being and be a sort of second womb from which young
people are born to all-round human maturity. . . The common ex-
pression ‘to get’ an education is significant. It lights up the entire
fallacy of the prevailing system, for education can only be experi-
enced; one ‘gets’ only information or ‘facts’—and the ‘facts’ ac-
quired in the average college have to do with the past and are
mainly worthless to one destined to live in the future. To put the
emphasis upon the teaching of ‘facts,’ the giving of mere informa-
tion is to train people in and for the past. . . . There are some stub-
born facts in the early stages of the physical sciences, in mathemat-
ics and biology, that must be learned. But once these stages are
passed, you are in the realm of imagination, and often get lost be-
cause of the meager training the imagination has had. It is a com-
monplace that many scientists are, through long years of training in
the factual world, unfitted for this leap into the world of the imagi-


Louis Adamic visited BMC for three months in 1935 and in the fall of
1937. He was impressed by certain facets of its educational and commu-
nal character and life, especially as pointed out and underscored in the
ideas and vision of its founder, John Andrew Rice. What follows are bits
and pieces of what Adamic rendered from his 1935 conversations with
Rice. Although only a glimpse of Rice’s educational vision can be pre-
sented here, it is remarkable how much of his fundamental ideas come
through, ideas that remained viable and affective throughout the life of
the College. Louis Adamic’s chapter, “The Long Road,” originally ap-
peared in My America (New York: Harper & Bros., 1938), 615—45.



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                    55

--- Page 80 ---
nation.   . . . But surely, you will say, truth, education,     not mere
head-stuffing, is the goal of the humanities. I should like to think
so, but, 1am sorry to say that, if it is, it is a long way off, such a long
way that the undergraduate in the average college seldom even
gets a glimpse of it... . Take the queen of learning: professors of
philosophy do not profess philosophy; they profess to be able to tell
what philosophers have said and, sometimes, what they meant. If
you are looking for a philosopher, stay away from departments of
philosophy; you will get only reporters there. .. . Well, there is lit-
erature; but if you make an inspection of our colleges, you will find,
for the most part, that the study of literature is concerned mainly
with biography and antiquities; and where ideas are alleged to be
the interest, the probability is that you will be served with stale
classicism. There is no life in it; it is like turning over empty cans
and poking about in the rubbish. . . . Ina last desperate effort to find
a trace of education that has some concern with living truth or
ideas, with the oncoming future, you go to the departments of fine
arts, music, dramatics; and all you discover there is the teachers
weighed down with foolish hope and painful duty which incapaci-
tate them for any sort of effective teaching. They hope some day to
turn out a professional dauber, designer, fiddler, or actor who will
become ‘famous,’ and they will be able to bask in his glory; mean-
while they are obliged to teach students ‘to appreciate.’ Every June
the country is swamped with appreciators. Later you will find
some of them in women’s clubs, listening flatly, unimaginatively,
uncritically to lectures and performances by all species of fools and
charlatans; the rest, in a state of beneficent oblivion. .. . Everywhere
the chief distinction of man, imagination, is neglected. .. .
     “... The question then, is: how are you to train the imagina-
tion?—or, more important still: is it possible to train the imagina-
tion? An enormous question, but I think it can be answered. I be-
lieve that we here in Black Mountain—not I, as you know, but all of
us, students and faculty—have reached the threshold of the an-
swer. We're trying, with increasing (though, off and on, faltering)
success to teach method as against content. Our emphasis is-on
process as against results. To us, the way of handling facts is more
important than facts themselves. Facts change, while the method of
handling them—provided the method is life’s own free, dynamic



56                     Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 81 ---
method which evidently works on the principle that nothing is per-
manent save change—remains the same; and so, if stability or order
is what is wanted in this world (and I take it that it is), it can only
be got by putting facts, results, the alleged content of life in the past
in second place, and placing stress on the way of handling facts
now and in the future, on the method, the process of life... . What I
am trying to articulate here is, as I say, new to us; but this idea of
method, of process, of imagination as against ‘facts,’ static con-
cepts, and concrete results is really not new. It is, indeed, very old.
It has been for a long time a fierce little flame leaping out of the
minds and feelings of a small section of humanity, the artists: by
which, of course, I don’t mean only painters, but artists of all kinds,
including (in fact, especially) those who are not painters, sculptors,
writers, musicians, or anything else of the sort, but who have the
artistic approach or attitude to life as a whole and to everything in
life; whose values are qualitative as opposed to quantitative; who
are eternally modern and so distinguished from the non-moderns,
not by what they know, but what they do with what they know;
and to whom men, in their vague desperation in the face of the
deep confusion of contemporary life, are at last, I think, slowly
shamefacedly beginning to turn. ...
      “The method, the way of education that I think—that I know—
is needed, is the method, is the way of the poet, the philosopher-
scientist-artist; and the reason we have not adopted it before is that
we have foolishly, ignorantly believed life was essentially competi-
tive: and we have pushed the poet aside because he knows compe-
tition for the senseless, wasteful thing it is. As long as we cling to
this old idea, we shall continue to look toward       one another and
spend most of our energy scheming how to harm one another, in-
stead of looking toward a goal, toward something that we want to
become. The measure of your success, as long as you compete with
men, is the distance between you and them. You are forever look-
ing forward to the leaders and backward to the followers, and they
are always men, usually not better than yourself. The measure of
your success, if you are a poet, a philosopher-artist, a creator, is the
distance between you and the thing you want to do, between you
and the thing you want to create, between you and the person you
want to become. You learn to follow ideas and ideals, which are




An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                     oy

--- Page 82 ---
alive and headed    for the future, instead of men,     most of whom
(nowadays, partly because of education) are largely dead creatures
of the past; and you don’t look back, except to find how other poets
before you have created their goals, how they in their time have fol-
lowed ideas, handled life, and looked into the future. .. .
     There is a technique to be learned, a grammar to the art of liv-
ing. Logic as severe as it can be, must be learned, if for no other rea-
sons, to know its limitations. Dialectic must be learned; and no feel-
ings spared, for you can’t afford to be nice when truth is at stake.
The hard intractable facts of science must be learned, for truth has a
habit of hiding in queer places. Man’s responses to ideas in the past
must be learned. These things are the pencil, the brush, the chisel.
But these are not all. There are subtle means of communication that
have been lost by mankind , as our nerve ends have been cauter-
ized by schooling. These nerves must be re-sensitized. We must
learn to move without fear, to be aware of everything around us, to
feel as well as to see mentally our way into the future—the poet is
also the prophet. And we must become tough, awfully tough—
tough poets, as I say—for this is a big arduous job we have got to
do, this job of making a decent humanity that is fit to live on the
decent earth. . . . We teachers—we artists—must begin to get tough
with the politicians, the militarists; with the cheap ignorant schem-
ers who blindly juggle with humanity and mar the face of the
world. We must start to treat ‘em rough. We must commence to
bore into the pretense and sham of the world-savers—the world, as
it is, isn’t worth saving: it must be changed. . . . The job before us,
that of remaking ourselves, is not only a big, an arduous one but a
long one. Centuries. But we must begin. aa
     “In general, the effort of Black Mountain College is to produce
individuals rather than individualists, in the belief that the indi-
vidualist is bound to be a misfit in modern life, while, at the other
extreme, the subordination of men and women to a uniform and
consistent pattern of action will inevitably prevent the creation of a
better society than we now have. The first step in the process is to
make the student aware of himself and his capacities; in other
words ‘to know himself.’
     “A beginning is often best made by persuading the student to
submit himself to the discipline of one or more of the arts. . . . It is



58                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 83 ---
not expected that many students will become artists; in fact, the col-
lege regards it as a sacred duty to discourage mere talent from
thinking itself genius; but there is something of the artist in every-
one, and the development of this talent, however small, carrying
with it a severe discipline of its own, results in the student’s becom-
ing more and more sensitive to order in the world and within him-
self than he can ever possibly become through intellectual efforts
alone.
    But the individual, to be complete, must be aware of his rela-
tion to others. Here the whole community becomes his teacher.
Wood-chopping,     road-mending,    rolling the tennis courts, serving
tea in the afternoon, and other tasks around the place done by
groups composed of students and members of the faculty, help to
rub off individualistic corners and give people training in assum-
ing responsibility. They tend to involve the person into participa-
tion in the life and issues of the community as a whole. Last year
the students of their own accord took over the running of the Col-
lege farm, and during the summer some of them, by taking turns in
staying on the place, got the crops ready for harvesting by the
whole College this autumn;      which was done, of course, without
conflict with the academic schedule, and is a help to the College
economically.
     “There is—naturally—an element of fun in all these tasks for
which the students and the faculty assume responsibility, but along
with other things they help to give the experiences that these stu-
dents have while they are in college the quality of their experiences
in later life. Attending to these tasks (which, incidentally, in a meas-
ure take the place of purely artificial sport activities one finds in
most other schools), they have to overcome all sorts of very real
difficulties within and outside themselves; which—again, in con-
junction with their other experiences here—helps them toward
maturity. ...
     “This then is the kind of man we should like to produce, one in
whom there is a nice balance of forethought, action and reflection.
What is the best medium? The discipline[s] of the humanities have
been tried, and have been a failure; so has science, although the sci-
entists don’t yet know it (if science were made a free elective in this
country, the registration in courses in science would drop fifty per-



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                    59

--- Page 84 ---
cent at once). Now we are in the hands of social scientists, who
make brave promises. In history, sociology, psychology, eco-
nomics, and the rest, there is plenty of action to be reflected upon;
but it is not the action of the student himself. This is why they will
prove as useless in the end as the others have already proved. As a
beginning point they will not do, because they do not and cannot
begin with the individual student as the actor, as the one who is
doing the doing. This is why we at Black Mountain begin with art.
The artist thinks about what he himself is going to do, does it him-
self, and then reflects upon the thing that he himself has done. Here
is the concatenation that we want to use at the beginning, one in
which each step involves the student inevitably, and one in which
no step can be omitted without obvious injury to the rest. Observe,
however, that I have said, ‘in the beginning.”

--- Page 85 ---
Allan Sly
                                                  (Faculty 1935-39)

           Excerpts from Taped Reminiscences
                                        of Black Mountain



I want to stress the importance of what John Evarts [faculty mem-
ber in music and mathematics, 1933-42] had accomplished before I
came. He’d got the whole community—every man, woman, and
child—quite warmly related to musical experience. If they didn’t
sing—unison songs, choruses, chorales in parts—at least they lis-
tened with great attention. This was noticed by many visitors to the
College, and is due in large part to whatever John Evarts did when
he went [to BMC] first. He went further than giving his music ap-
preciation classes. He got those people to be so absolutely devoted
to the sound, for instance of the Bach Brandenburg Concertos, or the
repertoire of the New    York Philharmonic,   which Toscanini    con-
ducted on the radio every Sunday afternoon—that practically the
whole community listened to those broadcasts. When I came in
 1935, I think we had about fifty-three students, and the faculty had
grown to eighteen or twenty. The scope of our musical activities
was able to increase. We had more students who could play instru-
ments, and some of the faculty and their wives played, so we were
able to do ensemble music. I won’t say it was chamber music, or
orchestral music as such, but under the heading of ensemble we
were able to do things that a group of well-intentioned and fairly
skillful people could play. It may have left some people out, but
they were always ready to listen to the results and feel some rela-
tion to it.
     Many of the students sang and had good singing voices. I think
Jane Mayhall perhaps was the only one with soloistic style. But the
others could all sing assigned parts very well: madrigals and other
ensemble music. And while we wouldn’t have taken such perform-
ances to New York, we did take them on tour to a limited extent,
for instance, to a state teachers’ college nearby. There was a time


An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                 6]

--- Page 86 ---
when a lot of us boned up on the parts of Mozart’s Requiem, to take
part in a performance at Converse College in Spartanburg, South
Carolina under Ernst Bacon. Our people were scattered through the
orchestra. I think I played tympani. Many of them sang in the cho-
rus, and I can see old Dr. Straus standing up there with his white
hair now. I suppose he’d sung it many times in Berlin. He obvi-
ously was enjoying himself enormously.
     In this way we were all quite used to the effort involved in per-
formances of the major works. Everyone felt that they were ex-
pressing themselves, and there was never the slightest hesitation in
getting these things organized. When I got down there first, John
Evarts had taught them to sing a lot of the Brahms Requiem, with at
least two or three of the choruses, quite acceptably—with piano,
that is. And there came a time when we applied ourselves to the
Hindemith “Ploéner Musiktag.” [This piece uses different combina-
tions of voices and instruments for each part of the day and eve-
ning.] Having done all three of these pieces [morning, noon and
evening] independently, each with its own accompaniment, they
then all go together. And you have never heard such a glorious
noise! When we prepared this piece, there was no one in the Col-
lege left to listen.
     When I speak of ensemble music, this rather presupposes tak-
ing a lot of Renaissance and Baroque music and writing the parts
out, copying them by hand. And there was an enormous amount of
copying done. Dante Fiorillo, being a busy composer, showed us
how to write better, to use special pens and inks, and how to hold
the pens in a different way from the way most of us had done it. I
was very much obliged to Dante for initiating us into a music cal- -
ligraphy which was enormously better to read, and much easier
and quicker to write.
     [Allan Sly also remarks that because he married Elizabeth
Ware, who played violin and viola, and was a very active chamber
music player, they used to give frequent concerts together there.
Sly was a pianist. At one point they made tours of various colleges
in the South, giving concerts. He says that if circumstances of the
Depression and the coming European war had not supervened,
there probably would have been more interchanges between BMC
and other schools, and more tours. He also gives Thomas Whitney



62                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 87 ---
Surette credit for having begun music at the College, and says that
at his death in 1941, Surette left the school a considerable music li-
brary.]
     In addition to the regular ballroom dancing which took place
with John Evarts at the piano every Saturday night, or every night
after dinner, I was also able to teach them English folkdancing. I
learned this as a boy. Wherever I happened to be, sooner or later
we all got to doing English folkdancing, and so at BMC that became
quite a regular thing, which we would do after lunch, or sometimes
in the evenings. I remember the French boy, Bertrand, who couldn’t
catch on at first, and then it suddenly dawned on him what it was:
“Ah! Zeeg-Zag!!” Everybody knew then that he was all taken care
of. The folkdancing did play a part in our recreational moods. .. .
Albers once said that the way we were doing [the dances] was all
wrong, and that we had no business to jig up and down—it was
like horseback riding—and     that the dances should be gliding, in
gliding movements. I felt that the thousands of people who had
danced these dances had been living on a plane a few inches higher
than the one on which life is normally led, and though Albers may
well have been right in his view, the sustained activity of the danc-
ers a few inches off the ground was the very spirit of it. His idea of
crawling around like a crocodile was quite at variance with the na-
ture of the English country dance.
     I think it’s very important who one’s successors are. Mine was
a very distinguished conductor from Vienna, displaced on account
of the circumstances [in Germany at that time]. He had been con-
ductor of the Volksoper in Vienna. His name was [Heinrich] Ja-
lowetz [music, 1939-46]. He stayed for a few years after my leav-
ing, and then died suddenly of a heart attack after one of the con-
certs. When John Evarts left, Edward     Lowinsky   [music, 1942-47]
joined the faculty. He was one of the most distinguished musicolo-
gists in the country and actually succeeded in holding down the
highest salary paid to any musicologist anywhere [after he left
BMC].
     I remember there was a time when they took the farm truck to
Asheville to bring back an upright piano they'd seen in the want-
ads, and on the way back John Evarts played jazz all the way over
the highway from Asheville to Black Mountain. I also recall the

     When I was taking a drawing class with Albers, I recall how he
constantly held up my work in the class as an example of a disci-
plined hand. He loved this word “discipline.” He would tell the
class, “Who likes not discipline, let him leave soddenly!”
     Leslie Katz would play the early Benny Goodman records to
me, and I’d notice the extraordinary subtlety of Goodman’s play-
ing. I said to Leslie, “Do you realize how subtle that playing is?” He
answered,      “Well,           I was wondering             if you realized how subtle it
was!”
       The opening of the music cottage in which there were a few


                                                                                                         65
An Anthology of Personal Accounts

--- Page 90 ---
upright pianos and a few fireplaces was celebrated with hot dogs
and Coke, or it may have been autumn cider in the fall of 1935. Al-
bers was amongst the many who came. When it came to toasting
the hot dogs over the open fire, most speared their dogs with un-
bent coat hangers, but Albers preferred to bend his coat hanger into
a letter S—laying his hot dog on top of it, which he then held over
the fire. We pointed out to him the advantage of spearing it with
the prong. But he said, “I like very much the S-form.” His dog fell
off into the fire.
     When Albers was involved in some construction using a cross-
cut saw, he said, “A saw should always say ‘Sie!’ and not ‘Du.’” He
also offered at one point that when you drink beer you should taste
it at the back of the throat, and not with the tongue and palate.
Open the throat and pour it down! These two notions that Albers
introduced to me about getting the most out of a crosscut saw and a
glass of beer have persisted in my experience all these years. I also
recall a time when we were all shellacking our floors, and Frank
Rice said that there was nothing that pulls a room together so much
as a well-shellacked floor.
     The thing about BMC we all liked was that everyone was will-
ing to learn. Most of us were learning things we would not have
learned otherwise. In my own case it had a great deal to do with my
Americanization, so to speak. The process of adaptation was very
much helped along by the years I spent at the College. We really
did have the ideal liberal arts community just because everyone
learned from everybody else. And there were all the arts, the things
that adorn life and make it possible. At BMC we did music more for
its own sake. Our music wasn’t what you would call intense. It had »
a much more comfortable, almost domestic, character.
     I think we were free. I don’t think we were victims of any
strong prejudice, either on color or on political questions. We were
free from being propagandized. And for all that, we were a liberal
arts college, as well as a community. I have no hesitation in saying
that the experiences I had later at “normal” American colleges bear
no comparison at all as to the values or quality of life at BMC. This
school had an enormous advantage over all these other places. And
it could not have happened except in America. I do recall those
years as extraordinarily fruitful and interesting.

--- Page 91 ---
Morton Steinau
                                                     (Student 1935-43)


                                                      Early Lenses



 I had always been pretty much a loner, even though active in
 school and church organizations, so not being in the thick of things
 right away at BMC was neither new nor frightening. I had always
  loved camping and the woods, and the Black Mountain area was
  paradise for a woods-lover. Dozens of peaks were within a long
  day’s hike from Blue Ridge, and except for a few settlements, the
  entire region was heavily forested. This was before the Blue Ridge
  Parkway; there were no roads to any of these mountain ridges, and
  few trails. I had, among my few possessions, an ax, a skillet, coffee
  pot, mess kit and utensils, and a canteen. Blankets were always
  available on the bed (I had never owned a sleeping bag, and looked
  askance on those who used such effete equipment). I went for days
  at a time, alone, through the mountains. On some of these trips I'd
  see no other people the entire time. Teachers and other students
- questioned my safety—perhaps my sanity—alone in the wilder-
  ness, often off any paths or trails. What if I broke a leg or fell off a
  cliff? There were definitely wildcats in these uninhabited moun-
 tains. Slowly, over time, and indirectly, I learned of another danger.
 Here and there, hidden until you stumbled out of the woods into a
 clearing, was a cornfield, almost on the top of a ridge, miles from
 any house or road. Talking with one of the maintenance people at
 the College, who lived in the town of Black Mountain, I said, “It
 must be difficult, hauling that corn down the mountain, with no
 roads, over a path so small you can’t see it unless you happened to
 cross it.” I was told the corn wasn’t hauled down in sacks or bushel
 baskets or by mule, but carried down        as water-clear whiskey, in
 gallon jugs, and that I’d do best to avoid such cornfields: visitors
 were most unwelcome.
     Weather would change suddenly. Starting out on a pleasant
 spring day, I’d find that the night was so cold I could hardly sleep. I



 An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                     67

--- Page 92 ---
didn’t own a tent, and one would have been too heavy to carry
through the mountains: lightweight tents for backpacking were still
to be invented. I was surprised and angry that snow fell on my face
and hair during the night, and that my water froze in the canteen.
At times I heard wildcats, never saw any. I was scared. I’m aware
now, more than half a century later, looking back through the
lenses of a wise and fearful seventy-year-old, that such odysseys
were dangerous in the extreme. I’m glad I had then the lenses of an
eighteen- and nineteen-year-old.




                            Marian (Nacke) Teeter
                                                   (Student 1936-39)


                                       Letter, 24 April 1939



... The biggest disruption, and the things that will explode in the
next six weeks, concern Rice. There are a lot of machinations going
on here, too complicated to explain, but the point is some of the fac-
ulty are trying to get rid of Rice and threaten to break up the Col-
lege if he doesn’t leave. Ostensibly their reason is that they can’t get
money and students as long as he continues to insult people away
from here, as he has done all his life. And, in fact, the very existence
of this College is an insult to many stick-in-the-mud people.
     Actually, the reason goes back to what happened last year, be-
cause I think his disciples (and that’s what many of the faculty
were) feel somehow that he betrayed them then, and they no longer
have security in him, so they are looking for a new kind, which is—
success. These things are, I think, at the bottom of it all, altho it is
enormously complicated by personal rivalries and jealousies, and
by certain solid criticisms on their side. I don’t know what will hap-
pen. I hope he will leave peacefully, because there is no possibility
of reviving his prestige here. There are people here, however, and




68                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 93 ---
people away from here who still believe in him, and if they gather
 together it may result in another college or community.
       I hope you won't be alarmed by this. There is no cause for
 alarm. It is fascinating to watch such a decline from magnificence,
 and to see all the shifting relationships of people, if you can do it
 objectively. But sometimes it is terribly sad. But still magnificent—
 still on a heroic scale. Rice was the College. It no longer has any
 place in the central lives of people here—it no longer holds them
 together. When I think of the College, it is like thinking of my past.
 But still it is a wonderful place to me, partly from nostalgia, partly
 because of the pleasantness of the living arrangements and all the
 nice people here.
      ... Allan [Sly] is not coming back next year. He, too, is looking
 for security and fame at this point. This, of course, is a terrific blow
 to the College, because he is a big man now, and music has been the
 most important thing here this year. But for myself, altho I hate to
 see him go, I am looking forward to working with a new man. Al-
 lan, lately, has been a very inhibiting force. He thinks I am more
 mature than I am and he doesn’t give me enough direction in my
 work, and also, I can’t play [piano] for him at all now when I have a
 lesson, because I care so much what he thinks, and I know his stan-
  dards are absolutely unattainable for me. That is a terrible thing—
_ to know at last the one thing you want to do more than anything
 else, and to know    that you can never,     never   do it because   you
 weren’t born with the right combination of chemicals, or some-
 thing. Of course, it doesn’t diminish the joy of doing what you can,
 but it makes you more fatalistic. . . .
