Dick Andrews
                                               (Student   1936-40)


                                         A Few Vignettes



Black Mountain College was begun by a group that had left Rollins
College in protest over certain developments they considered to be
rather phony and arbitrary. They were American academic rebels,
with roots deep in the American soil. Rice owed much to John
Dewey, not the least of which was Dewey’s view of the importance
of art.
     Certainly the Bauhaus group were to a degree rebels in their
own context. Albers could be very funny taking off [on] a conserva-
tive German portrait painter. But their real goal was—and had to
be—gaining a foothold in America by making the Bauhaus part of
the American establishment—retroactively. So these two ships
“passed in the night.” There were faculty at BMC not at all in-
volved in art, and who must have felt that the situation was a bit
out of balance. The art group did seem later to have moved in. And
an American radical academic group again seems to have been in
charge at the end.
    Frankly, I remain fascinated by the highland South, and made
many excursions. I wonder how much of that is left. It was an ex-
traordinary landscape and people. Was there ever such a place for
spring? The fall in New England, but the spring in the Carolina
highlands!


The Southern Railway in the Swannanoa Valley
Black Mountain town was a station on the Southern Railway’s
Asheville-Salisbury line, and the track climbed up steadily from
Asheville along the Swannanoa Valley, past the town and Lee Hall
on its hillside, and went through a long tunnel at Ridgecrest. Then
it wound way down around a series of loops and curves to Old
Fort, at which place one was out of the Smoky Mountains.
     Great black steam locomotives with five sets of driving wheels


70                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 95 ---
would blast their way along the valley up to Ridgecrest, pulling
 and pushing freight cars. One locomotive in front would pull on
 fifty cars, and then came another, pushing, and then the caboose.
 These big locomotives were worked “flat out” all the way upgrade,
 and their exhausts rolled off the hills. You could hear the valley!
 Then for comic relief, a little train would come along with freight
 cars for local industries. It would be pulled by an antique loco-
 motive of small size and a squeaky whistle. Then, onto the great
 stage of the valley, there finally swept the star of the show—the
 passenger train! The Southern Railway was proud of its passenger
 trains, which carried not only travelers and their baggage but the
 mails and express parcels. So the passenger locomotives were
 freshly painted green and striped and lettered in gold, with silver
 wheel rims and red spokes! And they had high driving wheels for
 speed, so the valley resounded with the quick cadence of their ex-
 hausts—and then they had “chime” steam whistles! This flying star
 would pause briefly at Black Mountain station in a flurry of activ-
 ity, getting mail and parcels, bags and people off, and then a blast
 on the chime whistle, and away! It was a grand show. We had no
 idea that in a few years the steam railroad would be practically
 banished from the planet.


- Anna von Meullenhoff’s Biology Class
 Anna was a gentle lady, of great intelligence and refinement, who
 gave us excellent courses in biology. She lived with her daughters
 at the College while her husband, Fritz, an able M.D. from Ger-
 many, interned in an American city hospital to qualify for an
 American practice. If memory serves, Paul Goldberg, Hyalie Yam-
 ins, Willie Rice, and I formed the class that year. We all loved her.
 Anna taught us the material in a beautiful structured manner. Some
 basic embryology made evolution obvious and implicit. We dis-
 sected frogs, and, in the only situation in which anything went the
 least bit awry, we dissected beef hearts obtained from Jack Lipsey,
 the cook. Beef hearts were on the menu for dinner that night. Anna
 had put them on the lab radiator to warm up from refrigeration,
 and they cooked ever so little—in formaldehyde. We survived that,
 but could not handle supper that night!



 An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                  #I

--- Page 96 ---
Jack Lipsey
John Rice told us of Jack Lipsey, who knew as much as anyone in
the College about human nature. Rice said he could tell what Jack
thought of a person by the way he handed his plate to him/her.
Jack had taken the job as chef for the College at Lee Hall with one
particular stipulation: that each person in the community come into
the kitchen every morning and ask Jack for his or her breakfast.
Thus it was that each of us had to come face to face with Jack first
thing in the morning. And though Jack was a kindly autocrat most
of the time, with pretense or imperiousness he could be hard.
     I recall a morning in which a “left-leaning” person had been
demanding. Jack was amused. The leftist stamped out of the
kitchen and the cadence of her heels on the concrete floor was ech-
oed by Jack’s ladle on the edge of the stove, and then by Jack’s help-
ers. One did not try very often to push Jack around!


Josef Albers
When Albers left BMC     he became the head of the Yale School of
Design. I read in Eye—a publication of the Yale Arts Association—
the statement of one of his students, who mentions, “After seeing
my new work, Albers said, ‘You were better last year.” Telling a
                                                          wt




student he was better before—ha! He gave me the same line. I
showed him something I did at fourteen, and he said, “You were
better then.”
     Albers was philosophically a “monist,” and didn’t offer much
diversity to students. At the professional-school level, that isn’t
expected. The problem at BMC was that most of his students were
not on the professional level, and his was the only game in town.
He recognized this and talked at one point of getting another art-
ist/teacher on the staff with a different approach, but BMC could
scarcely afford that. Albers just really did not care for twentieth
century French art (nor Picasso). Nor German Expressionism! Es-
sentially, he taught very straightforward drawing in charcoal on 18
by 24 inch paper tacked on boards. He did not teach painting,
though there were color and texture studies, and exercises such as
using one substance (say, paper) to imitate another (say, leather).
     As a teacher of children, adolescents,    and adults, I do not



72                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 97 ---
force a group to stay on one track. Gustave Moreau was the final
teacher   of Raoul   Dufy,   André   Derain,   Henri   Matisse,   Georges
Roualt, and other major figures in French art. They were greatly
various. And none of them copied Moreau’s work. So there is more
than one approach to the mystery of launching professional aspi-
rants!




                                     Anne G. Mangold
                                           (Faculty Spouse 1937-42)


                                                   An Overview




           My fondest memory was the view from Lee Hall
           during the dogwood season.

The history of BMC is not only a “prelude” but another instance of
timeless questions and problems being worked out at a specific
time in history with similar choices available. It was not a “pre-
commune” but a college. If it had been a commune, none of us
would have been there. A college is not a political democracy.
“Community” was emphasized as another way to learn something
about the question of individual freedom and authority—even if
unconventional—as a new dimension of college learning. It was a
way of teaching people that they are part of a whole, with certain
kinds of decisions properly belonging to different parts: academic
ones to the faculty, community ones to the community, faculty ap-
pointments to the Board of Fellows, with students being repre-
sented on the board and most committees. .. .
    BMC, in our years, realized that community was complex and
always imperfect, and arrogance somehow did not belong to it. It
was neither anarchistic nor authoritarian. It was an atmosphere at




An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                      73

--- Page 98 ---
4eqoy
  “gy ae] ‘TRH euroy
                  JO   DW   “[P-Ee6l AsaynoDsypAyg sydasof ‘sewoyy

--- Page 99 ---
its best, of high-mindedness that no one who was there has forgot-
ten. The greatest effect of general education that BMC made during
our years there was to make the arts a legitimate part of the curricu-
lum. It also saw responsibility and maturity as serious personal
goals, and intellectual effort as a living and imaginative part of cul-
ture—beyond that of fraternities, organized sports, and a materi-
ally successful life.




                            Phyllis Josephs Thomas
                                                   (Student   1938-41)


                                                Looking Back
                                    on the Lee Hall Years



I have just been reading over all kinds of original old papers and
documents on BMC: the introduction to the first college catalog, an
article on “Work with Materials” by Anni Albers, an address by Dr.
Erwin Straus on “Education in a Time of Crisis,” a clipping from
the New York Times of August 1940 on plans for students and fac-
ulty to erect their own new building—and many more. They all
give me a sense of memory of the old excitement I felt when I first
learned about BMC years ago.
     I think if I were a student today, searching for a significant
place to continue my higher education, and came across all this
material,   I would become fired up about BMC     all over again—and
possibly fall heir to some of the same illusions and mistakes. Look-
ing back from today’s art-pervaded ambience, I realize that I went
to BMC with certain preconceived fields of interest in which, unfor-
tunately, the College turned out to be weakest.      I never took full
advantage of the strengths it had to offer (the arts) in which I felt I
had little or no drive or talent. I did go in for creative writing and
sampled some aspects of music, but the visual and handicraft arts I



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                   75

--- Page 100 ---
evaded (a thing I’ve long regretted) in favor of a rather rigid aca-
demic program covered by a nonexistent or shaky curriculum and
faculty.
     In that upbeat period of the late thirties and early forties,
intensified by my own family background of strong idealism and
utopianism, I regarded the social sciences as my best approach to-
ward helping make a more just world. In some ways I’m surprised
to find I might still embrace an experimental institution with such
enthusiasm, after many years of sporadically bemoaning my lack of
“practical” education. Yet today, when my own son complains
about the lack of relevancy, the sense of remoteness and alienation
in the modern university, I can well understand the continuing
appeal of something billed as true democratic education. Almost
thirty years ago John Dewey referred to BMC as a “living example
of democracy in action,” and though the school did have many
flaws, it probably came closer to his characterization than most
schools before or since. . . .
     BMC’s first home was Robert E. Lee Hall (“Blue Ridge”), a
huge old frame, hotel-like building leased from the YMCA. This
was the College as I knew it. In June 1941, just after my final depar-
ture, it moved to its own nearby Lake Eden property (purchased in
1937), where I had put in many hours of hard manual labor in the
“work program.” The words      “work,”   “work list” and “work pro-
gram” refer to a concept and activity at the core of the community.
Everyone—faculty and students alike—contributed [the] manual
labor necessary   for the operation,   maintenance,   and even   actual
physical building of the-College. Each student shared a bedroom
with a roommate and, in addition, had a separate study of his/her
own—all in the same massive building. There were no dormitory
restrictions, and today’s stormy issue of “intervisitation” was un-
known.
     The College prided itself on the fact that it imposed no rules or
regulations of any kind, but depended on the developing maturity,
responsibility, and self-discipline of its members. Major problems
were taken up and thrashed out at townhall-like meetings of the
entire community.
     BMC was a unique place, but probably very few of its alumni
could agree on the actual essence of that uniqueness. . . .



76                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 101 ---
Harold B. Raymond
                                                      (Student    1938-42)


                            The Other Black Mountain



A recent review of Mary Harris’s fine book, The Arts at Black Moun-
tain, is typical of some one-dimensional views of the College. The
reviewer,   Elliot Weinberger,   states:   “Perhaps    the most   shocking
aspect of BMC during the Albers years is its studied obliviousness
to dramatic contemporary events.” He goes on to say that neither
Harris nor Martin Duberman, who wrote a history of the College,
mentions any interest there in the Spanish Civil War. I recall one of
my first uneasy nights in the vast cavernous reaches of Lee Hall
wondering . . who among my busy fellows would pay any atten-
tion to a bewildered new student, when suddenly I was ap-
proached by several eager students who hurried me off to the
enormous lobby, where a good part of the community was already
engaged in an excited debate on whether a telegram should be sent
to Washington demanding the United States give aid to the Span-
ish Republic! My recollection is that, after about an hour of some-
times emotional    discussion,   the consensus   was    yes it should.   In
writing class, several students regularly wrote antiwar and anti-
Fascist poetry and short stories, which in retrospect had more pas-
sionate commitment than literary merit... .
     All through my years at the school, a large portion of the com-
munity was deeply aware of and fiercely debated war, isolation,
intervention, and their relationship to their own and what they con-
sidered Black Mountain ideals. In my last year at the College, after
war had finally come, I as a “student officer” had the unenviable
task of moderating a student meeting in the crowded lobby of
North Lodge on the Lake Eden campus to decide whether the com-
munity should send a message to the government in support of the
war effort. People filled the small room, sat on the floors and stairs,
and shouted from a second-floor landing. Pacifists and war sup-
porters carried on a long and at times bitter debate before, as some



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                        77

--- Page 102 ---
ironically put it, “Black Mountain College declared war on the
Axis.” By that period, some of the student artists had strayed so far
from Albers’s style and nonpolitical attitude that they were paint-
ing nothing but imaginative, if not very remarkable, battle scenes!
BMC custom rejected the obtrusive presence of radios in the dining
hall even in times of crisis, but nearly the whole community often
gathered after dinner to hear comment on events such as Roland
Boyden’s dry New England commonsense evaluations of what war
might mean in day-to-day life, anthropologist Paul Radin’s fantas-
tic speculations on the course of events, or student Robert Marden’s
summary of the news. Certainly there was no “studied oblivious-
ness to contemporary events” for much of the Black Mountain com-
munity.
     War   increased   concern   about the “outside   world,”   but even
before it came there were numerous individuals and groups as
dedicated to social problems as other students were to art or music.
To recent writers the thirties and early forties were “the Albers
years,” and the historians of BMC, such as Harris and Duberman,
have to some extent fallen into a practice dear to the older-style his-
torians of the larger world: the great man fallacy. To divide the his-
tory of nations, societies, or institutions into reigns or eras domi-
nated by great men is convenient, dramatic, and stylistically satis-
factory. It also unfortunately tends to miss the complexities and
diversity of life as it really was, whether in the reign of Louis XIV,
the age of Jackson, or the Albers years at Black Mountain.
     Few communities have also been less inclined to allow their
lives or their intellectual development to be dominated by a single
“great man” or teacher. In the years I was at BMC, there was cer-
tainly a large Albers influence, and most students recognized his
importance and took his courses, but there were also some           who
gave all their attention to creative writing, history, or the social in-
terpretation of literature. As war approached there was a sizable
group of pacifists who became increasingly absorbed in how they
could direct their own lives, the College, and the world toward a
nonviolent way. There were also many other individuals and clus-
ters of people interested in BMC as a place to study society and
politics.




78                      Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 103 ---
In its early years, BMC was, of course, not dominated by the
  social sciences or political awareness, but neither was it an art
  school excluded from the political world by a few famous person-
  alities. . . The BMC community contained a rich variety of atti-
  tudes, life-styles, and academic interests. It was this diversity [that]
  made [the] Black Mountain of those years such a wonderful, frus-
  trating, and inspiring place to live and study.




                                          sue Spayth Riley
                                                     (Student 1938-41)


                           Some Pieces of the Collage



  The Black Mountain experience resembled a many-sided, cut, but
  incompletely polished stone, some facets brighter than others,
  some dull and rough. This delicate balance of positive and negative
  imbued the experience with a distinctive and unusual reality, un-
_ like that found on a more conventional college campus. The loca-
  tion of the College was in itself a kind of utopia: those glorious
  mountains; the gently gurgling, constantly bubbling, sometimes
  crashing waters of the stream below our bedroom windows; the
  sunsets behind the blue wave of mountain peaks; walking to break-
  fast in the chill morning air.
      The sense   of community,     our isolation   from the rest of the
 world, provided ample opportunities for close contact with others.
 A friend with whom to talk, to share an idea or a newfound joy was
 so handy—just down the hall, up the stairs, or across the dining
 room table.
     College is for an education in its broadest sense. Some philoso-
 phy classes, writing seminars, the preparation for and staging of
 plays were meaningful and well remembered. The music, ah the




 An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                     79

--- Page 104 ---
music (in the days before tapes and just a few record players), was
a dazzling highlight for me: singing a Bach contata with Jalowetz,
listening to Betty and Allan Sly playing Brahms.
     Perhaps most important was the contact with a few select fac-
ulty in which one felt reached, touched, elevated, and instructed in
the best, most positive sense of the word. This happened at BMC,
but not often. When I was turned over to Mary Barnes for a tutorial,
our discussions on many subjects deepened my understanding of
the human experience. She was a rare gem of a person. With Mary,
as with Dr. John Rice, I felt reached, and elevated. John Rice genu-
inely believed in the value of the student (as it seemed many of the
others didn’t) and the importance of the communication of ideas
and feelings. When I attended the College, the creative and innova-
tive ideas of Rice were dominant—for it had been his thinking and
energy that had spearheaded the establishment of the experiment
only five years earlier. His Socratic dialogue method of teaching,
and his warm almost grandfatherly way of conducting writing
seminars were both stimulating to me, stretching my mind and
deepening my level of understanding. And beyond that, he was a
friendly counselor whose study door was always open, even late at
night, when I needed to talk.




                                                May Sarton
                                                    (Lecturer 1940)


              Excerpt from Unpublished Letter
         to Rosalind Greene, November 1940


The thing that holds Black Mountain together and keeps it from the
phoniness I had feared is that they are building their new building
with their own hands. Rosalind, it is a great sight to see the trucks
go down the mountains every afternoon filled with teachers and
students, boys and girls. It is something hard to describe in words


80                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 105 ---
to watch Straus, the ex—German psychiatrist with a wonderful head
of white hair throwing rocks to a young girl who throws them to a
boy who sets them in the wall which others have prepared with a
bed of cement. I helped on the wall one afternoon and felt happier
at the end, more whole and ready for thought than I have in years.
And how much better than putting all that into a football team! I
don’t know how colleges could be re-built by every incoming fresh-
man-year but somehow or other that’s what should happen. We
simply cannot afford the intellectual slovenliness, immaturity, lack
of reality and sentimentality which the average college produces—
if it is not snobbery—any longer.
     I attended the best, most disciplined English class I have seen
at Black Mountain. Everyone had his say, and yet the class had a
focus and an end and got there (it was a discussion of themes). I
gave a lecture on Yeats and as a result one girl spent a whole week
studying the Yeats books I had (every now and then at BMC they
“declare an interlude,” which means that people can work for a
week and not go to classes. The best work gets done then). And she
took the interlude to study Yeats. They are not perfect. I wrote them
a long bad but provocative poem on freedom which we discussed
at length. The College was founded on a negative—’We don’t want
this and we don’t want that” and they have made a fetish of free-
dom. But at least they are always asking themselves whether they
are right and there is continual dissatisfaction and improvement
and clearing of fundamental issues going on. Every single person
in the College feels responsible for it. And it was wonderful how
they took my really rather bold and perhaps superficial criticism
after being there a week. We had an excellent impersonal discus-
sion. But they lack homage (that was one of the things I brought up)
and awe. Some substitute has got to be found for religion—all these
things have got to be related to something larger and more absolute
than either the individual or even the community whether it be
Black Mountain or the U.S.A. How can that be done? There is not
enough silence for one thing. People should sometimes by united in
silence.
      There is so much to be done here that there is no reason for
despair.

James Hall
                                                 (Student   1940-41)


         Excerpts from The Berkshire Sampler,
                              6 February 1977



I came to BMC hot from my labors as a passenger brakeman on the
Long Island Railroad. A series of minor disasters . . . prompted cer-
tain railroad officials to hint that I might be more profitably em-
ployed elsewhere. In fact, when I joyously announced that I had a
scholarship to Black Mountain College, the passenger trainmaster
handed me a railroad pass to North Carolina, sighed mightily, and
firmly closed his office door.
     So off I went to BMC and the most amazing year of my life. My
interest in music quickly petered out, especially after my piano
teacher gave me up as hopeless. I did learn to read the scores of the
six Brandenburg Concerti by Bach. I read the scores and listened to
the music on records over and over for the entire school year. By
June the poor records, which were not high fidelity to begin with,
could only greak and queech. My only other musical activity took
place when   I helped the famous    harpsichordist,   Yella Pessl, as-
semble her harpsichord. She was there with Claude Monteux, the
son of conductor Pierre, and Yella and Claude played a number of
Bach flute and harpsichord sonatas with such success that they re-
peated their performance the next day.
     Wild to have a rounded education . . . I signed up for a course
called Werklehre,   which translated out to something like “work
with materials.” We were supposed to root about and gather to-
gether shapes and textures,    and arrange them together in some in-
teresting kind of way. I distinctly remember assembling on a tray
some orange-topped mushrooms, a rusty bed-spring, and some
blackened pieces of toast. We brought our “compositions” to Class,
slowly paraded them around the room, sneering at everybody
else’s. The generally elicited comment that my composition was too
somber led me to offer to scrape the toast a little.


82                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 107 ---
[At the school] there was a minimum of structure and organi-
zation. Classes were held regularly, but they were tailored to the
interests of the students and the abilities of the teachers and they
met at all hours. .. . The teachers of BMC .. . worked for virtually
no salary, asking only to be allowed to perfect their arts and to
communicate to their students an excitement about their work.
They were like medieval monks preserving on parchment the frag-
ments of a vanished civilization, while down in the valley the bar-
barian hordes were howling their menace.
     I left Black Mountain after one year, in June of 1941, for my
money was short and the world was calling. If I had been asked at
that time what I learned, I would have been hard put to reply. Now
I know. I learned a whole set of values. Not many skills, but impor-
tant values. Kindness, mutual respect, and the perfection of one’s
art are what make a civilization and a life.




                                                I. S. Nakata
                                       (Student 1940-43, 1946-48)


                                 In That Time Long Ago



There were many activities at Black Mountain depending only on
the seasons, the day’s weather, companions, or mood. Thus it was
easy to organize between ourselves or even singly, to go for a little
walk or on a longer hike, a swim, [to have] a conversation or to go
hunting mushrooms. Dr. Dehn interested many of us in forest
mushrooms, with which he was expertly acquainted. The idea of
benefiting from nature’s bounty was taken for granted. Not only
mushrooms, there were wild persimmon, hickory, and butternut, a
few chestnut and walnut, and apple trees on the property if not
nearby. On the upper pasture ridge grew wild ramps which were
pungently good with soup mixes. Near the farm there were healthy


An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                 83

--- Page 108 ---
clumps of wild cress, garlic, and dandelion for salads. Sassafras
roots were saved from clearings and diggings, as were young spice-
wood branches for brewing tea. In the summer there were the big
blackberries and the tiny wild strawberries. Once Alexa McLane
and I picked these small but wonderful strawberries near the Col-
lege dump, which was across the road and down a way toward the
highway. It was tedious but happy work. She was going to put up
strawberry jam into jars, cooking on a hotplate, to leave with me. I
think we got four or five jars of delicious jam which I thankfully
enjoyed into the next year while she attended the University of
Nebraska. It was a sweet remembrance, indeed! So fragrant too!
      Hauling coal was one of the most grimy chores at the College.
The coal was hauled from a railroad siding in Swannanoa involv-
ing shoveling the coal from a coal car to the dump truck to the
North and South lodges, the student housing, and to the large bin
for the Studies Building furnace. I think the faculty bungalows
were firewood or electrically heated. Some excitement and romance
attached to the job, seemingly. It was hard sweaty work and our
clothes got sooty, but I think we enjoyed our black faces. There was
something mysterious in the eyes that shone out that somehow
obliterated other prominent features of a face. It could have been an
underlying sympathy or kinship with the black person. Generally,
it was a two-person job, but sometimes three squeezed into the cab
of the dump truck. One of my jobs one summer was to drive the
dump truck hauling coal with Jane King as my helper. The special
bonus that went with the job was a steak dinner to be eaten out to
the back of the kitchen before we washed up. Jack Lipsey had a re-
ally special way with steaks which were generous in size and per-
fect.
     Tree cutting for various purposes was a popular work activity.
It was necessary for lumber/planking for concrete forms or rough
structuring or scaffolding, or for pile driving for the foundations of
the Studies Building-to-be. Ken Kurtz, who taught literature, led
the first teams [while wearing] a red bandanna headcovering, look-
ing much like a pirate with his dashing mustache. We carried-axes,
a two-man saw, peaveys for moving logs, and big steel wedges. We
mainly sought straight trees like pine and the tulip poplar tree. At
break time we would have water and some cookies somebody
brought along. Ken was a good raconteur, having done some trav-

84                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 109 ---
eling and knocking about in his student days in Europe and Amer-
ica. We'd all get acquainted asking questions and answering them
before going back to work. Trimming branches and piling them
neatly were mainly left to the female members of the crew. De-
pending on the use for the logs, they were cut into appropriate
lengths for later hauling out with a tractor or a horse driven by Ro-
scoe Penley, the resident farmer at that time. Many students be-
came expert in felling trees to the desired direction. Although there
were those who were clumsy in the two-man saw—pulling and
pushing instead of just pulling—we soon developed good teams of
compatible partners. Faf Foster and I made a good team.
     The after-dinner dancing was mostly for Saturday nights, the
frequency during the week depending on the piano player or re-
quests, begging or acclamation. In the earlier years John Evarts,
who taught music, was the piano player and also acted as social
director, in the latter role playing “Happy Birthday” at someone’s
birthdate with everyone singing. Before, during, or after dinner, it
was a rousing fun occasion for all. The dance music was fox-trot
and waltzes but there was a polka or two that John played with
gusto to some group singing. There was much sedate dancing with
so many watchful eyes picking up on couples but I think the atmos-
phere relaxed after the first couple of years at Lake Eden. Elsa Kahl
(Cohen) gave a short course on Viennese waltzing in ‘42 or ’43 that
was very impressive. Being an ex-ballerina (with the Joost Ballet),
she was most athletic and emphatic in her (and our) movements:
ONE-two-three,    ONE-two-three,     making   sure   that we   bent   the
knee on the first beat. I was a clumsy student for her, but I thor-
oughly enjoyed her uninhibited style and attitude. A few of the stu-
dents were accomplished dancers doing fancy waltzing elegantly
and gracefully. Though some students didn’t dance, dancing
brought people together to a somewhat festive occasion, especially
for the shy and awkward. It was a social highlight, light and relax-
ing. The concerts and plays were enjoyable too in a more heady se-
rene atmosphere. To be lighthearted, to be serious, were both nec-
essary.
     Bob Babcock’s class in political science my first year on the sec-
ond floor of Lee Hall stands out in my mind. Most of the class were
second- or third-year students and well read in the classical studies
of politics, government, and the nature of man. We read selectively

An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                     85

--- Page 110 ---
and wrote reports. The discussions in class, with Bob as a kind of
moderator fiddling with his usually unlit pipe, revolved around the
“heavy” thinkers: Jerry Wolpert, Lucian Marquis, Susie Noble,
Gene Raymond,     perhaps Eric Barnitz. It was a revelation for me,
still green from the sugarcane fields of Oahu, to assess the knowl-
edge being thrown up and debated. The class was an excellent ori-
entation for the studying required in the liberal arts; it was possible
and necessary to become excited by the thinking that was so much
a force and   the raison   d’étre   of countries,   movements,   people.
Words were ideas and ideals to transform the world or to modify it
to a certain perspective, for good or ill in presumptive action. Hitler
and Marx dominated the then world stage with Mein Kampf and
Das Kapital, but they came at the end of the course. More urbane
and careful scholars were the prerequisites—and rightly so—to lay
the groundwork for Western civilization. For the last meeting of the
class it was decided we’d have Bermuda         onions, bread, and beer.
Each of us would contribute an unsigned original paper of political
theory. It was fun to guess who wrote the piece as it was read.
Needless to say, I was thrilled when nobody could guess mine. It
was a Stylistic piece on self-discipline, somewhat utopian in con-
cept. Being well received made me proud. I’d stumped some future
scholars in that class.
     Roscoe Penley, the resident farmer who lived with his wife and
three sons in the two-story frame house next to the farm, was          a
ruddy, boxy man exuding strength. He’d worked in the Morgan
furniture factory down the road in Swannanoa but thought out-
door work suited him better. He was a rough-hewn man not overly
patient with his wife or his children but unduly so with the College
people. On the whole he was a good-natured soul. We’d ask him all
sorts of questions: What kind of tree is that? What’s the name of
that wild flower? Is that wild plant good to eat? How do you
sharpen a hoe? How do you use a scythe? Did he like the Navy?
Did he ever get drunk? Did he think he’d ever amount to anything?
He must have had his doubts. Though we were not lackadaisical,
we were on the whole not dedicated farmers. We tried in our-own
way, having fun as we learned and worked.
    For someone who made an impact on my life, albeit a short but
intense influence, Karl Niebyl was somewhat of an enigma to me.
A man of iron logic and biting sarcasm, Karl used his knowledge

86                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 111 ---
and experience, grounded in European Marxism, as a torch bearer
for a new social order. Unremitting, unforgiving in analysis and
observation, he forged his viewpoint in dialectical soundness.      His
polemics brooked no contradiction. He knew where he was coming
from: historical studies had shown him the place of “labor” in the
production process which created the inevitable “class struggle” in
the division of the surplus. When he left abruptly after only a year,
I felt myself in limbo for a while. Karl was to [have been] my fac-
ulty advisor with whom I would consult to prepare a senior thesis
and get ready for the graduation examination. I was well grounded
into Marxist thinking, but it had tended to be exclusive. I was to see
that it was one-sided with its emphasis on the class struggle to the
exclusion of the dynamism of the marketplace and entrepre-
neurism. For me, with Dave Corkran and Tasker Howard as my
new mentors, a cooling, less ardent, but serious studying came to
be. It was a down-to-earth step-by-step progression for my senior
paper or thesis. I had to become conversant, too, in related fields for
my examinations, written and oral.
     Trying Dave's patience, I waded in with Marxist zeal and per-
sonal anger, the baggage of past orientation. Dave and I often ar-
gued to no conclusion, amicably to be sure, for he was a gentle
kindly man. He was never abrasive or belittling, always encourag-
ing me to gather more insight into events, personalities, and mo-
ments of importance. He was a calming influence on me, forcing
me to assess myself, asking myself to be honest in my beliefs.
     Eric Bentley was a somewhat mercurial figure with wit, bril-
liance, and a touch of mischief. I took a course with him called
“Twentieth Century,” as I recall. In the brief time of a semester, Eric
covered the world for, or with, us. If you didn’t do the work or read
suggested books, you could have been left behind or have lost
something others in the class gained. I first encountered the theory
of geopolitics, of the world island, in Eric’s discussion of the theo-
ries of MacKinder. Another book for the class made a deep impres-
sion on me. It was Franz Neumann's Behemoth, an analysis of the
systematic betrayal of the middle class in Germany and the annihi-
lation of the small Jewish population. To me, Eric Bentley was the
most talented person to come to BMC. His talents were more in or
of the theater, wherein he directed a few plays at the college. He
was an intellectual through and through. If he disdained manual

labor on the work program, he worked, he told me once, on other
things like plays, amusements for the community. To him, that-was
comparable labor. It was hard to disagree on the quality or quantity
of labor when it was supposedly voluntary, be it intellectual or
manual, from the sweat of one’s brows or one’s palms.




88                              Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 113 ---
In assessing my experience at Black Mountain, I cannot sepa-
rate my individual growing-up, though I was twenty-one when I
started. I had come from a then provincial, if not backward, area.
Nearly eight years had passed. Much had happened to me, to the
country, to the College itself with its increasing inner struggles for
power or policy determinations, for financial liquidity. It was a liv-
able community, decent in most respects regarding human rela-
tionships. Academically it was makeshift with the burden on the
student to see his way clear to his objective. The inadequacy of the
faculty and the library, while important, was not decisive since
they were good or helpful. The gaps in staff availability were more
crucial in directing the student in the tools of his field. In my case, I
never could get a full course in statistics, and I was not interested
enough to get it by myself. But no matter. Who can forget the inti-
mate   concerts, plays, speakers, or art exhibitions; the hard work
and play; the walks into the woods or up the mountains singly or
together; the swims in the lake at all hours; gathering wild black-
berries or mountain ramps or mushrooms in meadows or in the
woods; or just gathering branches to admire, sassafras roots to
brew, or simply getting together with kindred souls for an ex-
change of lives. Or even to fall in love. It was a total experience, as
total as I would ever experience. It fulfilled my life and yet de-
tracted from it in its fulfillment. A part of me will never leave BMC.
It was a kind of adolescence, a process in maturing. The scars of a
forgotten hurt are still there, everybody’s, for there was contact in
unforeseen ways.
     Black Mountain was first and foremost and always the people
it attracted and held in its dream of a better community, a different
kind of educational adventure. If it didn’t succeed by objective
standards, that is only for the record. For myself, it was a period of
absorbing interests with no time to be bored, even alone. Especially
alone. Black Mountain was the base and sanctuary for my survival
and sanity during a most crucial period in my life, a home and a cli-
mate, distinctive, fascinating, supportive.

Jane Slater Marquis
                                                   (Student 1940-45)


                                       This Paradise Apart



A visitor to BMC once described the atmosphere as “erotically
charged.” It was. As I remember everyone in the place was always
“in love” or just out of it or planning to be or talking about it. But
that was only a part of the whole sensual world that made up our
daily life.
     It all took place in a landscape of heartbreaking beauty. Won-
derfully out of the ordinary world—isolated from the families,
communities, expectations, obligations, and banalities that we had
left behind and were later, inevitably, to return to.
     At eighteen I nearly fainted with wonder at the ferns and moss
thick on the paths outside the buildings that led up the mountain to
Blue Ridge. I was a girl from rocky Utah. My “first love” and I
would climb this trail late at night. I have forgotten him but not the
pungency of the fragrant woods or the moonlight on them. In the
fall the leaves turned not just red and yellow, but pink, apricot,
burgundy, ochre, and a hundred other heady colors. In the spring
the undergrowth bloomed canopies of dogwood, mountain laurel
(pink geometric blossoms sticky to the touch), and the surprise of
wild rhododendron. If your study was on the lakeside of the Stud-
ies Building, you had to close your windows, come April, against
the deafening racket of new frogs in the lake.
     Once I opened the yarn cupboard during a weaving class, and
on the shelf of the red yarns lay a shiny black snake. Anni Albers
called for a “hero,” and Ted Dreier came and gently wrapped it on
his arm and loosed it to the fields outside.
     The days were full of drama. Little and big. Who sat with
whom at lunch or dinner? How many dump truck loads of-river
rock could one crew make in one afternoon? One spring afternoon,
Polly and I stopped hoeing the bean field to watch a curtain of
warm rain slowly advance across the valley and pass softly
through us and beyond. Three times a week there was the theatre


90                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 115 ---
of “Juppi arrives for class.” For me, this was always a moment of
great excitement—that quickening instant of expectation as the cur-
tain goes up. And there he was in his unbelievably white jacket,
white shirt, white cigarette perched in his mouth, and we all knelt
together on the floor to examine with never-before-or-after inten-
sity each others’ work for each class—whether drawing, painting,
color, or Werklehre.
     One fateful summer, sounds of Schénberg floated all day for
weeks over the lake from the dining hall. Famous visitors were re-
hearsing for [the composer’s] seventieth birthday while the two
resident opposing factions tore this place, this promised land, this
paradise apart.




                                                     F. A. Foster
                                          (Student 1941-43, 1946-48)


                                Black Mountain College



A candid history of the College would admit that it could be a very
different place at various times depending on the skills, philoso-
phy, taste and participation of the faculty of the moment. One mis-
conception which must be met unequivocally is the belief that BMC
was only an art school. This view was merely cultish and at any
time reflected the interest of a small minority of the faculty and stu-
dents. It was a concept unjust to the philosophy and curriculum of
the College. Black Mountain was a Liberal Arts College; the Physi-
cal and Social Sciences, Literature, History, Languages and Mathe-

When Faf was living in Montreat, N.C., he printed a booklet that pro-
vided his own views of the history, characteristics, social and academic
structure of the school. He sold it for a small fee to interested people
visiting the area. Here is a portion of this informative and well-written
piece.


An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                     9]

--- Page 116 ---
matics were taught until near the end. Competence in these areas
as well as in the Arts varied, of course. Summer    Institute faculty
were not available during the regular year; however some of the
regular staff taught during the Summer. The Institutes were invari-
ably a success. Everyone profited from the diversity of academic
offerings. One value that the College stressed was that while inten-
sive study of a particular area was essential, it was perhaps more
important to establish the relationships to avoid parochialism.
Learning was continuous, not arbitrarily fragmented in content and
time. It was assumed that one was mature enough to profit from
this opportunity. One’s self-respect was never threatened in this
spectrum of responsibility by an academic “establishment” which
imposed a whimsical discipline on either the student or teacher.
Anyone who has heard of BMC knows something about its superb
faculty, however transient. Any list will seem arbitrary and the one
which follows is intended primarily to suggest the breadth of tal-
ent. In music one found Samuel     Barber, Carol Brice, John Cage,
Yella Pessl, Fritz Cohen. Among the art teachers were Lyonel Fein-
inger, Ossip Zadkine and Robert Motherwell. Bernard Leach and
Jiro Hamada held a pottery workshop. Walter Gropius appeared
occasionally. Buckminster Fuller held one of his unique and
indefinable seminars. Merce Cunningham taught dance. Anais Nin,
Alfred Kazin and Eric Bentley were among the literary types.
Beaumont Newhall taught photography. Among the more or less
perennial staff one would have to mention the Albers, and Charles
Olson.
    Any description of the College which suggested that Lake
Eden was an eponym would be quite misleading. Though there
was a surprising amount of amity in this often too intimate commu-
nity, there were of course some   negatives: vanities, divisiveness,
scandals, cults, and some shrillness from both sexes. Put together
Europeans and Americans, educated and uneducated, lazy and
energetic, the Left and the Right; add to this the snobberies of art-
ists and intellectuals and one can easily see how fertile it was for
problems, but this also created opportunities for problem-solving.
The College was a test of character as well as one of intellect for
both the student and teacher and there were failures in both
groups. But what a bore it would have been to live with the self-
sacrificing instead of among the comfortably hedonistic. It was

92                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 117 ---
quite pleasant most of the time—everyone being essentially an
amateur in community experience.
     Some community members had a particular handicap from
which they never quite found relief; they were the very sophisti-
cated European teachers. Although never diminished by their role
as refugees, nor should they have been, they could occasionally be
a problem to themselves and to the native born. Many were slow in
their understanding of American mores, others indifferent to them,
and a rare one intelligently critical. They could be naive, smug and
tactless; but occasionally, often almost accidentally, they were con-
structive. Still there was something almost classical about the dif-
ferences, a conflict essentially untainted by race, religion, politics or
allegiance to particular schools of psychology. At [worst] the es-
trangement   was   almost   tribal, but never   without   interest, when
well-mannered... .
     One community characteristic that was disappointing was its
indifference toward its setting—the beautiful Blue Ridge. Perhaps
this was to be expected from urban types. A few mushroom and
flower pickers got off campus, but Botany, Geology, even Folkways
were neglected. Only a couple of families went to the ridges and
more distant mountains. Most contented themselves with a swim
and sunbathing. In fairness it should be mentioned that even then
the other side of North Fork was being ravaged—a sad, destructive
activity which still continues.
     Lastly, an appreciative comment should be made about the
non-teaching staff: office personnel, farm labor, maintenance help,
cooks and maids. Most were locals and all were invariably compe-
tent and courteous. They educated many students in patience and
even manners. Being the College’s main contact with nearby com-
munities, they helped provide some entrée to them and .. . the de-
velopment of some mutual understanding.


Formal Organization.
1.   Owned by the faculty and students and totally controlled by
     them.
2.   Administrative and faculty appointments made by a Board of
     Fellows (8) elected by the faculty. No President (a Rector), or
     Deans. No academic spoils system.


An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                     93

--- Page 118 ---
3.   A chief student officer (Student Moderator) was elected by the
     students who was also a full member of the Board of Fellows
     with all voting rights.
4.   There   was   also   a Community   Council,   representing   faculty,
     students, and other staff, which considered problems relating
     to the total community, something like a New England Town
     Meeting.


Academic        Calendar
The academic year was divided into semesters: Sept. to Feb. and
Feb. to June. Summer       Institutes were also held, usually centering
on Music and Art.


Work Program
This was considered to be of fundamental educational importance
for all. There was no respite for students, but the faculty didn’t al-
ways play the game.
1. Intellectual activity was tempered or relieved by the physical.
2. It promoted insight into what was purported to be the pride
    Labor takes in a job well done—Veblen’s Instinct for Work-
    manship.
3.   Promoted self-discipline so essential to intellectual work as
     well.
4.   A by-product was physical well-being.
5.   It was of vital economic importance to the College: the dairy-
     ing, lumbering; agriculture, building, maintenance       and haul-
     ing.
6.   One worked with a staff of local persons, mutual interests were
     discovered and better community relations established.


Admission
1.   There were no fixed requirements A transcript of secondary
     and college credits was usually forwarded along with five ref-
     erences. Low grades did not necessarily disqualify if there
     were compensatory elements. An art student might submit a
     portfolio or a literature student poetry.



94                        Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 119 ---
2    The Admission Committee was composed of faculty and stu-
     dents.
=    Each student was assigned a Faculty Advisor.


Tuition
im   This varied from year to year. In 1943 it was on a sliding scale
     of $450 to $1200. In 1948 it was a flat fee of $1600.
     Scholarships were always available. In 1948 grants of up to
     $1100 were provided.


Graduation
i    Progress toward graduation was in two stages: a Junior Divi-
     sion and a Senior Division. The time spent in each Division was
     not fixed.
     To move from the Junior Division one had to pass a Compre-
     hensive Examination which was offered each year; this tested
     language, mathematics and one’s general educational back-
     ground with some unusual and provocative questions. A ma-
     ture and imaginative response was expected. The test was
     evaluated by the whole faculty.
     If the Comprehensive test was passed the student submitted a
     course of study designed to carry him through graduation. If
     this was accepted, the student was admitted to the Senior Divi-
     sion.
     When the student, with the concurrence of his advisor, consid-
     ered himself ready to graduate, the faculty was informed. The
     student’s work was then evaluated by the faculty in a written
     and oral examination. If the results were satisfactory, an Out-
     side Examiner was then appointed (Harvard, Columbia, UNC).
     The Outside Examiner submitted written questions and if these
     were answered satisfactorily he then came to the College to
     examine the student orally. At the Oral Examination the entire
     faculty was also free to ask questions. The Outside Examiner
     then submitted his report, which if favorable resulted in gradu-
     ation.
     The “diploma” was simply a letter signed by the Rector stating




An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                 95

--- Page 120 ---
that the Faculty voted to graduate the student and awarded
     him an A.B. degree.

      I do of course value my BMC experience. It was amateurism at
its best. An ideal place for an auto-didact. I could learn in comfort
and even with passion from other auto-didacts. I was taught Bot-
any by a mathematician, Ecology from an historian, Sociology from
a philosopher, and how to avoid social pathology, from observing
musicians and painters. The place was innocent of academic caste
and the associated mindless mechanics. One could learn in the
classroom of course, but probably the most significant learning oc-
curred outside of it. Almost everywhere one could learn in dignity
and comfort.




                          Zoya Sandomirsky Slive
                                                  (Student 1941 SS)


                         Another Kind of Hoe Down



I generally don’t like to look back, and my association with Black
Mountain was very brief. I was there only for one summer. But it
was a very significant summer. I was very young, having just come
to the U.S. as a refugee from wartorn Europe in the summer of
1940. My family settled in Chicago, and I was dispatched to a local
high school. It was not a good fit. I took exams at the University of
Chicago and was admitted without a high-school diploma. I was
reluctant to go. A friend of my mother’s suggested BMC as an al-
ternative and urged me to give it a try during the summer session.
     What I remember of that summer was being quarantined in my
little study cubicle for two weeks because of German      measles; a
very heady course with Eric Bentley; the warmth and friendliness



96                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 121 ---
of Frances     de Graaf, who    taught Russian    history; the smiling
wrinkled      face of a refugee musicologist,   Heinrich   Jalowetz;   the
rather obnoxious, “eingebildete” [conceited] professor of philoso-
phy, Straus; the modesty and great teaching abilities of Josef Al-
bers. I also remember hoeing corn a lot under the burning summer
sun and helping build a silo. This was the closest I ever came to a
kibbutz and helped to satisfy my longing for a communal, socialist
experience.
        On the other hand, I found Black Mountain extremely frustrat-
ing. As you may have noted, I tended to stay with my fellow refu-
gees. Yet I was eager to find out more about my new homeland.
Black Mountain was too isolated. By chance, a small incident al-
tered my course.
     My curiosity to see life beyond the College made me one day
sneak a ride with a truck to Asheville. While the driver did his er-
rands, I roamed around, stopped at a five-and-ten store, and in the
back of the store had a drink from a water fountain. Suddenly I felt
dozens of eyes piercing my back. I turned around and saw people
staring at me. What did I do wrong? A sign above the water foun-
tain read: “For Colored.”
     A few days later, while hoeing corn, I stopped in the middle of
the field, put my hoe down, packed my bags, and returned to Chi-
cago. I decided I needed to find out about this crazy country to
which I owed my life, and where I had come seeking freedom, but
a country which at the same time denied freedom to a large portion
of its own citizens. I felt Black Mountain was not the place to do
that.
    At the University of Chicago I majored in sociology, later be-
came a civil-rights activist, and then in the Nixon years changed to
psychology and now have a private practice in psychotherapy.

Mary Gregory
                                                  (Faculty 1941-47)


                   Speech Given at Bard College



Don’t you think it’s quite impressive that we are talking about
BMC in 1987? Still talking, wondering to a certain extent, disagree-
ing—militant or wistful. We are here to celebrate the arts at Black
Mountain College, to look at an exhibition of work done at the Col-
lege, and to join Mary Emma     Harris in the launching of her new
book. It is important that the Edith C. Blum Art Institute is includ-
ing in the exhibition plays and workshops which reexamine and
perhaps reinterpret what we did from 1933 to 1957. We speak with
pride of “impact on the American scene.” We can still look at the
art and, while looking at it, remember the people who produced it.
It is wonderful that there is this tangible evidence all put together
in an exhibit. Thirty years is a long time, and those of us who re-
member BMC or are living reminders of what we knew there are
rapidly slipping away.
      I am asked to talk with you about the work program . . at
BMC. As in everything we did, the work program was subjected to
all kinds of scrutiny and interpretation, positive and negative; and
as is almost always the case, there was an element of truth in each
explanation or definition. Some felt that it was here that leadership
skills could be developed. Some saw manual labor as a leveler,
rather like Chairman Mao’s Cultural Revolution. There were those
who thought it exploitation to ask people paying for an education
to wear themselves out hauling coal.
      We had graduate architectural students sent to us to get practi-
cal experience in building. We did build several buildings designed
by faculty and students. We did have a farm which supplied meat,
milk, vegetables. We did help with maintenance chores, worked in
a print shop. There was a woodworking shop where we built fur-

This speech was given at the BMC reunion at Bard College, 11 April
1987.


98                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 123 ---
nishings, lab equipment, utensils for parties, and small houses for
pigs. Our labor was needed. We worked closely with local profes-
sionals:   carpenter,   farmer, plumber,   a maintenance
                                                     genius. They
taught us, saved us from becoming too theoretical or even too self-
satisfied.
     The Bauhaus is credited with including crafts in their percep-
tion of the arts. Albers, in our classes, asked us to look at what man
had made, not selectively or chronologically but widely. We looked
at pottery designs, bridges, tools, buildings, paintings, at how
things went together, at how things grew. It was exciting. He asked
us to figure out what made each idea work. He asked us to look
and look but, in looking, to trust and to use our own perceptions
creatively and neatly. It isn’t a great jump to become fascinated by
how each job we do—be         it being a farmer,   a woodworker, or mu-
sician, or machine operator—calls for the same scrutiny, the same
excitement in finding how all these skills (or needs) may be used to
make one’s own contribution, perhaps even in a new way. It is one
thing to have a glimpse of the possibilities, but it is another to be in
a place where this insight may be tried and even used, and best of
all, needed.
      It is tremendously exciting to be in an environment where you
are called to use all of your faculties in every direction. To be for a
moment     the true Renaissance man, creator, theorist, administrator,
experimenter, student, teacher, laborer. To be in an institution that
is willing to be totally responsible for what it believes and what it
does. I think it is a mistake to think of the “work program” at BMC
unless our definition of work encompasses all that went on there.
The dictionary defines work: “Physical or mental effort or activity
directed toward the accomplishment of something.” That’s safe
enough!
     The College was self-administered without outside ownership
or trustees. Students were included on the governing board. It was
asked from the start that everyone who joined the community be a
contributor, not a purchaser. The faculty shared their books. They
were the administrators, raised outside funds, explained and inter-
preted, gave concerts or played the piano so we could dance to-
gether and forget our struggles. I suspect that it was in this area
that the College was unique; one was asked to give something of



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                    99

--- Page 124 ---
oneself. It is possible to be lost or suffocated in an institution, bent
on offering everything, apparently needing nothing. It was indeed
a great leap of faith and many were touched by it. They came and
were part of a creative, vibrant whole, needed and made alive. Per-
haps one irony lies in the vulnerability of this organism. To join it
and help it to grow, it is implicit that each new person wait to see
and appreciate what is already there and then, based on that under-
standing and respect, to move      ahead. Conversely, those already
there, who made it, are required to be open, always clear and ready
to answer.




                                      News from the Farm



The farm looks well. It was fun to see it change with three seasons.
It was a relief in the fall with the different greens of winter wheat,
rye and barley cover crops replacing the tired silage corn and soy-
bean stubble. During the winter the main cropping was compost; a
whole village street of rectangular piles which grew and were
turned over and over each time examined (for rate of decay) with
almost morbid solemnity.
     There is a milking machine, the cow stalls in the big barn have
been floored with cement, there are individual drinking cups for
the cows, the disk harrows have been brilliantly painted, the wheel-
barrow is bright blue. Also the beef herd has decreased (we ate a
good many) and the dairy herd enlarged. It was planned that way.
The community needs more milk. So the size of the beef breeding
herd has been cut down to enable the farm to support more milk
cows. Junket, an itinerant Guernsey heifer, upset the plans some-
what by having a black beefy calf.
     This spring the gully near the Jalowetz cottage has been cleared
for a calf pasture. The dairy and beef cattle have had nearly three

     months of grazing on selected, newly fenced, strips of cover crop
     which not only pleases the cows, but it also looks well.
          Now the potatoes are planted, silage corn, oats and landino
     clover. There are cabbages and onions well along above Black
     Dwarf; lettuce, radishes and (bewilderingly enough) cactus and lil-
     ies too, are in the Albers’ garden, and in front of the gradually
     growing farmhouse beans and sweet corn. Bennett’s land, eight
     acres which we rent, is in spring oats and barley, clover and le-
     spedeza. Forty-five hundred small trees have been planted on the
     eroded and steep parts of the mountain pasture; locusts, balsam,
     white pine: reforestation. There is a big unloading fork in the barn,
