--- Page 126 ---
three Guernsey calves rapidly growing, health records and tests,
and more which isn’t seen so clearly.
       Clifford Moles   and Raymond   Trayer have planned it all, di-
rected it, and done it with student help, hard work, and excitement.
They practically iron the fields after planting to insure order. They
are a mechanized pair and pretty gay with their tractor. Perhaps it
is all right for the time being that they have to use the horses to help
too.




                                      Gretel J. Lowinsky
                              (Faculty Spouse and Faculty 1942-47)


       Snapshots from Black Mountain College



It was during the winter after a Saturday-night concert in which he
had participated, I think, as a pianist. Jalo [Heinrich Jalowetz] went
over to Trudi and Erwin Straus’s for a drink, as he had often done,
before climbing the hill to his and Johanna’s cottage. Eddie and I
had left for Black Dwarf, our abode, to relieve the babysitter. Next
morning we learned that Jalo, stepping out of the Straus cottage,
told his hosts that he was “going home now,” and collapsed on the
lawn a few feet away, breathing his last breath. Sad though it was, I
always felt that this was a beautiful way of leaving this earth: filled
with music, and celebrating it with good friends.
     A year later, students and faculty, conducted by Eddie, per-
formed Brahms’s Requiem to honor Jalo’s memory. I recall singing
the tenor part with several other females and very few male addi-
tions. It was   a memorable    performance, and whenever     I hear the
work now I think back to this occasion when I first got acquainted
with it.
     After a concert during the Summer Session of 1944, we had in-
vited the performers and some students to our cottage for drinks. I



102                      Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 127 ---
walked up the hill with our star performer, gamba player Eva
  Heinitz, unlocked   the door to the living room,and turned on a
  light, while Eva walked through the door. I heard a scream, and
  turning around I saw Eva jump up on my coffee table in utter dis-
  may: a little green garter snake had entered my living room to-
  gether with her! She would only come down from her pedestal
  when I removed the uninvited guest.
       On my daily walk downhill to the dining hall, I saw in the dis-
  tance a strange figure leaning near the icebox outside the kitchen.
  Coming closer I discerned [the] dangling legs and part [of the]
  rump belonging to our poet, George Zabriskie. His head and shoul-
  ders were inside the box, whose top had fallen on his back while,
  apparently, he was searching for something.        An unforgettable
  sight! He looked rather pale when I freed him.
       George Zabriskie, we were told by his wife, was unable to tol-
  erate the sight and smell of warm milk; would we please abstain
  from serving it with coffee. The warning came too late: George had
  already swooned, leaning backward pale-facedly into the waiting
  arms of Elizabeth.
       The general atmosphere at BMC was full of life and excitement,
  sometimes too much of the latter. From one year to the next we
  would not know whether the College would open in the fall. Inter-
- nal tensions between faculty members or staff or students were
  high. Heated discussion ranged from length of shorts for the girls
  each spring to ideological political differences, which nearly
  stopped the functioning of the College in 1944. Faculty meetings
  were often upsetting, hot-tempered, and created rifts. Since I was
  not a faculty member, I heard this only secondhand. But I noticed
  the tension after such a meeting at mealtime: one had to watch not
  to find oneself at the same table as “the enemy.” I remember Sam
  Brown, student moderator at these meetings for a long time, who
 seemed to be better able to overcome tensions and animosities, and
 was better balanced than many a faculty member.
      When we hosted visiting faculty or had Summer Institutes, the
  air was filled with mostly positive excitement. I recall the visit by
  Erich Kahler, whose lectures created great enthusiasm. Painter Lyo-
  nel Feininger, and his wife Julia, experts in Bach’s music, were very
_ wonderful and wise people. Photographer Beaumont Newhall,



 An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                 103

--- Page 128 ---
member of the 1946 and 1947 Summer Institutes, taught Eddie and
me how to photograph manuscripts, as we were getting ready to go
to Italy, for which Eddie had won a Guggenheim Fellowship in
1946. (The trip had to be postponed for one year because of Jalo’s
untimely death. The College had asked Eddie to stay with the mu-
sic department until August 1947.)
     Other memories deal with the musical life of my BMC years.
My first violin student at BMC was David Schauffler, younger
brother of Anna, a fine cellist (whose untimely death shocked me). I
felt honored by the College’s and David’s trust in me, especially so,
since he had played the violin already for several years. He did
progress in the short time that we worked together. Teaching a
class on chamber music for Anna Schauffler’s benefit, Jalo involved
Trudi Straus and me in playing quartets and trios, sometimes hav-
ing us perform a work at a Saturday-night concert. Eddie and I per-
formed much of the Classical and Baroque sonata literature for vio-
lin and piano at these weekly occasions. One highpoint was getting
ready for the presentation of Mozart’s Divertimento for String Trio,
with Trudi Straus, violin; my sister, then Ilse Hoffmann, cello; and
myself, viola.
     I remember Fanny (Hobart) Dreyfus playing Bach’s Art of the
Fugue with Eddie on two pianos. Fanny had drawn a graphic repre-
sentation of the structure and development of each fugue, which
was shown to the audience while the music sounded. My mother,
Emma Hoffmann, a painter, who visited us at the College several
times, was inspired by Bach’s great work and the graphic represen-
tation, and ‘painted in shapes and colors—oil as well as water-
color—the emotional content of some of the fugues.
      When   Roland   Hayes visited for a Summer    Session   in 1945,
Emma Hoffmann painted in oil not only their daughter, Africa, but
Mrs. Hayes as well. The concert given by Roland Hayes is still viv-
idly in my memory: a small voice with great emotional strength
and expressiveness, that was able to hold in its spell a large group
of people from all walks of life.
     I also like to think back on a performance of the Sixth
Brandenburg Concerto by Bach, in which Marcel Dick and I played
the two solo violas. Under Erwin Bodky’s leadership we presented
Bach’s Musical Offering with Frances Snow Drinker as flutist.
     I know that the years I spent at BMC have been very important

104                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 129 ---
ones for me, and have greatly influenced my present life: teaching
the violin, making chamber music as violinist or violist, and play-
ing in small and big orchestras.




                        Herbert B. Oppenheimer
                                                  (Student 1942-43)


                                     Nine Months at BMC



I was at Black Mountain from July 1942 until April 1943, when I
was called into active service with the air force. I loved that year.
The Saturday-night dances; singing with Dr. Jalowetz in the chorus
that contained as many teachers as students; classes with Albers
and Bentley, and most of all the extraordinary people, the intensity
of this small, brilliant community. I constantly talk about those
days. They were mostly crowded with activity and pleasure. I re-
call then making some tortured figurines in clay, and Albers heard
about this work. I showed them to him and was thrilled by his high
praise. I realized then I could be a sculptor, and I have continued
this art as an avocation related to my work as an architect. I remem-
ber a class with Albers in the basement of the new Studies Building.
(We were still stuffing insulation into the space below the roof
slab.) There must have been six or eight of us, a large class, and we
were sitting about a large heavy table. He began talking about the
weakness of the Gothic pointed arch, the lateral thrust that had to
be countered with the flying buttress and the pinnacle to prevent
the arch from moving out to failure. And he was suddenly up on
the table, his arms thrusting down to his hands on his thighs, his
legs moving apart—the buttress, the pinnacle, and the arch. The
danger of physical collapse was frightening!
    Frances de Graaf and Eric Bentley were the faculty members
most concerned with the raging political world beyond the cam-
pus, and several of us would gather with them to talk and drink.


An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                 105

--- Page 130 ---
Fran was a large graceful woman, who danced sedately but I recall
one of our parties when she was inspired to place a chair on either
side, crouch    between     them,    and   then   to some      stirring   Russian
dance music, with an elbow on each chair, she began a tsatska, furi-
ously kicking out, bouncing up and down. Since that time and un-
der similar conditions, I have repeated that stunt.
      There were so few young men at the school that each time Bob
Wunsch or Fritz Cohen or Eric Bentley were staging an event, they
conscripted me. I loved those Saturday-night performances: Ethan
Frome, The Taming of the Shrew, and the singing.
     My nine months at Black Mountain were probably the most in-
tense several months of my life, and the happiest, but I have often
wondered at the irony of my pleasure at a time when so much of
the world was in a state of agony and horror, literally burning in
pain. I am glad I had those nine months. Living in an isolated com-
munity of limited size and scale is perhaps even more valuable to-
day, when we are in a world of vast scale and constant speed. We
seem to be producing more and more, with ever-decreasing sub-
stance.   Relationships    between     people     now   seem     more     limited,
more fragile. But then I was eighteen years old when I was at BMC.
It was an amazing time.




                                           Patsy Lynch Wood
                                                         (Student 1942-48)


                                           Impressions of Music
                                            at BMC in the 1940s



I entered Black Mountain during the summer of 1942, and after six
years, with time out here and there, I emerged in June 1948 an a
piece of paper saying I had successfully graduated in music. It was
signed by the chairman of the music department of Columbia Uni-
versity, who was my “outside” examiner. I was so happy and re-


106                       Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 131 ---
lieved this was over that I stayed on for the Music and Art Institute
 that summer and had the time of my life! For one thing, John Cage
 was there and got me involved in the Satie Festival. This proved to
 be a totally different musical experience from the years before.
 Cage’s presence was highly exhilarating, though it created an elec-
 tricity that tore the community into two musical camps. At meal-
 time, people stood up and made declarations about the very exis-
 tence of music! All kinds of feelings were generated and persisted
 throughout    the summer.    For me,   it was   time to be questioning
 music’s “status quo.”
      During the 1948 [Summer] Institute, I also took an Albers color
 course, which engaged me almost more than anything I had done
 before. Recently I [have been] making collages which I know are
 derived from my long exposure to the concepts of his art classes
 and the work of talented art students over the years. Many of these
 students were my friends. One of my favorite rituals at night was
 to take a look in the art room before walking down to the dorm.
 Perhaps someone was still up weaving or working on a project, and
 we would have a goodnight chat.
      Music at BMC in my first years centered around Heinrich Ja-
 lowetz, affectionately called “Jalo.” Here was a well-known Vien-
  nese opera conductor who found sanctuary in BMC from the dev-
_ astating events in Europe. Today there is a spate of documentaries
  on World War II that I’ve been watching. I keep wondering about
  the Jalowetz family, and what it must have been like for them be-
  fore and after their arrival here. The uncertainty of their lives, the
  pulling away from home, profession, friends. The grief and fatigue
  of the many losses. But as a young woman I only recall a generous,
  humane man, who gave to us everything he had experienced and
  knew about music. No question was too elementary for him to an-
 swer, and he often had to search for the English words he needed.
 We waited patiently because he was interested in meaning as well
 as facts. Our small classes would hover around the piano as he
 played and demonstrated. I can still see him sputtering—he was so
 enthusiastic and involved.
     Jalo had a sensitive, pliable face. He usually wore a white shirt
 and a bow tie, and his hair had been combed           back and slicked
 down. When he was in the throes of teaching or directing the cho-



 An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                  107

--- Page 132 ---
rus or performing, the hair flew around and sweat flew off. From
Jalo I learned what it is like to be passionate about something.
When we worked on Béla Bartok pieces at the piano, Jalo first
would   translate the text for me, and then he would      act out the
composer’s intentions as a coach would do for a singer. I believe he
had met Barték. He certainly knew Arnold Schonberg, and a high-
light of one Summer Institute was the presence of Jalo’s friends, the
Kolisch Quartet. They played Schénberg’s music while it was pro-
jected on a large screen. I turned pages from a place in the middle
of the players, surrounded by the sound, sensation, and visuality of
the performance. My knees dug into the wooden floor of the dormi-
tory. The feel of the still summer air in that intimate enclosed space
is easy to recall.
     Edward Lowinsky was my other important teacher, because he
started me on a long career in the field of early music. He intro-
duced us to the concept, sound and style of Medieval and Renais-
sance music that gave to me a basis for performing and teaching for
nearly thirty years. Lowinsky was stern and such a taskmaster it
seems ironic that through him I came to love early music. But it did
happen there. During this time we sang early religious music and
secular motets, which were very beautiful and memorable. The
scale of this music suited the size of our musical resources then.
     It was the choral singing that most people enjoyed and that
brought students and faculty together. Two people could be on
opposite sides of the fence during the day, and then singing from
the same copy of “Wachet auf” that night. Also, informal evenings
might be centered on the jazz and improvised playing of several
students with those talents. This was quite popular and a needed
respite.
     During the ’40s much of what we learned in music classes was
similar to traditional music departments elsewhere. For instance,
there was a preponderance of German music: Bach, Beethoven,
Brahms, Schubert, Mahler, and Strauss, and I recall my history text
by Willi Apel, and the harmony book by Hindemith, each with
their German viewpoints and interpretations. Though it did not
occur to me for a long while, I reflect today that this was music of
Europe at a high point of its patriarchal culture. I was studying,
playing, hearing, and analyzing (mainly from records) music com-



108                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 133 ---
posed only by males, conducted and performed or recorded pri-
marily by male musicians, for male hands and sensibilities. Need-
less to say I had a reaction to this heavy dose of the Romantics.
Loud, long, and large musical forms seemed strangely out of place
at BMC, and for years afterwards I rarely listened to symphonic lit-
erature from the nineteenth century, and I didn’t miss it!
     Of course we had other kinds of music, and occasionally an
American composer/teacher would spend a term with us. But for a
college that was “an experiment in education,” wouldn’t one ex-
pect something more daring and different? John Cage’s appearance
that summer of 1948 was timely. It shook up assumptions about
music as we knew it. It questioned the dominance of European cul-
ture on our modern world. It provided an experimental attitude
towards music that summer,      and thereafter, that I had been miss-
ing. It had a liberating effect.
     I am grateful that musical options are greater now and that
more and more creative, gifted, and persevering women are emerg-
ing as performers, composers, teacher, historians, and conductors
of music. Today we have wonderful access to many styles of con-
temporary music, including numerous Americans, as well as the
world’s rich ethnic music. All of this I have been happily discover-
ing since BMC days.
     Black Mountain’s unique environment, its concept of small, in-
teracting classes, its belief in, and encouragement of, student initia-
tive and imagination, and its opportunities for learning and friend-
ships that come from community life—these are the gifts I most
treasure. It has had a lasting effect on me, and has made me a stu-
dent for life.

--- Page 134 ---
Eric R. Bentley
                                                   (Faculty 1942-44)


                           Report from the Academy:
                            The Experimental College



I once heard T. S. Eliot say that young writers should avoid aca-
demic life and earn their living in some completely unliterary con-
cern such as an insurance company. Wallace Stevens is one of the
few who have acted on this advice; most of the reputable poets in
America today, and a fair proportion of other literary men, are col-
lege professors. When we realize what an extraordinary number of
PR [Partisan Review] readers and contributors are attached to an
academy we are bound, since PR is the least “academic” of the liter-
ary periodicals, to recognize that there never was so intimate a tie
between living culture (such as it is) and our institutions of higher
learning (such as they are). In these conditions it might possibly be
interesting to hear from some of the writers what is going on. That,
as I understand it, is the purpose of the PR “Reports from the Acad-
emy.” Here is my report.
     At some point between high school and entering the teaching
profession the academician is deeply disillusioned about the higher
learning in America. He expected campus life to be highminded, if
rather aloof; he discovers freshman English and campus politics. Of
the possible responses to this situation the simplest and most un-
compromising is a complete rejection of the present system of edu-
cation and participation in an attempt at educational reform. This
was my response. It will be of interest to those who are wondering
what function the experimental college has had, or could have, in
our present state of things. The years following the last war wit-
nessed a great vogue of educational experiment. As this war draws
to a close many will be wondering if we are to have new Bertrand

This article was originally published in Partisan Review, vol. 12, no. 3
(1945).



110                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 135 ---
Russell schools and the like. I offer the case of Black Mountain Col-
lege as an exemplum.
     Black Mountain College was founded in 1933 by John Andrew
Rice, a stormy petrel of education who had unfairly been fired from
Rollins College, Florida, an institution which also considered itself
advanced. In a summer hotel, otherwise unoccupied nine months
in the year, Rice gathered together something under a hundred stu-
dents and a handful of teachers, some of whom had followed him
from Rollins. The story of the first five or six years has been told by
Rice in his autobiography I Came Out of the 18th Century. They cul-
minated in Rice’s being driven out by a number of his colleagues.
At the same time one of the wealthy friends upon whom the college
depended for its existence provided the college with grounds and
some rather rickety buildings a couple of miles from the summer
hotel near Black Mountain, North Carolina. For one school year the
main task both of students and faculty was to build with their own
hands a building to study in. This unusual task was performed—
not indeed to the extent of completing the building but at least to
the extent of providing shelter for all. Primitive shacks dotted
around a dammed-up swamp ironically named Lake Eden served
as student dormitories and faculty houses.
     In this setting, which may be represented as idyllic or barba-
rous, informal or messy, refreshing or inconvenient, Arcadian or
Bohemian, according to the predilections of the critic, BMC worked
out is own scheme of education. It is nothing if not heterodox in
form. There is no board of trustees, no president or other head with
constitutional power to hire and fire, no donor with a controlling
interest in college policies. A constitution places all decisive power
in the hands of the faculty. Business is discussed and transacted by
elected boards and committees on which the student body is repre-
sented. Though the superior numbers of the students do not give
them a controlling voice in college affairs, the fact that they are on
all important governing bodies does mean that every issue, except
the academic progress of the students themselves, is discussed and
passed on by them. Within this scheme of government Black Moun-
tain worked   out a “way of life’ which has much to commend          it,

especially in its omissions. There are no organized sports, no frater-
nities or sororities, none of those institutions or mores which on so




An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                  111

--- Page 136 ---
many campuses serve to prolong adolescence and fortify Iehile
istinism. Black Mountain’s well-known fondness for lame ducks
has its partial justification in the fact that its students are immune
from the philistine pressures which elsewhere make life hell for the
sensitive   and   weak.   Black   Mountain   knows     few   hard-drinking,
hard-whoring, jalopy-driving hearties and even fewer giggling,
over-painted, movie-starrish, jitterbugging girls who spend the
week waiting for Saturday night. The Black Mountain “way of life”
is a way of endless activity scheduled morning, afternoon, and eve-
ning, weekday and week-end. Instead of sports there is farmwork,
maintenance work, building, typing, entertaining. These things go
on every afternoon, and in the evenings a steady stream of lectures,
concerts, and meetings takes place.
      The achievements of an educational program not dictated by
footballers, rotarians, and oil magnates are considerable. Education
is run by educationists. Furthermore the smallness of the experi-
mental college is a tremendous asset. Where, as at Black Mountain,
there is a teacher to every three students the advantage is evident:
intimacy between student and teacher can be the means to the most
concentrated and lively interchange that any education could af-
ford. Where the faculty are a separate world the students continue
their high school habit of avoiding study, boasting of idleness, and
the like; at Black    Mountain,     on the other    hand,    diligence   is de
rigueur. The good taste of the art and music teachers permeates the
community. Walk through the campus any afternoon and you will
hear a good deal of music         from student     rooms,    but no musical
trash. Look into the rooms and you will see a good many pictures,
but no pin-up girls or signed portraits of Clark Gable. At Black
Mountain quite ordinary students acquire a certain taste in the vis-
ual and auditory arts.
     For the place has always had its quota of highly gifted and
zealous teachers. Perhaps Rice himself was the most brilliant; even
his enemies granted that he was an extraordinary and magnetic
personality. He used to shatter his pupils’ illusions, disturb their
complacency and send them on their way greatly changed people.
The college has believed in the peculiar efficacy of music and art,
and the teaching in these fields has always been ardently partisan,
anti-academic, iconoclastic and personal. A Bauhaus man preaches



112                       Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 137 ---
a passionate evangel: art history is the bunk; art criticism is gossip;
the student must learn to use the materials himself, become himself
“related” to forms and colors; the Graeco-Roman tradition is over-
rated, forget history, think of nothing but form and color, and you
will prefer Byzantine, primitive and modern work. The music
teachers have been equally enterprising: you are more likely to hear
Perotinus or Schénberg as you walk round the campus than
Tchaikovsky and Chopin; there is little music “appreciation,” that
unctuous monster; ear-training and musical technique replace and
surpass all that.
     BMC has done many remarkable things. It has, so to speak,
made the blind to see and the deaf to hear. Students there some-
times seem to grow in front of your very eyes. Timid spoilt children
have turned into controlled and disciplined people. Graduating
students have sometimes surprised their examiners—chosen from
the faculty of “regular” universities—not so much by their erudi-
tion as by their keen interest in their field. Some have used the aca-
demic freedom of the place—few compulsory courses, few tests,
few requirements, few rules—to pass far beyond an undergraduate
standard of work. In some ways the faculty have a good time of it
too. You teach whatever you wish, and you have in your classes
only students who chose your course and who desperately want to
know what you have to say (and who may even try to have you
fired if you’re a bore). Black Mountain has gone far beyond what
are considered the functions of a seat of learning. It has created a
pattern of living—”community” is the magic word—in which star-
tling things happen. In a day of mass organization, a day of gigan-
tism and machines, it shows that one may try to approach the indi-
vidual as an individual, a thing more often talked of than done. At
a time when one is tempted to give up the fine dreams of the liberal
philosophers of education, Black Mountain affords glimpses of pos-
sibilities.


     In my opinion, however the possibilities will have to be real-
ized elsewhere or not at all, for Black Mountain has defects which
outweigh its merits. Though any brief enumeration of these defects
will by its very brevity distort and oversimplify the truth, I will
avoid the alternative of saying nothing by specifying three schools



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                  Kis

--- Page 138 ---
of thought and practice which have prevented Black Mountain
being the most successful educational experiment in America. They
are: the Progressivists, the Platonists, and the Primitivists.
      According to Time, Black Mountain is “progressive education’s
most famous outpost,” but PR readers should be warned, in case
they are out of contact with the educational world, that Progressiv-
ism is no longer Deweyism; by this time it is about as far from
Dewey as the Pope is from Jesus. In the form in which it cropped
up at Black Mountain Progressivism was a crude body of negative
and rather anarchistic doctrines such as that good education results
from having no rules, no examinations, no punctuality and the like.
Latterly this body of doctrine has been more popular with the stu-
dent body than with the faculty, for the latter, like all tired revolu-
tionaries, have been growing steadily more puritan and disciplinar-
ian. On both student body and faculty, however, there did persist
much suspicion of learning (called “book learning” or “mere learn-
ing”) based upon that old chestnut of a doctrine that Education is
for Life and upon the assumption that dilettantism or boy-scoutish-
ness is somehow more to be identified with Life than “mere knowl-
edge.” When students are discouraged from being studious and
critical (in the interest of the active and the creative) the result is
sometimes a new brand of philistinism, sometimes just the old
brand of intellectual sloth, and always an abdication of one of the
main functions of higher education, namely the training of the in-
tellect to the point where a man can reject what is bogus. Unhap-
pily the “creative” writing, painting, etc., of the normal student, be-
ing itself bogus, or at least limited, is actually dangerous as training
if not constantly accompanied by critical training. And Black
Mountain has always been provincial and out-of-touch in all criti-
cal studies—literary, philosophical and social.
      In education we have at all times to turn attention from the
pretty phrases—Life not Mere Learning, for instance,—to the actu-
alities which they produce or are meant to justify. In practice Life
not Learning means a fingering of the personality which leads to
neurosis and thoughts of suicide. In a way the great pride of Black
Mountain education is the detailed attention which not only the
work but also the daily living of the student gets from the faculty.
Yet this close and expert attention is damaging both to the watchers



114                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 139 ---
and to the watched. The watchers become unhealthily inquisitive
like the prying neighbors in a Scots Presbyterian village; the
watched grow introspective or rebellious.
     But few of the Black Mountaineers like the word Progressive,
and in later years a philosophy made its appearance which was di-
rectly counter to Dewey, to vulgarised Progressivism, and indeed
to all liberal schemes. This was Platonism. The experimental college
was to turn out philosopher-kings and was to endorse not democ-
racy but True Democracy, that is, anything but the majority rule. A
wistful note entered into the discussion. Were not the masses a
menace? An essay was written which referred to the masses as “the
great number of the uncalled” and asked us to trust rather to “our
compass, our fate and our fortune” in seeking a new order. There
was definitely to be a new order, but it would be discovered by the
pure contemplation of the pure and contemplative few. In other
words the shadow of Mortimer J. Adler had fallen across BMC, and
positivism, materialism, science, pragmatism and communism be-
came bogies.
     More powerful that either Progressivism or Platonism was
Primitivism. In the early days students read a book called Flight
from the City and acclaimed the Simple Life. Ever since, a gospel of
manual labor was preached, not in Marx’s sense but in that of Wil-
liam Morris or the later Tolstoi. Physical labor combined with
study was the synthesis which our world needed. Contact with the
craftsman’s materials would sensitize the individual so that he
would gain in the control of himself and his environment. There
was much talk of Organic Growth, but the higher type of man
which the synthesis was to engender did not appear, and the sensi-
tized individual in Black Mountain as elsewhere frequently con-
trolled neither himself nor his environment.
     One of the tenets of educational Primitivism is that modern
specialization is wicked, and that, instead of having a man for each
job, each man should do a bit of everything: furniture making,
farming, mining, tree-felling, entertainment,     as well as teaching,
advising, lecturing and endless conferring in meetings. Especially
should he take over the functions of the administration which
otherwise will be a non-educational body controlling education.
The first result of this line of thought and action is sheer disorder in



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                   115

--- Page 140 ---
the whole organization and administration of the college. The
second is arbitrary power: for those who choose to devote them-
selves to administration become the official administrators and
thus, in a college with no president and no trustees, they become
the bosses too. If the same people also secure the donations which
keep the college going their power is permanent.
     Bureaucratic chaos and highly centralized control: the elabo-
rate forms of an extreme democracy and the reality of a simple au-
tocracy: the formula is familiar. The Black Mountain system, simple
to the point of naiveté in theory, is cumbersome to the point of im-
possibility in practice. Since there are so few set procedures, let
alone rules, there has to be a discussion to decide upon procedure
and principle before anything can be done. It is as if a parliament
should have to open every session by discussing and finally fram-
ing a new constitution. Sometimes group A tells group B that it
does not agree with the fundamental principles of the college
though there is no record of those principles, and members of
group A cannot agree as to what they are. Since in addition Pro-
gressivists, Platonists and Primitivists all agree that voting is a bar-
barous custom and that unanimity should be attempted, deadlock
is inevitably frequent.
     So much for the different schools of thought. All schools seem
to have in common two important things: an exaggerated opinion
of what the college might be and an inability to make the college
actually work in any even half-satisfactory fashion. The exaggera-
tion of the role of education is something all our modern reformers
have in common. Dewey says: “Education is the fundamental
method of social progress and reform.” Hutchins preaches a “spiri-
tual revolution” to which “the only way .. . is through education.”
But the level of Black Mountain’s educational panaceism is
exemplified in one of its champions, Mr. Louis Adamic, who once
(Harpers, April 1936) described the portentous process by which a
Black Mountain student, passing through the dark night of despair
and isolation of his few months there, is spiritually reunited with
the group. One of the happy results which Mr. Adamic reported
was this: “Some of those who had been there longest can also ex-
change complicated communications without saying a word. The
lift of an eyebrow to them is a sentence.”



116                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 141 ---
So ridiculously ambitious and Utopian in intention, the experi-
mental college is limited and inharmonious in its results. Personal
antipathies occur, of course, wherever there are men; group differ-
ences emerge wherever there are aggregations of men; and no cam-
pus can hope to be free of universally human troubles. The peculiar
difficulty of the experimental college is that small numbers and
community living make of every personal irritation a communal
fever, a fever which is caught and carried by the students as well as
by the faculty. If the students have a voice in all affairs, and if fac-
ulty and students live cooped up in a valley miles from anywhere,
people are going to get on each others nerves, the more so if they
consist of nervous, “progressive” adolescents and highly individu-
alized intellectuals, each with a very definite set of his own theories
which he wishes to try out. Black Mountain never works smoothly
for long at a time. Somebody is always leaving in a huff or being
pushed out. Occasionally there is a splash-up crisis. Other activities
are suspended or at least ruined and the community devotes itself
to weeks of uninterrupted feuding.
     The atmosphere of such a college makes the students grow up
in some ways, for they are drawn at once into educational contro-
versy, but those who want an education suffer. At the best of times
the Black Mountain    faculty is small, and many fields of study are
not represented on it. Inadequate pay (your keep plus $5 to $25 per
month per person) and constant trouble cause such a rapid turn-
over of teachers that a student is never sure how long he can study
any subject. History breaks off at 1669, as the ex-professor runs for
the train. A major in French has to finish up at Middlebury when
the only French teacher is hurriedly removed. So it goes. A course
here and a course there are brilliant, but nothing can be planned
ahead of time. In fact some of the Primitivists say that poverty and
an atmosphere of crisis are the making of the place. They are cer-
tainly of its essence. In eleven years less than thirty students have
graduated.
     How shall one sum up such an undertaking? Up to now the
shrewdest judge, though PR readers may be surprised, is Bernard
De Voto, who ina reply to Adamic wrote in Harpers: “It sounds like
Charles Fourier to me. ... Mr. Adamic expects Black Mountain to
multiply; but its predecessors multiplied by fission, by division,



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                   HZ

--- Page 142 ---
and that is the history of experimental societies and colleges in
America. Black Mountain itself came about by secession. . . .” The
latest I hear is that BMC has only about forty students and that a
group of ex-Black Mountaineers in New York is thinking of making
another try of it elsewhere.
     I sincerely wish them luck. But they should ponder the ques-
tion whether the defects of Black Mountain and other experimental
colleges are all accidental and avoidable or inherent and inevitable.
Black Mountain    has had many accidental failings. But I do not
think the conflict of personalities, for example, can be numbered
among them. One need not attack particular persons. The root er-
ror of all builders of special rural communities for the improve-
ment of mankind is the same: they overestimate the extent to which
we can in these ways escape and surpass our world. The economic
problem, for instance, is not evaded    at Black Mountain.    Policy is
still economically determined, only by the money-getters, not by
the money-givers. An educational system is part of a social system.
As a determinant it is weak; it is itself largely determined. The writ-
ers who are now located in the “regular” colleges and who may
have wondered if they should not join the educational avant-garde
of the experimental college can, in my opinion, stay where they are
with a good conscience.




                                              Lorrie Goulet
                                                  (Student   1943-44)     »


        Memories of Black Mountain College



One of the first things people ask me when I tell them that I was a
student at BMC is “How did you happen to go there?” Back in 1943
not many people had heard about the College, and it was quite by
accident that I learned about it myself. I was reading Louis




118                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 143 ---
Adamic’s My America and was so taken by his description of BMC
that I wrote to the College for a brochure. When I received the bro-
chure I decided immediately that I wanted to go there.
     I started at Black Mountain as a major in sociology with Dr.
Miller. After a few months I decided to major in art under Josef
Albers. I had had previous training in ceramics since the age of
eight and wanted to study sculpture. As there were no sculpture
classes I went into the drawing and painting classes. I will never
forget Albers’ wonderful instruction and his constant chiding,
“You have to learn to see.” He taught me to draw the figure after
many exercises of drawing tea cups and chairs. He taught me to see
shape and form and how to be sensitive to color. I once asked him
when was I going to learn to paint abstracts such as he did. He told
me not for a long time—only after I had learned the fundamentals.
     I remember working with Anni Albers. Weaving was a very
complex study, but somehow she managed to make the experience
enjoyable and understandable. The many hours spent at the loom
were in some ways like meditation.
     I will never forget the aura of music that surrounded the
school. Most of the day and night there was the sound of the stu-
dents practicing in their cubicles. The concerts were highlights of
the week. I learned to love Bach at BMC and the joy of living with
music every day. Music is my only companion when I carve. Dr.
and Mrs. Hansgirg used to invite some of us to their place on Sun-
days for coffee and music. How well I remember the coffee heaped
with whipped cream, and the beautiful records they played.
     My experiences with the other members of the faculty were
positive for the most part. They were always accessible and quite
helpful in general. The only part I found disturbing was being
caught up in the internal wranglings between them that was going
on during my year there. However, my enthusiasm for work and
study was never dampened because of the turmoil—and in spite of
it, I had very good academic associations with members of both
sides of the conflict.
     The summer session of 1944 was the most exciting time of my
stay at BMC. The array of distinguished members of the art world
invited to teach there was incredible. The whole summer was one
of total immersion in, and celebration of, art. Albers had put me to

--- Page 144 ---

                                                       WYSII   Bue}YIM   ‘sjUapnys

--- Page 145 ---
work translating an abstract relief in paper into a clay sculpture
 that when completed was to be placed in the student building. I
 especially stayed for the summer session to study with José de
 Creeft, the sculpture instructor. When     de Creeft saw what I was
 doing, he rejected the whole idea of the project and insisted I study
 carving and work in a material of real worth. He felt I was being
 “used” by the school in doing something that someone else had
 made instead of an original work of my own. The panel was never
 completed. I took his carving class and five months later married
 my sculpture teacher. In the thirty-eight years of marriage that fol-
 lowed, I rarely worked in clay but did produce fifteen one-woman
 shows of carvings in wood and stone.
       I received my first teaching position at the Peoples Art Center
  in MOMA in 1957. Victor D’Amico, the director then of the Art
  Center had been a participant in the Summer Session of 1944 and
  had remembered me when my name was suggested to him. My
  teaching career began then and continues today. Teaching will al-
  ways be one of the joys of my life. I, too, chide my students to learn
  to see.
       In retrospect, I see how my experiences at BMC shaped my life.
  My marriage, my professional career in sculpture, my years of
  teaching all stemmed directly or indirectly from having been a stu-
_ dent there. My life of the past forty-four years has been very crea-
  tive and productive. I can truly say I did what I set out to do. Al-
  though I have had some very difficult times during those years, I
  can’t say that I would change much of what happened. Everything
  was and still is a learning experience. In the spirit of adventure, I
 accept life and continue to work at what I love to do, in spite of the
 problems and obstacles that invariably arise.
      Being a student at BMC was a wonderful experience for me. I
 can’t imagine what would have happened if I hadn’t read Louis
 Adamic’s My America. Perhaps he is the one I should thank for set-
 ting me in the right direction at the right time.
