                                                          (Student   1943-46)


                                     Black Mountain Recalled



Spring 1944
A palpable silence hung over Black Dwarf, where Ted Dreier had
closed the door behind him. There had been many strained faculty
meetings. The issue was not an easy one. The southern com-
munity’s reaction      was     a matter   of deep concern,      as well as the
standing     and   welfare    of the entire    College.   Nevertheless,   with
Dreier’s consistent urging, the faculty that spring had, at last, com-
mitted themselves to the policy of accepting black students. Dreier
had gone far in his work with the Rosenwald Foundation to bring
about its realization. It was at this juncture that another tremor
went through the more conservative-minded. “We must not go too
fast,” it was said. “We’re not yet ready.” And suddenly the entire
thrust was     halted, and the direction reversed.         Threats were   felt.
Possibly fires being set on the College grounds? Dreier was in-
structed to break off all negotiations with the foundation. This also
broke a sense of trust in the commitment of the faculty to a new
goal. Dreier was left standing alone. So also was the issue of black
student admissions,          which    would   not be resolved    affirmatively
until the fall of 1945.


Spring 1945
Molly’s study is elegant and empty and white. Tatami mats on the
floor, a dark green corduroy couch with white and grey pillows at
one end, a golden oak table and stools at the other. Parties: the ex-
cuse for bringing rhododendron branches and vegetable decor
from the farm, and for spending a day in the woodworking shop
making impromptu table settings: grapefruit plates, shallow circles
cut on the lathe into six-inch mahogany squares: a set of delicate
three-pronged forks, and little butter knives from the wood of an
old walnut tree—lathe-shaped, sanded, and oiled.



122                       Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 147 ---
Alfred Kazin sits by himself on the top steps to the Round
House. It’s after lunch, a sunny peaceful interlude of emptiness be-
fore classes or the work program begins. He is playing the Act II
Finale of Mozart's Nozze di Figaro from the record collection, and
the sound of mingling voices drifts softly over the still lake. He
does not notice me, who has slipped into the Round House from
the backside, as I have each of these past days. Each day he plays
the finale, with eyes closed, his face tipped up to the spring sun, his
concentration complete. At first noncomprehending, then amazed
by this single-minded attention, and curious, I begin to listen too.
Slowly the sextet becomes understandable to me, becomes beauti-
ful, becomes     the first piece of music   that I’ve ever truly heard.
Kazin leaves quietly, unaware;     the Round   House falls silent. I am
left with the discovery of Mozart.


July 1943
Albers stands on the dirt road in crumpled white pants and white
shirt with rolled-up sleeves, a rake in hand. His is the road crew.
Four girls are grading a section from the dining hall to the front
gate. As though polishing a marble bust, Juppi’s hands smooth the
air as he explains the curve needed for good drainage. We hoe and
rake. Powdery white dust fills our nostrils. The heat is monstrous.
From the Round House comes the sound of piano arpeggios, softly
gliding up and down, stopping and starting again. One of the mu-
sic students, probably Patsy Lynch, is practicing in the shade, be-
hind the glass doors. Juppi’s voice continues, steady, low-pitched,
almost conspiratorial. Out of a collage of random English words,
German      sentence structure, verbs bent into nouns, images emerge
as vivid and arresting as Albers’ colors. There is nothing that is not
commented on, questioned. The angle the rake is held, the mixture
of stone and dirt, the slope towards the embankment. Had I ever
seen a dirt road before? When we are finished, the piece of road is
ours.
     Albers painted, and stored his paintings in the white basement
room of Black Dwarf. From time to time we were allowed to visit
and could look at some of the work in progress. Little scraps of
blotting paper, with two or three pallet-knife strokes of color on
them, dappled the walls and big central worktable like vivid flow-


An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                   M23

--- Page 148 ---
ers. In spite of our gentle but continuous begging, not even the lit-
tlest of these magic scraps was ever given away. They were the pre-
prestudies for the large oils, which Albers would pull out from the
racks for viewing, and which glowed with coal-like intensity under
the neon lights. Side by side we sat in silence looking at them, Al-
bers’ knees, in their white cotton trousers, softly and rhythmically
flapping. “Why do you paint at night, Juppi? I asked. Don’t the col-
ors change under artificial light?” “That’s the way they will be
seen,” he replied. “That’s the intention.”
    Upstairs, in Black Dwarf, in the small white living room with
the brown leather sling chairs, Juppi was sharing a German treat:
drawn    goose fat, which   he spread with precise little strokes—as
though with a palette-knife—onto slices of pumpernickel before
handing them out.


Fall 1943
Eight cows had disappeared from the upper pasture. It was a ques-
tion of searching for them in the woods of the mountain above the
farm buildings. Ted Dreier asked if I would like to help look. At
6:00 A.M. we were climbing steeply up through thickets of mountain
laurel and underbrush, through the cool, misty mountain air. Ted’s
pace was almost nonstop until we arrived at the upper pasture
which, close to the ridge, was radiant in the early sun. Far down
below us Lake Eden and the valley were lost in a river of white fog.
In the next hours, as we traversed the ridge and surrounding
woods, the fog gently lifted until, at last, we could see every detail
of the lower hills and fields. Only the cows continued to be invis-
ible. Ted never paused. Three times we climbed down through
wooded    gullies and ravines, and three times climbed back to the
top of the mountain. By noon I was so exhausted that I thought my
lungs would burst. Flattened out at the end of the pasture, I
watched Ted sprint away*over the rocks to the east, as though
bouncing on rubber balls. By midafternoon he had located all the
missing cows, and we had herded them back to the pasture. Fol-
lowing the stepping stones of a stream bed from the top down, we
descended at a full run, Ted flying ahead of me like water ricochet-
ing off rocks. There was in that speed a knowledge and a delight
which I never forgot, on many later paths, up and down hill.


124                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 149 ---
Féte 1943
  Wild activity like an anthill. For days every department, faculty
  and student member has been in a fever of preparation. There are
  long ladders in the dining hall where the decoration committee is
  struggling with paper and glue, while Albers, clicking his tongue,
  pads rapidly back and forth giving directions. At the stage end of
  the dining hall the English Department, muttering and giggling, is
  rehearsing some skits. “The Pharisee and the Publican,” one of
  Heinrich Schutz’s sacred pieces recently conducted by Jalowetz, is
  reconstructed as in a musical revue. The subject is BMC life. “God
  pity me a sinner” has turned to “Pity me at dinner,” reflecting an
  anguish over kitchen offerings. Pink-faced Eric Bentley, spectacles
  glinting, sings with choirboy expression to Professor Straus’s
  basso-Pharisee. Fritz Cohen has added some new composition and
  satirical libretto that touches on the most vulnerable spots in the
  community. Molly, deadpan, is doing her Martha Graham solo:
  “The Death of the Fly.” Elsa Cohen and her dancers are practicing a
  spirited cancan down the length of the dining room. Single-
  handed, Liese Kulka is singing the count’s and Suzanna’s duet
  from the Marriage of Figaro, alternating her reliable tenor and so-
  prano range. She stands facing backwards, her black-sleeved arms
  wrapped around to hug her waist in front. We see Suzanna wiggle
- in the Count’s embrace. Sam Brown is playing boogie-woogie on a
  faraway piano. Young Swackhammer is on the drums.
       In the dining hall, tables and chairs are being moved to make a
  big horseshoe. There will be dancing between courses in the center
  section before the silver curtains are drawn for the performances. A
  candle-lit great meal, befitting the occasion, awaits us: turkey, as-
  sorted side dishes, even asparagus. . . In the girls’ dormitories the
  begging and borrowing of belts and necklaces has been hectic.
  Long skirts, lace blouses are being ironed and pinned. We will all
  assemble, metamorphosed from working jeans to black velvet ele-
  gance, by the fire in North Lodge, for wine and appetizers. One of
 the great BMC parties is about to begin.


 The Split—1944
 “The tenor of this past year has been one of increasing dissonance.”
 Those words described the early summer of terrifying tension and


 An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                 125

--- Page 150 ---
division at BMC. For me it was a time of personal accountability.
Never before had it occurred to me that how I thought about some-
thing mattered, or that I had responsibility for my thoughts, or for
the positions I took. Now I saw faculty members taking stands on
opposing principles, and saw the consequences of their decisions.
They were grave enough to sever ties of friendship, and in some
cases to sever connection with the College. In the cool silence of the
Studies Building basement I sat at a loom throwing the shuttle back
and forth, and tried to find answers for myself. What did I believe
in? What made sense to me? What could I live with? From across
the lake came the faint sounds of music in the dining hall. The
Kolisch Quartet was practicing a Schonberg quartet. The Summer
Music and Art Institutes were in full swing. The classrooms and
halls alive with visiting faculty and summer students. They were
rushing from class to concert to class, untroubled by anything but
the pursuit of art. For us who had_ been involved in the
community’s deep struggles, an education of a different sort had
been in progress. We had felt a terra trema and had each to find firm
ground under our feet. Perhaps BMC’s aim “to educate the whole
person” was being brought home to us.
     What did it all mean to me? Nothing I can find the right words
for. It was too strong, too manifold in richness, too intense and too
magic a time. I know that every moment there seemed alive in a
way that few have since. This had to do with being asked to be fully
awake, to be at a new threshold of perception, whether in class, in
the work program, in our own work, or in the life of the commu-
nity. It seemed to me to be a time of hazard, at the very edge of the
known and the unknown—a time of risk-taking for each of us—
that involved our whole being. And I thought that we were given a
kind of respect that gave meaning and consequence to all we did. It
let us perceive how much we, each of us, had meaning in the proc-
ess of life of the community. That was our education.




126                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 151 ---
(Name withheld)

                                   A Time Remembered



It was the highlight of their summer. Neither thought of the finality
of their relationship, then, nor cared. For it was a fine day and they
were young or thought they were. Without really planning but
with a little forethought, they would seize a complete moment. In
the sun together. Away and alone to each other. In seeming non-
chalance they would go. It was not wise to flaunt behavior that
could be construed as immoral, antisocial, or food for gossip. The
constraints were self-imposed, largely, as a practical matter even as
there was always a warm free ambience to the summers at Black
Mountain, especially the Art Institute of 1946, with the entourage
of shakers, movers, and followers—all artists to the core. It was a
good time to be there and alive.
    With food and water they set out for the ridge or upper pasture
owned by the College, soon after breakfast, on a weekday. The late
summer sun was warm but not hot, just perfect for their day to-
gether. She’d said she was innocent before she met him, not that it
mattered to him. But he was patient and kind and inspired her in
the ways of love, and, in turn she flattered his manhood.
     They hiked quietly together side by side on the path when pos-
sible, or singly, she following on her long youthful legs. From time
to time, they stopped, kissed, held each other tightly. There was
much anticipation, a promise of carefree fulfillment. She was full of
wonder pointing out wildflowers, birds, red lichen on rocks, trees,
sharing her discoveries, asking him to identify and delight in their
presence. He would take notice, smile. It was like that from the be-
ginning—they were each separate even when together and close.
That was the understanding, the independence of their relation-
ship.
     The opening to the grassed pasture was empty of any life, and
quiet. He knew the perfect place—to the far right corner of the
ridge among the outcropping of rocks. It was a steep climb, but, re-




An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                 127

--- Page 152 ---
freshed with the cool spring water splashed on their faces, they
were there in no time. Spreading his army blanket over the green
grass, he lay down with her. There were only a few clouds, the sky
deep blue. They could see the Swannanoa valley far away and com-
mand the more immediate approach to their boulder hideout. As a
caution, they kept down their voices, kept a periodical lookout for
strangers.
     They made love and ate their lunches. Then they took off their
clothes to feel the warm soothing afternoon sun on their bodies.
They talked of many things, not important things; they were in har-
mony. They were much in each other’s embrace, in complete physi-
cal fulfillment.
     When it was time to go down from the ridge, it was with no
regret. For it was understood as a summer fling, like this day, to be
completely enjoyed for itself, without complications. They laughed
and smiled and seemingly floated down to earth, happy with a
most perfect day.




                              Janet Heling Roberts
                                       (Student 1943-45, 1946-47)


                  Over All These Forty-five Years!



I remember working in the mica mine on the College property with
the two mountain men miners, who often disappeared with their
harmonicas and jug into the depths of the tunnels (out of sight, but
not out of hearing). We were doing this for the war effort. I think
the mica was used for gun sights and other weaponry, so it was
patriotic to put up with standing mid-calf in ice-cold water at the
bottom of the pit, filling buckets with debris which was hauled up
to the ground level by some sturdy soul on top, winding it up by a
rope on a winch. Wheelbarrows were filled with this weighty stuff,




128                  Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 153 ---
and the trick was to move the wheelbarrow, then to maneuver this
  heavy load over the loose board track to the dumping ground.
  More often than not, the wheelbarrow never made the destination.
  I remember I’d get down in the hole and shovel so my partner
  could negotiate the runway with the load. The mica was found in
  veins, and large hunks were chiseled out. These pieces were taken
  to the book bindery in the Studies Building, where we pared off
  Slices of a certain thickness and then trimmed them up to govern-
  ment specifications. This war effort came to a sudden halt after we
  found the main tunnel collapsed one morning when we reported
  for work. As far as I know, the mine was never reopened.
      The survey of the town of Black Mountain, directed by Dr. Her-
  bert Miller, was a unique educational experience. His sociology-
  class members walked, scrambled over the hills to interview town
  and mountain inhabitants as to their vital statistics, such as income,
  age, number of refrigerators, and children in the family—and pages
  of other information. Our survey was patterned after the famous
  Middletown survey. Although many mountain people resented such
  intrusion, I did get to know and like the mountain people. A friend
  and I even hiked several miles on many a Sunday to sing and play
  the piano at one of the tiny mountain church services. We were also
  welcomed in the black church, which was to us a foreign land at
_ that time.
      The College harbored     some   of the Freedom   Riders, who had
  been hauled off buses in Asheville and beaten because they were
  sitting together at the front of the vehicle. The riders [paired black
  and white] were doing this to test the new federal law prohibiting
  segregation on interstate buses. How admirable that the College
  could be that brave, that caring, that committed. In those years, this
 was a very daring thing to do.
     Alfred Kazin’s course on Moby Dick had to be and is to this day
 one of the finest classes I’ve ever attended. Although rather a stern
 and distant person, he proved to be an instructor par excellence.
 And he expected you to hand in papers, come to class, and even be
  on time. Dave Corkran also held such high expectations.
      The exodus of a large number of the student body happened
  every so often when one of the teachers could not be reconciled
_ with the position adopted by his colleagues, and either he or they



 An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                  129

--- Page 154 ---
could not be placated. The teacher would leave the College, taking
his followers with him. Eric Bentley is one teacher who comes to
mind. At one point during 1943, there were as many faculty mem-
bers as students!
     BMC teachers and friends have greatly enriched both [my hus-
band] Dick’s and my life, and I was lucky to find my mate, compan-
ion, and best friend. Hard to know   when   to stop as there are so
many memories crowding in to be looked at and acknowledged.




                             Elizabeth Jennerahn
      (Student 1943-44; Student Teacher and Resident 1948-51)


                            “Betty Schmitt, how ever
                                     did you get here?”



Yes, the living experience was a part of what the education at BMC
was all about, and the times were rare, unique. The absolutely best
dancing that I remember was waltz nights, those Saturday nights
with Fritz Cohen using his powerful hands to produce the Vien-
nese waltzes that got us swirling in the air in our elegant long
gowns which we made of dyed flour sacks. After a lifetime of expe-
riences, that stands in my memory as the most elegant social event,
with elegant gentlemen like Ted Dreier and Alex Reed wearing
jeans and cashmere sweaters and Harris tweed jackets. That was
long before such things were worn in the rest of the world, and it
just made my eyes pop.
     When I used to work on the publicity I was so often struck by
those who should have been our boosters, those who had found
themselves in one way or another and then left, turned out to bad-
mouth the place once they were gone. I used to think that it was
like adolescents leaving home and speaking against their parents.
At the time I never thought that I would spend so many decades of


130                  Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 155 ---
my life sort of erasing BMC from my memory. I think that perhaps
 it was the ideology, the heady conviction of most, that we really did
 have the key, the understanding     of how to make a better world,
 whether through the arts or whatever it was we did. When I first
 set foot on the place, in front of the dining hall, I do remember
 thinking, “I have reached it, utopia.” I had read books on utopian
 American communities and felt that here it finally was, the whole
 ball of wax. And it was a heady thing to feel that you were truly
 doing something significant for the world, for culture, for society,
 for the future—when you were making butter or whatever. The
 balance and roundedness and lack of artifice of daily life and
 schoolish life made for a fullness, day by day, and so the ideology
 just seemed inevitably true; one took it in as with mother’s milk.
      In the year when I was first a student at BMC, the summer art-
 ists and musicians used to come with their wives, mistresses, and
 families. I remember one quite sophisticated blond woman, who I
 think came with a member of the Kolisch Quartet. It was clear that
 she had never been to such a rural place in her life, and each day, I,
 the innocent from Milwaukee, watched as one more layer of ma-
 keup, stockings, shoes, hairdo, scarves, and such was removed un-
  til what was left was a kind of coming-apart-at-the-seams floozie.
  Those visiting New Yorkers used to hound the Reading Room at
- the top of the Studies Building. They were always looking for the
  newest copy of the New York Times. Some of us couldn’t figure out
  why a newspaper from far away could have such an attraction. We
  felt that the really interesting things were happening right HERE,
  and that they were acting very strangely. Now that Pete and I have
  moved to Arizona, we go to quite an effort to have that same paper
 mailed to us.
      The dining hall was such a comfortable place to me. Each day I
 swept the whole place before my dance classes. Some people
 thought it kind of odd that I was always so attentive when glasses
 fell and broke at mealtimes. I always tried to see that every sliver
 was swept up, as I didn’t want any of our bare feet to find these
 glass pieces later. I remember Paul Williams once saying at dinner
 that I simply must sit down, as he couldn't digest his meal, with me
 constantly going from table to table transacting my business, for
_ rehearsals or whatever.




 An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                    131

--- Page 156 ---
I remember especially rehearsing “The Kingfishers” with Char-
les Olson.   We   were   near   the kitchen; Olson   was   reading; I was
dancing; Pete was playing; and a sharp light was on me, and the
whole rest of the dining hall was dark. And there were a lot of
LIGHT SOUND MOVEMENT rehearsals with Nick Cernovich and
Tim LaFarge, and moving in our green- and red-striped tights
through red and green saturated light created by the red- and
green-striped slides. When we were getting ready for the Spring ‘87
Bard reunion and show, and Linda Weintraub, the director, wanted
Pete and me to work with the students doing some of the old LSM
stuff (we didn’t respond to that idea), she said that, of course, they
didn’t have the equipment or budget to do anything so elaborate as
the Red and Green thing. There was no way I could explain to her
that our “equipment” was just a few borrowed primitive projectors
and a bit of cellophane. Pete used to take the tiny red bands from
cigarette cellophane wrappers.
     I remember exhibits of Albers in that dining hall, especially his
stained glass windows. As I had grown up ina stained glass studio,
I was especially taken that he could have made such beauty out of
broken bottles and old screening.
     I have a special memory of the breakfasts, coming back to that
familiar place after a night away, with Mallory serving out huge
piles of pancakes.
     And of course, the evening performances offered what still
seems to me an ideal cultural life—tables pushed to the walls, and
chairs now placed for an audience. Beautifully designed and
printed programs, often made by Pete, for just about every event.
Were there as many as fifty people in an audience? String quartets,
plays, dance performances by visitors like Merce Cunningham,
Katy Litz, and Vashi, and by me and my students; lectures by
people like Bucky Fuller, and just on and on—all part of our food.
     The fact that I finally had the nerve to ask Mrs. Jalo to give me
voice lessons was an indication of the support I felt there to do
something that I had always been told I was terrible at. The nuns at
St. Jude’s School would shut me up after just one peep in tryouts
for any performance. After I left BMC, and years later, I joined the
Waldorf Choral Society, a fine group, and I have now sung much of
the world’s great choral music. Many times in thrilling perform-



132                      Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 157 ---
ances in the Garden City Cathedral,       I would   for a moment   catch
myself and think, “Betty Schmitt, how ever did you get here?” And
I certainly wouldn’t have gotten there without Mrs. Jalo.
    Life is really something, isn’t it?




                                   Yella Pess| Sobotka
                                                     (Faculty 1944 SS)


                 A Summer of Harpsichord Music



I had my harpsichord shipped to North Carolina with the help of
Mr. Theodore Dreier, who knew me from previous performances,
and as a teacher of Nathalie White Coleman, then a student at Black
Mountain College.
     I played for the students and faculty chiefly solo Couperin, but
also solo Bach, and played under Dr. Jalowetz, the conductor at the
school, in some concerti. Dr. Jalowetz had an authority of conduct-
ing which could live up to the one of Arturo Toscanini, under
whose leadership I had performed numerous times for the NBC
Symphony of the Air. When I was not at the school, Dr. Jalowetz
conducted madrigals after dinner.
    Jean Charlot, who as one of the faculty of artists and listeners
when I was active at BMC, did a small portrait of myself, which I
still own.
      I also remember Mrs. Bobbie Dreier, who proved to be a friend
and inspiration to many of the students and faculty.




An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                   133

--- Page 158 ---
Roberta Blair
                                                   (Student 1944 SS)


                                    The First Day at BMC


I was privileged to attend the first Summer Art Institute at Black
Mountain in 1944. There is no way to describe the magic, electric
atmosphere of the place, and I’m sure there has been nothing like it
before or since. I arrived at the station early on a June morning,
tired and dirty in my new clothes after a night on the Southern Rail-
way. Civilian travel was discouraged then, but, with gas rationing,
there was no other way to go. When I changed trains in Washing-
ton all the seats were taken and the aisles filled, and I had to stand
on the platform between coaches in my new high-heeled shoes all
the way through Virginia. Soot rained down from the black smoke
of the coal-fired engine as we clanked and fumed through the
night. A little after midnight I found a seat in the grimy roach-in-
fested coach. There was a two-hour wait in a dingy station in Salis-
bury for the Asheville train, which chugged slowly up to Black
Mountain through the morning mist.
     I was to be picked up at the station, but no one was there ex-
cept the Albers, who had come for a package of materials. Luckily a
woman I had met on the train knew Anni Albers and introduced
me, and they loaded us and our suitcases into their old car and
drove us up the bumpy road to the College. They let us.out at the
dining hall and took off up the road with our suitcases still in the
trunk. Having skipped dinner the night before, I had just finished a
big breakfast on the train and wasn’t interested in food just then.
What I wanted most urgently was a hot shower, a few hours of
sleep, and a change of clothes.
     Not many summer students had arrived, and I felt a little out
of place among the casually dressed college students who were
staying for the summer session. That was the first year of the “natu-
ral look,” and long hair with bangs, shirts, jeans, and sandals were
standard dress. One of the students walked me over to the Studies
Building and helped me find the room I was to use as a studio dur-



134                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 159 ---
ing the day. It contained two desks and a daybed with springs, but
 no mattress and no chairs. There was a shower room at the end of
 the hall, and I found a student who brought me a towel. The
 shower was ice cold, but at least it woke me up. The rest of that
 long day is hazy in memory, but I did recover my bags at some
 point. After dinner all the bewildered newcomers were packed into
 a dilapidated station wagon for the bumpy eight-mile ride to Lee
 Hall, half way up the next mountain. The door on the driver’s side
 was     missing,   and   there didn’t   seem   to be any springs, but the
 young girl who drove us was full of confidence and good spirits,
 and nobody complained. Our sleeping quarters were to be behind
 Lee Hall, an imposing building with spacious porch and columns
 in what we usually think of as Southern Colonial style. From the
 porch there [was] a magnificent view of the opposite mountain
 range, always changing under the moving clouds and mist. This
 was where BMC had started. It was dark by the time we arrived.
 We stumbled up the rocky hill with our suitcases, and without
 flashlights [we] found our assigned cabins. A bare electric bulb
 lighted my dingy room and the bank of wet red clay right outside
 the open window. I felt the mattress. It was wet from the mists that
 drifted through the open cabin all day. I picked up my suitcases
 and made my way up to the next cabin. There I found an unoccu-
 pied room on the front, away from the clay bank, and settled in for
 the night. My unauthorized move must have started a mix-up in
 the room assignments, and the following week I was given the job
 of straightening it out—a good experience in the democratic proc-
 ess.
     Classes started the day after I arrived, and all physical incon-
 veniences began to fade in importance. Every day was filled with
 new experiences and thought-provoking ideas. Most of us were
 used to the safe atmosphere of conservative academic institutions
 where we had learned how to give the right answers. We were not
used to asking questions, and the constant challenge to think for
 ourselves was a new and not very pleasant experience.
        A dazzling succession of celebrities in art, music, and dance
 came to teach or lecture that summer. The art faculty included Jean
 Charlot, José de Creeft, James Prestini, and Josef and Anni Albers.
 Walter Gropius and Amédée Ozenfant were among the lecturers.



~ An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                   135

--- Page 160 ---
The musicians were equally famous, and very much in evidence all
summer. We had a concert at least once a week and heard the musi-
cians practicing in between. Jalowetz, the much-loved music direc-
tor, led a choral group that practiced in the dining room all sum-
mer for presentation of a Schonberg composition at the end of the
session. The sound, coming across the lake to the Studies Building,
was very beautiful.
    My first day at BMC was a suitable introduction to the two
months of mental challenge to follow. I believe the physical incon-
veniences, always in evidence at the school, may have helped
sharpen the mental processes. At least we were certainly wide
awake all summer.




                                             John J. Reiss
                                                (Student   1944-46)


                             Behind the Silver Curtain



One tended to forget about being shy with celebrities. I walked
Agnes de Mille—clad in a yellow straw hat, red poppies, and blue
cornflowers—down to the dining hall. She also wore a dress! She
also wore the hat. And I did not intentionally snub Eleanor Roose-
velt. I was just sunning on the Studies Building apron, reading
Proust in English. When she asked directions to Bob Wunsch’s
study I told her the route. That night they told me who she was.
      The Harris book did not, I think, mention the inviolability of
the DO NOT DISTURB sign. Not even faculty could cross it!
     If Albers liked something, it was “Wunderbar!” or “Donner-
wetter!” There was also Molly Gregory’s expression for something
elegant as “katish,” when, for instance, Jane Slater wore a Mexican
pink and orange jacket with white skirt.
    There was Lily B. Converse, “grand dame,” who carried a pink
and black parasol, a small camp stool with an inflatable cushion,


136                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 161 ---
and spoke to Jean Charlot, the art teacher, only in French, so most
 of the other students would not understand.
      Bob Motherwell’s classes featured “automatic drawing”: drop-
 ping a piece of string on a surface and drawing it. He and Mary
 Callery both felt at the time that the grand tour to Europe was most
 desirable,   and   that   if one   became   commercial   (as with   Alvin
 Lustig’s design class) one would lose one’s soul! I chose to become
 a graphic designer.
     When I think of the mist rising from Lake Eden in the early
 morning, I wonder if anyone recalls the cinnamon-brown newts
 with vermilion spots which came out on the road to the Studies
 Building after the rain?
       One frosty morning there was the tinkling, shattering sound of
 glass over Lake Eden. Layton Coalscott Noel had become BMC’s
 first “Polar Bear Club” member by diving off the pier near the din-
 ing hall and swimming through the thin ice. There were no follow-
 ers.
      Jane Slater once gave Albers two white pigeons for his birth-
 day. Who got them when [the Alberses] left?
       The sculptor José de Creeft staged a bullfight in the dining hall
 one Saturday evening after dinner. He was the matador, and his
 future wife, Lorrie Goulet, was the bull. I was el presidente, who
_ gave the command that the fight begin. I wore a black cardboard
  stovepipe and was very dignified. I don’t remember awarding any
 “ears,” though.
     It should be remembered that this was wartime, and the choco-
 late bars that the storekeeper, Janet Goldsmith, sold were VitaSert
 bars. Jean Charlot, the teacher, ate them between         two pieces of
 bread.
      Certain sounds: the bleating of the Dreier boy’s goat; the great
 triangular railroad iron, which was rung for afternoon tea and
 meals; the famous water pitchers which one rang to get the atten-
 tion of people in the dining hall in making an announcement; the
 gurgling brook near the office. Certain sights: bursts of orange azal-
 eas in the spring; ivory-carved mountain laurel blossoms; spiky
 rhododendron blooms.
     There was the intensity of student work, an almost physical
 exhaustion when coming down to the dining hall from an Albers



 An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                     137

--- Page 162 ---
class in watercolor painting: make a painting in local color, ie.
green leaves, add red scarf, etc., then change it in another painting
to a “higher key,” i.e., red or magenta   leaves with an acid green
scarf. I’m sure the classes in Bergson’s “Creative Evolution” were
equally exhausting.
      Imagine when the outside examiner, Karl Veet (Hamilton Col-
lege) came for Jane Slater’s two days of orals [and] three days of
written exams in art history. You’d better know which Uccello is at
Oxford!
     There was a kind of tacit prohibition about a too ostentatious
display of wealth. By and large students walked down North Fork
Road to the highway and took the bus to Asheville, usually on Sat-
urday afternoon, unless someone (namely Molly Gregory) was tak-
ing the truck into the city, sometimes to get “treats” at a local bak-
ery, or art supplies, and perhaps rarely a steak dinner at the Oyster
Bar. Willi Joseph always asked anyone going to Asheville to bring
him “bear claws” from a local bakery.
     Dr. Hansgirg had a cream-colored Cadillac convertible called
the “Cream Dream.” He was also working with Henry Kaiser (who
built the Liberty ships in World War II), and I believe it was the
occasion of his birthday that I was invited along with many others
to his cottage. It may have been for a brief concert with the
Shaufflers at piano and cello, Dee Carr on violin, playing some
Bach trio sonata. We were served champagne with slices of
peaches. The peaches had been soaked in 100-proof alcohol for a
week. One tended to float across the lawn after one or two. And,
during the concert, two mice came out and danced.
      And there were—all around the College—fields of daffodils in |
the spring, mountain laurel and lady’s slippers in the forest, and
great orange torches of azalea along the road, as well as rhododen-
drons near the mountain brook at the office.
      The magnet of Black Mountain was not luxurious living but the
luxurious mind. That is why we came. It was incumbent on the stu-
dent to make his or her way in the community by intellectual or
artistic prowess—not by his or her automobile.
      The library’s collection of artbooks contained some of the rar-
est volumes on modern art—including surrealism, which Albers
was not in sympathy with—proving how catholic the library was.

I wonder if Rauschenberg’s famous “bed” painting or Kenneth
  Noland’s “target” paintings would have been made if [the artists]
  had not been [students] at BMC. Some of what is contained in those
  works might have come from the matiére or color classes of Albers.




                                               ee        Coron
                                                    (Faculty 1944-50)


                                    With Eyes on the Ground:
                                       Black Mountain Years



  From Black Mountain stemmed my interest in wildflowers and in
  the American Indian. The Lake Eden campus was a scene of com-
  pelling natural beauty. At times I had great difficulty concentrating
  on intellectual and collegiate matters, and many a time I walked
  away from the storm of college politics and the issues of education,
- art, and society to refresh myself in the natural scene. To give an
  objective to my numerous walks in the mountains, I became inter-
  ested in the local wildflowers. In college I had been a
  Wordsworthian imbued with the doctrine that “Little we see in
  Nature that is ours... . ’
                               iv




        —Great God! Id rather be
      A Pagan suckled in a creed outworn;
      So might I, standing on this pleasant lea,
      Have glimpses that would make me less forlorn;
      Have sight of Proteus rising from the sea;
      Or hear old Triton blow his wreathéd horn.

  The mountains were Old Proteus and the flowers were Triton’s
  wreathed horn. Within half a mile of the dining hall a dozen mem-
  bers of the orchid family flourished, some on the shores of the lake,
- some in the woods beyond the Quiet House, some up the hill to-


 An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                 139

--- Page 164 ---
ward the pasture—beautiful things—some small, some flamboy-
ant; some white, some yellow, some magenta, some pink. One
could drop onto one’s knee and gaze full in the face at something
inexpressibly wild reaching toward one from out of the ages—pro-
tean. Altogether I identified over three hundred species of
wildflowers in the Black Mountain area, ranging from the small
racemes of delicate white flowers of the rank and noxious jimson-
weed, to the tall-stalked subtly pink meadow beauty. Way up on
the highest point of the pasture, among rocks and sparse weeds,
grew the small flowers of the squirrel corn, a relative of the obvious
and grossly named Dutchman's breeches of the north. Near the
falls of the brook, on the way to the pasture, grew in the middle of
the path a pink orchis; and down by the path from Meadows Inn to
the farm, the purple and white showy orchis; and on the far edge of
the lake, the purple-fringed orchis standing next to a tall brilliant
cardinal flower. The pilgrims of the new order could talk about the
“exciting” showmen, the pitchmen of modernism who lectured at
the College, but excitement to me was a newly found species of
wildflower.
     I suppose my greatest thrill came one day when Marion, my
wife, called from the backyard of Meadows       Inn, “Come     here and
see what this is!” I ran out of the house and followed her pointing
finger to ten yards away under some scrub pines to see a long-
stemmed hooded white flower. I jumped with delight for it was an
oconee-bell, the Shortia galacifolia that the French naturalist André
Michaux had named in the 1790s and which for a long time no one
else had been able to find. During the two or three years before, I
had climbed all over the two-hundred-foot cliffs of Toxaway Falls,
fifty miles away, where it was said to flourish, and I had been un-
able to find a trace of it. Here in our backyard, out of its natural
habitat, it was growing! transplanted from the Highlands region by
our predecessor,   Clark   Foreman.   Nevertheless,   it was   wild and
beautiful.                   3
    In my wildflower walks I walked many miles in the hills and
mountains, encountering rattlesnakes and black bears and interest
ing mountain characters, one of whom was a bootlegger, maker ©
apple brandy, a vicious liquor which went down the throat like
broken glass and left one smelling like a barroom full of drunks.



140                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 165 ---
Another was a small farmer who lived on the cash from his tobacco
allotment with a cow, a pig, a vegetable plot, and two acres of to-
bacco plants, and [who] took two weeks off to go with his family
into the lowland swamps”bear hunting. Not that I went about
prying into other people’s business. One simply did not go into
those mountains without stopping to pass the time of day with
whomever [one] met. To do so was not only an expected courtesy
but a security measure, for the mountain people were suspicious of
strangers and wanted to know who was wandering about in their
purlieus. Once a slouch-hatted sheriff's deputy with a gun arrested
me and put me to work on the fireline of a local brush fire. He sus-
pected me of having caused the fire, it not being an uncommon
practice of the hill people to start fires in order to get cash employ-
ment as fire fighters.
    My mountain      walking got me     into the practice of leading
mountain hikes of students, sometimes with groups of girls, some-
times with ex-Gls. On one occasion I led a group of girls on an over-
night trip to Mount Mitchell, the highest mountain in the East, and
but fifteen miles from the College. We stayed in the ranger’s cabin
at the top of the mountain. In the middle of the night one of the
girls came to my door and knocked to inform me that one of the
girls was deathly sick. I roused the ranger, who immediately got
onto the telephone and got a call through to the College to Molly
Gregory, dean of girls. Molly got into her car and drove the fifteen
long miles, by circuitous roads, arriving at the ranger’s cabin at two
o'clock in the morning to take the girl down to the College. When
we got back the next day, Molly grabbed me to tell me that the girl
was all right. She had smuggled a bottle of “mountain dew” onto
the trip, had imbibed freely, and become sick. Nothing as drastic as
that happened on my trips with the Gls. These were into rugged
high places. Once, however, we got arrested for illegally fishing in
a federal game preserve and had the gang of us hauled into a
justice’s Sunday morning court and fined.
      But my greatest find at the College was the American Indian.
One day, stepping out of Meadows Inn after a heavy rain, I noticed
  , the washed gravel a small beautifully shaped arrowhead. That
set me to looking for more arrowheads, and before long they were
jumping out of the ground at me, principally in the hillside



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                  141

--- Page 166 ---
cornfield. I soon realized that the wide valley of the neighborhood
at the base of the Black Mountain range (Mt. Mitchell) must have
had a long period of Indian occupation as a favored site. Later I
learned from Shorty Christman, the local hillbilly barber, who was
born and raised on the Lake Eden site before the lake was made
(the old log library house in front of the Studies Building was the
Christman cabin moved up from the lake site), that he had picked
up many Indian artifacts from the lakeside area and sold them to
collectors. I conjectured from the numerous projectile points in
unfinished condition that the region must have been a scene of ar-
rowhead manufacture for a great many years, and that it had been
Cherokee hunting grounds. I even conjectured that it had been in
the 1750s the site of an illegal trading post operated against Caro-
lina law by Virginians. It was clear that the site had had centuries
of Indian occupation, probably from pre-Cherokee days.
     I set out to learn all I could about the region’s prehistory, and I
soon found myself in the Asheville Public Library researching the
fine Sondley collection of eighteenth- and nineteenth-century books
on southern Indians. Old Sondley had been collecting materials for
years, preparatory to his writing a history of the Cherokees (the
manuscript of which was never published but remains at the li-
brary). The librarians were very helpful, particularly Margaret
Ligon and Ida Padelford, themselves much interested in the musi-
cal programs at BMC, and who obtained microfilm of eighteenth-
century colonial newspapers and manuscripts for me. The end
product of all this research was three books and several articles in
journals on the southern Indians, a position teaching American In-
dian history at Roosevelt University, Chicago, and a fellowship at
the Newberry Library in Chicago. My course in American Indian
history was at the time one of the two or three such courses offered
in American universities. As you can see, BMC, despite its travails,
was very rewarding to me—and not the least rewarding were the
friendships among the faculty and students I made there, some of
which have lasted to this day.

--- Page 167 ---
Margaret Kennard Johnson
                                                     (Student 1944 SS)

              Six Short Weeks at Black Mountain


What drew me there was a tiny clipping enclosed in a letter from
my first art teacher, my mother. It announced that José de Creeft,
New York sculptor, would be teaching direct carving in stone dur-
ing the coming summer at BMC. I did not know the name “José de
Creeft,” but the “direct carving in stone” convinced me to go.
      When I arrived at BMC I learned that top people in each of the
major arts had converged in this remote mountain valley to share
their vision and knowledge and friendship. This was to be one of
the most broadening, enlightening, exhilarating experiences of my
life. I soon realized that, though I wanted to devote most of my
time to sculpture, there was a much broader spectrum of knowl-
edge and inspiration to be gained from this remarkable collection
of personalities. For the first time, I learned that painting, sculpture,
architecture, drawing, designing were not separately wrapped
packages, but all were forms of the same visual language. Painter,
architect, industrial designer, art educator, sculptor, photogra-
pher—Josef Albers, Amédée Ozenfant, Jean Charlot, Walter Gro-
pius, James Prestini, Alvin Lustig, Victor D’Amico, José de Creeft,
and Barbara Morgan—all spoke the language of vision. This was
the most important concept I learned during that summer. And it
was Josef Albers who so clearly and lastingly helped deepen my
understanding and ability to use this language. I am still learning
from Albers’s   sessions on color relativity, drawing with wire in
space to create air volumes, 3-D paper constructions—and from his
discussions of Paul Klee, other artists, and his own philosophy. I
think Albers’s teaching held meaning far beyond the problem at
hand, providing a base for aiding visual solutions throughout life—
a base likely better understood and more freely interpreted as for-
mer students matured and developed their individual creative
ways. Studying with Albers during that all-too-short summer in-
creased my understanding of the visual language and thereby pro-



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                    143

--- Page 168 ---
vided a foundation from which I was able to develop my own way
of teaching as I taught basic design courses down through the
years: at Victor D’Amico’s Art Center at the Museum of Modern
Art, at Pratt Institute, at Drake University, at Texas State College
for Women, in Tokyo, and, even now, forty-four years later, in Prin-
ceton. Also the rooting in visual fundamentals from Albers, along
with the observation of my students’ solutions to design problems,
has helped immeasurably to strengthen the visual structure of my
own work.
    Studying direct carving in stone with José de Creeft was hard
physical work and pure delight, both for the satisfaction of reveal-
ing forms from the resistant diorite rock I was chiseling, and for the
pleasure of knowing something of the philosophy and warm hu-
mor of de Creeft. We students could scarcely let him go. His cha-
risma and colorful means of expressing what he wanted to say—a
creative blend of English, French, and Spanish—captured us all
and was part of the immense love we all had for carving our hard
rocks.
      During the summer I first met Victor D’Amico, and it was then
that he invited me to teach at MOMA. But it was not until the fall of
1946 that I began to do so. Had I not met Victor at BMC, I would
never have had the privilege of being on his staff. Over the years I
learned much from him and others of his staff, three of whom also
attended the 1944 Summer Art Institute: Mickey Masur, Jane Coo-
per Bland, and Lorrie Goulet. Victor encouraged us all to share
ideas and explore ways to better bring out the creative potential of
each individual in our classes. Again, the Albers grounding was a
continuing thread to assist in my teaching. The Art Center was a
thriving, inspiring, model art-educational institution. My adult stu-
dents came and went over the twenty-five-year period I was teach-
ing there, and the most recurring comment I heard from them re-
garded how much their hands-on experience with the makings of
art enhanced their appreciation of the museums collection. Teach-
ing at MOMA opened all sorts of doors elsewhere: even abroad,
when we lived in Zurich, and much later during our residence in
Tokyo (1975-83).
    Study with Albers at BMC helped me to become quickly and
deeply involved in the contemporary art world of Japan. Being ex-



144                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 169 ---
perienced in printmaking myself for many years, I discovered that
Japanese artists were doing outstanding work in this art form, us-
ing old and new processes with woodblock and other techniques
that combined Eastern and Western approaches. It seemed that
such work deserved more exposure in the West than it was yet re-
ceiving. Working with Dale K. Hilton, a friend and art historian liv-
ing in Tokyo who was also enthusiastic about contemporary Japa-
nese prints, eventually resulted in the publication of Japanese Prints
Today: Tradition with Innovation (Tokyo: Shufunotomo, Ltd., 1980)
and a two-year traveling show across the United States that fea-
tured   the work,   processes,   and   philosophies   of the twenty-two
Japanese artists shown and discussed in our book.
    I shall ever be grateful for those six short weeks at BMC that
broadened my vision and enriched my life.




                                       Ruth Lyford Sussler
                                                      (Student 1944 SS)


                                       Mixtures and Impacts



The summer of 1944 was crucial to my whole subsequent existence.
Impacts like being in the South with Jim Crow, chain gangs, boot-
leg moonshine, that incredible mix of sophisticated Europeans
gathered in the amazing “island” of creativity and cross-pollination
in the heart of hillbilly redneck country—are all part of it.
     A great thunder and lightning storm was passing us, rumbling
all around our valley. It was a fine summer heat-storm going by af-
ter supper, one to notice only incidentally. We were gathered in the
dining hall, listening to a panel discussion which turned to the im-
portance of dance as an art form, its varying fortunes in different
historical periods. One of the lively participants on the panel was
Agnes de Mille, visiting BMC only a few years after doing the cho-



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                   145

--- Page 170 ---
reography for Rodgers and Hammerstein’s Oklahoma. At one point
in the panel exchange, de Mille proceeded to make a very strong
point, her eyes flashing, about the exclusion of dance in early
church ritual. One hand was raised up, forefinger extended to add
emphasis to her depth of conviction. Just as she stabbed the dark
space in this manner, saying it was THE CHURCH that forbade
dancing in worship rituals, a deafening clap of thunder rolled
through the enormous silence. A great shudder swept over the au-
dience, followed by a collective gasp of amazement and then all
collapsing in delirious laughter, led by de Mille herself. The forces
of nature seemed to be working in unison with the discussion.
     A girls’ dormitory upstairs in North Lodge, called “the attic,”
was connected to our large airy bathroom. This room was a large
irregularly shaped one, with bays and dormers facing in three di-
rections [and] the cots . . . arranged in appropriate summer-camp
style. Often the dressing-up for supper took place after a good soak
in the old-fashioned cast-iron tub on claw feet. The tub stood on the
bare wooden floor just above where the string bass stood during
chamber-music rehearsals that took place each afternoon. During
those late-day immersions in bathwater enclosed in the iron tub, I
imagined the rafters supporting this heavy load to be vibrating
gently to the plucking and bowing of the enormous thick strings
belonging to the double bass down below. Those baths were musi-
cal ones. I stayed very still in the hot water so as not to make any
weird superfluous noises that might interfere with the notes played
by the bass player, Simon Sadoff, who was unaware of this happen-
ing above him.
     Simon also played miraculous piano duets with Fritz Co-
hen. ... Everyone feasted their ears as these two sat down to play
on the pair of grand pianos in the dining hall. The magic they cre-
ated was especially glorious when they played an evening of music
from Carmen. Voice students sang to their accompaniment. The
“Habanera” brought down the house with the outrageous tempo
improvisations and rubatos they executed.
     Jean Charlot directed a project of making frescoes for a wall of
one of the wide pilings or cement supports under the long Studies
Building wing that projected out from the hillside. We worked in
the cool shade under the building, mixing and shoveling the ce-



146                  Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 171 ---
ment. Charlot had sections of brown paper diagrams laid out to be
 traced on the wall and worked on piece by piece as drying took
 place. This began my fascination and love for wet mortar, pigment
 mixing, and the mystery of the binding process. The body move-
 ment and effort of shoveling wet cement was similar to cutting
 cornstalks in the field for silage, [which] was part of the community
 work program. This was a task in which everyone supposedly took
 part. The flow of energy that bristled from this gathering of souls
 was powerful. The day’s work swallowed you up, carried you
 along in the rhythm of swinging machetes. The whole cornstalk
 was cut down and then tossed onto piles which were gathered and
 fed into a grinder, sucked up and blown into the top of the silo. The
 noise and dust created in this process were enormous. The excite-
 ment and energy released in this ritual of harvesting the corn gave
 magic to the hard physical labor taking place in the hot sun, mak-
 ing the work seem effortless. The importance of community man-
 ual labor as a balance to solitary thinking and artistic effort could
 be well understood. This was an inherited belief held strongly then
 and throughout the College’s life.
      One day I walked confidently into the little town of Black
 Mountain, sure that I would find the library. Feeling dismayed in
 not readily spotting it, and inquiring of a person I met on the dusty
~ road that was the main drag, I learned that the library was upstairs
 above the jail and “t’weren’t open ‘cept now and then.” I couldn't
 imagine any town without a library, nor could I picture a library
 that was not open most of the time and that was located above the
 jail!
      Most eerie of my glimpses of a different world in the South
 were those of chain gangs [which] seemed a normal occurrence as
 we came across them during carefree hikes along the mountain
 roads and paths. I was really seeing them, not watching a movie or
 imagining such a sight from descriptions in a book. There they
 were, men wearing ankle irons, or they were chained together, or
 they wore heavy iron balls which they dragged along behind them
 on the dusty road, seeming to walk in slow motion from the weight
 of their burden. They wore striped prison uniforms and moved in
 ant     lines.   The   rifles   held   in the   arms   of the   guards,   whose

_ forefingers grasped the triggers, had a terrifying effect on me. I saw



 An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                           147

--- Page 172 ---
the gun barrels shoved close to the faces and eyes of the prisoners.
It was hard to take in this spectacle of human beings carrying on in
such conditions of humiliation and deprivation.
     I skipped in and out of the Edenesque world of the College col-
ony which was so removed in its richness of personal discovery
and artistic sharing and expression—from those surrounding Ap-
palachian pockets of the rural poor. [Meek and tentative] questions
about what I was seeing beyond the gates were pushed onto a back
burner to simmer. I don’t remember if there-was much or any dis-
cussion of these things during the summer of ’44. I only remember
that the College mission and focus [were] mercifully to accomplish
ambitious artistic goals within its own environment.

               Janet “Bingo” (Ramsey) Aley
                                                  (Student 1944-46)


                                          A Little Upstream



Why did I choose Black Mountain? It was preferred that I attend a
more Ivy League school, but the combination of not the highest
marks and my fervent idealism lent itself to my arrival at BMC
from Westport, Connecticut, into a beautiful but frightening world.
I had heard of the school through earlier camp experience at Put-
ney, Vermont, and further from Sammy Brown, a current student,
who visited my concerned family (radical kid, me) to describe life
at BMC and what might be expected. Mac Wood, who taught car-
pentry and small-house design, picked us up in a College truck at
the railroad station.
     Thank God they conferred upon me an immediate identity,
that of crew coordinator for the furnaces, the Studies Building
stoker, and the formidable high-pressure boiler that lurked beneath
the dining hall and which had to be fired up—not too dangerously
high—so that Cornelia, George, and Mallory could cook us our fine
southern (with a little German thrown in) meals. Dee Carr, my
study mate, was also my assistant. Others who aided were Lorna



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                               149

--- Page 174 ---
Pearson and Earlene Wight. Ben Sneed, the College handyman,
sleepy drunk and abed in the coal bin there, relished telling us evil
stories of boiler explosions and horrors that befell those caught
within them, as we anxiously monitored the gauge. If I had not had
that identity,   I might have floundered seriously at BMC.             The shy
idealist, awed by the apparent sophistication and wealth of the in-
tellectual   environment,   as   well   as   the   “free   love”   available,   I
walked tightly on my mental two-by-four, quietly absorbing the
teachings of Bob Wunsch, Dave Corkran (a wonderful teacher who
spotted my uncertainties during study of “Documents in American
History”), M. C. Richards, Albers (briefly), George Zabriskie (poet),
Alfred Kazin (who was in his own transition and taught Emerson
and Melville and frequented the dart board in our study), Jalo and
Eddie Lowinsky (beautiful music), as well as Bass Allen, whom                   I
adored and who taught me plumbing. The work/study aspect of
BMC appealed to me immensely: haying at the farm, feeding the
pigs with Lorna Blaine, shoveling coal into the truck from the
freight car siding at Black Mountain station—these shared in im-
portance with my studies and somehow gave me a base for sur-
vival.
     Images abound without sequence: a glowing fireplace in the
Quiet House while sharing philosophies with another new student,
Judd (Eddy) Woldin; dancing in the dining hall after dinner to
Judd’s great piano music; a hike to Mt. Mitchell where we stood
transfixed above a thunderstorm, a little snipe-hunting [a hoax that
mountaineers perpetuate on flatlanders] up to the pasture and
along mountain trails near the College; bear tracks and a coon dog;
lazing on the dining hall steps singing rounds, which all my chil-
dren now know, fire drills with our prehistoric pumper, which we
hauled up the road like horses; the calming view across Lake Eden
toward the Blue Ridge Mountains; the ducks and wildflowers;
playing Chopin preludes on the piano in the Round House well
into the far reaches of the night; the smell of George Zabriskie’s
pipe as he puffed and pondered one of our poetic efforts; the bleak
night when news of Jalo’s death stunned us with despair; the beer-
less beer at Roy’s in Black Mountain; actor Swack (Egbert Swack-
hammer) clowning his way into a closed door, and dubbing me
with name of “Bingo” (perhaps because of my dogged ways); the



150                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 175 ---
horror I felt when I saw new Albers’s students destroy all evidence
of their earlier training in order to begin anew; stealing cigarette
butts from empty studies in order to roll our own with their
remnants   (Agh!); listening to Beethoven’s Seventh on our quaking
record player, piled high with books to keep it stable, while curled
up comfortably on the old car seats (our furniture) gleaned from
the dump; the marvelous looms that stood like fragile sculpture in
the lower level of the Studies Building, which, with Anni Albers’s
guidance, produced accomplished and talented weavers like my
friend, Joan Sihvonen; the faculty kids running up and down the
Studies Building halls with their dogs; and the tales we would hear
of the vibrant Summer Sessions—with people like Gropius, Varda,
Agnes de Mille—that showered color upon our seeming black-and-
white regular student experience.
     There was my own moment of glory, which I suppose taught
me one of the important lessons of my life: a book critique of
Zuleika Dobson (Beerbohm) requested by Elliot Merrick, my litera-
ture teacher. This was a book I did not enjoy but faced up to in an
all-night session by transcribing it into a ditty. Merrick read my
piece to the class with delight and chuckles, commending me for
my work. Then he slid the paper down the classroom table to me. I
opened it with pride. It was marked with a large F with comment
that this was not the assignment requested. Story of my life—a little
upstream—doing things differently. Maybe that’s why I elected to
go to a college like BMC.
     After I left the school, and after a short period of soul search-
ing, I started writing for local newspapers, and after my marriage
to Jonathan Aley, in 1948, I worked      in New   York City for seven
years for Look magazine. Then came the mid-’50s and the arrival of
kids—now all good human adults. Much of what has sustained me
and filtered down to my children has roots in the experience I had
there, which provided a continuing liberal outlook upon life and
politics, less awe of the professional intellect, an eye for design via
Albers, and memories      of so many    people I would    love to hear
about. The experience resulted in a deep appreciation of life, its
music, its art, its smells, its feelings, its vagaries, its animals, its
people.




An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                   151

--- Page 176 ---
John R. Corkran
                                             (Faculty Son 1944-54)


                                                           Sports




There was much more to BMC       than the intellectual ferment, great
as that was. I think one can view Black Mountain as perhaps the
best example of what. a true liberal-arts education should provide.
The people I knew best there, and those whom        I have gotten to
know since, were multifaceted and multitalented. But they all had
a compassion, an interest in the well-being of others, that certainly
has helped me shape my view of how the world should work and
how I should function within it.
     Sports at BMC was the one area in which I really was able to
participate during the time we lived there. Being active in the
games at the school helped my development in a variety of ways.
For one thing, I saw some talented athletes who gave me a good
idea of how football and baseball, in particular, should be played. I
also learned that sports should be fun. There were lots of fierce
competitors at BMC, but there were few instances in which they let
winning supercede fun as the reason for the game. I also learned,
however, that when you ask to be allowed to play a position, you
accept the consequences that come with that position. Two in-
stances come to mind at once.
     Catcher was a position usually reserved for one of the adults
(my brother Dave got to play once in a while, which made me jeal-
ous), but Alvin Charles Few, Eddie Dreier, and I rarely got a
chance. And always when there was a play at the plate in the
offing, one of the male students took over. One day I insisted on
being the catcher, and when the heavy hitters came up no one
could get the glove away from me. John Bergman, a powerfully


John went to high school in the town of Black Mountain; he then went
to Wesleyan University.



152                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 177 ---
built and feared hitter drove one deep between the outfielders, but
one of them ran it down. The relay throw beat Bergman by about
five yards, and I caught it and stood on the plate. “Boom!” The next
thing I remember is Bergman looking down at me, asking if I was
all right. He was safe, and my desire to catch was satisfied for some
time to come!
     Left field, like catcher, was usually reserved for the more
skilled players because virtually every ball was hit to that side.
Well, I wanted to play left field, and not just when the girls were
hitting. I got my wish. When Ike Nakata came up (Ike was another
stockily-built athlete) he said, “Okay, Johnnie, I hit you one, you
ready?” I pounded my hands together (few mitts in those days for
softball) confidently. And Ike hit a rocket. I am sure that many of
the students would have felt perfectly fine if that ball had done me
in. As I chased it down I could hear Ike shouting as he rounded the
bases: “How you like that, Johnnie? How you like that?”
    Then there were the Thanksgiving Day football games. That
was the time, I think, that the continuity between the old culture
and the life of Black Mountain was best demonstrated. The early
games were intense, well played, and yet a way of drawing people
together. The first game in 1946 or ’47 was most memorable for the
names of the competing teams: the “Cro-Magnons” and the “Sticky
Attitudes.” I sense that if BMC could have continued to work out
its philosophical tensions on the playing field the way it did early-
on, it might have done more, for more, for longer. I still feel that
even those whose names never became household words had an
outsized impact on the world because of the special combination of
work, play, and study.
     On the opening play of that first game, Ike Nakata and Faf Fos-
ter were lined up across from each other. Although they were good
friends, they were also fierce competitors. When the ball was
snapped, Ike, on offense, lunged at Faf, who wasn’t there, having
neatly leaped over Ike. Second snap, same result. Third snap, Ike
stood up. The game was resumed shortly after Faf had recovered a
bit, and there was no more vaulting over linemen that afternoon. I
think the game ended in a six-six tie, no provision having been
made for extra points.
    It may have been that year or the next when my brother Dave

A program of a memorable football game on Thanksgiving of 1946—it was
indicative of the wholesome and vital spirit that vets brought to BMC—
this game was planned as an informal satire on college football—a game to
end all games—everybody kept this hilarious feeling during the game ex-
cept the players—they became rough and dead serious. The cro-magnons or
PREHISTORIC SPECIMENS (ironically enough, little nervous men com-
pared to the modern neurotics) won 8-0.
                                              —Stuart “Squeaky” Atkinson




 An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                       155

--- Page 180 ---
of “doing what needed to be done.” Dick Roberts, when he played,
was generally stationed during our softball games in right field.
That was a position often occupied by women and/or children be-
cause so few balls were hit there. So “Glass-Arm’” got very little
business most of the time. But once a game, there would be a ball
hit to right centerfield. In that situation Roberts always made the
right play, picking up the ball and gunning it on a line to home, or
third, usually throwing out the base runner. Unfortunately, there-
after his right arm was useless, having been “thrown out.” He ei-
ther used his left, lobbed underhanded         with his right, or retired
from play.
    Merv Lane was the first person I ever saw throwing the discus.
He had brought a regulation high-school discus to the College and
used to go down to the playing field and throw it. That was an en-
thralling experience, which I never forgot. When it turned out I
couldn’t hit a college fastball, I turned to track and lettered two
years as a weightman [at Wesleyan], throwing shot some and dis-
cus a lot. A third in the discus against Springfield enabled
Wesleyan to win by one point, sixty-eight to sixty-seven, which was
a high for me. Later I coached discus in high school for a couple of
years.
     When you reflect on those afternoons in which a combination
of kids, girls, men,    students,   faculty, skilled, unskilled, serious,
whimsical—all played together, you feel that was how people were
meant to relate. Sports at BMC could accommodate all of those per-
sonalities and personal qualities and leave people feeling they had
expressed themselves and been appreciated in the effort. I think it
gave me a basic capacity to appreciate the contributions of others,
and their inherent worth, something which has been invaluable to
me in the educational work I have chosen. And there was one other
quality that came out of that experience, and that was being pre-
pared to do what was needed in a moment’s              notice. When,    as a
youth, you got a chance to play in adult games, you knew you had
to hustle, stay alert, go a little harder or a little farther to justify that
faith. It made me better at some things than natural tape hn            alone
would have permitted.

John Urbain
                                                   (Student 1945-47)

                                   Matiére Studies—1946



Many remarks have been made concerning the word matiére. I have
heard students use the word at the dinner table when a not too
appetizing plate of food is placed before them. A pile of trash in
one of the studies is almost sure to provoke some one to exclaim,
“Oh! A matiére.” Then, there is the retort, “You name        it and I'll
make a matiére out of it.” These remarks are, of course, entirely
unharmful and not without humor. . . .
      Mr. Albers’s design students, who are familiar with the term,
should be able to give a clear explanation, but I find that only a few
of them can eloquently present a comprehensive definition of ma-
tire.
      In paintings we find an arrangement of lines, spaces, and col-
ors. The planning of these elements, according to the various prin-
ciples in art, involves a matter of selection—the selection of lines,
spaces, and colors. In creating a matiére we are concerned        with
appearances of surfaces and materials. As in painting, one color af-
fects another, one value affects another, or one space affects an-
other, so surface qualities influence other surface qualities. The vis-
ual arts involve the optical senses. With matiére, there is involved
an additional factor, that of the tactile senses. We desire to touch
and feel the matiére studies.
    The appearances of surfaces of materials are classified as struc-
ture, facture, and texture. To quote Mr. Albers, “Structure refers to
those qualities of surface which reveal how the raw material grows
or is formed, such as the grain of wood      or the fur of an animal.
Facture refers to those qualities of surface which reveal how the raw
material has been treated technically, such as the hammered or pol-
ished surface of metal, or wavy surface of corrugated paper.
Texture is a general term which refers to both ‘structure’ and ‘fac-
ture’, but only if both are present.”




An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                  157

--- Page 186 ---
Through the combination of materials we become sensitive to
the subtle transitions and transformation of surface qualities. Hard,
soft, smooth, and rough characteristics begin to take on more em-
phasis and meaning. To experience this, you need only think of cof-
fee grounds, which are usually seen in a heap or pile. Spread the
grounds evenly on a flat surface and form rectangles, squares, or
triangles, or circles out of the grounds. You will readily see that this
material no longer has its original appearance. To further change its
character, place dark soil next to the grounds. The soil influences
the coffee grounds, forming an affinity. An entirely different effect
can be achieved by placing another material next to the coffee, such
as broken glass, sawdust, flour, etc. This would be using a contrast-
ing element which would also change the original appearance of
the grounds. The combinations and results are limitless.
     Through these experiments with matiére studies we develop
sensitivity in observing and attain new vision which has a stimulat-
ing influence in all our visual experiences.




                                          Fannie Hillsmith
                                                    (Student   1945 SS)


                              “Does This Have Color?”


My stay at BMC was relatively short, but all the time I was there
the Second World War was very much on all our minds. Listening
to the news   every night had, of course, more      impact while sur-
rounded by Europeans whose friends and relatives were in con-
stant danger.
     While I was at BMC I was completely captivated by the charm,
the surroundings, the students, the other instructors and most of all
by Albers, who, besides his other talents, gave an authentic Euro-
pean atmosphere to the College. The dinner hour was utterly de-
lightful with three-quarters of those present breaking into French
or German totally unconsciously.


158                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 187 ---
One of my clearest memories of Albers is his Saturday morning
classes where he would make his students participate in such an
effective way that I have used the same device with variations. All
the students’ paintings were lined up against the wall and with his
pointer he would call out dramatically, “Does this have color?”
And the audience would scream “YES!” or “NO!” They had
learned that COLOR did not necessarily mean brightness but rather
the vibrant relationship of one color to another.

                                         Excerpts from Notes
