                                     —Summer 1945



Arrival Black Mountain, August 6.
I’m amused by the idea that the exiles are to be considered trans-
mitters of the culture of a fallen world—the European world: as the
medieval monks were for classical antiquity, whose achievements
they preserved from oblivion. This occurred to me as I observed
Bodky’s activity, which is so intensive, eager, enthusiastic. I came
straight from Black Mountain station to his performance of the


These excerpts are translated by Martin’s daughter, Denise Martin
Gaines, who writes in a 1988 letter: “My father was in his early 50s in
1945. He taught piano privately in Staten Island, and at Wagner College.
I was a student at Barnard College at the time, and also an avid flute
player (in the journal notes my nickname, Nina, is used), and I studied,
during the summer we spent at BMC, with Josef Marx, a great oboist
and teacher, whose general musicianship had a great influence on me. I
think the five short weeks at BMC have been among the most important
in my   life, in many   subtle   ways,   but...in   addition   to those    in-
tangibles ... my friendship with Angie Bodky Lee started there.”



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                         159

--- Page 188 ---
Second Well-Tempered Klavier, whose first twelve fugues he played
well.
      Standing close to the water, I saw three butterflies of unusual
size and beauty, playing near the sand, wings like sails. Mrs. Straus
brought news of Russia’s declaration of war against Japan.
      Bodky is definitely a “character” here—great authority on
Bach—not quite as good in explaining and playing Beethoven so-
natas, but even here always stimulating, witty, in fine spirits. ...
Bodky on the composition of an old master: “These chord combina-
tions are hopelessly wrong and silly”; about Beethoven passages
(“Waldstein” Sonata), which he plays slowly for greater effect:
“Here Beethoven   produces only applesauce”;     “Beethoven,   when-
ever he speaks of love, uses the key of E-flat major. Now listen to
the beginning of the Sonata Op. 31, where one thinks he is saying
“Liebst Du mich”: it is precisely E-flat major!”
     Gropius (or Josef Marx) remarked that sometimes an age pro-
duces one of its greatest figures just as it is coming to an end, so
that the next, quite different period, into which such figures are
placed, cannot really consider them contemporaries. This remark
fits Proust very well, who is not at all “modern” (as they consider
themselves “modern” here at BMC).
     Further enrichment of the technical vocabulary of art (thanks to
Motherwell): subconscious automatism—one stands before a sheet
of paper, preferably with eyes closed, and lets the pencil draw lines
which are controlled as little as possible by the human mind. “This
way,” says Motherwell in his modest and winning way, “Picasso
and Klee also made some of their most masterful pictures.”
     America sets the clock back. Could we have guessed in 1918
that our audacious pranks, that even Dada, would be taught in
1945 at an American college as art of the masters? Kauder, between
an omelet and some bread and honey, remarks that these artistic
movements, which originated in a world of disintegration, where
they are understandable, are being transplanted in America into a
healthy environment with different presuppositions. To him, the
most absurd part is that even Europeans come here to carry on-the
imitation.
     Brilliant performance (at harpsichord) by Bodky of pre-Bach
masters and Bach contemporaries. He approaches Bach from the

     most   diverse   directions:   this   time     Polietti,   Boehm,   Beckmann,
     Buxtehude, Pachelbel.
          Swimming in the creek daily, or lying on the dam because
     there is plenty of sunshine there. Country road: at the right a hedge
     of beech, on the left corn (twice my height). View straight along the
     narrow path towards purple mountains. Grazing cattle. Quiet of
     noon. In the distance, near the idyllic farm house, in the shade of a
     tree, sheltered by foliage, saw Angie and Nina sitting on the
     ground. Just within waving distance. The most pleasant locality,
    the best place in which I could think of them resting.

    nately the encore is a bad joke in the style of A. Templeton on Bach
    motifs. One has to be ready for anything.
         Morning stillness around the little lake. One can hear Straus
    practice the violin. Three small clouds drift over three small hills.

--- Page 190 ---
If living in close community with so many people forces us to
be considerate and disciplined, one must ask on the other hand
whether one would make enemies with the same zest (as it hap-
pens here occasionally), if one had the prospect of living together
this way for a long time? Maybe practical considerations would
sometimes hold the passions in check?
     “ ., that the greatest thing a human soul ever does in this
world is to see something, and tell what it saw in a plain way. Hun-
dreds of people can talk for one who can think, but thousands can
think for one who can see.” (John Ruskin, Modern Painters, vol. 3).
This, among other phrases, is tacked to the wall in Albers’s class-
room.
    It is possible here to ascertain the fact that friendships between
persons of different races can be of an ease that is entirely sponta-
neous, not acquired.
     . . . One brings along the supper (consisting of hard-boiled
eggs, salads, fruits, milk, cheese) which was given out in the morn-
ing (Sunday) because the kitchen staff has a day off, eats it together
(after it has been arranged in a more decorative way) sitting on the
floor, since there are never enough chairs available, and graciously
turns it into a social event. John Reiss showed some charming
paintings, objective and nonobjective. It was the first time that I...
saw student work at BMC with spontaneous pleasure. . . . While
viewing a painting of a landscape with road, Mme. Chareau spoke
of the “tenderness of the telegraph poles”; she also made the fitting
remark about the “combination of poverty and luxury” that could
be observed here. . . . I played a little recital at the North Lodge,
where this group followed me. Meanwhile, Nina, who yesterday
had an almost sensational success in the Fourth Brandenburg Con-
certo .. . has probably found a picnic place either in a cow pasture
or on a mountaintop, with some of her friends and partners from
yesterday’s performance. While writing, I hear a rehearsal for the
Art of the Fugue nearby, led by the tireless Bodky.
     The picture of BMC would be incomplete if one forgot the
mushrooms, which grow between the grasses and the rotting wood
around the lake in so many varieties that they are difficult to take in
ata glance....
      Quotation from E. R. Bentley, Partisan Review, 1945, #3: “In this




162                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 191 ---
setting, which may be represented as idyllic or barbarous, informal
or messy, refreshing or inconvenient,      Arcadian   or Bohemian,    ac-
cording to the predilections of the critic, BMC worked out its own
scheme of education.”
    The alchemy of artistic experiments. In medieval alchemy
some important discoveries were made as a result of playful or
imaginative experiments. Something similar might conceivably be
possible in the area of constructions and essays in form. Much of
this may lead to nothing, yet a “nothing” can occasionally lead to
much. For the aesthetic eye, the students produce astonishing
things: a small world of sensations, stimuli, illusions, fascinations,
using all sorts of materials: old foliage, coal, leather, bottle caps,
matches, cereals, fur, mildew, wire, eggshells, soap flakes. There
are unlimited possibilities. Still-lifes 4 la Braque are created. A feel-
ing for matter is to be awakened. The expression “matiére” is used.
Sometimes the result shimmers like the gold of ancient necroman-
cers.


Final Wednesday.
Morning: Jalo and Bodky perform their Schénberg (Op.7) on two
pianos. Afternoon: Art of the Fugue (strings). Evening: Albers’s
slides. —Rain.   New York... We had already touched the solid
ground of Manhattan in Pennsylvania Station and were sitting in a
taxi, when Nina suddenly stretched her head far out of the window
and whistled. “What are you whistling for?” “I’m whistling at Mike
... Michael Kuttner, second violin of the Gordon String Quartet.”

Angelica (Bodky) Lee
                             (Resident 1945 SS; Student 1947-50 SS)


                                               A Flautist’s View



Since I was only about sixteen when my parents, Erwin and Lilly
Bodky, were at BMC, my life was more or less peripheral to events
and College activities. Such as:
     Weeding in the vegetable garden on the farm, crouching be-
tween plants, somewhat       stupified by the hot sun, when suddenly
there was a pair of snow white sneakers on the red dusty soil. My
gaze followed the sharp vertical crease of freshly pressed jeans to a
white shirt, and up towards a wide friendly smile, as Andy Oates, a
fellow New Englander, introduced himself. This instant friendship
has survived as the red soil penetrated our clothes and became part
of our daily experiences.
     In the summer it rained every afternoon—a good time for a
nap. One day there was a real mountain deluge. My mother shook
me awake. “The creek is over its banks.” And we rushed out of the
house. A human chain was quickly formed to get the cook and fam-
ily out of their cabin, with water up to our knees. Luckily the wa-
ters soon subsided, but the cook’s clothes were ruined, and we col-
lected donations for them.
      Snakes   were   a   constant   menace,   lurking   near   furnaces,
stretched across walks looking like sticks, and even on the stairs of
the Studies Building. Roland Hayes, walking with my parents,
casually picked up a rock and with deadly accurate aim threw it
into a tall clump of grass, behind which a rattler lay coiled up,
which only Roland’s sharp eyes had seen. The cook skinned and
prepared it for Dad’s supper. “Tastes like chicken.”
     Some beautiful quiet moments were to be found with Hazel
Larsen in her room where she spent much of her time due to-her
polio paralysis. Hazel always had a ready ear for problems, a great
deal of sensitivity and insight. I can still see her in my mind’s eye,
standing hot and dusty, braced by her crutches, Leica pressed to
the eye for a memorable shot.


164                       Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 193 ---
Some of the most memorable hikes I went on were with Max
Dehn to the “Garden of Eden,” the steep ascent to the ridge behind
the College. The path went near a little waterfall where beautiful
red flowers grew, and chanterelle     mushrooms    (harvested   for my
father). Little orchids grew in fairy-tale settings. Dehn had a vast
knowledge of trees and plants besides his field of mathematics—a
very generous man. Black angus cattle were grazing on the upland
pasture, and The View—an unforgettable climax.
     One summer night there was a powerful moon that floodlit the
valley and silhouetted the mountains darkly against the sky. There
was so much energy in the air, one simply could not go to bed. A
group of us, including Judy Davidoff, walked down the dusty dirt
road deeper into the valley. The moon cast our shadows and farm
dogs alarmed our presence in the distance. Eventually we came to
“the Waterfall” with its slippery rocky pool below. “Let’s go
skinnnydipping!” 1” And so all inhibitions aside, we happily splashed
in. The water was freezing cold, exhilarating, and the bravest stood
directly under the fall, whilst the rest of us paddled our eerie-white
bodies about. Teeth chattering, we climbed out and pranced about
to dry off, struggling hastily with our clothes. We walked on for
miles, gradually warming up as the road circled the valley, finally
returning at dawn.
     Every morning I woke up to a “swish-swish.” David Stetzel
was sweeping the gathering area and the steps to the dining hall for
a new day. Later the cranky truck was started to fetch milk and
eggs from the farm. Peter Nemenyi was already in the kitchen grat-
ing potatoes into a bowl for “Kartoffelpuffer” (potato pancakes)—
his generous contribution to our culinary delights. In this way
many volunteers cheerfully set tables, cleared away chairs after a
concert, shucked corn, plucked chickens, and helped with chores in
the kitchen on extra-busy nights. One mystery remains: how is it
we only could get steaks if we went on mountain hikes? Of course
it was war time. ...
     The Summer Music Festival Concerts on Saturday nights were
very festive occasions, as the College presented some of Europe’s
and America’s    finest to the students, faculty, and visitors from
Asheville and outlying communities. Everyone even dressed up a
bit. Some concerts were exceptionally moving, as Carol Brice in
Brahms’s “Vier Ernste Gesange,” and Dad and Eva Heinitz in

An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                  165

--- Page 194 ---
View of College from the upper pasture, looking to southeast. Light speck on far
mountain side is Lee Hall. Lake Eden campus in foreground,   with the north fork
of the Swannanoa River and North Fork Road running just above the lake.

                                Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 195 ---
Beethoven’s A-major ‘cello and Piano Sonata—where you could
have heard a pin drop in the silences between movements. What
made some of the summers I spent at BMC so memorable was the
depth and grandeur of the lectures and Saturday evening concerts,
by some of the greatest artists in their fields, and ina truly paradisi-
acal setting. Joseph Marx gave wonderful coaching sessions for
small   woodwind    ensembles,      and   with   Frances   Drinker   (flute),
Emanuel Zetlin and Trudi Straus (violins), and Eva Heinitz (‘cello)
gave us all great chamber music evenings.




                                    Howard            Rondthaler
                                                      (Student 1945-48)


                                          Now It Can Be Told



I can tell you for sure that I will not write 1,000 words. And you can
tell Dawes Green that it was not “Mush Day” but “Meatless Tues-
day.” I recall a meal of soaked wheat prepared by Ann Treichler,
called “Wheat Bubbles.” Willie Joseph did not eat it.
     Peek’s Tavern was near the town of Black Mountain. It may
have been called Brown’s at some time. I’ve heard tell that Stephen
Forbes [a student at BMC] bought a tavern. In an issue of the Satur-
day Review in about 1978 there was a photograph entitled “Peek’s
Tavern.” It was obviously intended to be a work of art, but there
was nothing along with it to explain the place or the artist. The
photo depicted ruins with fallen walls and grass and ferns growing
betwixt the remains. In 1985, my sister, Katherine, and I revisited
Black Mountain. I wish I’d saved this photo, for I drove by the place
where Peek’s Tavern once was, and everything, ruins and all, had
been cleared out. It was just an area of grass. It looked nice, but I
couldn’t believe it was all gone.
    Jake was the Rondthaler dog. When the Rondthalers left BMC,




An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                       167

--- Page 196 ---
Jake went along with them to Ocracoke Island, where he continued
to chase airplanes and their shadows on the wide beaches of the
Outer Banks of North Carolina.      Eventually Jake died, but before
this happened Jake visited upstate with his owners and got lost.
The Rondthalers put ads in newspapers in an attempt to find the
dog. After about two months, someone responded, saying he had
Jake. Alice and Theodore drove up from Ocracoke to pick up their
long-lost dog. When    they called, Jake didn’t pay much      attention,
but did come over to take a sniff. Jake looked like Jake, so Alice and
Theodore took him back to Ocracoke. But he no longer chased air-
planes, so no one was sure whether Jake was really Jake.
      I operated the bookstore, which sold mostly candy and ciga-
rettes. It was in the dining hall where we had our dances and where
concerts were given. Once I was working in the store while it was
closed to buyers. I might have been taking inventory. Eddie Low-
insky and Charlotte (“Bimbus”) Schlesinger, and perhaps Erwin
Bodky came in to practice for a concert. I remember grand pianos
and harpsichords and a grand rehearsal. When the practice was
done, I recall hearing about eight bars of boogie-woogie. At just
that time I walked out of the bookstore and locked it up. Eddie
Lowinsky ran over to me and said, “Don’t tell anyone about this.”
     To this day I’ve not told anyone, but I think it’s time to tell the
truth. Boogie-woogie on a grand piano and a harpsichord is hard to
beat, especially when played by Lowinsky, et al., at BMC, and
heard from a secret hiding-place: the bookstore.

--- Page 197 ---
Hannelore Hahn
                                                     (Student    1945-47)


                  Excerpts from a Work in Progress



It was the permissive openness that spoke to me most. Anyone
could sit around the seminar table and participate in discussions
with the great minds of renowned persons. No one said you could
not take part because you lacked some previous requirement. Once
you had arrived, you were part of the community in every way. It
was up to you how much or how little you wished to participate.
The majority of the ninety students were considerably older than I.
Some had been married and were starting life anew. Many had
been to war and were returning as students on the GI Bill. I felt like
a mouse amongst the biggies. Although I was not yet ready to dis-
course, the sessions at the seminar tables were not wasted. I learned
and saw with my own eyes that the passionate pursuit of ideas and
meaning was life-fulfilling. Just bearing witness seeded my life for
years to come.    And   then there were the interludes, those super-
blessed seven days during each semester when all classes were sus-
pended and we became immersed in the exercise of the serious and
creative use of leisure time. That was the proof of the pudding.
Everyone easily shifted gears to projects of their own choosing
which had been emerging all along, but for which there had not
been sufficient time. It was a pleasure to dig in and get to it. Even
the mouse knew what to do... .
     The College without locks and keys, without prerequisites and
administrative regulations, was also like an open dish in the uni-
verse. It allowed ideas, like particles or spores, to fall into it, to de-
velop like organisms according to their nature, and to attract and to
repel. No individual particle, no individual thought or point of
view was to infringe upon or take away freedom from the others.
This was Black Mountain’s great attraction, but also its Achilles
heel. Spores of dissension germinated in the BMC bell jar, and after
a period of time, usually two years, a point would be reached when



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                     169

--- Page 198 ---
opposing concepts, their protagonists and their followers, could
not coexist. One faction would       then make its exodus, leaving the
other behind as the drained victor. This rhythmic reoccurrence was
in the nature of the College and was called a “split.”
      But the creative use of time, self-directed, self-initiated, is a life-
long blessing. Perhaps BMC already attracted those who were self-
directed and did not have to teach this lesson. It is hard to say. But
boredom,     the great bane of existence elsewhere, in areas of work
where initiative goes unencouraged and where, as in government,
one is under other people’s direction, did not exist at Black Moun-
tain. Nor did it spawn the type of behavior typical of other schools
and in the army, where one yearns for school to be out in order to
loaf or go on binges. School was never out at Black Mountain, nor
thereafter. I consider this to have been one of its greatest lessons.




                                               M. C. Richards
                                                       (Faculty 1945-51)


                     Excerpts from “Black Mountain
                               College:          A Golden Seed”



Isn’t it interesting that the muddle of one’s personal search turns
into human history of others! Doesn’t it feel substantial that what
we choose to do individually makes a supportive fabric for others
in due course. The interweavings of time, past, present, and fu-
ture—the interweavings of personal imagination and community
endeavor—this spiritual cloth, this gold of our life-looms is being
woven whether we sleep or wake, a garment we are making for
each other and the planet. Or so it seems. Though it be beaten thin


From Craft Horizons [now American Craft] (published by the American
Craft Council), June 1977. Reprinted by permission of the author and
the publisher.


170                      Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 199 ---
by the impact of experiences, still the gold of our lives keeps its na-
ture even when worn through, even when dust, gold dust.
    This is the way it strikes me this morning as I begin again to
respond to Craft Horizons’ request for a memory piece on BMC. The
College no longer exists on the physical plane. It was pulverized by
poverty, fatigue, and legal challenge. Yet how         its dust flies and
shines in the inner eye 20 years later. How its form resurrects and
prophesies, like a hologram, visible through all physical and emo-
tional disasters. That’s what the alchemists said long ago, that spiri-
tual gold is incorruptible. . . .
     Black Mountain is inviting more interest and attention now
than it did then. Obviously it is still alive in spirit and still doing its
work. Charles Olson, the poet, who was College Rector during its
last half-dozen years, said Black Mountain was “a spirit place,” and
it was important to keep the faith with it even if only one person
were left to mind the light in a cave on the hillside up to the Pas-
ture. Times changed. It wasn’t so easy to get faculty to come and
teach for almost no salary and students to learn without grade
points. The distinguished European refugees who had found crea-
tive opportunity at the College were moving on. The New Deal was
over. The GI Bill ran out....
     The legend of Black Mountain, as it is currently told, gathers
round the names of people who were there. For such a small place,
it seems   remarkable that Robert Rauschenberg,        Kenneth Noland,
Arthur Penn, Ruth Asawa, Dorothy Ruddick, James Leo Herlihy,
Fielding Dawson, José Yglesias, Francine du Plessix Gray were stu-
dents there. Or that Josef Albers, Anni Albers, Trude Guermon-
prez, Ben Shahn, Peter Voulkos, Eric Bentley, Robert Creeley, John
Cage, Merce Cunningham, Buckminster Fuller were teachers, some
for long periods, some briefly. But even the short stays—like
Voulkos for three weeks in 1953, Fuller for the summer            of 1948,
Franz Kline for a term—made an indelible impact on person and
school. . . .
     I was drawn to Black Mountain because of its wholistic ap-
proach, its informality, lack of conventional institutionalism, and
its earnestness. I liked the combination of intimacy, spontaneity,
avant-garde, and seriousness, high standards, and commitment.
We were making something new together. There was no one to



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                      17]

--- Page 200 ---
blame for mess-ups but ourselves. I liked the strong personalities
and vivid talents, the residential community       taking its meals and
recreation together. It was a place for growth and tempering. When
my life and the College fell apart, I took it very hard. I used to say,
if we can’t do it here where we are free to do as we wish, where can
it be done? A good question! I felt we should be wiser than we
were, more    able to keep our College together through thick and
thin. I was critical of the gap between our ideals and our perform-
ance, our creativity so far from human perfection. Now I am able to
praise the Lord that we did as well as we did, lasted as long as we
lasted, sowed the seeds we sowed!
       The paradox of openness and assumptions was a source of
conflict. The College appeared to be “open.” If you pressed hard,
you would discover boundaries. As I see it now, the boundaries
were    simply the forms   of each other’s being. But then as now,
people were willing to sacrifice each other for ideology. The Col-
lege split apart time and again over non-negotiable principles. Indi-
viduals would rather be without a college than without what they
considered their integrity. Anni Albers always urged that when
conflict arose between two positions, we continue to work together
to discover a third! It was similar to the Quaker approach of no de-
cision without unanimity. But this would mean yielding on both
sides, making boundaries permeable. Idealists find this hard to do.
So the College flourished and suffered and bled and pressed on
creatively for 23 years. The Alberses left in 1949, after 15 years. I left
in 1951 but returned every year until the end to participate in the
summer programs and to give support to Olson, whom I-had per-
suaded to leave Yucatan and come to BMC.
     For six years I taught courses in creative reading and writing. I
put my poems into the folder with student work for class discus-
sion. We found a stash of type in an old building and gradually
unscrambled and cleaned it. Anais Nin, who was printing her own
books in NYC, came to help us set up our print shop and to talk to
writing students. They printed their own works, college announce-
ments and programs, the first issue of The Black Mountain Review.
My favorite concert program was printed by Tommy Jackson on
cigarette paper rolled around tobacco—we smoked our programs
after the show. I learned woodworking with Molly Gregory, dance



172                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 201 ---
with Betty Jennerjahn, translated and produced Cocteau’s Marriage
on the Eiffel Tower, also directed Yeats’s The Death of Cuckalain, a
unique production we performed underneath the Studies Building,
choreographing for stairways and graveled terrace. Original music
by Joseph Fiore and Mark Hedden. In 1948, when Fuller came for
the summer to build a geodesic dome with his students from the
Chicago Institute of Design (poet Jonathan Williams among them!),
Cage was there to do a festival of Erik Satie’s music. He asked me
to translate Satie’s Le Piége de Meduse (The Ruse of Medusa). Penn
directed it, with Fuller in the lead as Baron Medusa, Elaine de
Kooning playing his daughter, and Cunningham dancing the inter-
ludes of the mechanical monkey, accompanied by Cage at the pi-
ano.
    In the summer of 1951, David Tudor came as accompanist to
dancer Katherine Litz. He gave piano concerts of works by Anton
von Webern, Arnold Schénberg, Cage, Lou Harrison, Stefan Wolpe,
and BMC student composers. He was preparing a first performance
of Pierre Boulez’s second piano sonata for New York in the fall. I
went along. Through Tudor, I came to translate Antonin Artaud’s
The Theater and Its Double, which helped to create the atmosphere
for what is now called “the first happening,” composed by Cage
and performed by many of us at BMC. ...
    [John Andrew    Rice, BMC’s   founder]   knew   that the human
mind has more than one mode, that indeed it has an “ecology” of
being. He knew that imagination, intuition, inspiration are basic to
psyche. He knew how artistic process tends to be separated from
thinking and social behavior in our education. . .
     A philosophy of education began to form: imagination as fun-
damental to all learning; artistic making as a model of integrating
vision, materials, structure, and imagery. The Alberses brought to
the College tremendous strength of disciplined work and high
standards and sustained focus. They initiated the Summer Art In-
stitutes for art teachers and the success of these gradually drew to
the summer faculty such artists as Richard Lippold, Esteban Vin-
cente, Leo Amino, Robert Motherwell, Willem de Kooning. Sum-
mer Music Institutes also flourished and distinguished musicolo-
gists, instrumentalists, and composers   came. Theater was strong,
dance began to strengthen during the years I was there. Creative



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                               173

--- Page 202 ---
writing drew summer     faculty of Paul Goodman,     Isaac Rosenfeld,
Clement Greenberg. Aaron Siskind and Harry Callahan came for a
summer institute in photography. Guest teachers, like regular fac-
ulty, received no fee, only a modest travel allowance plus board
and room....
     In 1950, Robert Turner came with a kiln builder to set up a pot-
tery studio on the far side of the lake beyond the Studies Building:
two or three wheels, an oil-burning and an electric kiln, table space
in the middle, ware shelves, an alcove for materials, and a view of
hayfields and mountains. Turner was my first teacher at Black
Mountain in the potter’s art. He taught us to make a mold and to
cast a simple form, which we decorated in different ways. I made
“tumblers” and learned to trail and sgraffito and inlay. I was cross-
ing a threshold into a different language, though I didn’t realize it
consciously. At the time I was only beside myself with amazement
and longing. Turner taught me to center the clay. I thought if I
could do it and learn to throw off the hump, I would die of joy.
Turner was only potter I ever knew who would throw a pot on the
wheel, study it slowly, put it in the damp box, and then back on the
wheel the next day to throw it some more. He took his time. This
has been an important guiding image for me through the years: to
take time, making the contact deep and personal, low key, attuned,
listening to the breathing of the form, to what the clay is saying.
      After Turner left, Karen Karnes and David Weinrib came to be
potters in residence. They taught and also produced ware, which
they sold in Asheville. In 1953, we held a Summer Pottery Institute:
Warren MacKenzie, Voulkos, Daniel Rhodes, each came: for three
weeks. . . . | turned more surely into the curving spiral where divi-
sions between verbal and nonverbal arts are dissolved—found my
way more surely into that artistic center that is everywhere, radiat-
ing from the inside into all creation and destruction. MacKenzie
was passionate about the rhythms of throwing and trimming.
Rhodes warmly and generously introduced us to glaze formula-
tion, the development of forms on the wheel, texturing, and hand-
building. Voulkos taught us to remember “there are always more
where these come from: and “to work with the clay till it collapses.”
What a supportive atmosphere to grow and change in! What last-
ing friendships made! . . .



174                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 203 ---
Why did Black Mountain fail?” I am asked. Fail? It didn’t fail!
It lived its life passionately and earnestly; and because it was alive
and not artificially preserved, it ceased in due course to exist as a
body. How vividly it is living now in the imagination of persons!
    Apparently it is refreshing to hear that we don’t have to be
large, rich, funded, well known, successful in order to inspire and
to endure. In a society that promotes values of status, money, pro-
duction, fame, we like to be told the legend of a college where the
arts were central to its life, a college that was poor, confused, small,
off-beat, and a college that drew creative people to it for no other
reason than that they wanted to be there. A spirit drew them. We
don’t like to hear the part about how it suffered schism, personal
tragedy, and early demise.     But without    this shadow,   its light is
falsified.
     When people ask me whether there can be another Black
Mountain, I find myself laughing a little bit and with some pain. I
wonder whether they understand what is involved in living so
close to dream, as we did there. If they do, then wherever a few
individuals are gathered together in courageous creativity, ready to
initiate a common undertaking, paying for freedom with voluntary
poverty, integrating art with thinking and community work, there
may be another epiphany of whatever it was that shone in the face
of Black Mountain College.

                                                               Three Poems



Organization is not interesting, why

Organization   is not interesting, why.

If   |am the chairman and you are on my committee it is not very interesting.

Or if you are the leader and we are your group and stay so who isn't bored.

Organization is all right while it is new until you thoroughly understand
what is expected of you and then what is expected of you
is soon no longer interesting.

It gets to be like a minuvet or a masque that isn't play any longer but
the whole show.

Some people like to know what is expected of them and what
the consequences will be but not me.

Some like to set themselves goals and be clear about aims so that they can
formulate standards and have an institution to which they can feel they
belong

But | don't want to belong to an institution.

Organization   is not interesting but sometimes   necessary   to get things done
if there is something that simply must get done and better done wrong
than not done at all, are there such things maybe.

Governments are well organized but the people in the governments
do they see it all so clearly, who makes the policy and is he well organized
with the right leadership and support or is he a house divided.

Organization and institutions and government are not interesting to me
and perhaps that's not important

But | see more and more people expecting from them whatever they are
benefits he would not expect from any of the men composing them,
| mean what'rare bliss will organized humanity bestow that a man will
not give his brother.

When | was a child | liked to be a member of a gang and have a leader
and a code and meetings and by-laws and a constitution and a declared
purpose and a stern loyalty to our side. What was good was that this
public life instructed me privately in the possibilities of attitudes.
But as | grew and filled the rooms*inside me | found the converse true:
public life became a mirror. And so to play with governments
is to toy with mirrors rather than with men.

Will my bold anarchic dream come true and we will govern ourselves
unprofessionally, mirroring as universe our hearts’ terrain.
The private world is where men meet. It is much more interesting
not to say what you do not know, much more interesting to look
than to see what is not there.

Love Sonnet—Christmas            1950
      Love clapped his hands he surely did and laughed
      To feel the breast, the soft blue wool, to drink
      In milky streams that run from heart to lips,
      To wake and feel his form a man’s. I think
      The cow and the ass both lowed and brayed,
      And the magi bowed and the shepherds prayed
      And the angels sang. The birds flew in
      And out the eaves, looking sideways at Love
      And blinking, and jumped into space and swam.
      And carillons played and the saints gave in
      And the world was rocking with magic waves
      That spill at our feet. O my love, my love...
      This time of our year he is ours to bear,
      The infant God, and we are his heirs.
                               M.C. Richards




                                        Albert William Levi
                                                    (Faculty 1945—50)


                    The Meaning of Black Mountain



On the occasion of the tercentenary celebration of the founding of
Harvard University some years ago, Alfred North Whitehead re-
marked, “About twenty-five years for a man and about three
hundred years for a university are the periods required for the at-
tainment of mature stature.” For those of us who are here this
morning I am sure that it must be discouraging to realize that we
shall have to wait with considerable patience for the next two


This address was given to the entire BMC community during opening
exercises, Fall 1947.



178                     Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 207 ---
hundred and eighty-six years before Black Mountain College
comes of age. Since I am naturally an impatient man, I have no in-
tention of doing this.
      Instead, I should like to say a few things about this rowdy
brawling infant which was ripped so timely from the womb of
Rollins College fourteen years ago. Of course one cannot carry
Whitehead’s analogy too far. There is certainly only the most tenu-
ous relation between chronological age and inner maturity. And I
have for a long time suspected that the larger and older universities
are suffering not from maturity but from senility. The real vitality
of any thrust into new ways of living and being seems tragically
limited to the generation of the founders and shortly thereafter.
Dynastic houses, the early Christians, and the early Communists
seem to say as much. It seems almost contradictory to speak of the
life of an institution. New ventures live only until they become insti-
tutions. It is at that point that hardening of the arteries sets in, that
primitive adventure has become mere routine, that former energy
has turned into living death.
    “Youth,”   said George    Bernard   Shaw,   “is such a wonderful
thing—it’s a shame that it is wasted on young people.” The youth-
fulness of Black Mountain College is its first asset. It is our job to
explore its meaning in order that we shall not waste it.
     The first expression of the youthfulness of Black Mountain lies
in the fact that it is an experimental college. But experimental in
what sense? We are certainly not so naive as to believe that the
method of education and the method of the natural sciences [are]
the same—that experiments in human living are subject to the same
techniques of calculation and control as the material of the physical
or biological laboratory. And yet Black Mountain is an experiment
in education. It is an experiment in that its future form cannot be
given in advance. It is an experiment in that it is free to submit to
constant formation and re-formation. It is an experiment in that it is
itself not so much an answer as a question.
      And being itself a question it can afford to ask questions. How
can education be made a process of joint participation rather than
the passive acceptance of persuasive dogma? How can the irrele-
vant formalities of differences in age and status be overcome
through the magic of the relations between persons and the miracle



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                    179

--- Page 208 ---
of real communication? What ways of living lead to the stimulation
 of the mind, to the candid development of the feelings, to the slow
 growth of self-knowledge?
      These are perhaps the major questions which we at Black
 Mountain ask and I suspect that dimly and falteringly they are
 questions which lie behind the educational process wherever it
 blossoms and flourishes. But we ask other questions which are
 more uniquely our own. What is the role of close community living
 in the transformation of the human person? In what ways does the
 performance of manual labor and even the more menial tasks of
 daily living contribute to the acquisition of fraternity, cheerfulness,
 and patience? How can the phony Renaissance distinction between
 the fine and useful arts, and the even phonier nineteenth-century
 distinction between the artist as bohemian and the rest of society as
 stupid bourgeoisie, be overcome so that the practice of and famili-
 arity with the arts become not one of the gratuitous accessories of
 life but an indispensable part of its living center?
       And finally, there are other questions so naive, so simple and
 so unsophisticated that it is doubtful if they can be raised anywhere
 else. How large ought an educational community to be? And, how
 long should a student remain a part of one?
       Ten years ago while walking home from the golf course one
 day I fell into casual conversation with the dean of the New Eng-
 land college at which I was then teaching. “I wonder,” I said specu-
 latively, “how long a college education ought to be.” “Why,” he
 said, “exactly what it is—four years.” “But why,” I asked, “why
 exactly four years?” “I have been at the college for longer than you
 have been alive,” he said, “and it has always been four years.” “But
 do you know exactly why?” The dean looked at me with some an-
 noyance. “I wonder if you know, young man,” he said sternly,
 “that it is just questions like that which undermine the soundness
 of American education!”
      If the good dean was indeed right, and it is just questions like
 that which undermine the soundness of American education, Black
 Mountain has much to answer for. For we do from time to time ask
 ourselves: how long should students remain a part of our commu-
 nity? We are far from agreed among ourselves as to the answer, but




~ 180                  Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 209 ---
we do seem to agree that the question must be raised and answered
anew for each individual student. Some find that they have learned
the lesson of Black Mountain after two years; others leave with the
greatest reluctance after six. No two cases are alike, and we are
pretty well content that it should be so. In this sense too we are an
experimental college.
    How large ought an educational community to be? Two thou-
sand three hundred years ago Plato in the latest and ripest of all his
works—the Laws—raised that same question for the ideal commu-
nity. Plato’s answer, that the commonwealth should contain exactly
5,040 households and his reason for this, that the number 5,040 is
highly admirable because it is divisible by all the integers from one
to ten, has been the source of considerable merriment to political
philosophers since his time. I could show that even Plato’s answer
was highly reasonable by the standards of Greek culture but, disre-
garding his answer, it is clear to me that he was asking an emi-
nently reasonable question. Last year the educational community
of New   York University numbered     35,708, of City College 27,430,
of the University of Minnesota 18,594. I am afraid that these num-
bers do not have even Plato’s reasoning to recommend them that
they are divisible by all the integers from one to ten.
     The fact is that New York University, City College, and the
University of Minnesota and most other colleges simply cannot af-
ford to ask the question: how large ought an educational commu-
nity to be? Their gigantic plants, their enormous overhead costs,
their top-heavy administrative machinery automatically answer
the question for them.
     The Empire State Building is a tremendous building. During
the last depression I have heard that its rooms were practically
unoccupied above the thirty-seventh floor. One of the problems
which this brought to the management was that of plumbing.
Plumbing deteriorates if it is not used. Consequently the Empire
State Building employed two men full time simply to go from room
to room flushing the toilets from the thirty-seventh floor up. If the
trustees of Yale University should suddenly decide that educa-
tional effectiveness demanded that the student body of Yale should
be reduced to 250, I have already been at some pains to calculate for




An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                 18]

--- Page 210 ---
their benefit that they should have to employ not two but three men
just to flush the toilets in the empty buildings between Yale Station
and Chapel Street.
     So you can see that Yale University cannot afford to ask the
question: how large ought an educational community to be? For it
is one more example of the truth of that ironic motto which Scott
Fitzgerald placed at the beginning of one of his novels—’to the
spoils belongs the victor.” We at Black Mountain are more fortu-
nate. And our good fortune is probably due partly to our youth and
partly to our poverty. For we have no generous endowment, no sol-
idly established board of trustees, no magnificent plant to cloud
our vision and persuade us that the heart of education is anything
other than that kind of personal growth which inherently depends
upon the intimacy of the educational environment and the vitality
and sincerity of our human relations within it.
     And yet it is only fair to say that our good fortune in this re-
spect is only precariously maintained. For we are flying in the face
of a trend which has increasingly pervaded American education
during the past twenty years—a trend which in the phrase of the
late Justice Brandeis I can only call “the curse of bigness.” It is the
obsessive urge to expand, to prosper and grow large at all costs, to
achieve quantity at the expense of quality, to attain stature even at
the sacrifice of excellence. It is difficult to protect an educational
community from overexpansion, to stand apart from the spiral of
educational inflation. But at Black Mountain this is precisely what
we are trying to do. And in this effort we fall back upon the same
principle which animates the most advanced thought in‘ the fields
of city planning and monopoly control. If a small business firm
finds that its correspondence is too large to be handled by a single
typewriter, it does not thereupon order a typewriter to be built
twice the size of the one which it already possesses; it buys a sec-
ond typewriter like the first. For there is an optimum size for type-
writers fixed by long trial and experiment.
     Just so the implication of the necessity for modern mass educa-
tion (a necessity which is undeniable) is not to increase the size of
units beyond maximum      effectiveness, but to increase the number
of units best fitted to do the job. But how large ought an educa-
tional community to be? Here again we at Black Mountain are not



182                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 211 ---
completely sure. Year before last we had an average of sixty-five
students, last year our average was eighty-five, this year it will
probably closer to ninety. Is this too large? Will classes be un-
wieldy, will the dining hall be impossibly noisy and crowded, will
all community members know one another well? It is clearly now
too early to tell, although I confess I have deep misgivings, but we
shall watch the results with care and test them constantly against
the stern requirements of our educational ideal. In this sense too we
are an experimental college.
     You have probably noticed that in speaking of Black Mountain
I have sometimes called it an experimental college and sometimes
referred to it as an educational community. This is because in my
own mind the two mean very much the same thing. But it is cer-
tainly only right to tell those of you who are new and to remind
those of you who are old here that this has been by no means a
matter   of universal   agreement   among   us, faculty and   students
alike. During the past two years there has been much earnest, soul-
searching, but I think superficial talk as to whether Black Mountain
is a college or whether Black Mountain is a community. Last spring
two members of our faculty resigned within a week of one another,
and I think it is completely fair to say that the chief reason which
one gave was that Black Mountain was too much of a college and
not enough    a community,     and the chief reason   which   the other
gave was that Black Mountain was too much a community and not
enough a college. Before the close of the summer both of the faculty
members in question had reconsidered and had asked permission
to withdraw their resignations, permission which was of course
cheerfully granted. In these requests there was certainly expressed
expediential adaptation, but I think it would be a mistake to believe
that that was all that was expressed. For it showed also that college
and community are by no means mutually exclusive ideas, that in
fact it is especially appropriate that they should be combined, and
that those who work for group cooperation and those who work for
intellectual and cultural excellence can remain partners in the edu-
cational enterprise.
     In fact it is the particular genius of Black Mountain that it is
both an experimental college and an educational community. We
are just beginning to discover the multiple functions which com-



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                  183

--- Page 212 ---
munity serves in the process of general education. But this in turn
does not mean that community or even the ability to live in a small
community is an end in itself. It is, as Aristotle showed long ago,
the grossest of errors to separate the intellectual and the social vir-
tues. The aim of Black Mountain is not to narrowly choose between
them, but to cultivate them both.
      The reason, of course, why it is fatal to separate the intellectual
and the social virtues is because both are perfections appropriate to
the human individual. This suggests that any educational program
must depend for its rationale upon a theory of individuality and of
the human person. Much has been said elsewhere about that other
unique feature of Black Mountain—that it attempts to create an
educational atmosphere which places about equal emphasis upon
academic studies, manual work, and creative labor in the arts. I do
not know enough about the early days of the College to be sure
whether this was part of its idea from the beginning or whether it
evolved in the course of time, but I do believe that it may be se-
curely grounded in a theory of the human person.
     The self—like any object (which I hasten to add the self is
not)—exists in space and time, although it is not a physical but a
psychological space and time. And I think it follows and is a fact by
common experience that consequently the self has four dimensions.
It can look backward and it can look forward in its own peculiar
time. And it can look inward and it can look outward in its own
peculiar space.
    If education is, as I believe, the attempt to promote the growth
and development of the self in ways which are admirable, then it
must address itself in all sincerity to these four dimensions of self-
hood. It must devote itself to and must attempt to service man’s
inwardness and his outwardness. It must cultivate and serve his
backward-looking and his forward-looking.
     Our program of community work and indeed most of the
shared aspects of our community life together is an attempt to cul-
tivate the self in its outwardness. Here the emphasis is upon com-
mon action, social cooperation, and our responsibility to the-total
group. Sometime it must almost seem as if this type of activity may
lead to a kind of self-transcendence, but deeply considered and in
its most real sense, I do not think that this is ever the case. Such ac-




184                     Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 213 ---
tivity always remains the undreamed-of limit of expansion of the
self in its dimension of outwardness.
     Our general program of academic work including all of its ac-
cessories of intellect and émotion is an attempt to cultivate the self
in its backward-looking. I would not like to be misunderstood on
this point. I am not using “backward-looking” in its invidious sense
as primitive or as unprogressive. Nor do I mean it in its literal his-
torical sense either. I only mean that our engagement with the natu-
ral sciences and psychology, with languages and history, with lit-
erature and the social studies is with material which is our heritage,
which     has been   established,   which   has in a sense   already been
made secure. To assimilate the given to mind and understanding is
of the greatest importance—nor am | unmindful that what is given
must     also be taken,   and   that at a certain   point the backward-
looking almost imperceptibly undergoes a sea-change into the for-
ward-looking. Here, too, sometimes the preoccupation with learn-
ing and the immersion in the depths of knowledge seems to lead to
its own kind of self-transcendence. But again, I do not think that
really this is ever the case. Such preoccupation always remains the
undreamed-of limit of expansion of the self in its dimension of
backward-looking.
       Our program of creative work in the arts, which sees in the fine
arts something useful and in the useful arts something fine, is our
attempt to cultivate the self in its forward-looking. An act of real
creation is always a projection of the self into the future. It is not
only that here nothing is given and everything must be wrought, it
is also that to take responsibility for novel creation is to establish
for the self a position of extreme vulnerability. Whether in writing,
in painting, in the composition or creative performance of music, in
woodworking, in weaving, in printing, in sculpture or in drawing,
when we quote the words of others we find safety in their daring;
when we speak with our own voice we take all of the risks upon
ourselves. Here too it easily seems as if art and creation with their
passionate commitments lead to a level of self-transcendence. But
here again I do not think that this is really so. Real creation always
remains the undreamed-of limit of expansion of the self in its di-
mension of forward-looking.
    Up to now I have spoken about the four dimensions of the self,



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                     185

--- Page 214 ---
and I have somewhat casually related them to our educational pro-
gram at Black Mountain: its outwardness to our community life
and community work, its backward-looking to our academic stud-
ies, its forward-looking to our emphasis upon the arts. But I have
said nothing about the dimension of inwardness. Does this mean
that at Black Mountain the cultivation of the inwardness of the self
is not our concern? By no means! In fact, so much is the reverse the
case that I would almost say that the heart of all education lies in its
ability to promote the growth of the self in this dimension. For this
only means the realization of the self in its true identity and poten-
tiality, the achievement of insight, of self-knowledge—in a word, of
wisdom. And what single range of our community activities can be
entrusted with this task, what single subject from our curriculum
enjoys this coveted monopoly? Poetry? I am afraid not. Psychol-
ogy? Heaven forbid. Philosophy? Would that it were so.
    No, the inward growth of the self marks the successful prose-
cution of the educational process as a whole, and if at Black Moun-
tain we do not provide for it specifically in the formal structure of
our common     life, it is only because at bottom it is the whole raison
d’étre and meaning of that common life. It is the essence for which
all else serves as instrument and occasion.
      And here is where, I think, in the deepest sense of all, Black
Mountain is an experimental college. For it is a standing invitation,
even, if you will, a challenge to all who come to it, students and fac-
ulty alike, not only to change and re-form it but even more for them
to submit to the possibility of change. A long time ago a student
was talking to me about a faculty member who is no longer with
us. The student said: “I don’t know why he is at Black Mountain,
for in all the time he has been here, he has not changed us, nor has
he in the slightest changed himself.” That student, I think, under-
stood the meaning of Black Mountain.
     What Black Mountain uniquely provides is the opportunity for
all those who enter our educational community to investigate the
nature and the sources of their personal identity. There is no harder
task in all the world. And this is the reason why I must remind
those of you who are old, and warn those of you who are new that
to live in our community is so profoundly dangerous and so pro-
foundly uncomfortable. Increasingly in the world outside, with its



186                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 215 ---
bigness, with the formalization of its institutional structure, with its
clouds of anonymity, the individual self is protected against the
disclosure of itself to others and even to itself. What you have, what
your family is, what your offices are—engage the attention of your
associates and so by a kind of perverse imitation engage you like-
wise. Income, deference, status, become at once the coinage of so-
cial intercourse and the almost invulnerable defense against both
self-disclosure and self-knowledge.
     But at Black Mountain it is very difficult to be protected against
the exhibition of what we really are. Possessing and officiating do
not absolve us of the responsibility for being. And since many of
the little devices of defense which operate elsewhere cannot be so
easily employed—anonymity, personal charm, smoothness, bully-
ing, the imperturbable assumption of omniscience—we are often
left with having to live in the same house with the public exhibition
of our weaknesses. To continue to do so requires its own kind of
strength and its own kind of courage. And frankly, there are many
who do not have it, who can bear neither to live with their weak-
nesses nor to submit to the intolerable risk that they may be
changed.
     Black Mountain is subject to an enormous turnover of both stu-
dents and faculty. It would be foolish to deny it. And though I
know that there is much which we must change and improve here,
and that many of the customary rewards of teaching and learning
are not to be found; that we offer no college degree, that we are to-
tally uninterested in grades, that we are more than a little casual
about credits and transfer credits, that faculty salaries are meager,
that no one can become a full professor or chairman of the depart-
ment, that living conditions are primitive, still I do not honestly
believe that these are the real reasons for our turnover. I have come
tentatively to the conclusion that they lie deeper and that they are
not unrelated to what I have said just before.
     Perhaps there are two other matters which I ought to mention
here because they relate so closely to the danger and discomfort
which is the price we pay for our own particular brand of intimacy
at Black Mountain. They have to do respectively with the function
of criticism and the concept of order.
     One does not need to be a member of our community for a very



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                   187

--- Page 216 ---
long time before he discovers that we are utterly lacking in contem-
plative peace and that the most obvious quality of our life together
is its hectic intensity. If any of you are under the impression that
you have entered the ivory tower of serenity in full retreat from the
hurly-burly of the world, permit me to disabuse you here and now.
If Black Mountain is an ivory tower, it has not been constructed on
the model of a Tibetan monastery but upon that of Bedlam, that is,
a noisy madhouse in the heart of London.
     One of the areas in which our hectic intensity communicates
itself is in that of attitudes toward Black Mountain itself. It has in-
terested me a great deal that both in students who are now at the
college and in those who have left there never seems to be either an
attitude of indifference or of calm judiciousness. Either they love it
with a great love or they hate it with a great hatred, or more charac-
teristically with a fine ambivalence: they at the one and the same
time love it with a great love and hate it with a great hatred. I have
always been interested in exploring the sources of that hatred, and
I have come to believe that it is not unrelated to the conception of
criticism which prevails in our community.
     At Black Mountain everyone has strong opinions. And more-
over there is established a sort of tradition in which the expression
of these opinions may be aggressively pursued. As one student
said, “Hell, if I were at any other college, things would be run for
me, and I would expect to be told what to do and that would be OK
and I would never open my trap. But here they invite me to shoot
off my face and I take full advantage of it.” I am afraid that I cannot
do justice to the raciness of the original dialect, but I can assure you
that both of his last statements are true.
     But you must add to this two other facts. First, the ancient
mechanism of projection which enables us to evade the cruelties of
inner weakness and self-dissatisfaction by finding the ills to lie not
inside the self but outside in its surrounding environment. And sec-
ond, the illusions of the quest for social perfection which lead so
many of our students and perhaps faculty also to choose Black
Mountain rather than some other college. They read or hear ac-
counts of what is done here in a kind of final desperate hope that
here is one place where all doubts may be resolved, all personal
problems solved, where social utopia reigns supreme. They even



188                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 217 ---
read our own catalogs and publicity not wisely enough nor well
enough but through the lavender haze of their ultimate hopes
rather than through the perspective of their sense of reality. The
resultant disillusionment is fearful and wonderful to behold. And it
gives to their consequent criticism a peculiarly bitter twist.
     Again do not misunderstand me. I am not appealing for an end
of criticism. It and the freedom which it implies are the living stuff
of our common life together. But it is also clear that it is the func-
tion of criticism to be responsible. When criticism springs from a
simple love of criticism itself, it is less than serious and cannot be
taken seriously. When criticism springs from the unacknowledged
inadequacies of the self it is serious but is not directed toward its
proper object. When criticism is the result of a disillusionment
which is the violation of a sense of reality, it wants the understand-
ing which alone make criticism meaningful. But when criticism is
serious, when it does not spring from self-deception, when it is not
the violation but the very expression of the sense of reality, then it
is not only responsible, it is also indispensable. It is the only group
safeguard against the fallibility of its leaders and the irresponsibil-
ity to which the exercise of power itself is, | am afraid, always dis-
posed.
     If one of the most obvious qualities of our life together is its
hectic intensity, another is the almost complete casualness with
which we exist with one another. Community rules are few; public
discipline   is almost   nonexistent;   informality   characterizes   our
dress, our living arrangements, and our manners. But this very ca-
sualness can be a source of real misunderstanding if it is viewed
upon its most superficial level. For there are those who mistakenly
assume that this casualness is but the outer manifestation of an in-
ner indifference and that we have neither an interest in nor a con-
cern for the concept of order. Nothing is further from the truth. If
one seriously considered that the aim of our program of commu-
nity work is work well done, that the aim of our academic program
is understanding responsibly come by, that the aim of our program
in the arts is to instill the ambition for mature craftmanship and
impress the sense of harmony so deeply upon the personality that
it will find reexpression in all of the multiple areas of living, then it
would at once be evident that order, far from being a marginal con-



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                     189

--- Page 218 ---
cept, lies close to the heart of our educational enterprise. And   so
consequently, if work at Black Mountain is frequently uncompleted
and shoddy, if our group expectations are insufficiently structured,
and if the occasions of our social intercourse seem deficient in the
sense of form, it is not because this is the product of our consent.
Rather, I think it is the temporary price we pay for having commit-
ted ourselves to our own peculiar educational methods.
     These methods concern themselves less with symptoms than
with their underlying causes and consequently less with the exter-
nal manifestations of the disorderly than with the immaturities of
the self from which they spring. That we do not rigidly and dog-
matically impose standards from above is due not to our denial of
the concept of order, but from our faith that order must originate
within the self, and that such creative origination within the self
flourishes and grows best within an atmosphere of consent. For I
suspect, at the risk of complete misunderstanding, that the ultimate
ideal of our Black Mountain community is anarchy which, contrary
to all that is ordinarily claimed, would not be the most chaotic and
disorganized of societies but the most highly organized and the
most sensitively ordered precisely because its order and its organi-
zation would be but the reflection of the internal order and integra-
tion of each individual self.
     I have tried this morning to say a few things about Black
Mountain College: its youth, in what sense it is an experimental col-
lege, some of the questions which it asks, some of its ideas and as-
pirations which I have found meaningful and philosophically true.
I have said less about the general problem of education in the mod-
ern world because I believe that in the end this is simply the same
as the problem of the human individual in the modern world. The
theme song of the modern individual and consequently of modern
education might be summed up in the phrase—’caught in the
works.” At Black Mountain we are trying steadfastly to work to-
ward the sources of our freedom. Let me again welcome those of
you who are new to our community, and invite you, new and old
alike, to join in this common enterprise.
