Harry Weitzer
                                                   (Student 1945-48)

                                  Anything Was Possible



When | arrived the first year, there were so many things of interest
going on that I neglected to secure any furniture for my study.
When I got to it, there was none to be had except a creaky slatback
chair. When I asked around, no one expressed sympathy. People
said, “Why not go up to the woodshop and make something?”
Being ever the optimist, I went on up. The people there were of a
very independent cast of mind, and I soon caught on. The ethic
was: invent, be creative, don’t copy, and don’t bother me. But the
atmosphere was positive, so that my inexperience was seen as no
major obstacle. At home, we owned no tools except a ball peen
hammer and a pair of pliers. We used a table knife for a screw-
driver. I was as innocent as Adam.
     I decided to make a table, or as it turned out, a writing surface.
There were few materials at hand: some quarter-inch plywood,
wood scraps, even some corrugated aluminum. I said to myself:
corrugated cardboard is remarkably strong for its weight; what
about a kind of corrugated plywood with an aluminum center?
There was no glue for this kind of thing in 1945. That left screws,
tiny little short screws, hundreds of them. This sandwich took a
very long time to fabricate, so there was no time left for legs. In-
stead, it was hung from the ceiling with wire. It was a miserable
desk. The screws through the top snagged my legs, and it swayed
like a hammock. But it was all mine. There was not another one in
the world like it. I felt a hot flush of creativity. The end of my first
year, Molly Gregory announced she was leaving BMC. This left the
woodshop untended. Without a hesitation I approached the power
in hope that a way could be found to keep the shop open. My
search ended with Rondy (Theodore Rondthaler), who said: “Why
not take charge of it yourself?” I jumped at the chance. As business
manager, he had some concerns that the tools not wander off and




An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                   19]

--- Page 220 ---
that materials be accounted for. We agreed that I would take this
on if the door was to remain unlocked. Almost instantly I became
the proprietor of a woodworking shop. What was to become my
career was launched in this most natural and effortless way. The
obstacles that are now the norm didn’t exist. There were no consid-
erations of insurance    liability, bookkeeping,   or the image of the
school. Genuine interest was honored with responsibility.


Dehn
Max usually wore a small black porkpie hat, and under the brim his
square Northern European face could be serious as a Viking or
wear a puckish smile. His five-two frame was often viewed from
the rear as he led a pack of hikers up and down        the ridge trails,
never seeming to tire. It was an inspiration to watch him waltz with
one of the Junoesque ladies of the College, slightly Prussian but
done with joy. Few subjects failed to interest him, and through him,
you. He offered a course he titled “Philosophy.” We had no text,
and his assignments were such as “Next time we will talk about
Knowledge: think about it in the meantime.” The strategy was So-
cratic, with Dehn playing Mr. Interlocutor. He was very patient,
and would not give up until you had said exactly what he had been
hunting for. Only then did we get a smile and an “Ach so!” There
were about six or seven in our Philosophy class, which met once a
week in his study. I remember the session with the assignment
“Progress.” We were all postwar cynics and thought we were men-
tally tough. One by one we crumpled under his questioning which
had taken several hours. I remember Hank Bergman was the last to
admit that there seemed to be at least some progress. In the silence
that followed the “Ach so!” we began to turn the tables and tried to
find out what Max thought about progress. Finally, he said that he
had not been playing fair. At his age he could allow himself the
luxury of doubting there was progress, but at our time of life that
was something we could not afford. Another session of this class
found us talking about knowledge and what it meant to the an-
cients to measure the length of the day. Max suggested that we
should duplicate the experience ourselves with the only instrument
available, a surveyor’s transit. We were to measure the movement




192                     Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 221 ---
of Orion. As the most accuracy could be had with the stars high in
the sky, we found ourselves on the roof of the Studies Building at
midnight, infused with mulled wine against the bitter cold. We
measured, went down for more wine, and remeasured. The results
were astounding: a day was twenty-two hours and eleven minutes
long. That we knew this to be inaccurate held no ground against
the fact that we had done the measurements along the lines of pure
logic.
    Once Max told me he had seen a rare orchid in the woods, but
he was reluctant to tell me where. I told him I would never pick
such a thing, but he replied that I might tell someone else, who
would. His concern was the deep one, that these plants were very
rare, that the balance of their existence might hang by a thread, that
the plucking of a single bloom might start the inexorable slide to
extinction.


Singing and Death
BMC had almost no rules. I seem to recall that one had to register
for at least one class, but attendance was not mandatory.      As for
singing, there were no rules against it, so I joined the chorus under
Heinrich Jalowetz. Jalo placed the novices near more certain sing-
ers, and several basses and I clustered around John Wallen. Years
later, John said he was not at all sure I was going to make it. But I
did. Under Jalo, we plunged into the “How Lovely” from the
Brahms Requiem. With practice on practice, | memorized the entire
bass part, and used the music as a stage prop. We performed it one
spring night with considerable feeling and conviction. Alas, within
the hour, Jalo had a massive heart attack and was dead! The Col-
lege was paralysed with the loss, and those of us who knew little of
death were milling in confusion. Then we were asked for action: a
grave was to be dug; the bass section was to be the pallbearers.
Being hyperactive and morbidly curious, I volunteered for both
duties. The diggers were a mismatched crew led by Ross Penley,
who was then the College farmer. Ross was the only native Black
Mountaineer, and he spoke with a smooth and twangy drawl. The
gravesite was in the woods, and soon proved to be centered over a
half-acre-sized rock. Several other places were probed with a rod



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                 193

--- Page 222 ---
and finally time dictated we settle on one. At three feet we again
found an auto-sized glacial boulder. By scratching and strategy we
increased the hole to four feet and then managed—by removing
rocks the size of bushels—down to five feet in one corner. The legal
depth was six feet. Jalo was to be brought in a simple pine box at 4
p.M. Others and I ran to the dorm, sponged armpits, and put on
white shirts and ties. We then ran up a shortcut to meet the hearse.
Shouldering the hideous purple velour coffin, stumbling down the
trail, we got there first. We waited. Word was passed up the line to
the Jalowetz house that things were ready, and after a small eter-
nity, people descended the path. Johanna was leaning on Max
Dehn. And then it struck me. All this heady Viennese sophistica-
tion and culture knew nothing about burying a man worlds away
from his roots, by people only vaguely connected, in a beautiful but
foreign place. So, Johanna and Max approached, and we awk-
wardly lowered the coffin which made the grave look even shal-
lower. No one knew what to do. There was a long silence. Finally
Max   said something,    who   knows   what,   but at least it brought
things to a head. Johanna      threw in a flower, a handful of earth.
Then she fractured and would have become hysterical except Max
and others physically carried her up to the house. And so it ended,
or almost ended. Everyone threw in their flowers and clods and
faded away. Soon all were gone except Ross and me. It was getting
on twilight. Nothing was said for a long time. Then Ross said we
might as well fill in the grave. Which we did. Hard work with a
necktie on. Finally took it off. We didn’t have enough soil as so
much of the grave had been rocks. But, as night fell, the hole looked
OK and we went our ways, united at least in the desire to do the
right thing, the complete thing.


A Dugout Canoe
Harry Holl thought that .any self-respecting college needed a
smithy, and before long Harry had finagled an anvil and forge. At
BMC, anything was possible. Harry’s enthusiasm soon rubbed off
on me. Why not make tools, woodworking tools? The most exotic
tool I could think of was an adz. So, one was made. Then, the ques-
tion was what to do with it. Naturally, a dugout canoe. I couldn't



194                     Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 223 ---
bear to fell a live tree for such an experiment, so a dead chestnut
near the road was selected. For days and weeks I could be found in
a pile of chips at the edge of the lake. Then came the launching. The
fate of the American chestnut had been explained to me: a terrible
blight had killed them all the the end of World War I. Ever heard of
wormy chestnut? Well, my tree was very wormy, thirty years of
wormy. So, when the boat was in the water, the interior looked like
a continuous showerhead. Hundreds of tiny geysers filled her to
the gunnels. Remember, anything was possible at Black Mountain.
So I bought about ten big boxes of wooden matches and began to
pound them into these wormholes. The bad part was that they had
to go in tail first, and when struck with a hammer, they would light.
For days, I reeked of sulphur. The maiden voyage proved the craft
successful, as long as the water was 100 percent calm. She had a
freeboard of about half an inch. The last time I saw the dugout was
a Sunday morning just before lunch. Alex Parker had found the
boat under the dining hall, and in his Sunday best took to sea. The
porch was lined with the pre-lunch crowd, and I tried to call out, to
tell him, “No, don’t!”   Too late.   A small breeze   came   up raising
wavelets, and the boat went down. Alex was quite calm, gradually
raising his body as the craft disappeared. When the water reached
his armpits he waded ashore to applause.
      In retrospect, I see that Black Mountain was like an immensely
fertile and well-prepared seedbed. Anything that landed on it with
a spark of life took root and sustenance. This quality was a turning
point in my life. Anything was possible, and often it began through
chance.




An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                   195

--- Page 224 ---
Jim Tite
                                                    (Student   1945-48)


                               The Black Mountain Press



Spring unwinds early in the Carolinas and 1946 was no exception.
New to the South, with its extremes of natural beauty and malevo-
lence (i.e., flowering dogwood vs. venomous copperheads), I flour-
ished in an experimental academic environment with self-directiv-
ity as one of its cardinal tenets.
     One of those “night people” in my pre-BMC life (a professional
jazz drummer), I had become an early riser here, addicted to soli-
tary strolls at daybreak, while my collegian colleagues slumbered.
     On this particular dawning I chose as my direction the ascend-
ing path leading to the College woodworking shop where I occa-
sionally indulged that hobby, noticing for the first time that the
overall shop building was a good twelve feet longer than I knew it
to be on the inside. This suggested an attached space, unused,
abandoned perhaps, and vaguely mysterious. A grimy window on
the path side prevented a view to the interior. A flimsy, rusted pad-
lock “secured” the only entrance, a narrow door consisting of three
weathered and knotted boards, one of which was loose enough to
pull away. My curiosity, now thoroughly aroused, and having
gone this far, it was only a matter of minutes, with a stout piece of
fallen branch, that I was able to pry an opening large enough to
crawl through. The musty smell of talcumy dust and mold claimed .
my nostrils, and sticky strands of spider web brushed against and
clung to my hands and face. Rubbing my cheeks and forehead, as
my eyes became accustomed to the gloom, I spied, against one wall,
a squat, cast-iron, hand-fed, foot-pedal-operated printing press, an
antique even in 1946. To the left of this mighty Challenge-Gordon
press stood a sloping rack cradling several type cases. The type was
mixed up (“pied” as we old-time printers called a hopeless clutter
of em-quads, en-quads, leads, and characters) all spilling over
amidst   the lifeless bodies   of moths,   flies, wasps,   a few matted
leaves, twigs, and acorns.


196                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 225 ---
It was to “Rondy” that I broke the news of my private discov-
ery, and received his blessing to resurrect and renovate the Black
Mountain Press. I shared my enthusiasm with Ann Mayer and sub-
tly set about proselytizing her and others. Together we spent most
of our spare hours cleaning up the shop, expanding it, sorting and
redistributing type, oiling the press, replacing the platen bearings,
and installing a motor from a defunct faculty Kelvinator. What fun
the trips to Asheville, to stand reverently behind a local job printer,
badgering him with questions while he worked, watching, absorb-
ing, learning, then going back to Black Mountain to practice the ar-
cane art he proudly preached.
     Having learned just enough to be dangerous, I boldly ap-
proached Rondy and Bill Levi with the proposition that a course in
the practice of printing just might become a part of the established
curriculum, co-teaching with Ann, my ardent acolyte. I don’t know
how I had the gall to assume such a role, but this, I believe, was the
first experiment by the College in student-teaching, a concept that
readily gained credence and was picked up and exercised by such
talented individuals as Harry Holl in sculpture, Arthur Penn in
drama, and other extroverts of their persuasion. One of my classes
totaled twelve students, working as we did toward the goal of pro-
viding all of the College’s printing needs to save it money.
      Space here doesn’t permit reciting the ensuing saga of obtain-
ing, at no cost to anyone, a handsome Kluge job press which had
been in mothballs (actually Cosmoline) since its delivery, brand
new from government surplus to the VA hospital in Asheville.
With our “battery” of two presses we really went to town produc-
ing letterheads, bulletins, concert programs, student and faculty
creative writing—you name it. Harry Weitzer was also an excellent
co-teacher, having “graduated” from the ranks of my first class.
     Then along came Frank Rice, a new faculty member in 1947,
hired to teach German and supervise the printing activity. Frank,
who had made something of a career for himself elsewhere in the
graphic arts, was also, it turned out, a devout grammarian. It was
he who taught all of us to call it correctly a “printing shop.”
    “A ‘print shop’ is where they sell prints,” Frank would intone
dogmatically. “Was richtig ist, ist richtig. Nicht wahr?”

--- Page 226 ---
Leo Amino
                                              (Faculty 1946, 1950)


                                             Working It Out



In 1946 we had Jacob Lawrence, Leo Lionni, and in 1950 there were
Clement Greenberg, Theodoros Stamos, Paul» Goodman, Robert
Klein, and others. 1950 was much more lively than 1946.
      What struck me most of all was that we were to do “our own
thing,” and if students wanted any help, we were there to work it
out rather than to teach. So that students learned out of their own
need.
    Due to BMC’s location, the community was rather cliquish and
“incestuous.” But this was a minor problem.




                       Evelyn Oxenberg Swann
                                                (Student 1946-47)


        John Wallen’s Human Relation Classes



At BMC academic offerings approximated those of a liberal arts
curriculum. However, there was no formal grading system. Stu-
dents were responsible for planning their own study programs,
with a faculty member available for guidance. The classes taught
by John Wallen were most in accord with my interests. In his Hu-
man Relation classes he used the innovative technique of role play-
ing, which enabled the students to try out different approaches to
conflict resolution. His lectures conveyed sincerity and the depth of
his own convictions. Outstanding were three concepts which have
proven useful through the decades. (1) With regard to child rearing,



198                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 227 ---
he taught that “will equals counter-will’; that a demanding atti-
tude on the part of a parent will invite a counter-resistant response.
(2) In situations requiring conflict resolution, his guideline was to
“stay on the subject,” in order to keep the focus on the real issue. (3)
In the area of choice making, he emphasized the value of diversity
of thought, of taking into consideration all aspects and possibilities,
of examining the peripheral as well as the obvious. These concepts
have proven their validity throughout the child-rearing years, in all
kinds of relationships, and as my modus operandi for decision
making.
     The College was a special place with a particular kind of ambi-
ance. It was the beauty of the natural surroundings, the respect for
the individual, the variety of musical involvements, the Saturday
night dances—all indelibly imprinted and still unfolding.




                                  Jesse Dawes Green
                                                    (Student   1946-48)


                                A Note on the Mush War



Along with the other matters I turned up I am sending along for its
possible interest, as a glimpse of political life in the BMC commu-
nity, a note which I posted on the Study Hall bulletin board one
night .. . after a student meeting. Later on that year a small delega-
tion of us attended the annual convention of the Southern Negro
Youth Congress at Charleston, where we met Howard Fast and
Herbert   Aptheker   and were    received, to our awe, by W. E. B.


These letters from the spring of 1967 to Mike Weaver (“A Note on the
Mush War”) and William Wasserstrom (“Stepping into a Whirlpool”)
address questions asked about Black Mountain history by these authors
of a book on that subject that had been projected by MIT Press. We print
most of the first letter; from the second we excerpt a fragment.



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                   199

--- Page 228 ---
DuBois     himself. Anyhow,   the note itself, touching as it does on
some of the kinds of questions we argued, has reminded me of an-
other episode which belongs in the annals of the College—one
which I once tried, in fact, years ago, to set down in a kind of mock
epic because it seemed such an epitome of one of the possible, even
characteristic, excesses   into which we could fall in our palace of
wisdom. Among the aborted remains of my literary effort I have
found what must be my copied version of a document. This too
was a notice, tacked to the Dining Hall bulletin board. It reads as
follows:

      The Community Council has decided that on two evenings a
      week the dessert will be omitted from dinner, the proceeds of
      this saving to be contributed to the fund for European Relief.
      Those who do not wish to participate in this effort will please
      sign below.

      This notice, posted as I recall sometime in the Spring or Sum-
mer of 1948, precipitated ... But no, that can’t be right. I must have
made up the part about desserts, because the great issue was rather
to eat or not to eat mush. The affair should have been named, if it
wasn’t, the Great Mush War. Feelings ran pretty high for a time.
      Hazy as it all is in my memory by now, a few fragments of
scenes remain. One of these is the Community Meeting in which
the issue actually originated, with someone’s proposal that a com-
munity sacrifice be made to raise money for those left destitute by
the war, part of a nationwide effort at the time. It was typical in the
first place that instead of a box being pointed out for individual
contributions the thought should have been of collective sacrifice.
Our economy of shared parsimony. I can remember some of the
responses: speeches about moral responsibility and universal guilt
for the plight of the persecuted, and a sort of bandwagon series of
eager suggestions from the more earnest of the gathering. Ted
Rondthaler, for instance, a non-smoker as I recall, proposing that
everyone give up smoking. For one or two days a week? And
finally the motion that one day a week we substitute mush for our
regular dinner and contribute the difference. The motion passed by
acclamation and the meeting adjourned. But amid the affirmations,
only half-attended and perhaps not really articulated ... were some



200                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 229 ---
voices of dissent, suggesting, for instance, that Ted Rondthaler
should give up milk instead of tobacco. And in the course of a day
or so it developed that the community was not unanimously behind
the mush movement. Some were offended at the way the plan had
been swept through the meeting by a wave of feeling which had
made rational objections impossible to raise: among them the ques-
tion of what to do about those who might not wish to go along with
the majority. Another line of dissent had to do with the questions
of guilt: a feeling expressed most strongly as I remember by a num-
ber of American Jews, that those who had come to the College from
Europe were trying to project onto the rest of us their own guilt for
having escaped the fate of their kin, and that to accept this idea of
sacrifice amounted to acknowledging this guilt in oneself, which
they refused to do. A subtle, perhaps, but not a petty consideration;
for to give freely, without compulsion, not out of guilt or commu-
nity pressure but freely, all those who voiced this objection pro-
fessed themselves ready to do, and may have done. To the major-
ity, on the other hand, and to the Community Council, any objec-
tions to such a small sacrifice in the face of such unarguably des-
perate need could only seem frivolous. Hence the notice, which I
remember as exactly like the version above, except for the opening
clause and a statement that the change would go into effect the fol-
lowing week. There were signers, of course. A few habitually con-
scientious   defiers of authority:   Krikorian,   Brody, Alice Jackson,
Alex Parker. And Willy Joseph, who liked ice cream with his din-
ner. For several days everyone watched to see who would sign, and
there was much comment abroad. Now there was an issue. The no-
tice was peremptory—not a call for voluntary sacrifice, but an ulti-
matum to the willful and gluttonous to declare themselves..
     Thus it happened that there were not only those who didn’t
sign up because they favored or were going along with the sacrifice
but, amid much discussion, those who didn’t sign up because they
refused to accept the role of defiance in which the ultimatum cast
those who did not wish to participate.
    When the first hour of sacrifice came around, then, there were
one or two tables in the Dining Hall very conspicuous for their loud
jollity—and also for the two or three diners at each of these tables
who sat stiffly silent amid the raucous, or who spoke only to one



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                  201

--- Page 230 ---
another. These were the Non-signers who had joined the Non-
mush line to the kitchen—and been accommodated by the cooks. A
few of these conscientious eaters (myself among them) also took
their plates and dined among the mushers, winning converts.
    And so it went on for several weeks, this ideological confronta-
tion—mushbowI to lushbowl, so to speak. The decisive factor, how-
ever, turned out in the end to be the cooks. Annoyed from the first
at having to prepare not one but two menus for dinner, they be-
came more and more disgruntled as, in the course of time, the Non-
signing Non-mushers gradually multiplied—until one night there
simply was no mush offered.
      Thus ended the Mush    War of 1948. As for European Relief, I
forget what happened about that, but unless I miss my guess, noth-
ing further happened about it at Black Mountain.




                            Stepping into a Whirlpool



Factionalism . . . is something you would have to ask a variety of
people about. My feeling . . . is that it was inherent in the nature of
the place; though I also detect a tendency on my own part to
overdo on the element of conflict, just because it was/is engaging
to one’s impressionability and sense of drama. It certainly is not
something easy to generalize well about. It was all a matter of per-
sons and temperaments, and of small numbers and remoteness of
place, and of intense convictions in discord (all of which were es-
sential not only to the factionalism but to everything else as well).
What I keep coming back to as the central fact, however, that both
made the place what it was and did it in, is that this small remote
group of individuals—most of them quite individual and devoted
to their own ways and works owned the place. Total power,-total
responsibility, born of protestant utopian revolution and pilgrim-
age and shared alike. And if this ownership (at first they were revo-
lutionaries who only rented) was a factor of institutionality and



202                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 231 ---
“Establishment,” it remained in the very nature of the place to
draw and receive and distribute ownership among (however tran-
siently) the powerful and various individuals. Hence the particular
political (= personal) intensity. And the way legislative and admin-
istrative questions had, continuously, of keeping constitutional
questions open. The place could split, and finally split out of exis-
tence, but it could never ossify. But BUT BUT. Life was not all, or
even mostly, faction and conflict. For all the intense convictions in
discord—and concord—it was hardly as if everybody was con-
stantly struggling over the institution. On the contrary, everybody
(speaking loosely) had his or her own     life, work, studying, teach-
ing, clique, whatever—and    was much of the time centered on his/
her own affairs. . . .
     And also I am reminded again of the place’s own complexities
and resistance to nice formulations. For one who was there the for-
est is always complicated by the trees. Anyhow, I would caution,
gratuitously, against focusing the history as a whole in terms of the
final phase   (which   is also, among   other things, merely the last
phase, when the history ends and the place collapses). As if the
place had a “history”—in fact, it was all fiction, and what its diver-
sity requires is a novel.




                                              Irwin Kremen
                                                      (Student   1946)


                                   Three Anecdotes Plus



Self Sufficiency Black Mountain Style
We planned to go to New York together at the term’s end, my
friend and I. One morning I found her hammering away at an ob-
ject I couldn’t make out.
     “You can’t be on the work program, it’s too early! What the
heck are you banging at?”

                “I’m making a ring to use when we go to New York!”
                “A ring? Out of what?”
                “All I have to do is hammer this quarter long enough on its
           edge. The core’ll come away and the silver will form a ring. It'll
           pass a hotel clerk!”
               Gracing her finger some days later was a silver ring when we
           checked into a hotel near Grammercy Park. The comfort there sure
           beat the thin, narrow mat on her study floor.
                                        ”

                 Grand luxe, in fact.


           On Redness
           One weekend I had to hitchhike from Black Mountain to Chapel
           Hill. The Alberses, who were setting out on a sabbatical, offered to



           204                      Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 233 ---
drive me a way. By the time we were out of town Yuppi had loos-
ened up and began to talk about Mexico, to which they were
headed eventually, and about Aztecs, Indians, the Americas. As we
descended the pass over the mountains, we came to a stretch of
road whose dirt shoulders were a startling orange-red. Excitedly,
even jubilantly, he pointed to that brazenly pigmented earth and
declared how fit it was that the “red” Indian was native to such
soil.


Revisionism
People these days apparently go on organized retreats at the old
BMC property. Two people came up to me this past year to say
with starry eyes that they had spent a week there, communing with
the “creative spirit of the place,” as if a beneficent miasma curled
up regularly from Lake Eden. I couldn’t resist myself the mischie-
vous delight of saying, “Didn’t you also hear a voice calling from
the depths, ‘Buy your rubbers at the Black Mountain Drug Com-
pany!”




Wish Fulfillment
When I was a boy I read a lot and-daydreamed more. Much of that
reading concerned early America. Thus, when I rushed down to
Black Mountain after coming upon a report about it in the old Parti-
san Review, a special excitement gripped me as the train wound
through the Smokies at sunup and, in the early mist, I saw a log
cabin with smoke rising from its stone chimney. A landscape out of
childhood dreaming!
     That feeling returned several times while I was at BMC, in-
duced by activities evocative of those early fantasies—driving
cattle down to the pasture, or logging in the stand of trees across
the road, or harvesting and shucking corn at the farm, or battling a
forest fire up the mountain.
     Images that stay with me keenly.

Dick Roberts
                                                    (Student 1946-47)


                                           “Carolina Moon”




I never was a scholar, an activist nor causist.   I went to BMC   to find

the reasons for war and was frustrated academically in my pursuit
of this objective, except for an introduction to Thucydides, a copy
of which I had to get from a friend at St. John’s who stole it from
their library.
     Coming from Montana       (the only one ever at BMC?)        I was
mostly bewildered.    I remember only two Jews, two Negroes, and
one Oriental in all my days in the Billings, Montana, school system.
At BMC I was agape at the rich young, and at people from New
York in general.
     We had some fun playing “Carolina Moon” on Bodky’s harpsi-
chord in the dining hall one summer midnight, probably under the
persuasion of corn whiskey we’d obtained somewhere off in those
Smoky Mountains. Sometime late in my calendar year there I was
stunned by orders from Albers to lower the stacks of cases of grape-
fruit juice which showed unaesthetically above the three-quarter
partition in the dining hall. At that time, I was food purchaser for
the college, for which I received sixteen credits. These credits were
later accepted without question by Eastern Montana College of
Education, where I sought a degree in elementary education.
     All the people were kind to me; some of them still are after all
these years. They let me in cheap and made few demands. There I
walked   the streets of ancient Athens    with Bill Levi, and played
some duffer golf with him at the Black Mountain town course. With
Dave Corkran I even turned in [papers on] assigned history read-
ing of my beloved Northwest, and we climbed Mount Mitchell to-
gether as well.                                                    os
      A rich experience, BMC, not much digested at the time. And
how lucky I was not to fall off the farmhouse roof while nailing on
shingles. There   I was   enlightened,   if not educated,   befriended,



206                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 235 ---
love-struck and married for good, and sent back into the world
with friends, nostalgias, and a knowledge of how to plant potatoes
and shovel coal. Who can ask more of higher education? My great-
est triumph came when [caught a pass out of the blue and scored a
touchdown at the Fall Classic touch football game. (We were not to
be outdone by Chapel Hill or Duke.) Jean Markoff, with her wash-
tub bass drum, led the ersatz marching band up and down the
field. I have a photo of that event, as well as a program.




                   Sheila Oline Marbain Ravin
                                                   (Student 1946-48)


                                         Some Bright Spots



My memories of the period that I attended BMC are still very pain-
ful to me now. At age nineteen, I was unprepared for the richness
and the drama of the place. I felt very handicapped by my stam-
mering and was experiencing yet-unidentified learning disabilities.
I was neurotically anxious, self-conscious, and afraid of everything.
There just weren’t enough occasions wherein I could escape from
my fears and feel part of the community.
     Despite the heavy self-immersion, I want to try to evoke some
of the images, tastes, smells, and feelings of Black Mountain that
occasionally raised my spirits. To help me along, I looked through
some old photographs that Ilya Bolotowsky took of four girls:
Sylvia Girsh, Anne Banks, Bernice Bernstein, and me. These were
taken on an outing with Ilya, who drove us madly around the
countryside in his jalopy. I think that we were each experiencing
some private torment, yet on that magic afternoon, we were able to
relax and play. Ilya too was enjoying our playfulness. We cavorted
and posed while he shot film continuously. He drove us around the
curvy mountain roads to dreamlike spots: ornamental lakes, mica




An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                207

--- Page 236 ---
mines, and mountaintop views. The world felt very peaceful at
those moments.
     There were some moments of awareness of the immediate en-
vironment. The dusty road to the Studies Building around the lake,
the deep dark red soil, and the sight of mountain laurel blooming
in the hills in the spring; the heavy itchy feel of the animal feed corn
that we cut, tied, and threw on the tractor.
      One student, whose name I forget, wore a rattlesnake-skin belt
that she had made herself after having killed the snake. I see a
winter’s image of Hannelore Hahn triumphantly figure skating
across the frozen lake—and then, the entire college community
coming out in springtime to plant trees on the hill. The tree plant-
ing felt very solemn and important.
      In summer I watched the hummingbirds amid the honeysuckle
vines that grew along the railing of the Studies Building. And I
remember the music! How Paul Williams would set up hi-fi equip-
ment and give us informal record concerts of Bach and Beethoven
string quartets. There was my first introduction to Bartok. The live
concerts were wonderful. One joyous memory of Bimbus and Merv
Lane playing keyboard Bach and Mozart—and how my regard for
the performers so much enhanced my pleasure. I was powerfully
moved by the performance of the Schutz St. Matthew Passion, at the
local Negro church, and particularly by Arthur Penn’s recitation.
      A great thrill for me was to be part of Merce Cunningham's
summer dance class where John Cage would accompany us with
exciting rhythmic playing and plucking. He never lost the beat.
Dance talent was fortunately not a necessity in that class: I learned
to move as though my head were suspended from a string, and that
when I rose, it was because I had been pulled by the string above. I
still use that technique to climb stairs with less effort.
      I still remember the shock of my initial arrival, being met by
sooty members of the coal-hauling crew.
     I have good memoriesof some of the people: Harry Holl col-
lecting wild garlic and mushrooms to make a giant Italian spaghetti
on the cooks’ night off; of bull sessions in the Studies Building,
where Arthur Penn and Dick Spahn would vie with one another at
telling outrageous jokes and stories. I admired people like Arthur
Penn and Merv Lane who seemed to be brimming over with talent.



208                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 237 ---
An outing with Ilya, ca. 1947. (From left: Sylvia Girsh, Ann Banks,
Sheila Oline, Bernice Bernstein.) Photographer: Ilya Bolotowsky.
Courtesy of Sheila Oline Marbain Ravin.

      Or Martha Hult, who spent a year’s apprenticeship with a carpen-
      ter in the mountains of Peru. I liked Jesse Green, the intellectual
      poet. M. C. Richards fascinated me. She seemed to experience life
      deeply and intensely—sensitive to the slightest happening.
           I was drawn to the literary people, despite my feeling unable to
     enter discussions with them. The dancers and the artists, with the
     exception of Ilya, were not usually very talkative.
          Why doesn’t somebody talk about the baseball games, to
     which there was brought a mock-seriousness? They were great fun
     and people did let their hair down.
          My deepest regret is that, having been so deeply immersed in
     my own problems of emotional growth, I was unable to partake of
     more of the world around me. I wish I could have attended Black
     Mountain at, say twenty-five, a mature enough age to have been
     able to absorb more of such a unique experience.

Kenneth Noland
                              (Student 1946-48; Student 1950 SS)


                                 Some Talk about BMC



Ken’s home town is Asheville, North Carolina. He was working in
a clothing store there one day when Oli and Joan Sihvonen came in.
Ken noticed they were both wearing sandals, so he asked them if
they were from BMC. They were indeed. Ken says Oli talked to him
about the College and encouraged him to come out and visit. Ken
attributes his going to the College to Oli as well as to his brother,
Harry Noland. When he finally came to look the place over, Dr.
Herbert Miller took him under his wing, serving subsequently as
Ken’s mentor and faculty adviser.
     Ken was already painting. When he wasn’t working and had
some time on his hands, he would invariably sketch and paint. He
was also interested in music and sociology, and in the latter area
took classes with Miller. He also took a course in logic and seman-
tics with A. W. Levi that first year. The music he loved and regu-
larly listened to was jazz; he took a tutorial in harmony from “Bim-
bus” Schlesinger and music theory with John Cage. But almost
immediately he became a student of Ilya Bolotowsky, who was
teaching painting during the time Albers was on sabbatical leave in
Mexico. Ken recalls learning a great deal from both men.
Bolotowsky’s approach to painting was from the base of Cubism,
and Albers’s from Bauhaus design principles. When Albers re-
turned, Ken took his design class. He recalled that the class was
important as an influence in his own teaching of design later at
Catholic University in Washington, D.C.
    Ken felt that there was a good deal of difference in the ranks of
both students and faculty in their approaches and attitudes toward


The first section summarizes some of Ken’s answers to questions I
asked him during the summer of 1988; the second section is a portion of
an informal taped interview I conducted with him.



210                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 239 ---
ideas and information. He felt this tension himself in the art classes
between students of Albers and those who were interested in ab-
stract expressionism.   But whatever       tensions   existed, the insights
were valuable educationally because they taught the student about
issues and divergencies in theory, method, and values. In retro-
spect, this was all positive. At the time it was not to [Ken’s] liking.
No one relishes conflict and tension between human beings; none-
theless, disagreement and conflict are instructive.
      The kind of teachers that were invited to come to BMC were
also unusual. Members of the regular faculty like Rice, Albers, Levi
were instrumental in being able to procure teachers of merit rather
than of reputation. That a painter like Bill de Kooning was asked to
come before he was really recognized in the art world—or that per-
formers and teachers of the caliber and gift of Erwin Bodky and
John Cage, or writers like Paul Goodman and Isaac Rosenfeld, or a
designer and scientist like Bucky Fuller came—indicated a special
kind of recognition of talent and a unique communication between
artists both in and outside the school. Ken thinks that this was most
unusual and rare, not the kind of selections or process of selection
ordinarily made by standard universities. And all this was done
with an unpretentiousness,      a naturalness    that made    the students
and participants feel that this was part of the “stride” of the place,
part of its natural sophistication.
    Ken   remembers     with   affection   the entrance    to the College,
which had been designed by Albers. There was no gate, but there
was a fairly long run of three or four white horizontal boards on
both sides—and    for a good stretch, leading toward         the entrance.
Ken remarked that those bright white boards gently angled
through such interesting and gradual changes toward the opening
that the visual effect was uninterrupted, so that someone entering
was sort of “streamed” through and on up the slight incline of the
dirt road into the College. This was another kind of sophistication.
     Ken tells the story about Albers talking one day about how
during the noon hour when there is the most light, you’d expect to
see a greater distance and with more detail. But no. At noon, dis-
tance is hazy, details unclear because of the amount of light be-
tween the eye and something distant; whereas at dusk or dawn
these factors are clear and distinctly visible. Mountains look further



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                      at

--- Page 240 ---
away at noon, and much nearer and more dimensional at dusk. The
kind of observations and statements—like this one—that Albers
made were of immense value for people concerned with the visual
arts.
        Ken's overall estimation of his time at BMC was “just great.”
He knew when he was there that the experience was unusual, the
people rare. He said that so much was always going on day and
night, seven days a week, that the amount of possibilities in com-
munication of “data” was phenomenal.


Interview, 21 July 1988
Ken: There were very significant turning points at BMC. Things
    that happened to you were so enlightening at the very mo-
    ment, that they’d change you forever. They did me.
         About the influence that BMC had on subsequent culture. I
    think that a large part of that influence came from the Bauhaus.
    Pop art used symbolism or visual devices that came from the
    Bauhaus. Oldenburg is an example: to enlarge something out
    of context and make it or put it in an ambiguous size and scale.
    Which is intriguing. I hadn’t known that Rauschenberg went to
    BMC one summer. And I think he learned a lot—and very
    quickly—about the Bauhaus and its principles. And what was
    permissible in terms of making art. I mean that art just didn’t
    have to be confined to sculpture, painting, and/or, and/or—
    that you could mix these things up and make it environmental.
    And that came out of not only the Bauhaus but all the kind of
    theatrical things that went on at BMC too. All that decoration,
        combinations of costume, pictorial devices, and dance, audi-
        ence participation and involvement. All that went into making
        “happenings.” I wouldn’t say that the total influence came
        from BMC, but probably a fairly crucial one did. Because it re-
        ally became kind of recognized there that art could be any-
        thing, and could be made out of anything, and that it didn’t
        necessarily cross boundaries—they thought—between theater,
        the visual arts, dance, music, etc., that you could mix all this up
        and make a multi-media—or whatever they call it—environ-
        mental art. And that’s where a second kind of theatricality




212                       Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 241 ---
came in that made all of these various conceptual works—tab-
    leaux, and what Rauschenberg called it: “combines.” It allowed
    the general attitude of this or that, or both this and that. I mean
    that you could use as a basis for making art an ambiguous con-
    dition as a way of making it. What that did though was to
    make it become theatrical, a performance. And that this could
    become a valid form of art. Anything can be art, but a poem is a
    poem, a novel’s a novel, journalism is journalism. And [in] the
    difference between one art form and another—Clem Greenberg
    said—any developments that take place—in the difference—
    have stressed the difference and have caused the difference to
    be more “planted” and the distinction made more definite in
    the discipline. And this distinction is not usually blurred. And
    that took place at BMC: that this and that could be used, each
    giving up something of its essential difference. And it was the
    idea of a new kind of freedom, a freedom of expression, a very
    democratic kind of principle. But it also lowered the level. The
    level began to come down.
Merv: What level is it that you’re talking about?
Ken: High-brow level, elite level, or art level. It’s not that entertain-
    ment can’t be art; there can be an art of entertainment surely,
    but entertainment ultimately is a consumption activity; you
    have to consume within the limit of time and space—but it gets
    consumed; it’s entertainment, and is fulfilled therefore in that
    end. But art is not entertainment; the experience of art is not
    entertainment because the experience of art is not consumable.
    I mean you can’t just hear it and laugh or cry or have a reaction
    and forget it. It resonates. And it becomes. ... Although you can
    remember, for instance, a theatrical performance, a dance per-
    formance as having been a great experience, you can’t reexperi-
    ence it—unless you see it on film, unless it can be reproduced.
    But a painting is forever. ... You don’t even have to see a pic-
    ture over and over. You don’t have to see a Rembrandt over to
    recall the strong kind of entity it is. So when this entertainment
    and nowness entered art, and the greed for that, the hunger for
    that, as experience, rather than the other kind of hunger—
    which got supplanted so that nobody cares whether anything
    lasts, or if it has that long-range resonating effect—anyway, I



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                    213

--- Page 242 ---
think a lot of that started at BMC, this ambiguous kind of thing.
      And the Bauhaus.     It can be this or it can be that, or it can be
      both this and that.
            I think I’ve done a different thing because I don’t make
      ambiguous work—and the kind of art that I make is not an
      “entertaining” kind of art, not that [when] someone looks
      around they’re not going to find something that depresses or
      excites them. But the emphasis or the expression is not ambigu-
      ous. See, I think the experience of visual arts is like the experi-
      ence between one person and another person. That the paint-
      ing or a piece of sculpture is something that takes place be-
      tween a person and this object—as if it were a person telling
      you things, and you’re telling it things. You ask it questions
      and it questions back, as a kind of exchange that’s very physi-
      cal. It’s kinetic, like back and forth, back and forth. And you
      have to seek that personality of the painting out. It can attract
      you, but you have to experience like we experience each other.
      It’s real... . A lot of people just look at art on the basis of ico-
      nography. Depiction. Not that this can’t be a factor in this ex-
      change back and forth, but it has to take place inside the pic-
      ture. The depiction has to relate to the artistic expression of the
      painting—and the color, and the form has to be part of that. My
      “take” is that the closer a work of visual art is to the threshold
      of materiality, of its actualness, the better it is; the less illusion-
      istic it is; the less reference it is to something else, the closer the
      illusion is to the “stuff” itself—so that there’s very little differ-
      ence on that threshold between the actualness of the “stuff”
      and what the illusion is. There’s a very slight threshold in the
      back and forth, back and forth. Rather than trying to destroy
      the look of paint in the service of depiction—like say Dali—
      which a lot of people want, but which means that they don’t
      want a real thing. They want something that’s pictorial, some-
      thing that’s mentally imageable. That’s all. . . .
           I think there was an unspoken and unacknowledged atti-
      tude at BMC of always being alert to any kind of thing that was
      going on. I mean, to be alert to anything that happened around
      you. You know that kind of intensity when you're teaching a
      class, when there’s a high moment where everybody really and



214                      Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 243 ---
truly as a group of people are focused on something that’s rele-
      vant, and nobody’s attention is particularly wandering but it’s
      got everybody’s attention. In other words, it’s a kind of truth
      that’s going on that galvanizes everyone. It’s the kind of truth
      that comes through sometimes in really good writing. It’s not a
      matter of logic. It’s meaning. Sometime the truth just comes
      through.
  Merv: Do you think this alertness was because the place was so
      small that people more readily noticed what was going on?
  Ken: Yes. Right. And I also think that it was probably atmospheric.
      It might have had something to do with the sheer freshness of
      the environment, of the air there. Don’t you think that’s right?




                                                    Merv Lane
                                 (Student   1946-48; Student    1950 SS)


                                     A Black Mountain Book



 If I wrote a Black Mountain Book, it would be in connection with
 the people I knew, how they impressed me, and what they im-
 pressed upon me, at that particular time in my life. The community
 was small enough that you noticed personal idiosyncracies. For in-
 stance, Nancy always ran, and rarely walked anywhere. Once out-
 side a building she broke into a run, so on the face of it you couldn't
 have a conversation with her outside. She is memorable to me be-
 cause of this unusual habit. Everyone would remember her by this
 particular accent, rather than by the fact that she was a large, blue-
 eyed blond, with an oval face, hair in a bun or a very long ponytail,
 loping out of a Rubens painting. Her individuality is unique; her
 physical characteristics could be mistaken for other girls and other
 Nancys.

- From Going to Town, 2d ed. 1987.



 An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                  215

--- Page 244 ---
The very first human image I have from Black Mountain is a
woman skipping along the dirt road leading from the Studies
Building to the dining hall. She’s in front of me, a long-legged lanky
lady with swinging brown hair, and she stops with her dog, a black
cocker spaniel, at a Y in the road, in the center of which is a big lilac
bush in full bloom. She leaps to it, and with one sweeping motion
takes the lilac in her arms, loving, smelling, embracing it. She turns
and sees that I have been witness to this spectacle, and to avoid
embarrassment—not so much hers as my own—she laughs and
chortles out to the road again, looking at me as I come up, to see
how I’ve taken it. “Eh, now, Blackie, what think you of all this fool-
ishness, eh? What’s-all this funny business and crazy goings-on
about, eh Blackie?” I thought she was neat, and was drawn to her
immediately.
     Much to my delight, the lilac-embracing lady was there in the
class in literary criticism, which I had decided upon taking. It was
the first meeting, and to the few who were gathered in the teacher’s
study, she introduced herself as “M. C.” I flushed, realizing that the
woman whom I was so smitten with was to be my teacher. But I fell
back in the ease of her questioning who we were, where we had
come from, why we had chosen this class—the kind of questions
any of us would have been interested in asking and hearing an-
swered. Ice-breaking. Who were we?
     M. C. was light-hearted, outwardly breezy, tickled constantly
by the unlikely and unusual, serious, knowledgeable, with a keen
ear for not only what was said, but for the inflection and innuendo
of utterance. She listened, and as far as I remember never put any-
one down. At most, she kidded you out of a statement of unlikely
position. She laughed a lot. I noticed too that it was obvious when
she was in her own private space, in her musings, on a walk to be
by herself. Her face would be tilted down slightly, eyes to the
ground. This was in such contrast to her more “up” and demon-
strative manner that you knew when she wished to be alone. But
she was never irritated at being interrupted by someone seeking
her out.
      Jim Tite, M. C., Ann Mayer, and a few others resurrected an old
jobber press in what once had been a small printing facility. They
found four or five kinds of typeface, spilled all over the floor. After



216                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 245 ---
they had spent months getting the type separated and cleaned up,
and had repaired, cleaned, and oiled the press, they went ahead
and printed the first Black Mountain College Review. M. C. first
printed a little book of her own poems, utilizing all the typefaces
they had found. She printed the poems on regular onionskin typing
paper, but doubled over, which gave each page strength, opacity,
and was more easily stitched, which she did by hand. She bound
the poems in dull black cardboard, and when fifty books were
made, she gave them to friends and to her own students. Aside
from the satisfaction of printing her own work, I was impressed by
the fact that she not only wrote some very moving and interesting
poems (two of which are reprinted in this book), but the project
showed the labor of getting a ruined print shop back in working
and productive order. The ability to go the whole route with inte-
rior and exterior processes drew my admiration. Compared to
other teachers I have known, I think of M. C. as the young rabbi
who is asked by the older rabbi: “How is it so many people come to
ask your advice, and so few come to me, when even you will admit
that I know so much more than you do?” The young rabbi answers,
“Perhaps it is that you are not surprised when they come to you,
whereas I am.” When M. C. was “up” she looked vibrant, expec-
tant, surprised; her eyes would get larger, widen, and dilate. Her
eyes encouraged you to come out of yourself, and they expressed
her love and wonder. She was full of wonder, which I later read in
Plato was the first real act of knowledge, without which nothing
else really falls into proper perspective.
     Bill Levi used to say there were two kinds of people at the Col-
lege: the blue-eyed ones and the brown-eyed ones. The blue-eyed
ones were those interested in working primarily with their hands;
the brown-eyed ones were interested in talking about ideas. I think
this was his metaphor for the active and contemplative emphases
that were in evidence at the school. There were lots of exceptions,
but by and large the people in the community could be divided this
way. Bill meant the proposition in other senses: attitudes, interests,
priorities, positions; and he meant it as much for the faculty as the
students. One felt healthy with the combination of differences in
types of people and the physical, academic, and aesthetic activities
the community provided. You would be working out some design



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                 Za/.

--- Page 246 ---
problems in an Albers morning class, and after lunch be on a gar-
bage detail, then getting cleaned up in the later afternoon to sing in
the a cappella group that met an hour before dinner. After dinner,
you might walk, study, attend an evening class, go to a movie or
dance or a performance, perhaps a student meeting, or down           to
Peek’s Tavern to drink beer and watch the sand-dancing [a local
dance form in which sand, sprinkled on the floor, emphasized the
rhythmic stomping and shuffling]—any number of possibilities.
Whatever activities students arranged for themselves, the blue-
eyed and the brown-eyed came together in ways that gave them
more comprehensive regard for each other.
     Sweeping the strewn wet sawdust along the dining room floor,
two other students and I were interrupted by some people who
said they were the family of a prospective student, and would very
much like to be taken around the campus. I volunteered to run up
to the Studies Building to get Joe Fiore, who was in charge of show-
ing visitors around. When I reached his study there was a DO NOT
DISTURB sign posted on the door. Whenever that sign was on
anyone’s   door, no one, but absolutely no one, was      permitted   to
knock, enter, or in any way disturb the occupant. Whatever        pos-
sessed me to violate this inviolate rule I’ll never know, but knock I
did. And when there was no answer I turned the knob and opened
the door. At that precise moment, Joe was in front of a virgin white
four-by-five foot canvas, in the act of addressing it with a brush
dipped in paint, poised, and extended to strike. He was in the very
act of a first brush stroke! My heart stopped. Joe turned and looked
at the interruption with a sudden injured expression that turned to
a dark anger, then to an iron seriousness, then to a heavy disdain—
and all of this so instantaneously loaded, thrown, suspended but
expressed, that I was stunned. And the look was fronted by Joe’s
handsome, dark, flashing brown Italian eyes and face that had so
much life and fire in them anyway. I was turned into a piece of ash.
I gave my message. Joe said, “OK.” And then I wafted back, sidled
out, slipped the door shut, and slid and slinked my way down the
corridor, not knowing if I would ever make it into the light of day.
     I had seen many art studies come up the stairs from Albers’s
design class at the College, but nothing quite as startling as the pro-
cession of paper constructions that appeared at noon one day. They



218                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 247 ---
were astounding. I visited the class. I remember Albers going to the
window    and saying, “You will notice the farther away the range,
the darker the hue; and the nearer the range, the lighter.” Everyone
looked for a while, then continued working. I remained at the win-
dow because I was puzzled. I saw just the opposite: the farther
away the range, the lighter it appeared, and the nearer, the darker.
After class, I expressed my bewilderment to Mary. She said, “Best
to forget it; it’s too complicated, and besides, that’s Albers!”
    I’m still bewildered. But I’ve kept on looking at mountains.




                                     Manvel Schauffler
                                                    (Student   1946-48)


                                           Ahead of Its Time



Most people who know me realize that I was something of a fish
out of water at BMC. I was not an artist in any way, shape, or fash-
ion. I was interested in teaching young people and I was interested
in history. Nevertheless, I thrived on the experiences that the
school had to offer. I have been teaching middle-school kids for
thirty-nine years, and if I have had any success in that area it is
largely due to what I picked up from my two years at BMC. Classes
with John Wallen, Dave Corkran, Molly Gregory, M. C. Richards,
and long talks with Cliff and Ray while turning silage or doing the
morning milking, all went into helping me develop patience and an
appreciation for what the learning process really was all about. I
think the fact that BMC did not have letter grades was a very
significant part of its success. In every school I have been a part of I
have worked hard to see to it that letter grades were eliminated,
and in a few cases I have been successful. The results have been
great and have made it possible for young people to learn for
learning’s sake rather than for a grade. I also learned a lot about the



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                  219

--- Page 248 ---
value of discussion in the learning process and the need for all
teachers to be good listeners. The way the school was run was a fas-
cinating model and one that more schools today should adopt. It
was owned and operated by the faculty on the campus at the time
and everyone, including the students, had a piece of the action. I
remember many long conversations with Hank Bergman, who was
the student moderator     for two years, and who      later became   my
brother-in-law. He had a good feeling for the fact that the meetings
were run pretty much on a Quaker “sense of the meeting” basis.
That has been one of the principles that I have used in conducting
faculty and committee meetings ever since I left the school. While I
was head of the Catling Gabel School in Portland, Oregon, we were
successful in having the student-body president become a full vot-
ing member of the board of trustees. We changed the bylaws to let
that happen in 1971 and, in spite of the fact that no other independ-
ent school in the country has seen fit to copy the system, it is still in
effect today. Our feeling was that in as much as we are in the learn-
ing business, why not cut the student body in on those incredible
learning opportunities? We also had students appointed as full vot-
ing members of all board committees. All of that came directly out
of things learned at BMC, [learned by] the group of us who left the
College in 1948 to establish our own cooperative community in Es-
tacada, Oregon (about thirty miles southeast of Portland).
     Has anybody passed on the wonderful give-and-take between
Dick Roberts and Dave Corkran? [Dick Roberts remembers the
class as Bill Levi’s; Howard   Rondthaler and Manvel Schauffler re-
call it as Dave Corkran’s.] We had been doing some reading in a
philosophy text, and one morning in class Dave came in and sat
down and proceeded to get his pipe going. He took the pipe out of
one pocket and his pouch and gadget and matches out of another.
He carefully laid all of these on the table as the class settled down.
He picked up the pipe and packed it and tamped it and then, in an
elaborate fashion, which I can still see, he leaned back and took a
few long puffs and then said to Roberts: “Well Dick, what did you
think about that chapter?”
     At this point Dick reached in one pocket and took out his pipe.
He went into another pocket and took out his pouch and gadget
and matches and proceeded to pack and tamp it. All this time the



220                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 249 ---
class was waiting patiently for this quiet fellow’s response to the
 question. Finally, Dick lit the pipe, took a few good puffs, leaned
  back in his chair and said: “Haven't read it.” Well, the class cracked
  up, and Dave was wonderful. We went on to discuss the chapter
  and had a wonderful class. The informality and still the attention to
  learning that took place were marvelous.
       I spent a lot of time with Cliff and Ray over at the farm. I used
  to get up early and go over and help with the morning chores. One
  morning Cliff asked me to drive the tractor and manure spreader
  down to the dining hall before breakfast. He said that there was a
  field down at the end of the campus that he wanted to put some
  fertilizer on. The spreader was full and fairly heavy. I guess at one
  point I felt there was a brake on, so I got out and let off what I
  thought was a brake. When I got down to the dining hall I was
  hungry and went right in to have a good breakfast. Cliff came in a
  few minutes later and came over and shook my hand. He said he
  had always wanted to farm the roads but he had never thought of
  spreading manure on them to start the process. I ran out and
  looked at the spreader and realized that it was now empty. I had
  managed to spread a full load of manure all through the campus.
  We learned a lot of ways down there.
       Black Mountain was a school well ahead of its time. Some
_ schools have tried to copy what we had there: Evergreen State Col-
  lege in Olympia, Washington; Goddard College in [Vermont], and
  Hampshire College in Massachusetts. They are all successful to a
  point because they focus on the idea that students have to be really
  involved in their own learning process. These others fail in that
  they are too big and have grown administratively top-heavy.
       As to the cooperative community we established at Estacada,
  Oregon, in 1948, we broke up the group after four years, but we
  broke up very peacefully, and we still get together every Thanks-
  giving for four days on the Oregon coast. We include others from
  Black Mountain and would love to have others join us. We now
  number sixty people, and we have a great and informal time. It of-
  fers a chance to talk to a number of BMC people whom we all
 haven’t seen for years.

Knute Stiles
                                                (Student   1946-48)



          The Black Mountain Performance of
               Gertrude Stein’s “In a Garden”



My roommate at BMC was Arthur Penn, who was very interested
in drama even then, and has subsequently made a big name for
himself both on Broadway and in Hollywood. We lived in Deep
South, which was a long one-story barracks the army had declared
surplus, and it had been acquired and delivered in truck-sized
pieces, which we hammered     back together. Prior to that we had
slept in lofts with perhaps ten or a dozen people sleeping in one
room, but the barracks made it possible to pair off and live a
slightly more private existence. Of course, we did have private
studies anyway, and the lofts had just been for showering, dress-
ing, and sleeping. The new arrangement made it possible to get to
actually know another person instead of just experiencing a social
whirl. Arthur was already steeped in drama, and was wasting his
time being broadened into, I guess, sociology—which he never
mentioned, so I have to suppose that it bored him the way econom-
ics did me. We only talked about drama, and painting. We had both
read Bentley’s article in an earlier Partisan Review about his BMC
experience. The man who was the drama coach had the students
doing J. M. Barrie, whereas Bentley was interested in Brecht. Penn
was interested in the Shakespeare class, but he really wanted to get
involved in something more topical—newer plays. So, he decided
to do three one-act plays which he would direct—something to
give him some actual directing experience. He decided to do Edna
St. Vincent Millay’s “Aria da Capo, “ William Saroyan’s “Hello Out
There,” and Gertrude Stein’s “In a Garden.” I reckon it must have
been M. C. Richards who did the suggesting; certainly those plays
do not portend the violent stuff that became his hallmark later; I
thought he would do Brecht. Penn did also do some “psycho-
drama” in Wallen’s psychology class, which can become sort of


222                  Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 251 ---
violent, and he did talk about at least the psycho-drama facet of
  those classes. He was a bit worried that not enough people would
  come to the readings for the casting, and he was very insistent that
  I should come and try out. We had done a reading of Molnar’s “Lil-
  iom” earlier, and Penn had told me that my reading was pretty
  hammy, but he was sure he would be able to straighten me out. I
  read from the Saroyan at the tryout, but he gave me a part in the
  Stein thing.
      “In a Garden” is a very short play with only three actors. I was
  Kit Raccoon. Claire Picken was a perfect Alice in Wonderland
  blonde girl type for the play. She was sitting on a park bench talk-
  ing to herself. She was saying something like, “I wonder what it
  would be like to be a Queen.” And then Mervin Lane, who played
  the part of Phillip Hall (“I am Phillip Hall, and that is all”), with his
  mass of kinky black hair, and I with my reddish brown beard came
  along with our paper crowns and cardboard battle-axes, and in-
  vited Claire to choose between us. We asserted we were each
  Kings. She did not seem at all interested. But we persevered and
  tried to enter her game to win her. I said, “I’m Kit Raccoon, King
  Kit Raccoon; King Kit Raccoon the First. All you have to do is to be
  first, and then you are a King.” Or something like that. And then
  Merv said, “You can’t be King. There is only one King. And I am
_ that King.” So we slew each other with our cardboard weapons.
  And then Claire drew herself up very haughtily, with her head
  held high, knowing she was the veriest Queen, that Kings were
  quite unnecessary. Her key line was, “I am a Queen; I can tell by
  feeling. It is so lovely to be a Queen.” It was a gem of a play; it even
  had   anarchist,   pacifist—and   of course   feminist—meanings,     and
  anything that has more than one meaning may even be a work of
  art. I think we did it with great éclat; I felt that it was a triumph. If
  so, it was to be my only triumph as an actor. Everyone should
  triumph at least once as an actor, for one’s ego’s sake. I had had a
dreadful time learning my lines. I’m just nowhere with rote learn-
  ing. Perhaps that’s why I’m no good at learning languages. Art
  Penn probably only gave me that wee part because I was his room-
  mate. But learning the play was a good thing to do: Penn never did
  get exasperated with me for not being able to remember my lines;
_ he would explain each line in picayune detail, and why everything

was in an inevitable order, and had to come before each other for
such and such a reason. The play was a sort of linguistic declension.
I was delighted to find that Gertrude Stein actually made      sense,
too, and was not just a lot of nonsense, which I had suspected be-
fore that. I think Penn was a very good teacher—I suppose that’s
what a director really is: he teaches the actors how to act.
      After the performance, we new    celebrities circulated around
the dining hall among the fans, green-room style. The performance
had been in the dining hall without a raised stage, minimal lighting
effects, not in costume, and with just the bench and toy weapons
for props. One visitor from the audience said to me, “Well, Ill be
damned, the beard is real!” He had thought it was just a prop. Any-
way, we had not managed to project the fact that we were children
playing games, and who knows if anyone who had not had it all
defined by a director grasped the meanings.
     Martha Hult and Dick Amero got to triumph in the Millay
play. Amero was really typecast as Pierrot: he was Pierrot, with his
too tall, very angular figure, cantilevered with hips forward, chest
in, and the gesture from the elbow out, classically clownish. He was
Pierrot incarnate. And in “Hello Out There,” Sylvia Girsh decided
then and there to take up drama seriously. Prior to that I had read
her as a plain unassuming girl, but now she was a star fated for
greatness.
     There were only ninety students at BMC at any one time, and
any number of them have become more famous and successful
than we could ever have guessed at the time. In “Hello Out There,”
I only have the lines that Dick Spahn says to Sylvia: “Why don’t
you fix me some jello, or somethin’ good?!” The set of the jail was
very fine. That I remember: it was black bars built at an extreme
diagonal to look like deep perspective. And I do have a good visual
recollection of the girl who did the sets—she sawed off the end of
her pinkie making them—but I can’t seem to remember her name.
