Dick Soahn
                                                       (Student    1946-48)


                                   Learning the Hard Way
                                              as a Career



It is my experience that good people can choose, or be enlisted
without much resistance, to behave badly towards one another.
This is connected to my observation of how frequently a whole lot
of individual   positive   intentions,   taken   collectively,   produces    a
deplorable outcome.
     I became interested in these phenomena as a student at BMC in
North Carolina in the late forties. They became a lifelong interest.
Understanding the relationship between individual positive inten-
tions and collective negative outcomes became a career for me in
the same sense that the choice of a profession becomes a career for
others. My quest for understanding led me subsequently to become
a student of the Tavistock view of group dynamics. It’s English and
psychoanalytic. It brought me in contact with the theories of
Melanie Klein on individual development, Wilfred Bion on small
groups, and A. K. Rice on organizations. R. D. Laing has extended
some of these notions to interpersonal relationships and families. I
have had the opportunity to apply them in practice to enterprises
set up as communities. And I have developed what I think of as an
ideological commitment to Tavistock ideas as applied to an under-
standing of the behavior of social systems and nations.
     At the beginning, for me, understanding was associated with
preparation for practice. Where others saw antagonism and divi-
siveness, I saw the basis for intervention and healing. But times
change and things don’t change. And I change. I am more wary in
proposing cures for anything nowadays. Still, I don’t fully agree
with Werner Erhard’s teaching that understanding is the “booby
prize” in life.
     This brings me back to BMC as a place where one could learn
the hard way and understanding could occur later and elsewhere.



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                      227

--- Page 256 ---
Martin Duberman’s study (1972) provided me with some docu-
mentation and validation for my memories. Duberman’s focus was
on    the College   as a “community,”   an important   identification/
justification for the College leadership. Community      could soften
and round the hard edges of organization, as I learned when work-
ing later in the context of versions of the “therapeutic community.”
The College was a community with organizational tasks or an or-
ganization with a community veneer. Different emphases for dif-
ferent folks. In both cases, it was a large group and subject to the
phenomena associated with large groups, including the propensity
for factioning into small groups. Significantly, the history of the
College (1933-56) was characterized by splits. M. C. Richards, ob-
serving as long-standing core faculty, noted years later: “The Col-
lege split apart time and again over nonnegotiable principles.”
Some postmortem interpretation now on how and why.
     Autopsy: Functioning always more or less on the edge in terms
of finances and resources, in geographical isolation, with a strong
sense of mission but focused inward on its own membership, the
College managed to survive for a couple of decades by repeatedly
extruding a portion, sometimes up to half of its members. There is a
more traditional strategy in the service of large group survival.
United we stand. Given the intrinsic tendency for large groups to
act out ambivalences with respect to complex problems by splitting
into smaller groups, survival is most easily achieved by the availa-
bility or creation of an external threat or enemy. Non-traditional
Black Mountain seems to have rejected this solution. It chose an al-
ternative strategy: divided we survive.
     When pressures from either internal or external sources
reached the threshold of community-wide awareness, the College
found itself embroiled in irreconcilable intragroup-becoming-inter-
group conflict. It was as if disagreement was embraced and those
who were best at it became leaders. Ideally two camps would
emerge, as if wishes and fear were sides and not aspects of each
individual. Positions would be hardened. Argument would be-
come increasingly personal and bitter.
     “But then as now, people were willing to sacrifice each other
for ideology,” wrote Richards. Some people would be asked to
leave. The small-group loyalty of others would obligate them to



228                     Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 257 ---
choose to go. A lot of feelings and significant relationships were
damaged in the process. Some people would stay and carry on. For
a long time, they were those who represented the organizational
tasks of an academic   institution, however   untraditional, and had
access to sources of external support for these tasks. In short, they
had power, power that was previously disguised by the norms and
values associated with community. One day that group left, ac-
knowledging combat fatigue. Still the College had some bric-a-brac
assets, exploitable for a few more years. On investigation, what was
fascinating to me was that, following a split, including the one that
prompted my departure, the people who stayed would find them-
selves able, in the absence of their antagonists, to proceed to solu-
tions which creatively incorporated the viewpoints of those who
had been cast out.




                                               Ann C. Rice
                                         (Faculty Spouse 1947-48)


                             A View from the Playpen



Frank was invited to teach German at BMC the summer of 1947. We
had spent a difficult and lonely year in Mt. Pleasant (which it
wasn’t) across the Cooper River from Charleston, South Carolina,
where Frank was teaching in the College of Charleston. We were
happy to leave. We packed up our summer togs, baby equipment,
three-year-old Johnny, and six-month-old Frances, and boarded the
train for Asheville where we were met by Nell Rice and Fritz
Hansgirg, a faculty member who owned a Cadillac. I was delighted
with our living quarters. We were in Last Chance, two rows of
three apartment units facing each other, Army surplus prefabs. We
had two bedrooms, a living room, bath, a kitchen with kerosene




An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                229

--- Page 258 ---
stove, hot plate and ice box, a small front porch, an open space in
front of the buildings where children could play safely, and a
clothesline in the back. What more could one ask? The children
played out front and also down near the Studies Building on a
grassy plot with swings and a brook that fed into the lake. All of
the little children at the College gathered there with their mothers.
(The son of Carol Brice, the well-known black contralto, came with
a nursemaid.) Informally, we started taking turns at playground
duty. First we constructed a small pen of chicken wire to contain
the babies, and then we enclosed the whole plot for safety’s sake, if
not for aesthetic appeal. (There were grumblings from Albers about
the eyesore of a fence of chicken wire and rough stakes.) The brook
was a great attraction to the children, who were constantly getting
their clothes wet. Why fight it? So we stripped them on hot days
and had a little nudist colony! For a real splash we would go down
to a small beach at a shallow part of the lake near the dining hall.
Johnny fell in love with the water and made friends with Robin
Corkran, as I did with Robin’s mother, Marion. When summer was
over, many of the children had departed, but we mothers who re-
mained realized that we still had a nucleus for a play group of
nursery-school age. It included the little girls of farmers Moles and
Trayer, Robin, Kurt Wallen, and Johnny. We collected art and play
materials, claimed a large unoccupied room upstairs in a lodge
called Black Dwarf. John Wallen, a faculty member, recruited stu-
dent helpers from his psychology class. Faf Foster, an art student,
offered his help. The group met two or three times a week for two
hours. Mothers took turns and gained a little leisure. We were an
unselfconscious “community effort” which kept going through the
winter and into the spring, when we added a huge sandbox to the
playground. Hazel Larsen, the photographer, found the children at
play photogenic.                                           |
     Our first Christmas at Black Mountain we bought a sled for
Johnny, just in case of snow. We awakened Christmas morning to a
miraculously white world. We went up and down and up and
down the road with Johnny, and then students who hadn‘t gone
home for vacation arrived. Sleds were scarce, so Johnny had many
a ride down atop a student, and a haul up the hill again. The front
yard provided a gentle slope he could manage himself. Another



230                  Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 259 ---
day I tied a laundry basket to the sled and put both children it it. I
  attempted to run with it down the hill but unfortunately lost con-
  trol, and the sled upset in a ditch. Poor little Frances had a cut un-
  der her eye. I hurried her down to the infirmary where a male nurse
  student was on duty. Frances was so attracted to his beautiful curly
  light-brown beard that she didn’t cry but stroked its softness while
  he fixed her cut. A couple of weeks later the temperature dropped
  precipitously. The lake froze over. The nursery group was pushed
  around on the ice on chairs and in laundry tubs. Everybody was
  out on the lake, sliding, pulling sleds, taking turns with skates. Na-
  tasha Goldowsky, who had been a ballet dancer before she became
  a physicist, was the exuberant star.
        For the most part, the disagreements of the faculty did not
  touch me. I loved Black Mountain. The children and I had a good
  life with good friends and a superb outdoor setting, and I could
  even get away occasionally from maternal and domestic duties,
  thanks to Nell and student baby-sitters. I enrolled in a bookbinding
  class with kind, gentle Mrs. Jalowetz. I can hear her patient admon-
  ishment: “Don’t work in the air.” I could go to an occasional con-
  cert or on a hike up the mountain with Frank to view the colors of
  October. That first year, we lived well. The College was not yet in
  financial straits. It is true that our wants were simple. What greater
- luxury could there be than wild blackberries with sour cream! But I
  knew that all was not well with the College. I felt sorry for Frank
  and Nell when they would come in late and exhausted from end-
  less and rancorous faculty meetings. Students arrived in the fall of
  1948 to find a decimated faculty. When Frank found a better oppor-
  tunity at Quogue, Long Island, in February 1949, I soon followed
  him, exchanging the beautiful mountains of North Carolina for the
 ocean at Quogue.
     The last time I saw Black Mountain as a college was in the sum-
 mer of 1952, when I took the children to visit Nell, and to escape
  the heat of Arlington, Virginia, where we were living. A Summer
  Institute was once again in session. I remember a giant of a man,
  Charles Olson, a lecture on Zen Buddhism by John Cage, the paint-
  ings of Franz Kline, the stoneware pottery of the Weinribs. We
  were disappointed to find the lake still polluted, but off we trekked
_ each day to a swimming hole on North Fork—Johnny, Frances,



 An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                 231

--- Page 260 ---
Nell, Alvin Few (the nephew of the cook), and I. There we often
met a charming young artist, Bob Rauschenberg, and his friend, Cy
Thiel. After cooling ourselves in the mountain stream, we would all
sit on the bank, selecting and admiring the smooth pebbles.




                                             Leo Krikorian
                                                (Student   1947-48)


                                     Sentence Deferred



Most of the local inhabitants in Asheville and the town of Black
Mountain disliked the school and the students because of its liberal
policy allowing blacks to attend. Very few of the teachers and stu-
dents were from the South. One day I visited the town of Black
Mountain with my Model A Ford, which cruises at thirty-five miles
an hour, and when leaving town was stopped, taken to court and
charged with speeding and reckless driving, was judged guilty,
then was slapped with a moving vagrancy violation, which means
the sentence was deferred and if I ever entered the town they could
lock me up without another trial and put me to work on the road
gang.                                                    .
    When leaving town my car would not start, a dead battery. The
two cops that arrested me had to push the car; while they were
pushing one was lecturing to me on southern hospitality.

--- Page 261 ---
Charles Bloomstein
                                   (Treasurer and Faculty 1947-48)


         Memories of Black Mountain College



My being a certified public accountant was no doubt the main rea-
son I was recruited. It was apparent to all, but not to me at the time,
that the plan was for me to become the treasurer of BMC. I was to
be part of the new leadership team, to be the chief fiscal officer of
the College. I was so innocent, even at thirty-four years of age, that
it was not until several months later, when I was taken aside bya
faculty member and told the facts of life about BMC, that I came to
recognize why I had been invited to become part of the College
“community.” Ironically, the then treasurer was the man I came
most to admire and respect on the campus. He was the single most
honest, straightforward, decent, indeed noble, person I met during
that year there. It was unthinkable for me to even consider replac-
ing him. Which meant, of course, that my advent was ill-fated, from
the very start.
     It required a certain idealism to go to BMC, whether as a stu-
dent or a faculty member. The students knew that the school was
unaccredited and that a degree from it would not be of much aca-
demic or economic value. So, it took an unusual parent to send a
child to BMC. The faculty member knew that BMC was not on any
traditional career ladder. Furthermore, the pay was minuscule,
even by 1947-48 standards. As I recall it, each faculty member re-
ceived $25 per month, with an additional $25 going to each family
unit and $15 for each dependent. Thus an unmarried faculty mem-
ber would    receive $50 per month,     a married   one   $65. Plus, of
course, $15 per month for each child, an apartment on the campus,
and the right to take all meals in the dining hall. So, few signed on
because of the salary scale. This idealism led to a number of con-
cepts which were very attractive but which I now believe were
impossible to carry out. BMC was often referred to as a community
of teachers and students, a voluntary democratic association. This



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                  233

--- Page 262 ---
meant that each member of the community had the right to be
heard on each community issue—including those that had pre-
sumably been adjudicated in prior years. Given that most students
stayed two years or less, and all prior decisions were subject to re-
view (and frequently justly so), it became very difficult to consider
any matter settled—and to move on to new issues that required ac-
tion.
      The assumption was that, BMC being a “progressive” school,
there were no rules. But when a female student went hitchhiking
for a week with a male student, at a free time, she discovered that
she was to be punished. She thereupon posted a notice on the bulle-
tin board stating, “At BMC there are no rules—but there are rules.”
And indeed, there must be rules if there is to be   a community. The
College was at fault for allowing the perception that there were no
rules and for not making clear that there were      rules. Questions
never resolved were how rules were to be agreed upon and how
implemented—and     how flexible. If the rules are not clearly stated,
how is one to know if one is transgressing?
     The responsibility of the faculty to the students was high-
lighted during my year by the resignation of the only sociologist on
the staff. Clearly, budgetary considerations meant that only one
faculty member could be added. The question became: in what sub-
ject? The senior art teacher, acknowledging that BMC was a liberal
arts college, nevertheless wanted the replacement to be an art
teacher, claiming that “everyone knew that sociology was on the
way out.” Not considered in making the decision was the fact that
several students were majoring in sociology, one being already
quite advanced. If the sociologist were not to be replaced, how
would these students be affected? A small school (ninety students
that year, large for BMC—with eighteen or nineteen faculty mem-
bers who also had to handle administration) is forced to make
difficult if not impossible choices.
      During the late summer   of 1948, just before I left BMC, I had
occasion to be chatting with two of the summer faculty—a painter
and a sculptor. Both were complaining, with justice, of the lack of
seriousness of the students. Both had come to BMC to teach bud-
ding artists. Both discovered that they were counselors at what
they perceived to be a summer-camp. Neither was pleased. In re-



234                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 263 ---
sponse, off the top of my head, I replied that one had to be tolerant
  of these neurotic youngsters. Whereupon the painter drew himself
  up, shoulders square, and said, “I’m neurotic too.” I took that to
  mean that he had his rights‘and that these had to be considered. A
  most succinct and relevant statement, I felt then and still do. The
  painter was once asked if he would accept a job at BMC, he being
  poverty-stricken at the time and sorely in need of a steady income
  no matter how small. He replied to the effect that he preferred New
  York, where the human hostilities went down into the subways
  and were somewhat filtered and diluted. Here at BMC, he said, the
  hostility was direct and immediate. Another trenchant phrase from
  the painter—one with which I learned to agree.
       A final note on the concept of BMC. An unstructured college,
  with enormous, if not complete, freedom, provides unusual oppor-
  tunities for those who are disciplined, know how to work, and are
  able to take advantage of the opportunities offered. But it can be a
  disaster for immature young people who lack self-confidence and
  who feel the need to be taken in hand and led. These can be de-
  stroyed in such a setting.
       This presentation of the difficulties, the inadequacies of the
  concept, and the emotional turmoil that was characteristic of BMC
  does not, by any means, exhaust the situation. For there were also
_ great benefits, at least potentially, in trying to carry out what still
  remains an engaging notion. The students during the academic
  year were, to my way of thinking, by and large intelligent and
  hardworking. The resident faculty (at that time heavily populated
  by refugees from Germany) were highly qualified academically—
  although the idea of progressive education was not something they
  had come across in Europe. And the summer faculty, when BMC
  was more like a camp than a college, was talented, creative, and
  exciting. It was a heady time.

Lyle Bonge
                                                 (Student 1947-48)


           Some of the Greats and the Grands




M. C. was a splendid creature given to great gouts of laughter,
funny little dances, and ridiculous rhetorical speeches to her cocker
spaniel, Blackie. She was also willing to play endlessly with the lan-
guage. Once we were trying to arrange a time for her and Bill Levi
to visit me in Biloxi. There was endless hemming and hawing about
when and how to go, and I finally shouted, “Stand not upon the or-
der of your going, but go quickly.” Immediately M. C. replied with
another quote, and we were off in a rush of splendid craziness yell-
ing with delight at each new quote. This went on for days as we
played with the language, bending and changing quotations to fit
our needs and leaving Bill completely baffled by our foolishness. If
we ran dry we would try to speak in correct Elizabethan syntax,
analyzing and correcting ourselves as we went along. It was a
grand sharpening of the sense of speech, and today I still have a
good understanding of my “thee’s” and “thou’s” and am dismayed
when someone prays in public and buggers up the formal address.
Perhaps this only demonstrates my own lack of seriousness. I don’t
know. I know that I remember the richness of those crazy times, the
sense of play and fun that added so much to the learning that for
me went on every waking minute.
     I am one of those people who believe that the doing of hard
physical work on a regular basis is important. It is, or can be made
into, fun. It clears the mind and soothes the nerves, reducing anger
and annoyance to a level of tolerable irrelevance—and all of this
was of great value in the intense and introverted situation that was
Black Mountain. In fixed groups of five we would do dishes after
supper for three days running, then after a fair rest (there were
many crews) we rotated through each meal in turn. There was al-
ways great enthusiasm, and we worked furiously: laughing, boast-




236                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 265 ---
ing, timing ourselves, and always making sure that the kitchen was
clean enough that the next crew would not complain. In a different
group we hauled garbage or shoveled coal or did whatever rough
labor needed doing. It was important to us because we goddamn
participated! We helped make the whole bloody enterprise go.
Whether we paid full tuition or minimum,       were male or female,
student or faculty, we did the work that made it happen. Where
else in the academic world could students have the particular de-
light of doing garbage detail with someone     like Bill Levi, Ph.D.,
author of a number of works on philosophy? While the rest wore
work boots or heavy shoes, Bill appeared in sandals, and his toes
would curl back in dismay as he delivered howlers of calculated
banality.
     I studied composition with Rondy, going once a week to his
study in a shack under the hill. I always called out and he an-
swered, “Yes Lyle, come on in.” Then we discussed language, al-
ways the magic of language. We went over my stuff—word and
phrase, syntax and connotation—and we dug furiously through
Fowler. Always we sought clarity and flow. Sometimes he would
read to me from the translations he was then doing from Catullus,
reading first the Latin and then the English, and discussing the flow
of each. Sometimes he played records of Beowulf, read in an ap-
proximation of the original tongue, and we would have a splendid
time digging out Anglo-Saxon roots and structures. Many years af-
ter Black Mountain,   with Jonathan    Williams,   I was   looking for
Rondy’s shack on Ocracoke Island and called out to him. Exactly as
he had before, he called back, “Yes Lyle, come      on in.” It was a
shock to see him then because he was absolutely unchanged. He
wore the same old blue shirt, the same worn jeans and rough shoes,
and looked not an hour older. It was a shock again when he de-
scribed to Jonathan details of my writings that I could not have re-
called. He had paid more attention than I myself had. He was a
man of great consideration and endless and complete attention. (A
sad footnote is that Rondy’s fine translations seem to be lost in an
attic somewhere. If they can be located, Norwood Pratt, who once
heard him read them at Chapel Hill, North Carolina, is interested
in seeing to their publication. He can be reached in care of Lyle
Bongé, 1099 Judge Sekul, Biloxi, Mississippi 39530.)



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                 237

--- Page 266 ---
Theodore “Rondy” Rondthaler on porch of his house on Ocracoke Island, N.C.
Photographer: Lyle Bongé.




                              Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 267 ---
There was none more mad and splendid than Natasha
Goldowski. When she lectured, her passion for the numerical mys-
teries of theoretical physics made it all momentarily clear to me.
She wrote furiously at the board, leaping and whirling about and
breaking chalk, and, enthralled, I followed along, fog before and
fog behind, the magic unrolling in a narrow band of clarity cata-
lyzed by her marvelous intensity. She repeatedly said that it was
not important JUST that one arrive at the correct solution: “Only if
it iss the most elegant possible solution. THAT iss goot physics.” To
go back: She and her mother had escaped to Paris from Communist
Russia while Natasha was still a child. There, she had financed her
education at the Sorbonne by dancing in the Folies Bergére, taking,
I was told, doctorates in physics and chemistry, and discovering
Goldowski’s Law #1 and #2, which related to the migration of met-
als within the matrix of an alloy during corrosion. She later became
an employee of the Air Ministry, and when her fellow workers
abandoned the city during the German approach, she bravely
stayed behind with the janitor to burn the secret files. She and her
mother escaped later to unoccupied France, and then to the United
States, where   she worked   on the Manhattan    Project. “One day a
friend sent me some fine French roller skates, and I looked at those
long wide halls and so I started skating. I vass zooming around the
corner and there vass Doctor Fermi. Such a tiny man. Of course I
hit him and ve fell in a heap! He vass such a gentleman, Doctor
Fermi. He tried to pick me up! He said, “Ach, mademoiselle are
you hurt?” Natasha was a substantial Russian woman, and we
always waltzed together while some chap played the piano. One
night as we swung wildly about the floor, hitting other couples
who flew apart, Natasha explained, “Ah, Lyle, our mass and veloc-
ity are greater than their binding force, and so the couple iss split.”
About then we rammed the piano squarely, running it over the pi-
anist who huffed off and thereafter refused to play if we came on
the floor together. Of folk like Natasha was Black Mountain com-
posed.
    BMC was a crazy and magical place, and the electricity of all
the people seemed to make for a wonderfully charged atmosphere,
so that one woke up in the mornings excited and a little anxious, as
though a thunderstorm were sweeping in.



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                  239

--- Page 268 ---
Mary “Mickey” Miller
                                                 (Student 1947-48)


                                            Early Directions



Amongst all the unique people at BMC, Alice was an original.
While others discussed concern over their ivory-tower existence at
the College, Alice had not learned this sort of self-consciousness.
She seemed unaware of being at the same time self-centered and
centered in herself. Amongst the highly sensitive idealists, she re-
mained an unsensitive pragmatist, unable or unwilling to speak in
the idiom of the place. This isolated her. Perhaps she was an anach-
ronism. To me she was a mystery. She was definitely not your mys-
terious female type though. A reddish-blond with freckles, she was
more of a female Tom Sawyer. I think she told me her father had
sent her to BMC because he thought she might fit in there better
than other schools she had been in. She didn’t, and I believe she felt
like an outsider, but she carried it off jauntily, preferring her own
style. She wanted me to teach her to play the guitar, but she was
left-handed, she explained, and we would have to reverse the gui-
tar strings and hand functions so that she could learn in her own
way. I countered that one has to learn unaccustomed movements
with both hands and so it doesn’t matter what one’s handedness is
in playing the guitar—it’s like learning a new language. This expla-
nation was fruitless, and so I had to learn to play the guitar her
way, upside down and left-handed, in order to teach her. Perhaps |
the trust we built during this somewhat frustrating project led her
to confide in me near the conclusion of our time at the College. It
seemed that she was depressed and had determined to commit sui-
cide. She insisted in such a way that I felt at least the feelings were
serious, although I can’t recall that there was any specific reason or
crisis which would call for this. She planned to carry out this dra-
matic act in a typically pragmatic way. She provided for the possi-
bility of changing her mind by choosing starvation on a mountain-
top as the method. She did spend a weekend on a mountaintop. No



240                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 269 ---
one went after her, and she returned without fanfare or much no-
tice by anyone. I’ve often wondered how Alice’s life turned out. I
never saw or heard of her again.
     It seems to me that my most memorable experiences from BMC
are ones which foreshadowed later directions in my life, and I still
feel bonds to some of the people I haven’t seen in forty years. I had
been an undistinguished art student at UCLA, and the work-study
program at the College had a big impact. It was the involvement in
all aspects of learning, from building a desk to philosophy of edu-
cation. It was challenging to learn about tools and sculpting from
Harry Holl. There was the gallantry of the garbage brigade, the
shoveling coal in the snow, the hostility when the furnace detail
didn’t get up on time. As a female, it was the first time I felt like an
equal participant in a community. There was camraderie, bitching,
and constant discussion about how to make the system and people
work, and whose responsibility it was. But the can-do I got out of it
all was why, a few years later, I was helping my husband build a
house—between [having] two children. It strained the bonds of
matrimony, but the house came out beautifully.
     Maybe the reason they let me in the College was that I was a
budding folksinger. I collected some songs while there—one from
Tasker Howard, “Ring Them Charming Bells,” and ten years later
made a record for Folkways with Bess Lomax Hawes. However, I
never got over my shyness as a singer in spite of Bimbus, who ex-
horted me to sing-out in chorus. I remember Krikorian (he could
preach revival-style) suggesting we go to the beer bar at the end of
the road where I might swap songs with the proprietor, who maybe
didn’t sing, because he offered to fly me over a few mountains to
visit Jean Richie (a collector’s source). After 1 managed a couple of
songs, he spread some sand on the pine planks and showed us
sand-dancing (no music and like clogging). I took Penn’s acting
class thinking it might help me perform, and eventually concluded
that my love for music didn’t include being on stage. I became very
interested in psychology in John Wallen’s class, which dealt with
group interactions. His idea was that group decisions could go be-
yond compromises, and with sufficient communication represent
the will of all. Iwas chagrined when I missed a class due to a cold,
and returned to be told that the class had decided if anyone missed



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                  24)

--- Page 270 ---
class, they would write so many words as to why. While I admired
the ideal of unity (the College struggled ad infinitum with individ-
ual-group conflict, and side-taking was intense), I felt its applica-
tion to me was a bust. However, years later I became a psychologist
and founded the Topanga Center for Human Development, which
reflected some of the humanistic ideas I first encountered at the
school. The Center came to the end of its time in 1981, partly as a
result of flood. I continued counseling, and embarked on learning
to write fiction, which brings me to M. C. Richards and others. I
first heard   of her teaching from   friends     Herb,   Rod, and   Marie,
who’d   returned   from   BMC   for California    summer     surfing, and
from Merv and José on their trips to see the West. They were al-
ways talking about writing, M. C. and the College, as if they were
still there. I felt no such involvement at UCLA, and decided to fol-
low my friends to the College. When I did get there and took a class
with M. C., we sat around the floor in a study and decided what the
class would be. Each student taught one piece of literature, and
class-time was spent discussing it. I remember Peggy’s day when
she and M. C. had a lengthy dispute, regarding the writer’s respon-
sibility to communicate, with Peggy laying claim to at least half of
the process. Such lively interaction, compared to the conventional
classroom, was a freeing revelation. Yet, people had concern about
an ivory-tower existence: that the place was a hothouse and the
blooms would fade in real life, that the place was socially irrele-
vant, etc. Even the ordinary life there had a potent quality, like tak-
ing it easy with my study neighbor, Lyle, who had genius for en-
joying such irrelevant things as watching clouds. Or Dick, at the
dishwasher, looking like a revolutionary in his black beard and tur-
tleneck sweater. And then there was the time Herb attacked the
meat locker, after he’d overindulged in wine and hadn’t eaten all
day. He couldn’t find crumb in the kitchen, and the big refrigerator
was padlocked. He sank a meathook in the heavy door. Next morn-
ing the authorities, such as they were, announced that whoever did
the outrage or knew about it should come forward. As far as I know
it remained a mystery.




242                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 271 ---
Elaine de Kooning
                                           (Faculty Spouse 1948 SS)


                                  de Kooning Memories



Bill had his first one-man show at the Egan Gallery in April of 1948,
the month I began writing reviews for Art News. Nothing sold from
Bill’s show, and my reviews brought in only two dollars apiece. We
were looking forward to the summer with trepidation. We were
penniless with no prospects. The previous four summers of our
married life spent in the city had taught us that living hand-to-
mouth, at which we were expert, was more difficult in July and
August than during the rest of the year when “something would
happen.”
     Bill’s dealer, Charlie Egan, in desperation, kept the show up
through May and June until it became an embarrassment to Bill. It
made things seem more hopeless. The show went unnoticed in the
press except for one short ardently favorable review in Art News by
a brilliant young critic, Renée Arb. As a result of this review a letter
from a stranger changed all.
    Josef Albers,   whom    we   knew   only in connection    with   the
Bauhaus, wrote to Bill, asking him to teach at Black Mountain Col-
lege in North Carolina for the summer. Bill would receive two
hundred dollars, room and board, and round-trip railroad tickets
for both of us; and we would each have a studio. All of our prob-
lems were solved. Seventy dollars would be used for Bill’s New
York City studio rent for the two months we’d be away; thirty-six
dollars would cover the rent for our coldwater flat on Carmine
Street where I had my studio, leaving us ninety-four dollars and
few expenses except art materials and cigarettes. We were over-
joyed at the prospect of a vacation in the mountains.
     At the end of June, in great good spirits, we packed our
brushes, paints, pads, jeans, bathing suits, one “good” suit for Bill
and two dresses for me and took off for Asheville on an overnight

From Vogue, December 1983. Reprinted courtesy of Vogue Magazine.



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                    243

--- Page 272 ---
train. At 10 p.M., a porter came around with pillows he was renting
for fifty cents apiece. Bill and I looked at each other and a silent
agreement was instantly reached: if we had only ninety-four dol-
lars for the whole summer, a dollar for pillows for one night was a
disproportionate luxury which we felt we should not allow our-
selves—a decision we regretted as the night dragged on and we
found hard surfaces and jutting corners wherever we twisted and
turned to rest our heads. We finally gave up trying to sleep and
read the mystery stories to which we were addicted at the time, all
through the night. When dawn broke, the train was making an in-
credibly steep ascent up the side of a mountain and a gorgeous,
heavy green landscape unfolded before our delighted eyes.
      Groggy but exhilarated, we were met at the Asheville station
by a stooped, long-haired, bespectacled student who drove us to
the College in an old station wagon. We were somewhat dismayed
by our first glimpse of the campus, which seemed rather desolate to
us—evocative of Faulkner’s South. There were isolated students
sunning themselves on the grass, looking, we thought, peculiarly
listless and forlorn. “I feel like turning around and going home,”
Bill said apprehensively. But Josef Albers’s hearty greeting lifted
our spirits. “Ach so! The de Koonings!” he smiled, embracing us
like old friends. With his fresh pink skin, sunny blue eyes, spar-
kling white hair, and genial German accent, he radiated cheer and
enthusiasm.
     We immediately felt at home when Albers walked us to our
cottage. We approved of the bare wood floors and sparse furnish-
ings—a table, a couple of chairs in one large room, a bed,.a bureau,
and a closet in the other. We strolled around the campus with Al-
bers animatedly describing the manner in which each building was
erected and the care in the choice of location. We very much liked a
small, empty, high-ceilinged building nestled in the woods called
the Quiet House. It was a place for meditation, Albers told us, built
by a student as a memorial for another student killed in an automo-
bile accident—the son of Ted Dreier, one of the founders of Black
Mountain College.
    A long, plain building, with two tiers of identical tiny rooms
barely large enough for the narrow cot, the sturdy work-desk, and
the chair in each one, exemplified the frugal spaces and the stark



244                  Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 273 ---
unadorned surfaces of Bauhaus design. Bill took one look at these
studios and announced that he would paint in our much more
ample living room. Albers, defensive about his response to the lim-
ited space, explained that most of the students painted or drew on
paper on the desks. I immediately decided to push my desk over to
the window, use it as a palette table, and paint on wrapping paper
tacked to the wall. The window, the saving feature of the studio for
me, faced a dreamily beautiful lake with lush dark-green foliage all
around it. Off in the distance, there was a long dock with students
diving off and splashing around in the sunlight. I made another
immediate decision to join them after we finished our tour of the
grounds with Albers, who now led us to the great hall that served
as the dining room,   theater, dance studio, and lecture hall. “Be-
cause of the school’s strict budget, there is no choice of food. It’s
very democratic. Everyone eats the same things. Whatever the chef
prepares. That way, there’s no waste.
     The main course that day was frankfurter-and-banana salad.
Bill was incredulous: “Looks like something Elaine would make.”
Albers and I beamed at each other over the mutual compliment.
“Very unusual combination,” Bill went on but ate his portion as
uncritically as Albers and I. He was having two bowls of soup be-
forehand, he explained, so that he could regard the salad as dessert.
    Albers told us that meals were announced with a loud bell, and
we agreed to meet him at six in the dining hall and returned to our
house to unpack and to set up our studios. We were sitting on the
dock deep in conversation when the dinner bell rang. To our
amazement and amusement, we saw people running from every
direction to converge on the dining hall. “Look at them run at the
sound of that bell,” Bill laughed. “Pavlov’s dogs,” I said smugly.
We continued our leisurely conversation and strolled over to the
dining room some twenty minutes later to discover that the food
was all gone—every scrap of it. From then on, at the sound of the
bell, Bill and I proved to be more than adequate sprinters and never
again came late for a meal. We circled around the dining room,
pleased to discover some familiar faces from New York: sculptor
Peter Grippe, painter Florence Grippe, and John Cage and Merce
Cunningham. Merce told us that he would be teaching a two-hour
dance class six mornings a week. I had taken dance classes, on and



An Anthology of Personal Accounts         }                      245

--- Page 274 ---
off, from the age of six and seized the opportunity to study with
Merce, enrolling on the spot.
      “There are going to be quite a few interesting visiting instruc-
tors. In fact, too many,” Albers confided. “Buckminster Fuller and
Richard Lippold were invited and said they couldn’t come, so we
got Charles Burchard from Harvard for the architecture class and
Peter Grippe for sculpture and now we just heard that Fuller and
Lippold are coming after all.” Albers shook his head, frowning. “It
will make for complications.” He was right. This was our first tiny
whiff of the feuds and intrigues that characterized the summer.
Albers proceeded to introduce us to a bewildering number of regu-
lar faculty members whose names we promptly forgot; and, for
weeks afterwards, we’d refer to people by profession and build—
“that tall photographer” or “that fat weaver.” A weaver whose
name we immediately registered was Albers’s wife, Anni, a distin-
guished, tall, thin woman with a small face and a winning smile.
We were invited to a get-acquainted party to be held later in the
evening at the Alberses’ house. We noticed that certain faculty
members weren’t at the party—our second whiff of the open and
veiled hostilities that provided a constant and oppressive under-
current that whole summer.
      The next morning, we visited the work areas and met with stu-
dents, some of whom     made an immediate impression on us: Ruth
Asawa, Donald Droll, Joe Fiore, Ray Johnson, Albert Lanier, Hazel
Larsen, Pat Passloff, Kenneth Snelson, Ray Spillinger, Susan Weil.
For some reason, I had no trouble at all remembering their names.
In the following week Bill’s class expanded to some ten students.
     Albers’s class in color theory and Cunningham’s dance class
were much larger—about thirty students each. The many evening
lectures—Winslow Ames on printmaking, Beaumont Newhall on
photography, Isaac Rosenfeld on Tolstoi, Erwin Bodky on
Beethoven, Edgar Kaufmann, Jr., on industrial design—were dress-
up events to which everyone came. The most spectacular of these
was Buckminster Fuller’s introductory lecture. “He looks stuffy,” I
whispered to Bill as Fuller, short, stocky, dapper, strode briskly
toward the podium. “Wait until he opens his mouth,” Bill, better
informed than I, whispered back. Bucky then whirled off into his
talk, using bobby pins, clothespins, all sorts of units from the five-



246                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 275 ---
and-ten-cent   store to make   geometric,   mobile   constructions,   col-
lapsing an ingeniously fashioned icosahedron by twisting it and
doubling and tripling the modules down to a tetrahedron; talking
about the obsolescence of the square, the cube, the numbers two
and ten (throwing in a short history of ciphering and why it was
punishable by death in the Dark Ages); extolling the numbers nine
and three, the circle, the triangle, the tetrahedron, and the sphere;
dazzling us with his complex theories of ecology, engineering, and
technology. Then, he began making diagrams on a blackboard. He
drew a square, connecting two corners with a diagonal line. “Ah,”
he said affectionately, here’s our old friend, the hypotenuse.” That
did it for me. “I’m taking his class,” I said to Bill. “I knew you
would,” said Bill.
     Bucky’s classes (all male except for me) were attended by a
tight little clique of architecture and engineering students from
Harvard—elegant, businesslike young careerists, very different
from the rest of the Black Mountain students who seemed to have
vaguer aspirations, nebulous and idealistic. Bucky also attracted
some intensely motivated art students, most notably the sculptor
Kenneth Snelson, who immediately comprehended what was new
in Fuller’s concepts and who made some innovations on his own
that Bucky found very impressive. The shimmering pale blue ex-
panse of Bucky’s eyes—immensely magnified behind his thick
glasses—had us all mesmerized. They were, to us, the eyes of a vi-
sionary, a saint, all-comprehending, all-forgiving. We loved him
and we hung on his every word. In turn, Bucky was wonderfully
responsive, free with his praise, totally absorbed in each of us as he
directed his fabulous gaze from face to face.
     One day, he held up two jagged, irregular lumps of metal. “If
these are put together a certain way, they will form a perfect cube,”
Bucky explained. The two lumps were passed back and forth be-
tween the students for an hour and a half with much discussion of
how and why they didn’t fit. Then Bill wandered into the room.
Bucky had a special regard for the intuitive approach of artists.
“Let’s see what someone from another discipline can do,” Bucky
said to the class and explained the problem to Bill, handing him the
two lumps. Bill hefted them and studied each one separately and
then snapped them together, forming the perfect cube on the first



An Anthology of Personal Accounts —                                   247

--- Page 276 ---
try. Bucky nodded approvingly, not at all surprised. “How did you
do it?” I asked Bill later. “Well, I figured all those smart engineer-
ing students had tried all the logical ways,” he shrugged, “so I just
looked for the least logical way.” Bucky, with his emphasis on how
things worked and his total disregard for the Bauhaus concern with
design—with how things looked, was a bit of an irritant to the regu-
lar faculty. Albers listened in on a class one day when Bucky was
using the specialized terminology of an automobile motor. “They
don’t know what you’re talking about,” Albers scolded. Bucky and
the students all turned puzzled faces towards Albers and then
burst out laughing. “Well, she doesn’t know those terms,” Albers
said in an accurate but somewhat lame attempt to save face.
     The main work of Bucky’s class that summer was planning for
the erection of his revolutionary geodesic dome. The students and
Bucky worked on the computations for the junctions of the six
“great circles” that would crisscross the dome in triangulated for-
mations. We figured out the tensile strength of the venetian-blind
stripping to be use and concluded that double strips were neces-
sary for the size dome that Bucky had envisioned. The school came
up with half the amount of money needed, enough only for single
strips. If done with the double strips, the dome would have arisen
by itself as screws were put in place, radiating out from the cen-
ter—but as we had only half the tensile strength needed, we all
knew the dome would not go up. “Let’s put it together anyhow,”
Bucky said. “One never knows the ways of the Almighty.” The
whole school turned out that rainy day to scoff as Bucky’s dome, all
fastened together, lay crumpled on the grass like wet spaghetti But
we all knew, as the whole world would know a few years later, the
dome was a brilliant success.
     The school activities engulfed us like a warm breeze. After an
early breakfast, Bill would go off to find his students while I took
Merce Cunningham’s rigorous dance class from 8:00 to 10:00 a.m.
From 10:00 to 12:00 a couple of mornings a week when I didn’t take
Bucky’s class, I sat in on Josef Albers’s color-theory sessions, fasci-
nating to me both for content and for Albers’s authoritarian style of
teaching, which struck me as hilarious. Evenings, I would regale
Bill with my imitations of Yuppy (Anni Albers’s nickname for him):
“Ach so, Mr. Dublin. You are smiling. You are finding something I



248                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 277 ---
am saying funny? . . . Miss Wilson, you will please stop filing your
nails while I am speaking. . . .” The students singled out would
quail and snap to attention. I don’t think I’ve ever seen an instruc-
tor more involved with teaching as a military performance. How-
ever, all of us learned a tremendous amount       from him that sum-
mer.  He began demonstrating        what   we   didn’t know    that we
thought we knew.
     “Here are four different greens. One of them is a green you've
been looking at all your life—Coca Cola green! Which is it?”
Looked at singly, each one seemed to be exactly right. It was un-
nerving. He had us write our names with our right hands, eyes
closed; then, left hands with eyes open (for left-handed students, of
course, the other way around) to demonstrate that an important
element in drawing is kinetic rather than visual. He had us turn
each other’s signatures upside down and slowly and laboriously
copy them to demonstrate how effects of velocity and spontaneity
could be achieved in a slow and deliberate manner. But mainly, his
class was exciting for the complex problems in the optics of color,
working with combinations of primary, secondary, and tertiary col-
ors: finding the exact complement of a brownish purple is not that
easy. He liked to give us strictly limited projects. Once, I attempted
to solve several of the problems he posed in one composition. Al-
bers snatched it from my desk and tore it up. “Helen (his name for
me) runs away with herself. She’s too extravagant.” I was thor-
oughly flattered by his vehement responses, extravagance being a
virtue in my opinion.
     Bill and Albers had diametrically opposed methods of teach-
ing. Albers preferred to talk to students as a group, Bill liked to talk
to students one at a time. Albers’s students sat at desks and worked
cautiously on small, neat compositions; Bill’s stood at easels paint-
ing boldly on large canvases. Albers presented his students with
the same specific problems; Bill waited until they had evolved their
own set of problems on canvas before discussing the range of op-
tions open to them. Since most of the students worked under both
of them, the different approaches proved to be stimulating rather
than mutually exclusive.
    Bill was gentle and sympathetic with his students in terms of
the possibilities inherent in their work; but at his own      easel, the




An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                   249

--- Page 278 ---
possibilities he unleashed were Furies. At first he was at a loss
within the bare walls of the cottage. In his New York studio, sur-
rounded by his previous work, he felt the necessary sense of conti-
nuity. Here, he was in a vacuum that he began to fill with pastels,
working feverishly on one after the other for a couple of weeks un-
til the walls were covered with them.
      Finally he taped a sheet of paper twenty-five by thirty-two
inches to a board placed on his easel and began to use oils, scraping
off the excess pigment at the end of each day to keep the surface
fresh and smooth. Bill was now working with a rhythm I knew
well. Flaming pinks and oranges and yellow would sweep across
the paper in ever-changing forms, eventually crystallizing in an
image that suddenly presented an identity. For most artists, this is
the moment of truth when they know the picture is finished. But
Bill, in those days, had a built-in discontent that denied that mo-
ment. Time after time, I had the familiar but painful experience of
coming home to find an image I loved blasted away. This seem-
ingly endless process was the method by which he had arrived at
the paintings in his first show earlier that spring.
     Bill resented having to spend time away from his embattled
painting, but once outside his studio, he relaxed and enjoyed each
day’s surprises. Not only the days but the evenings were full. John
Cage gave twenty-five concerts devoted to the music of Erik Satie,
of great interest to us because until then we had heard him play
only his own music—to which we were passionately devoted.
These concerts, three times a week, were festive occasions. Some-
times, Cage performed in the great hall but more often,.in his cot-
tage while we all sat around on the grass outside. The brevity of the
pieces assured concentrated listening; and John with delicacy and
precision, communicated his reverence for Satie.
    The Ruse of Medusa, a light little play by Satie that had been pro-
duced only once in Paris, was translated by the poet and ceramist
M. C. Richards for John, who wanted it done at Black Mountain
with Bucky Fuller playing the part of the Barton Medusa and me as
the Baron’s daughter, Frisette. Since her only words were~“Yes,
Papa,” I accepted. The handsomest student on campus, William
Schrauger, was chosen for an equally mute part as her suitor. The
novelist Isaac Rosenfeld played the part of the Baron’s surly butler.



250                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 279 ---
But it fell to Bucky to carry the play. His role was a kind of W. C.
Fields aristocrat who talked constantly in non sequiturs. Bucky’s
marathon lectures had made it clear that he had a prodigious mem-
ory, and we were not surprised at his great gift for comedy. It took
many rehearsals before we could perform our parts without break-
ing up at his droll antics as the scatterbrained Baron. Arthur Penn,
a student-instructor, was the director; and he was ingenious, in-
deed, at extracting characterizations from total amateurs. I was en-
chanted with the pert, mincing walk and fluttery gestures he de-
vised for Frisette.
     Since nobody else seemed interested, I designed the sets and
costumes—if design is the word; allocate might be a better one. Bill
transformed a huge stodgy desk and two nondescript columns into
magnificent pink and grey marble with a technique he had learned
at a decorator’s shop in Holland when he was sixteen years old.
The list of props—telephone,    candelabrum,    feather pen, inkwell,
magnifying glass, thermometer, and Bucky’s top hat—was           turned
over to students who were told they had a whole month to come
up with the things that were suitably oversized and flamboyant.
Mary Outten, a pretty girl who was a wizard with a sewing ma-
chine, made a marvelous white Victorian dress for me out of gauze
we bought in town for sixty cents a yard. And she sewed stripes of
grey satin ribbon to Bucky’s khaki work pants for a comical effect
of elegance.
      John Cage played the Satie score, and Merce created wonderful
little entr’acte dances for his role, a “costly mechanical monkey.”
Everything went flawlessly the night of the performance. The par-
ticipants all felt it was a sparkling event and that was that. There
were no photographs to speak of and no recordings. The sets and
costumes were saved for a while and then vanished. I wore the
lovely dress the following year in a movie called The Dogwood
Maiden by Rudy Burckhardt (who also appeared at Black Mountain
that summer to show his extraordinary city movies), and then the
dress vanished too.
     While I spent much time in student activities, Bill had also be-
come   deeply involved   with his students.    Too   involved,   Albers
thought. He said to Bill at the end of the summer, “You had ten stu-
dents. Six of them are leaving the College to go to New York City



An Anthology of Personal Accounts |                                25)

--- Page 280 ---
this September. Do you know anything about it?” “Sure,” said Bill.
“I told them if they wanted to be artists, they should quit school
and come to New York and get a studio and start painting.” Albers
seemed not to take this amiss. When he was appointed chairman of
the Art Department at Yale two years later, Bill was the first artist
to be hired to teach there.
     With so much happening, so many new faces, so many new
experiences, the summer seemed to roll on endlessly. We attended
more parties during those two months than we had in the previous
five years—small cocktail huddles given by the students in their
tiny studios; much larger after-dinner affairs in the faculty cottages;
and one great big dancing ball at the end of the summer when I
waltzed with Bucky, who paid me a compliment that could have
been plucked from one of his lectures: “You're a terrific perform-
ance per ounce!”
     “I'm getting sick of crowds,” Bill said one night at a particu-
larly noisy gathering. “Let’s take a walk.” After ten minutes on the
moonlit road, none of the lights from the school were visible to in-
terfere with the vast, heavy, velvety blackness of the sky, nor did
sounds of laughter and music penetrate the almost terrifying hush.
We stood still, enveloped by the awesome multiplicity of stars.
“Let’s get back to the party,” said Bill. “The universe gives me the
creeps.”
      Despite all of the unaccustomed student and social activity, I
still managed to come away with a roll of enamel painting on wrap-
ping paper—a testimonial to the durability of paint, if nothing
more. And Bill, struggling day after day on his one painting, finally
completed “Asheville.”

Greg Masurovsky
                                                   (Student   1947-48)


                                     Joy and Early Sorrow



 There was a cat that would sit on the edge of the dirt road, and
 would stare, with its back to the road, into the drainage ditch, and
 this for hours on end. When     I asked someone    about this cat, the
 person replied that the cat was neurotic. One day, he just disap-
 peared.
      James Leo Herlihy had offered to teach me to drive the jeep.
 We started off all right, but within minutes I was headed      for the
 lake. My foot was on the brake, but I was more accustomed to stop-
 ping moving vehicles by putting my foot down on the ground. J. L.
 Herlihy, frightened by the prospect of being submerged in Lake
 Eden, simply crushed my foot and the brake at the same time. That
 was my first and last driving lesson.
      There was one incredible night, warm, liquidy, adorned with a
  full moon that literally bathed Black Mountain in a silvery light. No
  one seemed to be able to sleep, the enchantment was too great. One
- felt like baying. It was Schonberg’s “Transfigured Night” incarnate.
       There are vague memories of chores on the carpentry crew, of
  going off into the forest and chopping down small trees; the smell
  of resins and underbrush. I basically loved Black Mountain, and
  really began to live an idea of “an artist’s life.” I enjoyed meeting
  with other people who were serious about art, and talking with
  them. First enthusiasms are wonderful, and they happened at
  BMC. I loved Ilya Bolotowsky, maybe because he was a Russian
  Jew like me? He was family away from home. When I first returned
 to the Bronx after a first semester at the school, I realized to what
 extent I was unhappy in my “real home” environment. I don’t
 mean that I didn’t love my parents; it simply wasn’t my “world,”
 the world that BMC had revealed to me. From then on my path was
 traced. I just wanted to give my life to being an artist. I was very
 lucky in meeting at the Art Students League in New York the



 An Anthology of Personal Accounts          ~                      253

--- Page 282 ---
Greg Masurovsky, pen and ink drawing: Massif des Belles Cotes les Nuages
Vu des Arcs—1800, 1984. 63 x 48 cm. Collection of the artist.




          254                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 283 ---
woman who was to become my wife, and with whom I came to
Paris. Her name, Shirley Goldfarb. She was from Altoona, Pennsy]l-
vania, and had the same need as I to leave home to become the per-
son stirring within. She lived her life as a painter, here, in Paris. She
died eight years ago, and is buried in the Montparnasse Cemetery,
a few streets from our atelier.
     Perhaps the most poignant moment for me at BMC was when I
received a letter from my mother in early May announcing the sud-
den death, by heart attack, of my older brother. He was just twenty
years old. It was a glorious, sunny day, with puffy white clouds
floating lazily in the blue sky over the pine tree tops. The road was
reddish clay colored, and the lake was still. A small auburn-colored
dog (was his name “Red”? “Jake”?) ran up and accompanied me in
my grief as | made my way from the Studies Building to the dormi-
tory. That day marked me forever with one invading thought:
“What's the point of anything?” I was devastated. Did I get over it?
I’m not sure.




                              Gerda Slavson Cooke
                                                    (Student   1948-49)


                                                    Broadenings



BMC has had a lasting effect on our lives. For one thing, Stan and I
became friends working on the production of Lorca’s “The Lament
of Ignacio Sanchez Meijas” that our group put on in the winter of
1948. As with all BMC productions, it was a wonderfully creative
experience. Betty Jennerjahn choreographed the dances, she and
Nick Cernovich designed the sets, Stan wrote and played the mu-
sic, and I read some of the stanzas of the poem. It was Stephen
Spender’s translation. One scene had dyed cheesecloth hung from
the ceiling, through which the dancers moved. As performers, we




An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                    255

--- Page 284 ---
didn’t see it from the audience’s view, but it must have been quite
effective.
     Some of my fondest memories were while working on the
farm. I used to bring in the cows to be milked at 5 a.m., just before
dawn when the fog was very thick all around and everything cold
and damp and vague in the mist. I would try to differentiate be-
tween a lying-down cow and a large wet black boulder. Once I had
decided it really was a cow, I would give her a pat to get her up and
she would feel warm and alive—have you ever patted a wet cold
rock? I would get half drunk from the fermenting corn up in the
silo [while] shoveling silage down to be fed to the herd. It was al-
ways hot and fragrant up there in the silo. The climb was an adven-
ture too. I would get done with my chores about breakfast time and
after a while Albers would not let me into the dining room because
I smelled like the farm. He also disallowed me into the dining room
barefooted (which I always was), so I frequently ate in the kitchen
where it was toasty warm and the food supply right at hand. I
learned how to drive a tractor at BMC, as well as how not to. Once
while backing the tractor with the hay rake behind, I managed to
get the rake at too sharp an angle and cracked the tongue. The
teacher/farmer at the time, Ray Trayer, was most understanding,
and just fixed it. I have been driving tractors ever since and never
did that again.
    The art classes with Yuppi (Albers) were definitely a learning
experience, including how to see what I was looking at. Yuppi in-
timidated me to such a degree that I never got the courage up to
produce anything for him to criticize, although later I did paint us-
ing what I had learned from him. I have always wished that I had
taken weaving classes with Anni, but somehow      [I] didn’t. Madame
Goldowsky taught me French for one semester. What a taskmaster!
I can still hear her. Her daughter, Natasha, was quite an influence
on me also, but not so intimidating. However, her informal cyber-
netics group was a brain strain for me, as was Max Dehn’s philoso-
phy class, although I could follow that a little better, and both of
these broadened my horizons a great deal. Charles Olson was also
influential and a most interesting character with his Tarot cards
and readings of Ezra Pound, and his own Call Me Ishmael. He ex-
posed the connectedness of all things in a way high school never



256                  Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 285 ---
did. Professor Bodky’s playing of the harpsichord made clear to me
  that I didn’t like harpsichord music, but I sat through many a loud
  recital of his, for what reason I cannot recall. I still dislike the
  sound.                          ;
       Yes, the effects of BMC will never disappear. The design of the
  house Stan and I built originated from the Round House where
  Stan used to spend time practicing on the grand piano that was
  there, and rummaging through all the music on the shelves lining
  the walls under the windows. We stretched out the roundness to
  make our house oval, and put large windows all around as in the
  BMC structure. We spent three years making the concrete blocks,
  using a mold that Stan made with removable plates so that all the
  different shapes needed (for round houses) could be cast. We tend
  to make whatever we want or need whenever possible. BMC
  showed us that one can create for oneself and needn’t always use
  predesigned and premade (usually shoddy) manufactured goods.
       During Easter vacation of 1949 I was invited by Mim and Eini
  Sihvonen to go on a camping trip along the ridges of some of the
  nearby mountains. We were hoping for the beautiful views from up
  there. The trip was to be for five days, but it was shortened by one
  because I had frostbitten toes due to wearing only sneakers. It
  rained the first three days and snowed the fourth. It was foggy al-
_ most the whole time, so no views at all. I remember carrying my
  sixty-five-pound pack with my muscles hurting but my mind over-
  whelmed with the grandeur of huge virgin trees and spongy moss
  underfoot, scrambling up rocky ridges and over gurgling streams.
  The Sihvonen sisters knew the area, and the last night we slept
  under a cantilevered rock awaking to snow covering everything
  and the air cold and crisp. There were violets here and there that
  had been blooming, so the blossoms had frozen overnight and they
  were like exquisite, perfect fragile glass. We arrived back at warm,
  sunny BMC with snow still on the tops of our packs—a different
  world. On that trip I learned to sleep dry in the open during con-
  stant rain, build a fire to eat warm food, stay quite dry and put on
  dry, warm shoes in the morning—along with many other things. I
  remember one night when the moon broke through the clouds and
  we were walking along a woods’ road singing “Sleepers Awake”
  and other Bach chorales. It was a wonderful trip from which I per-



 An Anthology of Personal Accounts ©                             257

--- Page 286 ---
sonally learned a very great deal about survival, our Earth, and the
fact that physical pain can be disassociated from emotions like joy,
awe, wonder and the appreciation of beauty and friendship.
      BMC has had a strong and lasting influence on the people we
were and are, as well as on the way we live. Our house is in the
woods and we built it ourselves, from soup to nuts, while working
full time and raising four daughters, who have become admirable
adults, also influenced by our BMC days. Stan is a programmer,
and I work for a small landscaping company. We spent only one
school year at BMC, but it was an important and influential one for
uS.




                                              Andy Oates
                   (Student 1948-50; Apprentice Teacher       1951)


                       A Photographer Looks Back



Bob Rauschenburg, Sue Weil, and I took all the same classes: study-
ing color with Albers, building backstrap looms for Anni Albers’s
weaving class, taking music appreciation with Erwin Bodky, who
would bounce from clavichord to harpsichord to grand piano ex-
pounding his theory about which pieces Bach wrote for the harpsi-
chord and which for the clavichord. And then on the grand piano
he would play a Beethoven sonata. We all sang in the chorus under
Charlotte Schlesinger’s direction. Donald Droll, Bob, and I were the
only tenors and the weakest part of the group. As a matter of fact
we were terrible. None of us could read music and we would have
to have special sessions with “Bimbus” so that we did not destroy
the whole chorus. Sometimes Delores Fullman would turn around
and sing tenor with us when we began to flounder. My third room-
mate, Ted Dreier, Jr., would sing bass next to me, and I would con-
centrate on singing in duet with him so that I was not so aware of



258                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 287 ---
Bob’s wild notes on the other side of me. We all loved these ses-
sions as much as anything else that we did at school. Mrs. Jalowetz
said, “But you sound like a hundred voices!” and added, “No one
can completely destroy Bach.” Once, with great nervousness, we
sang in Asheville, Miss Schlesinger pounding away on an out-of-
tune piano with her nose about four inches from the keyboard.
     Sue and Bob and Delores and I would go to Asheville to the
movies, sitting in the balcony because Delores was black. And on
Sundays we would go to the Negro Baptist Church in Asheville,
where we would get as wound up as the rest of the congregation in
this wonderful world. We would all be welcomed as strangers and
stand up to be introduced. I owe a lot of the liberation of spirit to
Sue and Bob, as I feel I do to Albers, who was an inspired teacher.
He was a difficult man, a Tartar from Westphalia which, he said,
was worse than being a Prussian. Bob particularly had trouble with
him. The other chief influence on my days at BMC was Hazel-
Frieda Larsen, who was our photography teacher. She was as
gentle as Albers was harsh, but somehow they both accomplished
the same goal: lighting a spark and making life exciting. When the
Alberses and Trude Guermonprez left BMC I became a full-time
photographer.
     The dining hall was the center of life at BMC. It was a lovely
large room with a screened porch running the entire length of the
building overlooking Lake Eden. It was not only used for eating,
where students and faculty mingled together at small tables, but for
concerts and dance recitals and poetry readings and plays. The first
of all “happenings” took place there. They were a combination of
food and drink in a party atmosphere combined with all sorts of
performances. Almost everyone or anyone would get involved;
even the shiest seemed to be able to overcome their inhibitions to
do the oddest things. I even did a dance sitting on a chair to harpsi-
chord music of Scarlatti. I’d never done anything like that in my life
before. People would come from Asheville and from the towns
around to witness these events. After my performance that night
some stranger came up to me and in a western Carolina accent said
“T don’t know what you were doing up there but I was with you all
the way.” I took this as a great compliment. We all seemed like
creatures from some other world to the local people; equally, they



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                 259

--- Page 288 ---
seemed the same to us. Our relations were strained, but occasion-
ally there would be some genuine crossover. I remember one of my
first meals in the dining hall, if not my first, and it sort of set the
tone for the years ahead. I was sitting at a table with Ted Dreier,
then treasurer, and his wife, Bobby; Mary Fitton, who had started
at BMC the same time I did; Mark Hedden, a writer; and Vernon
Phillips, a painter. Bobby was gently teasing me about my interest
in Chinese paintings and suggested that I go off into the mountains
and become an ascetic painter of misty landscapes. I said some-
thing in reply which I do not remember          now, which made         Mark
jump up from his chair and come around to my side of the table
and kiss me. I had never met him before and I was very moved. I
thought (which turned out to be true) that anything and everything
could happen here, and I could be a part of it all.
    Well, all was not sweetness and light during the three years I
was   there, winter and summer.      There    [were] intense joy and in-
tense pain alternating, it seemed,       in rapid succession.    It was    not
only true for me, but for most everyone. I am sure at times we all
took it for granted, but it was a very special time and place. On my
last day of school Tommy Jackson took me to the top of Mt. Mitch-
ell in his Model A Ford to see the sunrise. Later that day Wes Huss,
Charles Olson, Fee Dawson, and a few others saw me off with the
Jennerjahns. Olson said, “Well, there goes the last of the old
guard.” I am awfully glad I did not see those last days, although
they too had a special meaning for the people who remained.
     I haven’t mentioned all the important people who were there,
that were important to me and important to the cultural world of
our day. I think I should, and I will—with gratitude: Buckminster
Fuller,    Paul   Goodman,   Remy    Charlip,      Katherine    Litz,   Stefan
Wolpe, Merce       Cunningham,    Theodoros     Stamos, John Cage, Lou
Harrison, Carolyn Brown, David Weinrib, Karen Karnes, Max and
