                      Warren “Pete” Jennerjahn
                          (Student, Student Teacher, Faculty 1948-51


                                                Rhythm and Color



With dance figuring so extensively in the BMC                community, it fol-
lowed that rhythmic instruments were in demand. There was the
much-used   piano in the dining hall, but there were basic rhythmic
needs which it couldn’t fulfill. So we made drums. All possible bar-
rels, firkins, and similar resonating chambers            were brought into
service. We used commercial drum heads made for snare drums
and tom-toms. But we were also enthusiastic about starting out
with a full-sized fresh hide, which we eventually got from the farm.
    We did have a manual on treating of hides, but we were short
of some of the necessary tanning ingredients and tools to do the job.
After a few days, the hide began to smell, but the little crew of stu-
dents gamely worked at scraping down the hide. Each passing day
the smell was more powerful and sickening. It was never success-
fully employed as a drum head. I think, when the hide was ap-
proachable it was finally used as a prop ina skit.
    And as to our other drums, they would so lose tautness of head
as to lack any tone. Think of thumping on a half-filled hot-water
bottle. We discovered the remedy to tauten the heads: we would
run out into the kitchen and hold the drums over the big institu-



An Anthology of Personal Accounts -                                         261

--- Page 290 ---
tion-sized stoves. As the heads warmed we would give them little
plunks and hear the tone rise a bit with each additional time over
the stove heat. Unrecognized in the dance program was the relay
team which ran the drums into the kitchen to revive the tone for the
drums’ contributions to the next dance number.
     It was the summer of 1948 that I arrived at BMC and first en-
countered Josef Albers. This was after five years of college, and a
year working at a studio in Milwaukee as a designer in stained
glass. The thought of spending more time as a student was not ap-
pealing to me. I’d had it with carrying out projects proposed by
some instructor. And it’s not that these instructors didn’t have
credibility; they were good, second-string exponents    of the Mid-
west Ash Can School. My reluctance to work with still another
teacher was overcome, however, since my wife, who had formerly
attended BMC    and had studied with Albers, was most persuasive
and enthusiastic about our going there. The ‘48 summer ran into
fall, and Elizabeth and I stayed on: I, continuing to work with Al-
bers, and she, developing a dance program. I began to get my bear-
ings and to function with some effectiveness in the ways of this
new world. During what turned out to be Albers’s last year at
BMC, I attained the status of a kind of trustee. Albers established a
Friday-morning workshop which I was given to oversee, and dur-
ing his absences over the year on fund-raising trips, or whatever, I
was left in charge of his classes. In other ways, such as assisting
with his exhibition on campus and other practical availability, I
became a willing right-hand man. When Albers left to go to Yale I
inherited some of his classes. Joe Fiore did the painting portions.
Without apology, I can say that I did the best I could in teaching
those classes. I reapplied all that I had absorbed from Albers in as
much the same spirit and procedures as I could produce.
      We eventually left BMC to live and work a year in Europe, and
returned to the States to live and work in New York City. I
struggled along with several part-time jobs, the most significant of
which was teaching courses in color and in basic two- and three-
dimensional design at Cooper Union. I’m quite sure that my-color
course there was the first introduction of the Albers direction at
Cooper, and perhaps the first as a full-term course in the city. I
know that Si Sillman followed up with Albers’s material at Parsons.



262                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 291 ---
During this same period I also carried the message in a couple of
courses at Hunter College, as I substituted for Richard Lippold,
who was on leave. Finally, I landed a 54 summer session teaching
job at Adelphi College in Garden City, Long Island. This developed
into a position for that following fall semester. I was hired primar-
ily to establish a color course, which at that point hadn’t been of-
fered. The spring semester      I was     made   full-time, and   held that
status until I chose to retire at the end of May 1987. The point of this
is that Albers’s color teaching has gone on in some form in a contin-
ual stream since the early ’50s. At Adelphi, I taught a color course
every semester   throughout a third of a century. Of course, much
changed and freshened. I grew and matured. Other influences
seeped into my life and into my painting. There even came a time
in 1979—and for periods during several years following—when I
relinquished my ego and put myself in the hands of another memo-
rable teacher, Gerard Wagner. This was more opening and growing
for me, joined with the still strong and valid principles of Albers.
Much time has elapsed since I first taught what I learned from Al-
bers, but the nature of ultramarine blue doesn’t change, nor does
the principle that colors are relative.




                                             Judith Davidoff
                                                       (Student 1948 SS)


             Different Fare from Harvard Square



One day as I was walking along the hall in the Studies Building,
Bucky Fuller grabbed me and said I must go look at something. He
pulled me into what looked like a dark closet and enthusiastically
pointed to some material that he had just discovered glowed in the
dark. I don’t remember     exactly what the substance was, but I do
remember his absolutely childlike joy. I’m sure he would have



An Anthology of Personal Accounts |                                    263

--- Page 292 ---
grabbed the first person who came along just to share his discovery
with someone.
     I was there only one summer to play in a series of trio concerts
(as cellist) with Erwin Bodky, who also that summer played all the
Beethoven piano sonatas. John Cage performed just about all of
Satie’s works the same summer, and somehow managed to split the
campus into factions by bad-mouthing Beethoven before his Satie
performances. I was really shocked to think that we were expected
to choose “sides.” I went to all of the Beethoven and Satie concerts.
But Bodky retaliated, mildly, at a party one evening, when he did a
rather witty takeoff of Satie on the piano.
      BMC couldn’t have been more different from Radcliffe, where I
was a student at the time. It was a dramatic change coming from
the Ivy League, with its long traditions, exams, lectures, parietal
dorm rules—to this free-wheeling campus with no exams, chummy
faculty, discussion groups, and mixed sexes in living accommoda-
tions. (Of course, Radcliffe is very different now!) I was allowed to
take courses in exchange for my playing duties, and had a great
time in Isaac Rosenfeld’s Tolstoy class and Beaumont Newhall’s
“History of Photography.”
     An art student asking de Kooning what he thought about when
he did his paintings; de Kooning pointed to one of his canvases and
said it was inspired by the sight of a lady’s heel in an open-heeled
shoe, as seen from behind; the way the heel-flesh overlapped the
edges of the sole.
     Frequent performances by Merce Cunningham and dancers to
the music of John Cage’s prepared piano were wonderful, and
again, very different from the fare around Harvard Square.
      I remember “minimum house,” the building made for $1,000. I
think this included all the furnishings. Everything was made on
campus, including the handwoven fabrics.
     Josef Albers’s students were always looking for “matiére.” I
wrote a poem called “Matiére is There.” It was put up on the bulle-
tin board in the Studies Building, but soon disappeared. I always
wondered if it offended Albers (it was rather irreverent) and if he
was the one who removed it.

--- Page 293 ---
John Cage
                     (Faculty 1948 and 1952 SS; Resident 1953 SS)


                                                           Anecdote




         One day down at Black Mountain College
         David Tudor was eating his lunch. A student
         came over to his table and began asking him
         questions. David Tudor went on eating his
         lunch. The student kept on asking questions.
         Finally David Tudor looked at him and said,
         “If you don’t know, why do you ask?”


From Silence, 1961. Reprinted by courtesy of the author.




                                         Mary Fitton Fiore
                                                    (Student 1949-56)


                             “The Fra-a-grant Flowers”



My father went to Princeton at the time Woodrow Wilson was
teaching history there. These classes reinforced in Cy his natural
bent towards liberalism, and one step he took was to become a life-
long subscriber to the New Republic. As I grew up I began to read it
also and one year answered a small ad I saw there about Summer
Session at a place called Black Mountain College. I was sent bulle-
tins about the Music Institute and the Art Institute. They sounded
wonderful, but I assumed that I hadn’t the talents needed ever to
be accepted as a student in one. After graduation from high school

An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                 265

--- Page 294 ---
I went off to college. It was World War II. My boyfriend was called
up. My cousins enlisted. I made up my mind to join the Women’s
Army Corps, and did.
    After the war was over I went back to the same school, but I
was no longer quite the little Ohio girl. I lived out of the dorms, I
got a new boyfriend, and good grades. But the boyfriend proved to
be unable to complete the sexual act of love. I took that hard in
some     way, in an obscure, not conscious not understood        sense of
feeling responsible and thus fearful or mistrustful of my full pow-
ers—of whatever nature—as a woman. I quit school and got a job. I
read D. H. Lawrence, Henry James, Virginia Woolf, Politics maga-
zine. I went to live with my mother’s sister and her family and
worked at a day nursery behind the Capitol building in Washing-
ton, D.C. Then I remembered those bulletins of the Summer Insti-
tutes at BMC, and sent away for fresh material. I applied to become
a student there under the GI Bill and was accepted, thus becoming,
as I understand it, the only student the College ever got in response
to an ad.
       In February of 1949, as a student at the school, I joined a chorus
Bimbus led. We were few, but we worked           and Bimbus drew out
every bit of musicianship each of us had, more than we thought we
had. We were invited to sing at the Negro Y in Asheville and went
off one Sunday. We sang first, parts of a Bach cantata, and to my
chagrin, I cannot now recall which one, although I hear it sounding
someplace in the back of my mind. The group gathered at the Y lis-
tened with attention and applauded our small sound generously.
Then their chorus sang. A rich, deep, black sound of a traditional
spiritual, “I Been ‘buked and I Been Scorned” reached into our very.
bones. After more spirituals, we socialized and shared the refresh-
ments. On another day I had an errand to do at the Sears in
Asheville. I was thirsty, and took a drink of water from the white
drinking fountain. Oh, I knew about Jim Crow, although reared in
Ohio, but I hadn’t before tasted the bitter flavor of white water.
       The next year Alvin Charles Few’s school had a fair one eve-
ning. Malrey, June, Vera, and I went         to it. There were    various
booths, and I went into the fortune-telling one. The woman inside
scanned my palm and foretold that the future would hold for me
several miscarriages. Later, trading notes, I learned from Vera and
June that each of us white women was given the same prediction. I

266                      Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 295 ---
admired that fortune teller. What a subtle reading she gave each of
us.
     Lou Harrison invited some of us to the gate house to com-
memorate Saint Cecilia’s Day. The gate house had no electricity so
we had candles. It did have a piano (of sorts) and so we had music.
Lou gave a class in the Round House where we talked about tuning
of instruments and the virtues of equal temperament versus just
intonation. Then we had to tune. It was so difficult and so ear-open-
ing. He had a series of brass bowls which gave beautiful tones
when struck. He also used them upon occasion as bowls to eat
from, and once in a while would invite Joe and me up to his apart-
ment in the Studies Building to have peaches and strawberries after
supper. He always picked the night when Olson’s class met. There-
fore, I, being   a member of Olson’s class, would, after the fruit and
berries and some music played by Lou on his piano, thank him and
leave. Thus he would then have more music and some time alone
with Joe, to whom he was attracted, possibly as much as I was. I
loved Lou and admired his charming arrangements.
      After Delores Fullman returned to Chicago, Mrs. Jalo had no
singing students. I roused my courage and asked whether I might
study with her. She said yes and we began lessons with Vaccai—
intervals, phrasing, ornamentation. Then, I, of no great voice, began
to study and to sing Schubert, Schumann, Hugo Wolf. “My body is
good,” (yes), and “the fra-a-grant flowers”—phrases she gave me
to practice placing my voice properly with the vowels. Together we
worked to improve translations of the Schubert songs I sang; Jalo
believed in singing in the language one understood, the words
were important. I also struggled with the difficult piano accompa-
niments as I did my practicing in the Round House.
    Many of us often went barefoot at the school, and I once asked
Mrs. Jalo whether she minded my coming to her house for my les-
son with no shoes on. She considered for a moment and then said
no, she didn’t mind because my feet were so childish.
     I cherish a phrase she once spoke to me in the back of the din-
ing hall after Rauschenberg and I each sang solo parts in a Bach
performance      for the school. The performance   was   poor, his and
mine notably so. Jalo’s comment to me was, “Now Marylein, do not
be discouraged.”
    “no jah no jah”

June (Rice/Lane) Christensen
                                                    (Student 1949-50)


                             Dance at Black Mountain




A dance class with Katherine Litz was participation in the creative
process. Whatever simple movement started the class was the germ
out of which composition emerged. A plié, add an arm, a twist of
the head, change direction, the other arm, torso position, change
dynamics, level, tempo—and       at the end of a two-hour     class, the
group involved was performing a Katherine Litz composition.
What Katie gave me in my search for what I expected of myself was
infinite possibilities. I learned, in time, how to allow myself the sus-
pended space and moments needed to let my kinesthetic body ex-
plore the direction the dance, and life, were calling forth. Katie was
constantly alert to movement     patterns all around her, and she fo-
cused my attention on the art of seeing. A favorite exercise was to
follow someone and copy their walk, their posture, their gestures,
the tensions they carried, until you were so inside their being that
you began to feel what they felt, then move as that person. It is
much the same to move into a piece of music or a tree, a flower, or a
rock. From this endless source of possibilities I have created my
dance, as well as an understanding of myself. I have had four great
teachers in my life, and Katie was the first. She liked working with
me because I moved the way she felt. From my position, it was as
though I was inside of her head second-guessing what would
emerge next in the sequence at hand. She validated a part of me
that I had never allowed expression, and in a way, that is exactly
what the Black Mountain experience did for me as well.
     Dancing at BMC in 1949-50 meant a good solid Graham tech-
nique class in the morning with Betty Jennerjahn, and each after-
noon an opportunity for anyone interested to work on composition.
We gave concerts whenever we were prepared. It was amazing
how easy it was to get someone to compose music for a number
(Joe Fiore and Mark Hedden come to mind), and how easy to have


An Anthology of Personal Accounts ©                                 269

--- Page 298 ---
sets or props or lighting emerge out of the creativity and interest of
the community. Programs got printed and audiences arrived.
     Merce Cunningham came down in the spring before Katie ar-
rived, and I discovered muscles that had never been noticed. There
was an excitement about physical perfection around Merce. We
worked so hard and felt so grand. Merce’s work implied that you
do not lift your leg unless you can lift it all the way, and we tried.
Then Katie came in the summer and Katie’s work implied that it
does not matter how high you lift a leg, it is how you feel when you
lift it that is important. Merce was using his “dance by chance” cho-
reography, and Katie was evolving composition from some organic
source. These two polarized views were the basis for my work all
throughout my teaching career in dance and carried over as essen-
tial when I started my research work in alternative education. The
better the instrument is tuned the purer the tone. Merce gave me
that drive of expression, but it was Katie who gave me the song.
      BMC is where it all could happen. It is where I grew up. There
are SO many memories: working in the kitchen with Malrey, Cor-
nelia, and George (Vera Baker Williams and I took over the dieti-
cian job for that year); the excursions into the farmers’ market and
exploring the neighborhood for fresh produce; the parties that
transformed the dining hall into artistry and abandon; driving the
garbage truck; exploring the apple orchards above the farm; swim-
ming in the lake; experiencing first-hand what racial prohibitions
existed in the South; the production of Marriage on the Eiffel Tower
by Jean Cocteau. Most of all, it was the people, the interactions, the
unique sense of responsibility that comes from living in a self-sus-
taining community, and being part of something that felt impor-
tant.
     Dance was my art form, but it also became a vehicle for educat-
ing. It was a way to teach process. Black Mountain College showed
me that there is more than one way to learn and more than one type
of intelligence. It took me many years to fine-tune my understand-
ing of learning modalities and how to use this in the classroom, but
understanding that learning is multidimensional and that-it is a
process has been the basis for my teaching. My whole career would
have been bland compared to what emerged because I had the
BMC experience.

--- Page 299 ---
Fielding Dawson
                                                  (Student (1949-53)


                           How Stefan Got Me to Sing
                                   at Black Mountain



Wes Huss was the Director of Theatre at school (I see his wife Bea,
baby in arms, crossing the field in front of the Lodges), and when
he decided to produce Brecht’s The Good Woman of Setzuan, wanted
to cast me in the lead, and we had some nasty tangles. But he was
persistent, ever see him use his charm? Don’t miss it, one of those
directors who can act. Cynthia was to play the opposite lead and be
my love (she was anyway, despite the violent affairs she had with
Tommy, Tim, Victor, Dan, and Creeley [before Jorge]). I accepted.
Although angry, didn’t mind playing the role of a pilot, but I was
also to sing several (no less) songs, and I hadn’t sung since church,
in Kirkwood, Mo. I was in a lot of plays in high school, but I hadn’t
sung on stage—ever, so being as I was going to sing, I would go to
Stefan for lessons, everything being decided for me, I went along.
You know, until the curtain went up and I was on stage, and but
for the prompter the play would have been the utter disaster I in
part wanted it to be, because I hated it because it embarrassed me.
And in the beginning I trudged up to Stefan’s house, with a heavy
heart and a head full of gloom. Hilda opened the door, cheerful:
amused. I went in. She (academic or not, she was young and there
wasn’t a straight guy at school who didn’t miss seeing her go by)
turned, gestured to Stefan at the piano, and departed.
    Their house was two storeys, wood frame, dark shingles. Nice.
Up the road on the side of the mountain.
    I crossed the room to him, who rose from his piano (grand),
and greeted me. Typical: effusive, head tilted, eyes bright, big grin.
Arms out, fingers spread, seeing I was nervous, assumed a look of
amusement, and slight reproach. Said I was nervous and I was
nervous, said not to worry and I was worried, said he’d help me to
learn to sing—we, together. We would do it together. Sit.


An Anthology of Personal Accounts »                               271

--- Page 300 ---
I sat.
    A small stand on top of which was a large plant, on a large,
round copper tray, to my right. In the front room. Good-sized with
windows. Sunny. Stefan very happy there.
    He had written music for the play. Wes had decided not to use
Kurt Weil's.

      On a certain day
      As was very well known...
      The poor woman’s son
      Will gain the golden throne.

Stefan at the keys, I, dutiful, beside him. He began to play and sing
the above lyrics, and as I could read music (Dan was teaching me
trumpet),       I read    along,   hummed a bit. Louder,      he said,   so    I
hummed louder, then sang, a little, and he said louder, stronger, so
I kinda did that and he got up, reached across in front of me, took
the plant off the copper tray and put the tray on top of the piano,
put the plant back on the table, sat down and began to play telling
me to sing loud, and strong, which I didn’t do and he stopped play-
ing, turned, put his hand on my shoulder, and said Fee.
      You      must,     he said, SING!   Whereupon   he turned,      and     as
both his hands hit the keyboard, he began              with    that   sort of
yell he had ON A CERTAIN DAY, AS WAS VERY WELL
KNOOOWWWNNN ...
     Sat back. Laughing at himself. Looking at me. I nodded. Unh
huh yeah right, almost blushing in amusement and fright. He sat
forward, fingers struck the keys, I sang pretty loud LOUDER he
yelled and began singing again as I sang louder, but still self-con-
scious and yelled SING FEE, SING! Which I did, a little louder and
then some, but still—he stopped. Jumped to his feet. Grabbed the
copper tray, and holding with his left hand smacked it with his
right BOOOONNNNNNNNNGG shook the whole house includ-
ing foundations, LIKE THAT! Put the tray on the piano, sat down,
glared at me, began to slam out the music. I began to sing. I mean, I
sang. STRONGER, he yelled, and stopped singing, but kept-play-
ing and began to yell and I mean I yelled that song JA JA he laughed
as I cut loose, THAT’S IT! and—a force, a call I never knew came
out of my throat, I saw his face shine, as he played, and I sang, until



272                          Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 301 ---
the song was over, and we fell silent. He began again and I sang
along, loud and strong GOOD GOOD he cheered, and finished be
began again and I sang with him, clear through, I loved it, and after
I'd finished, Stefan sat back, looked at me, expression warm, ten-
der, triumphant.
    “Good! See? What a good voice you have!”
    I might have reddened. I’m sure I did, in my pride. But I ac-
knowledged    what   he had said because      it was   true, and it was
Stefan who had done it.




                                Black Mountain Defined


Black Mountain was freedom.
And within that freedom I developed a discipline in drawing and
writing that involved listening and seeing with such continuous
intensity it became my way of life. As much an influence on me
today, and every day, as it ever was. Black Mountain was not some-
thing you grew out of. Like freedom, you grew into Black Moun-
tain.
      It had the vast, open space of early childhood: we lived that
pure, every day. It was a lot like Paradise, not involved in the ac-
cepted, understood geopolitical sense of reality.
      Black Mountain was the people who were there, which ex-
plains its sudden    changes.   From   1950   or, for sure,   ‘51, it was
Olson’s until it closed. Very different than Albers’s.
    With its lake, farm, mountains, shingled lodges and buildings,
in its community way of living a lot like camp, in both senses, but
as Dan has said, the school was the people who were there. It had
an organic understanding of itself being transient. People came and
went. The list of famous names of students and faculty (that I am
sick of), gives a false—wrong—impression, that leaves, however, a
lasting mark. Too bad, because there were all kinds of people there.
And all those are ignored: Harvey Harmon, Tim LaFarge, Andy
Oates were   Black Mountain,     like Victor, Nick, Phyllis Franklin,



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                    273

--- Page 302 ---
Trueman MacHenry, a real nice guy. Peter Nemenyi, who studied
physics with Max Dehn, and went away to Princeton around 1950,
sad to say goodbye but he was so happy, yet so sad. Leaving Black
Mountain, one didn’t do it every day, one didn’t want to do it at all.
Ever. We said goodbye to Nick in front of the Dining Hall no! Out-
side North Lodge, across from the dinner gong, and rear entrance
to the Dining Hall. We embraced him, and Herb. Seeing the car go
down that road, a vivid identity went out of my life, that day. With-
out Nick, the school would adjust to itself, which was its style, with
a vital organ gone. Yet he had to go. I knew the feeling. I was next. I
had come alive in Paradise, I’d changed a lot, matured, but I had to
go. Students and faculty on me, I was getting too good. My draw-
ing and writing were indeed good, maybe too good, maybe those
people were right. Well, too bad for me.
      I was drafted.

      The basic, most accurate criticism of the school, that it was out
of touch with reality, not involved in matters of the world, is true.
But that’s all. Nothing else can be said, there is no other criticism
possible. The people who manipulate the school for their own ad-
vancement,    who      never went   there, misunderstand   that criticism,
valid then, still is, always will be. Inside that criticism, like a little
vein, is a tone, an aura, a note, or jealousy, and it has that vindic-
tive, crease tinfoil quality where freedom equals irresponsibility:
Don’t want too much.
     If you went to Black Mountain from July 15th, 1949, until it
closed, you learned a lot about art and its various forms, you
learned nothing about the Real World. Black Mountain, it was true,
did not prepare you for that, and this point of view has a lot going
for it, but quite a lot not. The Reality and World defenders are arro-
gant in their reach, and intent. They mean         that Black Mountain
being out of touch with reality and the world, nullified and ignored
it. Wrong. Black Mountain was a place of its own being, like free-
dom, it didn’t nullify or ignore The Real World—it was away from
it. The world wasn’t there, like the other side of the mirror, or be-
yond the horizon (that fascinated Melville [and Olson, and me, and
Black Mountain]), every aspect intangible, invisible, except the lush
natural Paradise that it was.
     People who didn’t go to the school will never understand that,

274                       Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 303 ---
like freedom, the school was atemporal, which gave it its transpar-
ent character.
     If you didn’t look where you were going, at Black Mountain,
you might not know where you were. It was of the very essence of
dreams. Other aspects—its fossil and mineral life—lent an air of
Before History. In its boundless, often difficult to contain sense of
discovery, Stan Vanderbeek whittled the bamboo pens he used to
draw and letter with. Students cut the collars off white dress shirts,
dyed them fuchsia, chartreuse, for a trademark,        like the logo on
school bulletins: a circle within a circle, bringing cows down from
the pasture, Vic Sprague (biologist), and his quart Mason Jars full
of Georgia Moonshine, to the tune of a thousand frogs and crickets,
as I gazed across the lake deep into the night—talk of reality, and
the world.
       The story is within the crystal, not without. All the interview-
ing of former students and faculty, including a book and exhibition
of our works of art, are but shallow reminders, dim reflections. It is
too bad, and may seem unfair, but so Black Mountain was, and if
you weren't there you will never know, or understand.




                                                        Interludes




The dirt road into the college began at the white fence, went by an
empty wooden bungalow with a piano inside; the road passed be-
tween the softball field and the abandoned       tennis court, rose be-
tween the Dininghall, North Lodge and the Office, made a circle
around the house where the woman I had a funny thing going with
lived; the dinner gong in her front yard (a three pole pyramid with
a hanging iron bar); the road turned and passed the Quiet House—
back in the trees (“A place for people to think or worship” the bro-
chure said) passed the new Chem Lab and the Studies Building on

printed courtesy of the author.


An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                  275

--- Page 304 ---
a turn, and moved up the mountain passing wood and stone
houses where the faculty and married students lived; in heavy
woods it curved over a couple of bridges and ended up on a rise at
the farm. It was on this road, just below the Studies Building—un-
der the Chem Lab that M. C. decided we would have a game of
poker. So we got table and chairs, the cards, some beer and got it
going. The afternoon was sunny, and we were in good spirits, of
course cars or trucks stopped, and angrily had to go, back down
into and out of the circle, head down the road, leave the property,
and on the asphalt road drive to the back road that led to the farm,
another rocky road, but very spacious and country-like, with the
farm always ahead in the distance. I walked it many times, to bring
in stray cows. The Potshop was near there, just away from the is-
land at the end of the lake, the cows liked it there, push into the
bushes and gaze into the Potshop at girls and boys throwing. Curi-
ously, through a combination of openings between bushes and
trees, and small clearings, like fields, we could see a corner of the
Potshop clear across the lake from the Dininghall porch, and I re-
member one day at lunch Farmboy jumped up and yelled, THOSE
GODDAMNED COWS! We looked and there were three cows near
the Potshop, making their way to the lake for a drink of polluted
water and get stuck in the mud up to their chests. He ran out of the
Dininghall, raced up the shortcut to the road, followed the road by
the lake, went down the stone steps to the Library, through the tall
grass at the foot of the Studies Building towards the Potshop where
he intercepted the cows bouncing rocks off their skulls to our out-
cries, our cheers and laughter, as distant to him as his snarling
curses were to us. Alarmed, angry, and wounded, the cows lum-
bered rather agilely back up the mountain to the farm. He returned
to lunch furiously, glaring at us as we laughed.

     We teased Clark pretty much, and though he was straight, he
enjoyed it, and as he and I were friendly because we were midwest-
ern types, we played catch together, etc., we could communicate, to
a point, and sitting on the steps to the Dininghall by the tree and
the millstone, that day, I got the brilliant idea of saving time so we
could have two more daylight hours to work, for the Work Pro-
gram, which is how I proposed it to the faculty, during a meeting; I



276                  Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 305 ---
was an important member of the Work Committee, and I clinched
it saying as we were outside of world time anyway, it didn’t affect
us, why not operate on our own time? Charley thought it was great,
he loved to fuck up time, but Clark Kent, who was also on the Work
Committee with me, he got very excited—laughed and shouted,
clapped his hands, did a dance, etc., which meant we could stay at
the tavern until two in the morning, our time.
    Jack Rice, head of the Work committee, of course saw through,
we often got drunk together, he empathized, and endorsed it.
Other student and faculty members on the Committee also en-
dorsed it, my practical plan, and though there were objections from
the cooks because of delivery schedules from outside, I reasoned if
it was four to them and two to us, so what? They finally said okay,
it was crazy but okay, and the faculty and student body likewise—
all in fact everybody but one.
     The dear old stubborn wife of the marvelous old scientist Max
Dehn insisted daylight saving time would interfere with Max’s
diet, and in no time she convinced other faculty members, and my
grand plan was vetoed at the next faculty meeting.
     Max died while I was in the Army. They buried him in the rho-
dodendron below the reservoir.
     I had never really known him, but we—I never took any of his
classes—but we were friendly, always greeted each other, and oc-
casionally stopped to talk a bit, on the road, or the path.
    In his German accent, “How are you?” He had a little voice.
    “Fine,” I stammered, in awe of him.
    He gazed up at me. “Are you writing, painting?”
    I nodded. “I’m working on a book, and drawing, and doing
casein pictures of buildings.”
     He became very enthusiastic; his whole face brightened, and he
took my arm—”Dot’s good,” he gravely said. “Dot's good.”
    Just before I was drafted it was clear, though, that he was fad-
ing out.
     He loved to walk in the woods and up the mountain, and I very
intensely see him in his life, walking through his life in physics
with Einstein in Europe and America, silently dreamily flowing to
sleep at Black Mountain. Dear old man.
     One chilly gently misty autumn morning I was sitting on the



An Anthology of Personal Accounts         |                     Zhi

--- Page 306 ---
boulders at the top of the mountain, gazing down the long field at
my school in the valley, when I heard a muted rustle, and turning, I
saw Max on the path among bushes and rocks and trees, by the
barbed wire fence I’d helped string. I climbed down and we faced
each other. His cheeks were drawn but pink; his eyes were deep
and very bright. His white hair was windblown, and the collar of
his jacket was partly turned up. He was a little stooped. He smiled
to me. I smiled hello, and murmuring hello in return, his bent little
body shuffled past me and disappeared in the fog.

      Sometimes when I walked down the road from the Studies
Building, at night, with the distant light on the pole ahead—where
the path to the Dininghall intersected the road—often the pole light
was out—I would pause in the darkness, my bare feet almost feel-
ing the touch of snakes. The darkness of cloudy moonless Carolina
nights was   so dense it was   as if it was behind vision, my hands
were out as before a wall, standing there, I felt Iwas within some-
thing; the brute rising power of the mountain to my right, the dark
lake to my left, and the stillness—the stillness within which I stood
terrified me, yet astonished me, and I somehow, slowly gave in to
it, and began to receive darkness within darkness of the unearthly
validation of the earthly—so old, so old, older than old—the still-
ness of an archaic gulf, not yet formed, but so human it couldn't
find a form, the devastating feel of a primal valley fed by creeks and
rivers come down a black mountain from a dark and bloody sky:
nucleus. It was warm, and I listened to it all, I felt it all, deeply
awed by the slow prehistoric inner shifting stirring rock, hundreds
of miles beneath my feet expressed in the softest night moisture on
my half naked body, and the vast still threshold of the universe at
my fingertips, against the base of my skull—the reservoir.

Helen Frankenthaler
                                                       (Visitor 1950 SS)


                      A Few Days at Black Mountain



After a few weeks in Provincetown       that summer,   I headed south,
first to visit my New      York    City roommate,    the actress Gaby
Rodgers, at the Abington Theater, then to Black Mountain to visit
Clem Greenberg, who was doing a summer stint there.
    Postwar days seemed particularly grisly down there, yet shin-
ing through was a very lively (if a bit floundering) bohemian striv-
ing for experiment, learning, and quality. Everyone seemed bum-
bling along in that unique BMC style that they were famous for: in
the name of Art and Creativity. During that brief visit Imanaged to
do a lot of works on paper.
     In retrospect,   I remember   electricity at a premium—lots      of
flashlights and candles. And there was one horror memory: poison-
ous snakes coiled in the dark that slithered across the road, visible
by flashlight, as I walked home for the night. (I think this is one rea-
son I didn’t stay longer. I’m not a very good camper.) As for the
food, you can imagine.
      Two memories are refreshed by photographs I still cherish: one
is end-of-the-day drinks in Clem’s office. The groups varied, but
this photo shows Theodore Stamos sitting on the floor, Clem at his
desk. Another snapshot was taken (I think by Cora Ward) of Clem
and me at the swimming hole, holding our heads under the water-
fall.
      Automobiles were hard to come by (the gas situation). One tre-
mendous “luxe” outing was made to a mountaintop for dinner and
listening to jazz. As I recall, I think the pianist George Shearing,
blind behind dark glasses, singing songs in what seemed (at least
compared to nights at BMC!) particularly soigné.
      Looking back to that visit (having graduated in ‘49 from Ben-
nington College in Vermont), I found southern bohemia depressing
compared to the New England bohemia I loved during my college



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                   279

--- Page 308 ---
days. The former style seemed forlorn and tacky while, on the other
hand, Vermont bohemia was crisp. Each echoed its climate. This is
not a judgment on which atmosphere is more creative or valuable,
it’s merely a difference in style and preference. In either case, North
or South, I long for some of the qualities of those days. They were
instructive, productive, and real. Gone with the wind of the early
‘50s.

                                            RON Robertson
                                                       (Student 1951)


                                Maximizing the Options



In retrospect, I think of missed opportunities at Black Mountain
College. I was at loose ends—did not have a focus or strong com-
mitment—even though spending most of my time in art-related
studies and activities. I read a lot, searching for answers and direc-
tion (unaware at that time that we must create our own realities



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                 291

--- Page 320 ---
Ron Robertson, Dulce Bellum Inexpertis, 1984. Assemblage: media media,
28 x 36 inches. Photography: Daphne Hougard. Collection of the artist.




                               Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 321 ---
and meaning) and [was mostly involved] in just experiencing the
immediacy of life at the school, through the intense relationships
that developed. I was—again in retrospect—too judgmental and
not open enough to really listen to what others had to say, and in
many situations not understanding what was said, and probably
not confident enough to take advantage of the various contacts and
opportunities with both students and faculty that were presented.
         I remember    Carl Kent and I—aware       that BMC    was in
financial trouble—asked Doyle, who ran the farm, and Wes Huss, if
there was any possibility of starting some alternative enterprise—
other than the farm—that might produce some income. Doyle sug-
gested cutting the pine trees on acreage he could use for agricul-
ture, if cleared. During the fall of 51 Carl and I obtained the capital
from the council, bought a couple of two-edged axes, and began
chopping down and trimming pine trees to sell as pulp wood to a
local pulp mill. It was extremely hard and slow work, and after two
weeks we asked the College Council if we could invest in a chain
saw to speed up production and profits, and they agreed. Produc-
tion increased dramatically, sales were made, the pine trees were
cut—but not enough to really solve the growing financial prob-
lems. And so ended our lumberjacking adventure—and off we
went to New York stronger and healthier and also sadder because
we could sense we were seeing the last of a most significant and
wonderful learning community.
          BMC was the only school I’ve experienced where there
was such a close and intimate contact with faculty. At first, after
associating with faculty away from their lecterns, in work and play,
parties and bull sessions, I lost some of the awe and intimidation
and in some cases respect I had for faculty in general. Knowing
them as more than a voice behind a lectern was enlightening, and
soon I was able to appreciate and respect the wisdom and knowl-
edge they shared, in spite of the fact that they had all the human
qualities I had been judging them by. One of the most rewarding
educational experiences occurred in a class in anthropology taught
by Paul Leser. The course was called “A Critique of Sources,” in
which we were asked to compare and evaluate anthropological sur-
vey texts by Boaz and several other anthropologists, and determine
where the contradictions, differences, and sources might be. In ad-



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                  293

--- Page 322 ---
dition, we were assigned to do the same with the four Gospels of
the New Testament. It was my first intense experience researching
data, exploring the varying ideas presented, and evaluating the re-
search to determine who or what was the source and how they dif-
fered. Experiencing the exploration of relationships such as this has
since opened me up to the exploration of everything of relevance,
and in particular to maximizing the options I now bring to my
teaching and art.




                       Mildred (Adams) Harding
                                          (Faculty Spouse 1951-52)


             Excerpts from “My Black Mountain”



                                 Ah, did you once see Shelley plain,
                                 And did he stop and speak to you?

To many admirers of poets, painters, composers, and all the rest
who taught at BMC—to admirers of Olson, Rauschenberg, Cage,
Duncan, Oppenheimer, Creeley, Albers, Motherwell, and a host of
others—as'   well   as to devotees   of innovative   education,   Black
Mountain’s name is sacred, its place Holy Ground. Not so for me!
Remembering my year there as a young “faculty wife” in the early
fifties, I see that legendary community as more comical than sacred.
But my Black Mountain is worth commemorating, if only because it
is a part of history in one of BMC’s most important and least docu-
mented years. The scene that I recall throws some sobering light on
the “religion of freedom.”


“My Black Mountain” first appeared in The Yale Literary Magazine 151,
no. |. Excerpted by permission of the American Literary Society and the
author.


294                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 323 ---
From the beginning, our situation at Black Mountain was
ironic, and, inevitably, uncomfortable. Recently exiled by those
who seemed to us the Philistines, we were now, in some ways—or
in the eyes of our neighbors—Philistines ourselves. Given the reali-
ties of BMC—its physical setting and living conditions, the College
budget, the whole pattern of its administrative, academic, artistic
and social life—we were both misfits, |more so than John, who was
invited there to teach anthropology. Though sustained by a sense
of humor and a certain youthful toughness, we felt increasingly
disenchanted, puzzled, and frustrated . . . indeed, trapped—until,
at last, the trap was sprung and we could peacefully bide the sum-
mer months till our escape. .. .
    Take, for a start, the Appalachian setting. On first acquaintance
romantic and idyllic—Corot in the valley! Wordsworth on the
heights!—it   soon   proved   to be, instead,   Tobacco   Road,   and, at
times, Siberia. . . . The ordinary daily life that we would have fit
into, with two young children, called for pioneer qualities in which
I found myself somewhat lacking. I was no Ma Joad, I felt, nor was
meant to be. . . . After Branwen and I had clambered, peered in
windows,   and found a stove—a huge, rusty kerosene monster as
exciting to me as that footprint was to Crusoe—the driver of the
College’s erratic old pickup truck eventually helped me to haul it
down the mountain and into our kitchen. True, we had no pans or
food, only a kettle we brought with us, but I felt we had taken one
step out of the Stone Age. As for laundry, with no washing ma-
chine or washtub, I washed clothes, towels, bed linens, and diapers
in the bathtub, and on rare occasions when the sun shone, hung it
all—triumphantly—on a line John had strung between two trees at
one side of our clearing. Hung it three or four times, that is. Soon
the College Council pronounced the washing an aesthetic outrage;
it ruined the landscape and it would have to go. Thereafter, it fes-
tooned our kitchen. And the weather! By mid-October, when our
son Brayton was born in an Asheville hospital, the temperature on
the mountain was often below freezing. Our cottage, built as a sum-
mer house, had never been insulated. I remember 2 A.o. bottle feed-
ings at just above zero indoors, bundling in layers of blankets, the
baby in my arms, buried in them, only his nostrils and lips visible.
Day and night, icy drafts blew through the cracks in the walls and



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                    295

--- Page 324 ---
the broken-paned    French doors. All day, in the black earth-hole
beneath the kitchen, an aged, impotent little furnace sputtered and
choked on damp, soft coal, a small heap of which was occasionally
dumped outside the house. Throughout the house, the air was 1m-
pervious to the feeble rays of the tiny, rusty radiators, which were
cold until early afternoon, and then barely warm for a few hours.
Though tea and coffee were provided by our treasured stove, for
breakfast, lunch, and dinner John and J, with Branwen                   and soon
with the new baby, in all kinds of weather trekked down and up a
steep, winding path, to and from the communal                  dining room,    in
one place crossing on a tiny, slippery bridge, a ravine at the bottom
of which was a rapid, often frozen stream. In the bare hall over-
hanging the lake, the self-service meals were competitive and ca-
cophonous:       kitchen   help,   students,   and     most    of the    teachers
grabbed and shouted and banged pans, dishes, and trays. The food,
though monotonous          and increasingly scarce, was at least fresh, all
from the college farm. For years the farm had been failing, we were
told, but now it would be saved by the new farmers, Mr. and Mrs.
Doyle Jones. As winter settled in and less and less food trickled
from farm to dining room—for one long stretch, almost nothing but
milk, eggs, and red beans—I          pictured my children withered and
deformed with beriberi and scurvy.
     All year, poverty hung like a pall over the whole community
as, indeed, it did through most of BMC’s history, finally stifling the
College altogether a few years after we left. John’s actual salary for
the full twelve-month year was just over $1,000 before taxes. Every
payday, when he or I or both of us (usually with one or both of the
children in our arms) managed to outshout and outpush a dozen or
so yelling, shoving students and claim a seat or standing room in
the college pickup, we immediately spent most of the paycheck in
the town     of Black Mountain,       six miles      away,    on the children’s
emergency     needs    (baby food, fruit, vegetables, and secondhand
warm clothing)... .
    The resident of BMC who was closest to me and whose experi-
ence widened       mine the most was, of course, my husband..Many
things about John—his general cultural orientation, not to mention
his fastidious     cleanliness, short hair, shaven           cheeks, and gentle
Southern courtesy—might have isolated him from BMC life. But his



296                        Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 325 ---
artistic inclinations placed him in its midst. Almost from childhood,
John had painted abstract watercolors, fraternized with artists,
haunted    art galleries, and followed avant-garde art movements
with fascination and fellow feeling. He was, therefore, a welcome
participant in the community’s life. . . . In the daytime, he made the
rounds    of classrooms and studios, chatting with students and
teachers, from one of whom, the personable and gifted young com-
poser-pianist Lou Harrison, he gained new insights into music.
And nearly every evening he shared in Olson’s dionysiac sympo-
sia.
    Charles Olson! In any review of Black Mountaineers conspicu-
ously there for us, Olson towers over all the rest. New rector of the
College, he seemed, even then at forty-two, half mythic: bard, guru,
titan (6’7" or 6’8"), iconoclast, Nietzschean hero, King of the Moun-
tain. John and I gleaned early, from Olson’s disciples, that he was
prodigiously erudite; that he had written an important book on
Melville; . . . that he was a revolutionary force in contemporary lit-
erature; and that his classes were mind-boggling, inspiring, and—
for would-be writers—often shattering. Striding on the mountain
paths in poncho and straw hat (he was just back from Mexico), or
on the porch of a faculty cottage, or, still talking, drinking bootleg
rotgut half through the night, he was surrounded by students and
young teachers—Jupiter among his satellites. “Olson said” was ipse
dixit. With respect to literature, the gospel according to Olson was
in the air, the master’s ideas (as is the way with gospels) interpreted
by his disciples with varying degrees of understanding. As this aes-
thetic came through to us, Virgil, Dante, Milton, the neoclassicists,
most of the English Romantics, Victorians, Edwardians, as well as
moderns like Yeats and Eliot, were “homosexual artists,” or rather,
not artists at all, not worshipers of the White Goddess. Shakespeare
was all right, Swinburne sacred, Melville divine, and Charles Olson
was his prophet, nay, his reincarnation. .. .
     One evening, in the communal hall, Olson read his poems and
expounded his aesthetic theories: towering, his spectacled eyes
glowing, long arms weaving, words torrential, a genuine
rhapsodlist]. Most of the audience, scattered on benches, hard
chairs, or the floor, were breathless with adoration. Though dis-
tracted by the two restless babies in my lap, I felt the dynamism of



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                  297

--- Page 326 ---
the man, delighted in the music of his poems, caught an idea or
two, and strained—alas, vainly—to understand both poems and
theories. To both, my mind kept crying “No!” But aware that gen-
iuses have usually baffled their contemporaries, I suspended judg-
ment, assuming genius on his part and, regretfully, blindness on
mine....
      Around another head, much younger than Olson’s, some al-
ready saw the aura of genius. Robert Rauschenberg, a 26-year-old
art student—tall,   slender, dark, boyishly handsome,                soft-spoken,
courteous, shy—was obviously not just the “nice kid from Texas”
whom everyone liked at first sight. He had studied art at BMC once
before (in 1948, under Josef Albers), as well as in the Midwest, New
York, and even Paris; and, it was said reverently, he had just had a
one-man show in New York, at the Betty Parsons Gallery. ... As
Rauschenberg labored among his collages and empty pictures,
gluing torn newspapers and other debris, painting white on white
or black on black, the Mountaineers—delighted                    with his revolu-
tionary originality—debated: Would he be truly great? Was he des-
tined for fame? “No,” said Olson. “Yes,” said John, and compared
Bob with Mozart—a     “pure artist,”               “unerring.”
                                       Lido   PL




      ...A few middle-aged or elderly teachers were permanent
residents on campus, survivors of an earlier era in the College’s his-
tory when many had been refugees from Hitler’s Europe. In the
midst of the dominant bohemia, they perpetuated quite different
modes of education, art, and life. It was with these older residents
that I felt most comfortable. Johanna Jalowetz had a generous good
nature that charmed everyone. ... Another old-timer, Nell Rice, the
thoroughly American librarian, had been at the College from its be-
ginning: in fact, she was the former wife of its chief founder, John
Rice. .. . Of all the BMC people, it is Max Dehn that glows brightest
in my memory.... Knowing that Max had only one student—a
bright-eyed, blond, clean, and courteous young man with whom
I’d seen him walking the mountain paths day after day, talking,
talking—I asked him if it wasn’t frustrating to give all that time to
just one pupil. Not at all, he assured me, for Trueman was “a real
student, a real mathematician”; it was a privilege to be his teacher.
“In fact,” he went on, “I have been very fortunate. In my sixty years
of teaching, I have had at least fifteen real students.” Max asked if



298                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 327 ---
he might take Branwen for a walk. ... I agreed, but reminded him
that she was only two and a half and might be a trying companion.
He answered with characteristic gentle deliberation: “No in-
deed ... I have observed the child. She is a rare child...a pure
soul. It will be a great pleasure for me. I shall show her the wild
flowers.” The next day, hand in hand, their white hair shining in
the sun, the two of them walked across the clearing and up the
wooded slope. An hour or so later they returned, enchanted with
each other. Returning Branwen to me at the door, Max thanked us
both ceremoniously and said to her, “We will walk together again
very soon,   my   dear.”   A few days later, early in the morning,   a
hearse pulled up in our clearing, loaded a body, and departed. Max
was gone.
    About that time John and I were interviewed on the BMC cam-
pus for jobs at the American University in Cairo, Egypt, by its Aca-
demic Dean, Dr. Worth Howard. After we had shown him around
the College and spent a pleasant time regaling him with tall tales
about the school, he left amid smiles and handshakes. We wrung
our hands: that Presbyterian, half-missionary university in Cairo
would never want us now. Ironically, we got the teaching posi-
tions.... The decisive factor... was Dr. Howard’s report of his
visit: “If those two can survive BMC and laugh about it,” he had
told the trustees, “they’re tough enough for Egypt.”
