Francine du Plessix Gray
                                           (Student 1951 and 1952 SS)


                                “Black Mountain:
                The Breaking (Making) of a Writer”


      At eight thirty tonight John Cage mounted a stepladder
      and until 10:30 he talked about the relation of music to Zen
      Buddhism while a movie was shown, dogs ran across the
      stage barking, 12 persons danced without any previous
      rehearsal,    a prepared piano was played, whistles blew,
      babies screamed, Edith Piaf records were played
      double-speed on a turn-of-the-century machine. .. .


      This   journal   note   of mine,   recording   what   is said   to be
America’s     first “Happening,”    is dated August 1952, Black Moun-
tain College, N.C. I have long felt a need to pay tribute to this vi-
sionary community of men and women, and particularly to Charles
Olson, the poet who might well be responsible for my becoming a
writer.
      Black Mountain originated in 1933, in the same decade of edu-
cational ferment in which Antioch, Bennington, Sarah Lawrence,
and the University of Chicago designed their progressive curricu-
lums. I signed up for my first summer session there in 1951, at the
age of twenty, having gravitated to it for reasons that then seemed
eminently objective: how captivating, in the span of two months, to
study painting with Ben Shahn and Robert Motherwell, dancing
with Merce         Cunningham,   music   with John Cage, writing with
Charles Olson. A dissolutely aesthetic adolescent, I was drawn to
each of these possible vocations and several more; I had received
one of those antiquated European educations which encourages
young women to draw landscapes, play the piano, execute basic
ballet steps as fluently as they read and write.


From Adam and Eve & the City, 1987. Reprinted by permission of Simon
& Schuster, Inc.



300                       Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 329 ---
I arrived at Black Mountain straitlaced by many other conven-
tions: the protocols of France’s diplomatic corps and of its impover-
ished aristocracy (my father’s world); the decorum of my mother’s
fashionable    salons   and   of my   American   schools—Spence,   Bryn
Mawr, and Barnard, where I’d begun a major in medieval philoso-
phy. I brought these inhibitions to a community pledged to rebel
against all traditional modes     of behavior, in life as in art. And it
took me a few months to realize that I had gravitated toward Black
Mountain      not for detached   aesthetic purposes,   but out of an in-
stinctual need to purge myself of much ancestral folderol, to en-
gage in that symbolic parricide without which none of us can be-
come totally adult. It was with equally blind instinct that of all the
alluring mentors Black Mountain was offering in the summer of
1951, I gravitated to the most iconoclastic and dictatorial of the lot,
Charles Olson.
    Charles Olson was born and raised in Gloucester, Massachu-
setts. [Olson was actually born in Worcester, Massachusetts.] After
receiving a Ph.D. in American Civilization from Harvard (the first
doctoral candidate in that department), he worked as a fisherman
and a mail carrier between brief stints of teaching. He came to Black
Mountain in 1949 at the invitation of the artist Josef Albers, then the
rector of the college, and soon afterward succeeded him as rector
when Albers moved on to Yale. It now strikes me as odd that he
was only forty-two when I met him, for he emanated an awesome,
oracular majesty (in part innate, in part shrewdly cultivated) which
one only associates with the most seasoned shamans. He was a
mountain, a giant of a man, measuring six feet seven inches and 250
pounds. Thick lenses floated above his walrus mustache, giving his
steel-blue eyes a perpetually ferocious gaze. The balding pate of his
enormous head was fringed with a mane of graying hair that
flowed to his shoulders, like that of some Indian sage. Just before
coming to Black Mountain he had lived in the wilds of Yucatan
studying the Mayan culture, and a Mexican wool serape was
magisterially draped over his huge shoulders, even when he was
bare torsoed on the hottest North Carolina summer day.
     1951: Olson’s rebellion against all traditional literary forms, his
militant insistence on subjectivity, self-expression, self-exposure—
these were the first aspects of his teaching that struck me as revolu-



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                   301

--- Page 330 ---
tionary. The confessional journal engaged in with full sincerity was
an infinitely nobler art form, in Olson’s eyes, than any of those
courteous short stories published in The New Yorker which have
been the model of our literary taste. There was much of American
revivalism in Olson: bear witness by baring your soul, be redeemed
by the sheer authenticity of your individual emotions. The
uniquely American ideal of “finding yourself” (a phrase whose
equivalent might not exist in any other Western language) was ex-
pressed by Olson in the most native populist style: each aspiring
writer in his workshop must realize “what is his or her ground, get
to that, citizen, go back there, stand on it, make yrself yr own place,
and move from that.”
      So whenever Olson was pleased by a student’s particularly in-
tense self-revelation (I cite another instance from my Black Moun-
tain journals), he slowly rose from his chair to tower over its au-
thor:
      “Si, Victor, si!” Olson shouted. “You have it, Victor!”
     And he glared at the newly loved writer with fierce affection.
In the case of Victor, Olson’s fancy was caught by this visceral re-
ductive line: “I finger my innards for the truth.”
      “Si, Victor, you have it!” Olson announced.      And Victor was
held up as the workshop’s hero for the rest of the week, until an-
other equally raw metaphor of self-revelation caught the master’s
ear.

      Like all introspective writers who have theorized about their
craft, Olson was obsessed by the loss of energy between the rich
immediacy of our emotions and the relative poverty of our scripted
words: “The dodges of discourse,” as he called it, “the distinction
between language as the act of the instant and language as the act
of thought about the instant.” His infatuation with Mayan culture
came from the directness of its hieroglyphic writing, whose signs
“retain the power of the objects of which they are the images.”
There was also much neoprimitivism, much redneck yahoo postur-
ing in this Harvard-educated scholar who preached that we would
not be free “until we have completely cleaned ourselves of the bi-
ases of westernism, of greekism, until we have squared away at his-
torical time.”



302                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 331 ---
Olson proclaimed that traditional concepts of linear time must
make way in our writing for a native American concept of “space.”
Stated in the very opening of his best-known prose text, Call Me
Ishmael: “I take SPACE, to be the central fact to man born in Amer-
ica, from Folsom cave to now. I spell it large because it comes large
here. Large, and with no mercy.” The ideal contemporary text, in
his view, was    a “space-field”     organized    by an idiom    of shared
Americanness, an optimally direct, colloquial vernacular. As in his
own verse:

    It ain’t dreamt until it walks     It talks   It spreads its green
    barrazza
    Listen closely, folks, this poem comes to you by benefit of its
    own     Irish green bazoo. You take it, from here.

The lectures Olson delivered in his writing workshops were
equally iconoclastic and antilinear, random shards of culture as
purged of any historical coherence as the elements of John Cage’s
Happenings (which Olson hailed as one of the glories of the twenti-
eth century). His classes averaged four hours and could last six or
eight, and sitting through them was like seeing an archaeologist
throw a tantrum in a richly endowed museum. Within the span of
one summer class we might be assailed by snatches of Sumerian
history, of Fenellosa’s theories on Japanese art, of Heisenberg’s
Principle of Indeterminacy; by passages from D. H. Lawrence's Stud-
ies in Classical American Literature, Dostoevski’s Notes from the Un-
derground, Leo Frobenius’s books on African rock painting,
Pausania’s Descriptions of Greece (“more valuable than Plutarch .. .
because of its careful localism”), and from Ezra Pound’s Guide to
Kulchur (“just because it razzledazzles History”). We would be si-
multaneously assaulted by lines from the medieval poet Cavalcanti
and Renaissance poet John Skelton, and from Olson’s more recent
idols—Blake, Melville, William Carlos Williams.
    Olson’s collagist approach to culture, his stress on the sponta-
neous   and instinctual, tended      to breed a mayhem      of narcissistic
mumblings among his students; it led to adulation of several texts
as idiotic as Ezra Pound’s ABC of Economics, and more self-expres-
sion than there were selves to express. Yet his presence was magi-
cally fructifying because he did not so much engage in Oedipal re-



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                        303

--- Page 332 ---
bellion against contemporary fathers, which would become the
curse of the 1960s, as in a reappraisal of revolutionary great-grand-
fathers: “Big O.,” as we called him, transferred to us the momen-
tum of his gigantic, archaeological curiosity for all forms of “imme-
diate” discourse, past and present. The fineness of his ear had few
equals in his generation. No teacher I’ve ever had put greater stress
on tonal texture, on the notion that in all literary forms “it is by
their syllables that words juxtapose in beauty.” He forced us to re-
alize that prose is only as good as it approximates the condition of
poetry—that state in which not a particle of sound can be changed
without upsetting the entire page. And the lines of classical verse
he battered us with most frequently had a haunting, reductive mu-
sicality whose repetition would make anyone into a better writer:
Blake’s “Ah! Sunflower! weary of time!”; the medieval lyric “O
western Wynd, when wilt thou blow/ And the small rain down
shall rain”; these lines from Marvell’s “The Garden”: “Annihilating
all that’s made/ To a green thought in a green shade.”

      How did I fare in this scheme of things, an adolescent girl
skilled at all Olson most detested—Aristotelian abstractions, Euro-
pean proprieties? Parachuted into this virulently Yankee, predomi-
nantly male community whose favorite mottoes were Ezra Pound’s
“Make it New” and William Carlos Willisms’s “No ideas but in
things,” I fared with mixed results. Several aspects of my brief past
made me somewhat suited to Black Mountain: I’d been a pacifist
lefty and World Federalist since my early teens, I was a tomboy
who had always identified, in art as in life, with male heroes. Once
in college, I’d concentrated so heavily on my philosophical abstrac-
tions that I’d never taken a literature course beyond freshman Eng-
lish, thinking I was smartass enough to read all of it by myself; and
Olson thought that was particularly fine. “Girl,” he’d say pressing
his five fingers hard into my scalp until it hurt, “if you get the high-
falootin’ Yurrup and poh-lee-tess and stuck-up schools out of that
noggin and start playing Gringo ball you'll be okay.”
      That was a lot to get rid of in two months. And I'd brought
along some equally unsuitable possessions to Black Mountain: my
first short stories, in which I was always an adult male facing situ-
ations I knew little about—my favorite persona was a middle-aged



304                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 333 ---
alcoholic actor seeking salvation in a Bowery church. Olson was
equally capable of abysmal rudeness and of ambassadorial cour-
tesy, and I’ve always been grateful that he never assailed these
texts’ atrociousness publicly. He reserved his ire for our tutorials.
“Girl,” he bellowed at me after reading my trash, “this is pure shit!
You're going to do nothing but keep a journal for a year, an hour a
day minimum, come back next summer and show me what's in it!”
    So I went back north and obeyed Big O. and kept my journal
though the rigors of senior year of college, where I’d changed my
dissertation thesis from medieval themes to a more irreverent topic,
“Kierkegaard’s View on the Demise of Christianity.” Throughout
that winter I scribbled recollections of early childhood in my jour-
nal, noting one incident curiously akin to the one I’d just lived
through with Olson:
    In Paris in the 1930s, at the wish of my father, who deplored
the laxness of 20th century education, I’d been tutored      at home
from the age of five by a tyrannical governess. The two of us trav-
eled once a week to a correspondence school where we were doled
out lessons for the following week, Gallically rigid homework
(memorization of Asian capitals and Latin verbs, codifying of sen-
tence parts) which was hardly conducive to a fertile imagination.
But when I was eight an unprecedented event took place—a new
teacher came in and gave us the following assignment: “Write a
Story about Anything You Wish.” Filled with excitement and terror
by this novel freedom, I began as a severe minimalist:

  The little girl was forbidden by her parent to walk alone to the
  lake at the other end of the long lawn. But she wished to visit a
  green-eyed frog who would offer her the key to freedom. One
  day she disobeyed her parent and walked to the lake, and im-
  mediately drowned.
                                The End

“Pathetic dribble!” the Father stormed on his daily visit to my
study room. “You dare call that a story? What will become of you if
you never finish anything!” He grabbed the paper from my little
desk and tore it to shreds. It was a May evening of 1939, fourteen
months before he died in the French Resistance. My father had been



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                305

--- Page 334 ---
the love of my life, and he’d warned    me that I should never write
again. Always these male censors, silencing, silencing me... .
     During my senior year at college I also returned, in my journal,
to impressions of the past summer at Black Mountain:
     Supercool pose of silence or monosyllabic utterances, bare feet,
men’s dark glasses and long hair, easygoing nudism and bisexual-
ity—fads   of the just nascent   Beat Generation,   precursors   of the
1960s Movement style. (I hadn’t skimped on Black Mountain’s
brand of unisex macho, chopping off my hair as short and jagged
as a contemporary punk’s.)
     Everyone aping Olson’s Yankee-phonetic spellings and pun-
some divisions: waz, enuf, luv, kulchur, yrself, lawd, abt, ga-wan
for “go on,” Ru-man-tick, egg-zack-tly. All taken secondhand from
1920 vintage Pound.
     The girl who danced in front of the mirror in the main hall
from 7:00 A.M. to suppertime, stopping only to feed the baby bray-
ing in a basket by the exercise bar. She did not take any courses, she
never spoke to anyone, she simply moved about ten hours in a row,
staring at herself in a mirror.
     Olson, a serape flung over his naked torso, sitting in the dining
room next to John Cage, always most formal in a black city suit and
tie and very shiny pointed black shoes, punctuating the cultivated
laconism of dinnertime with his tinkling, Zen monk’s laugh as he
mused about his next Happening.
      Accompanying the poet Jonathan Williams to the Asheville,
N.C. draft board in 1951 when he refused induction into the U.S.
Army on grounds of conscientious objection: that was as important
a political education as any I had received from my family or col-
lege.
      I returned to Black Mountain in 1952 for another summer ses-
sion as Olson had bidden me to, and showed him my journal. There
followed a few treasured days when I was the victor of that
season’s workshop, then reduced to six students. The first week of
the seminar, Olson towered over us, glowering through his thick
spectacles, his finger poised on a stark reductive line of my-jour-
nals. It had to do with my father: “I pointed westward, towards his
grave.”




306                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 335 ---
“Si, si, girl! That’s space!” Big Charles triumphed. “Si, you’ve
got it, kid!”
     But I didn’t remain in favor long. For I’d brought Olson some
other texts to read: three very autobiographical stories about my
childhood which had followed his dictate to “Concentrate on what
you know about, Write from the Center.” Clearly inspired by my
obedience to Big O., they had won me the annual Creative Writing
Award given to a graduating senior at Barnard. Five hundred dol-
lars, a tidy sum for those years, and all the ego trips such minihon-
ors bring. But Olson didn’t give a damn about jurors from fancy
New York publishing houses doling out prizes at a Seven Sisters
school. He was pioneering a counterculture, and such details made
him all the more hostile. Once more he raged at me during a tuto-
rial, shouting: “You're still writing conservative junk! If you want
to be a writer keep it to a journal. .. .” The giant walrus rising from
his chair, six feet seven       of him    towering.   “AND   ABOVE     ALL
DON’T TRY TO PUBLISH ANYTHING FOR TEN YEARS!” More
censorship into silence, this time, perhaps, for the best.
        I remained, as ever, an obedient daughter. I again followed Big
Charles’s advice. I kept my journal in New Orleans while following
a jazz clarinetist on the rounds of Bourbon Street; in the dawns of
New York when I relished being the only woman on the overnight
shift    at   United   Press,   wrote    “World-in-Briefs”   about   Joseph
McCarthy’s purges, drank martinis with my colleagues at 8:00 a.m.
in sleazy bars under the Third Avenue El. I remained loyal to it
throughout a myriad of other transient aspirations that all pro-
tected me from the fear of Becoming a Writer—flirting with the no-
tion of entering Harvard’s architectural school, or going to Union
Theological Seminary for a degree in divinity. I persevered with the
journal throughout my most misguided phase, when I earned my
living in Paris as a fashion reporter, and dallied with a succession
of consummate French narcissists to whom I eventually gave their
literary due. I continued to write it after my return to the United
States and my marriage, when I realized one of my earliest dreams
and spent five years as a painter of meticulous landscapes and still
lifes. For my Black Mountain guru had offered me an important
metaphor: journal keeping is comparable to the process of sharpen-




An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                      307

--- Page 336 ---
ing a pencil: our emotions, and the power of their expressions, are
kept at maximum intensity by the daily routine of being inserted
into the journal’s sharpening edge.
     By 1962 I had two children. I lived in deep country and in rela-
tive solitude, encompassed    by domestic    duties. The journal was
becoming increasingly voluminous, angry, introspective. The no-
madic tomboy, finally denied flight and forced to turn inward, was
beginning to explode. One winter day I felt an immense void, great
powerlessness, the deepest loneliness I’d ever known. I wept for
some hours, took out a notebook, started rewriting one of the sto-
ries that had won me my college award. It was the one about my
governess. It was published shortly afterward in The New Yorker,
precisely one year past the deadline Charles had set for me. Twelve
years and two books of nonfiction later, it was to become the first
chapter of my first novel, Lovers and Tyrants. The process of finish-
ing that book entailed a solid and delicate psychoanalysis which
forced me to accept my father’s death. Epiphany achieved, I was
able to write the novel’s last three chapters—my first serious at-
tempt at fiction—in a mere six months.
     I may have had to bury the first Censoring father to set my
tongue free. I may have had to go to Black Mountain to bury many
grandfathers, many mothers. I may have had to abide by Censor
Two, Charles Olson, to let my tongue speak with any measure of
integrity.


      “A Frontier society sometimes raucous and raw,” so Martin
Duberman would describe it in his fine book, Black Mountain: An
Exploration in Community, “. . . bold in its refusal to assume any re-
ality it hadn’t tested, and therefore bold in inventing forms, both in
life styles and in art.”
     Charles Olson attempted to keep Black Mountain alive during
the last five years of its existence through the sheer force of his per-
sonality, with total disdain for all administrative detail. He was a
tortured man who had talked like a puritan about “clean writing”
being solely produced by “clean experience,” and left his wife and
baby to marry a student whom he had gotten with child. It was the
way he had treated many women throughout his life, and I suspect
he had too much integrity not to feel his guilt, which he allayed by



308                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 337 ---
severe bouts of alcoholism after Black Mountain’s demise. By the
time I wrote him to thank him for inspiring my first published
texts, in the mid-sixties, he was too far gone to answer. Charles
Olson died at fifty-nine, a great talent which may never have found
its true center.
      His leavening influence on American literature can still be felt
by reading through back issues of The Black Mountain Review,
founded by Olson and Robert Creeley with the intent of loosening
the grip of the New Criticism. It provided more fascinating read-
ing, for my money, than Partisan Review, New Directions, or any
other classy periodical of the 1950s. In its last number, dated 1957,
one finds Allen Ginsberg’s “America,” Kerouac’s “From October in
the Railroad Earth,” poems by Gary Snyder and Michael McClure,
a section of William Burrough’s then unpublished Naked Lunch and
part of Hubert Selby, Jr.’s, also unpublished Last Exit to Brooklyn.
     Notwithstanding Olson’s demanding, oppressive presence, the
more gifted writers of the community he nurtured were able to
avoid all sectarianism. Of the ten poets since categorized as “The
Black Mountain School” (a term originally coined by an outsider,
Donald Allen, in his 1960 anthology, The New American Poetry) only
six had some connection with the college. Others had simply pub-
lished in the Review. And the vast range of stylistic differences
among the writers indebted to him—be it Joel Oppenheimer’s do-
mestic lyricism, Jonathan Williams’s pungent sparseness, Fielding
Dawson’s Joycean memoirs, or my own discursive prose—makes
the very term “school” ridiculous. Olson himself had clearly
spelled out the terms of Black Mountain’s adamant individualism:
The writer “is not free to be a part of, or to be any, sect . . . the poet
can not afford to traffick in any other sign than his own, his self, the
man or woman he is.”
    Few communities of modest size—it seldom exceeded 120 any
given year—were ever nurtured within two decades by so distin-
guished a group. In addition to the ones already mentioned: Ed-
ward   Dahlberg,     Buckminster     Fuller,   Paul     Goodman,    Robert
Creeley, Aaron     Siskind,   Stan Vanderbeek,        Jasper Johns, Robert
Rauschenberg, Cy Twombly, Kenneth Noland, Willem de Kooning,
Arthur Penn, Alfred Kazin, Robert Duncan, Walter Gropius, Stefan
Wolpe, Franz Kline, among many others. But this proliferation of



An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                      309

--- Page 338 ---
stars led Black Mountain to a very incongruous self-image: While
trying to remain the City on the Hill, it saw itself both as frontier
and as frontier salon. There were also the contradictions of its last
rector: Olson’s Black Mountain was obsessed by its unique and re-
demptive Americanism and by a totally opposite 19th century ro-
mantic vision of the artist as pristine prophet—Olson’s favorite dic-
tum was “Only the artist is on time.” How to reconcile this narcis-
sistic sacralization of art with a community dedicated, in good part,
to abolishing all traditional barriers between art and life? “Write as
you breathe,” Olson always taught. “I want to erase all differences
between art and life” Cage said. “Rauschenberg just wants to fill in
the gaps between the two, which strikes me as a little too Roman
Catholic.”
      Like Brook Farm, Oneida, and most other Utopias ballasted by
a belief in their redemptive purity, Black Mountain would eventu-
ally founder on reality’s edge. By 1955 the few dedicated faculty
members who had remained had not drawn salaries for a year, and
many had used up their personal savings. Olson’s attempts at eco-
nomic revival were of a tragicomic nature. He tried to get approval
from the state to enroll Korean War Vets, and the college’s remain-
ing six students rushed from class to class, changing disguises ev-
ery few minutes, to convince VA inspectors that the community
was thriving. A few months later, Black Mountain decided that de-
spite its “principles” it would accept the mentally retarded son of a
wealthy Southerner who had suggested that he might put some
money into the community. The father was to fly his private plane
over the college and dip its wings in a certain way to make it
known he had decided to offer a bequest. For a few days members
of the saving remnant spent their time wandering about the fields,
staring at the sky and waiting for the sound of a plane which never
came. One can think of few gifted men put to such an absurd task.
     I don’t fully understand Olson’s magnum opus, The Maximus
Poems, but I believe he has an important, underestimated place in
American letters. He left many fascinating essays (“Projective
Verse,” “Human     Universe”) and a remarkable book on Melville,
Call Me Ishmael, a classic of engaged, iconoclastic criticism compa-
rable to William Carlos Williams’s In the American Grain. His legacy
went beyond published texts. I do not adulate Olson or Black



310                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 339 ---
Mountain the way most of its members have, feeling ambivalent
about the sham and the magnificence of the man, the dangers and
the vision of the place. But I thank him every week of my life for his
prophetic emphasis on the valor of subjectivity and candor, of dis-
obedience against form and state; and also for that fatherly rigor
which eventually enabled me to write at all.
    Decades later, as a woman nurtured by twenty years of femi-
nism, memories    of Black Mountain and Charles Olson still make
me worry about the constant temptation, and great hazards, of
being dutiful daughters. And they lead me to muse on these bitter-
sweet paradoxes: how certain male mentors can force us to ac-
knowledge our femaleness; how some male oppressors, by teach-
ing us to rebel, may eventually become our liberators.




                                         William McGee
                                                   (Student 1952 SS)

                   Some Memorable                 Personalities




Franz Kline
There was a very positive impact on the College community when
Kline arrived. Jack Tworkov was the visiting artist for the month of
June, and he brought to the College a very intellectual-scholarly
approach to the concepts and problems of contemporary art. Here
was a modern painter steeped in the history of art, who was very
articulate, and very much at home lecturing to his students. In con-
trast, Franz was smiling and very generous of himself with both his
colleagues and his students. It is important to know Franz was not
in any way “the teacher.” He was an extraordinarily original
painter, who possessed no ideas about being a teacher. He did,
however, have strong ideas about art and life. Being around Kline



An Anthology of Personal Accounts |                               311]

--- Page 340 ---
Bill McGee, View from Black Mountain, 1952. Oil on canvas, 32 x 36 inches—
gift to Stefan Wolpe. Photograph courtesy of the artist.


           was to be around a vibrant form of life and art. One learned from
           him on a philosophical, humorous      and ironic, as well as an intui-
           tive level. It took a lot of figuring out to get “with him.” He could
           be hard to follow. A person had to be dedicated to him in order to
           understand him. My own dedication was very intense as a young
           artist, for I was trying to learn as much from him as I could, admir-
           ing his personality and his art the way I did. It was a grand experi-
           ence to work with him—not really as a student to a teacher (for this
           he was not), but rather as man to man, artist to artist. It was in this
           form that Kline had, I believe, such a charming and memorable af-
           fect on Black Mountain.

Franz Kline, left, and Stefan Wolpe, right, in front of North Lodge,

     An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                 313

--- Page 342 ---
Charles Olson
One day toward the end of summer, Charles approached me and
suggested that I stay on for the fall and winter terms. The idea natu-
rally appealed to me even though I was already scheduled to begin
graduate work at Indiana University that same fall. I told Charles I
would   love to remain at the College, providing the school could
arrange for me to receive a master’s degree. This did not seem un-
reasonable, since Black Mountain was able to grant an undergradu-
ate degree for those wanting it. Charles rubbed his chin, and gave
me that giant-eyed look of his and said, “Okay, I’m going to call a
meeting of the Board of Fellows to see if this can be worked out.”
Well, the board did meet and pondered and discussed this newest
challenge for the best part of one morning, but in the end it was
decided that the College (with all its imaginative skills) could just
not manage such a feat. But the effort was wonderful, and the will-
ingness to consider memorable to me to this day.
     Oh how Charles loved his Black Mountain! Such visions, such
ideas he had concerning it. But the lack of funding from both pri-
vate and public sources in the end broke his heart. A few years later
he spent a few days with me in Provincetown, mostly talking about
poetry, and what a vital force it had been at BMC, especially in the
later years. By his time (about 1958) Charles seemed to me to be a
lost spirit trying to redefine himself in the midst of another younger
generation. It was the last time I saw him, but I can never forget
what he tried to do for me, what he meant to me, during the sum-
mer of 1952.


Stefan Wolpe
I came to know Stefan very well during that summer, since my
open-air studio was directly beneath his apartment in the Studies
Building, and the floors were not insulated enough to prevent my
being subjected to the mood and fits of his creative spirit!
     He was truly one of the European personalities in residence
that summer. And before it was over many of us thought of him
as—and even called him—our “Far-out Beethoven.” Stefan was a
strong, buoyant personality, and much his own man—and he loved




314                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 343 ---
any truly dedicated creative person. I felt a lot of his weight, his
influence, which sometimes took the form of an independent,
somewhat reactionary stance and tone. I remember precisely how
warmly he felt toward the musician and composer, Lou Harrison,
and how distant he kept himself from John Cage. By contrast,
Stefan seemed the more traditional composer-in-residence—even
though   he was   writing very avant-garde   music.   For me, Stefan
Wolpe was easily the most moody, volatile, and temperamental of
all that summer faculty.


Robert Rauschenberg
Robert Rauschenberg was the very first person my wife, Marty, and
I met upon arriving at the College. He was a singular-looking guy,
fairly tall and imposing in manner and appearance. For just a mo-
ment I was reminded of the world of Scott Fitzgerald—a fleeting
association with the sanguine young men of his novels. He imme-
diately offered us a hand unloading the car, and at one point he
noticed a bundle of small canvases, whose original compositions
had been painted out with a coat of zinc white—mostly painted
out, I should say, for one could still discern vaguely the original
shapes beneath the white overlay. “What's this!” he exclaimed
holding one of the canvases at arms length. “This is really an inter-
esting painting.” I recall as clearly as yesterday his enthusiasm for
those little whitened-out canvases, which for me were simply to be
reused for new work while at the College. And so my first meeting
with Bob Rauschenberg, while certainly memorable, did leave me a
little confused and a little less certain of what I would paint on
those small canvases.
     Later on I saw Rauschenberg’s first solo exhibition at the Betty
Parson’s Gallery in New York City. The paintings were the now
well-known “White Paintings.” They were beautifully installed in
symmetrical group arrangements. I stood there in the middle of the
gallery gazing at those white paintings, and my mind drifted back
to that first day at BMC when Bob gave us a hand unloading our
old ’39 Ford.




An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                315

--- Page 344 ---
Cy Twombly
He was really a lovely person, tall and somewhat aristocratic in
demeanor.      In    contrast   to   Rauschenberg,   he   was   quiet,   more
reflective in nature. I met Cy just a few days after our arrival at the
College. At the time he was sitting on the steps of South Lodge
helping Rauschenberg brush flat white paint onto a series of
stretched canvases. As it turned out these were some of the “white
paintings” that were later shown at the Betty Parson’s Gallery in
New York City. While I was very much under the spell of Franz
Kline, Cy was under the spell of Motherwell. But we shared a lot in
common as young very serious artists. Through the years, Cy’s ca-
reer evolved        in a consistent—almost     majestic—manner,      in both
form and quality. To this day I remember the prophetic spirit in his
early Black Mountain paintings, and how well he was able to for-
mulate ideas and concepts from the outside world as a means of
creating his personal inner world of visual poetry.




                                           Giddy Erwin Dyer
                                                           (Visitor 1952 SS)


                           A Black Mountain Anecdote



My husband, Bob, and I were visiting John and Mildred Adams at
the time John was teaching anthropology there. It seems that John,
as an amateur artist, often consulted other artists on the faculty.
One night he was working on a painting, and came to a difficult
point in his work. He walked over to where Bob Rauschenberg
lived, and onto the unlighted front porch, and knocked on the door.
The artist answered the door and turned on the porch lights.-To his
horror, John saw that he had walked across a canvas that Rausch-
enberg had apparently spread out on the porch to dry.
     “My God, Bob!” he said, “I’m afraid I’ve ruined your paint-
ing!” Rauschenberg didn’t bat an eye, just looked at him intently

316                        Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 345 ---
and asked quietly: “Did you have on interesting shoes?” Appar-
   ently he did, since John’s footprints became part of the painting.
       We think this incident, more than any other we experienced,
   expresses the avant-garde nature of academic life at Black Moun-
   tain College.




                                     Hilda Morley Wolpe
                                                      (Faculty 1952-56)


                             A Few Mixed Recollections



   It was good, at Black Mountain, to wake up to the quiet, the space,
   the sound of birds, though at times these might be interrupted by
   the noise of beer bottles rolling on the floor above us, or the scream
   of a new mother in the throes of a nervous breakdown. I’m speak-
   ing of the Black Dwarf Cottage in 1954-56. The balances of these
   experiences are not easy to establish on the different levels of mem-
   ory. One of the beer-bottle rollers, who would apologize most
   handsomely    (if the racket became very bad), has since become a
   trusted friend and someone I admire.
        I remember with affectionate gratitude the Christmas Eve
   when Mary and Joe Fiore, at the very nadir of Black Mountain’s for-
   tunes (with central heating cut off, salaries non-existent, and “food
   allowance” consisting for the most part of tough, over-age hens)
   came round to our back doorway (and everyone else’s as well)
   singing Christmas carols in the snow.
        On the other hand, I remember the discomfort I felt in most of
   my contacts with “Olson’s boys,” and even at the way they stared
   at me on my way to and from the dining hall, as they sat in a group
   on the wall with expressions on their faces which seemed to say
   that I was a questionable character because I’d never been seen in
   any of Charles’s classes. If I happened to mention Henry James's
  “name with respect, I was informed, mockingly, that this was—in


An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                    317

--- Page 346 ---
one of Olson’s terms—only “literature.” Eighteen years later, one of
the most faithful of his followers chortled to me with glee at having
made a great discovery: Henry James! And I believe that I can
match anyone in my admiration of Olson’s poetry.
     There was real good fellowship in our Thanksgiving celebra-
tions, and another kind of delight in walking with Johanna Ja-
lowetz into the woods above the College to look for a rare type of
wild orchid. But the gruffness and surliness of what I now would
name “Beat” behavior kept me from opening up and showing my-
self as long as I was in Black Mountain, except in the classes I gave
in Metaphysical poetry, and in the late nineteenth century aesthetic
movement through Joyce, in 1952-53.
     So I think of my life in Black Mountain with a mixture of pain,
irritation with myself at not being bold enough to jump into the
fray, and a kind of wry pleasure.




                                      Esteban Vicente
                                                   (Faculty 1953 SS)


                                   Truth Is Less Saleable



[Esteban’s] time there was before my time with him. It-was a time
during what he calls the “decline.” The Alberses had already left,
or at least Anni had, and apparently there was a huge amount of
yarn and various weavings, warps and woofs—and who knows—
maybe some odds and ends of Peruvian or Mexican antique weav-
ings. It was a time of poverty—and hope—and antique Peruvian
fragments of pottery and cloth were not exactly at a premium. In
any case, the place was quite in shambles. And certainly no one
was involved with fame or fortune—only with doing, being; teach-

idea of his general feeling about BMC when he was teaching painting
there.

ing, working and influencing living. The salary was a tot more than
the train fare, so many hitchhiked down. And the food was giving
out. Five minutes after the meal gong sounded was five minutes
too late for food.
     A favorite sport was poker—and with it the picking of the ice
box locker to perhaps find something to eat—sometimes even a
steak, or what passed for steak. This sport was frowned on by all,
except that the head of BMC, Charles Olson, who was 6 feet 7
inches tall, could not be faulted for his intense hunger, and his oc-
casional participation.
    If students were around in their pre-genius days, no one knew
it then. Everyone seemed equal and there were no unqualified, ca-
reer-minded and/or opportunists writing up the place and its in-
habitants. There was no bias for or against anyone. Esteban says the
bias was outside. In his black beard, he had many doors slammed
on him and learned to arrive at a southern town early enough to see
the town   librarian, who   usually could find the odd-looking       and
very often accented travelers at least a friendly sofa or cot. After all,
the staff was distinguished. According to the present chroniclers,
only those who are now super-famous were at BMC. But fame is
now a 15 minute money-thing—not interesting to Black Mountain-
eers, many of whom had escaped their own murderers in various
parts of Europe or Spain, which was certainly not like Europe as it
so gloriously is now. Perhaps the truth is less saleable.
     Esteban is almost 86 years now, has devils chasing him. He is
working much better than ever—freely and clearly—seven days a
week. Anyhow, at BMC no one complained too much, everyone
was poor—and working! Probably BMC failed because it was dur-
ing the time when Foundations and philanthropic institutions were
not really available to help the College to survive. There was no
way to get money. Of course now that money is available for “the
Arts,” one can still wonder if BMC could survive on 1988 grant be-
quests. One doesn’t know for sure.
    After all, BMC    was never part of an “old boy” network, nor
was there a viable financial return for or from the endeavors of its
staff. But we have very few humanists or philosophers or poets, or
for that matter artists, able and willing to function as they were
during the heyday of Black Mountain.



An Anthology of Personal Accounts |                                 319

--- Page 348 ---
Helen Hellman
                                                 (Student   1954-56 SS)


                                Olson, Wolpe, and Huss



When I arrived in Black Mountain in 1954, which was less than a
year after our return from Mallorca and France, where we had gone
in 1949, Robert Hellman had already been there for a week or two
and was already quite friendly with Olson. We had spent our first
semester in the States at the University of Iowa, where Hellman
had a fellowship in the fiction workshop led by Ray West. He was
awarded a Rockefeller grant for fiction, but this was sabotaged by
Robert Lowell, who cast a tie vote for Donald Justice in poetry. Nei-
ther writer was given or shared the award. I thought that was
pretty mean.     Hellman   had been in a classics course    on Homer,
where Lowell did the prosody, and the classics scholar Gerald Eise
taught Homer.
    Robert brought Olson to dinner every night, because Connie
had gone to Scituate, Massachusetts, with Kate. That summer           he
became involved with Betty Kaiser, whom I later became friendly
with in the little theater class of Wes Huss. I remember Olson as a
huge man who turned up every evening in a fresh white shirt. He
appreciated my French dinners. We must have talked a bit about
Europe, but he seemed not very interested at that time, almost as if
I/we were out of his great orbit. His ideas of the American space
were new and exotic to me, a geographical space as maximal as he
was physically. And I read Call Me Ishmael at the first opportunity. I
am indebted to him for reading and discovering William Carlos
Williams’s In the American Grain. During the early sixties Hellman
and Olson went together to visit Williams, whom I later heard read
at the University of Iowa in 1969, where I was then visiting profes-
sor of French. I was very attached to the old civilized and more
contained space of France and the land around the Mediterranean.
In 1954 I knew nothing about the aura and legend of Black Moun-
tain, nor of the mythical Charles.
      At Black   Mountain,   Robert   and   I shared   the house   called


320                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 349 ---
“Black Dwarf” with Stefan and Hilda Wolpe. I was always im-
pressed and charmed by Stefan and his way of articulating his
ideas, and by his enthusiasm.    At the end of the 1954 summer,    he
had completed a beautiful opera based on Ibsen’s Peer Gynt, which
was performed by almost the entire College, which was cast and
audience. Hellman sang Peer Gynt, Olson the Big Boyg and Button-
maker. I shall always remember Stefan playing songs on the piano
while Hellman sang—how he lingered over the keys and reluc-
tantly ended the rehearsal, and let go of the song and the piano.
     My most important, precious experience that summer was
studying and acting with Wes Huss as drama coach and director.
The few students remaining at the College, and the little group in-
terested in theater, included   Michael   Rumaker   and Betty Kaiser.
Wes was my first drama “teacher,” although I had acted in Aix-en-
Provence with a small international company (we had the Aix Ca-
sino theater available to us, and we toured to Marseilles and Tou-
lon). I knew Wes had been with an important regional theater out
of Philadelphia, and he taught me discipline in the dramatic uses of
the imagination by various exercises and insights. He directed a
very moving “rehearsed reading” of Uncle Vanya, with Hellman as
Vanya, I as Sonya, and Betty as the young wife. I saw Stefan’s tears
at that show, and I was in turn moved by that and proud. But the
most enduring acting moments—and they were literally moments
after weeks of rehearsing—were scenes from Ghosts, in which I
played Mrs. Alving, and Michael played my son. It was a revelation
to me—through his coaching—to feel my voice changing, my body
movements become someone else’s: to move and feel the emotions
of the mother who discovers her son has a syphilis given him by his
father. My enduring thanks to Wes for that.
     I met Connie about three or four years later. Hellman had be-
come friendly with her at BMC before she left, and we would spend
a few days with her and Kate, who were living in Scituate, Massa-
chusetts, on our way up to Maine where we had a house. After the
definitive break with Charles, we invited her to visit us on our
Maine island, where she met George Bunker, a painter, whom she
married shortly afterwards. They lived with us for about three
summers, which Kate loved, after which they bought their own
house on a nearby island.
    Charles’s last summer, before he became ill, Kate brought him

An Anthology of Personal Accounts                                 321

--- Page 350 ---
\


Charles Olson at Black Mountain, ca. 1953. Photograph: Jonathan Williams.
Courtesy of N.C. State Archives, Raleigh.

           to stay with us. It was an unexpected visit, and they stayed for
           about two weeks. It was Charles’s third visit to our house. He
           found it very difficult to go to bed before three or four in the morn-
           ing, but my old friend Ashley from Aix and I could not survive his
           schedule—so   Charles had no court nor courtiers, and he had to
           yield as best he could. He begged us to stay up, said he would
           dance with us, but Kate hanestly kept him company and we would
           get up early in the morning to work or garden right beneath
           Charles’s attic room. He must have banged his head against the low



           322                   Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 351 ---
slanting attic roof every morning when he got up. But he was a
good sport, and charming. He had anxiety about his health that
summer, but did not impose it on us. He was very interested in
Ashley’s paintings and saw him as a shaman or evangelical figure.
It was a warm    and loving visit, as I remember it, on that beautiful
island. I never saw Charles again. I have a journal of that summer.
It would be interesting to uncover it to see what more I have noted
of his visit.




                                           Robert Hellman
                                          (Faculty 1954 and 1955 SS)


                                                     Four Poems



Fort Square
   What am I to say to this?
   to this face of me
   hills and houses and
   the light that lifts the sea
   over the last green of summer
   I come out of the door
   onto the rickety porch
       that yet is secure to me having
       carried his weight
   greeted by the Fort children
   as though I too had lived in was


Helen Hellman writes: “Robert Hellman died in Denmark in 1984. He
had been living there for about ten years with his second wife, adopted
son and their small daughter. He had sent a collection of his verse to a
small publisher in New Jersey, I think, at the recommendation of Hilda
Morley Wolpe, but died before its acceptance or rejection. I have it now
and would very much like it to have readers.”

free to move in
  The light out of the painter’s eye
  that undoes memory that
  breaks the platform of piety brings us
  to the ground yet
  no way diminishes us makes
  a marvel of our ignorance
  as wide as the air where
  boats riding out of the inner harbor
  cross the wakes of leviathans.




  As the mirrors darkened he
  took great pains
  to ascertain the exact time the
  numbers of minutes and seconds
  moved like cilia at the
  bottom of his poems
                         The intervals
  of death’s labor
  growing shorter and shorter his bulk
  diminishing towards birth
  shuffled from bed to chair
  hardly distinguishing
  what was action and
  what respite

                        I dreamt
  I dreamt I dreamed of you

The Burial
  I come to be dead by graveside
                                   I find
  many winners complaining to the heaped earth
                                               their wives
  dipping hands in the
  blue openings          their children
  posted like dolls to
  mark perspectives
                       While the High Rabbi
  speaks money to me
  who must go into the ground a debtor
                                          I had expected
  a company of peers a
  convocation of old bones
                             not
  this distinguished assemblage       this lustre
  spread by suave hands and voices
  over all that was grey and familiar to me.

Contributors




Louis Adamic (1899-1951): After Mr. Adamic left BMC as a visitor,
    he noted his observations of the school and its founder in a
    chapter of his book, My America, 1938, and went on to write
    other books, fourteen in all. He served as both publisher and /
    or editor for Trends and Tides, the Peoples of America        series,
    Common     Ground, and also contributed articles and fiction to
    many journals and magazines.
Anni Albers: In 1949 the Alberses moved to New York City where
    she became the first weaver to have a solo exhibition at
    MOMA. She has published two books: On Designing (1959) and
    On Weaving (1965), and from 1970 on devoted her energies ex-
    clusively to graphic art. She is the recipient of numerous
    awards and honorary degrees, and her most recent exhibition
    of prints was in New York City in 1989. She continues print-
    making, weaving, lecturing, and writing, and runs the Josef
    Albers Foundation in Orange, Connecticut.
Josef Albers (1888-1976): After he retired from Yale in 1958, Josef
    Albers continued his work as artist, teacher, and writer with
    undiminished vigor. His exhibition at the MMA in New York
    City was the museum’s first retrospective devoted to a living
    artist. His books include Despite Straight Lines (1961), Interaction
     of Color (1963), and Search Versus Research (1965). The Photo-
     graphs of Josef Albers exhibition opened in 1988 at MOMA and
     traveled throughout the United States. That year the Guggen-
     heim Museum’s retrospective of his work traveled to Germany
     and Finland.
Janet (Ramsey) Aley: “Married and raised four children; have done
     feature news writing, taught nursery school, grown flowers
    and garden vegetables, served on boards of local Art Council,
    PTA, an intercommunity camp, a mental health agency, and
    currently help the management of a Westport, Ct., homeless
    shelter.”
Leo Amino: Born in Japan in 1911, came to United States in 1929.


Contributors                              |                         329

--- Page 358 ---
He   is a sculptor    and   teacher,   has   had    numerous    one-man
      shows at a host of galleries since 1940. His work is represented
      in MOMA,     the Massillon and Whitney             museums,    the Smith-
      sonian Institute, among others, as well as in public and private
    collections. He lives in New York City.
Richard L. Andrews: “After a year of public school teaching, and
    graduating BMC in Art in 1940, did war service in aerial pho-
      tography, and then a year of studying with Hans Hofmann.
      Continued teaching art at primary and secondary levels. Re-
      tired in ‘87, but continue teaching children and adults. Have
      shown in NYC galleries and elsewhere. Live on an old Maine
    farm.”
Peggy Loram Bailey: “I married David Welles Bailey, one of the
    first three graduates of BMC. We started a co-ed boarding
      school in Woodstock, Vt., in 1945, whose community                aspects
     were largely drawn from our BMC experience. I taught English
     and Drama there until 1966, when I retired. My teaching life
     was greatly enhanced by BMC in a slow and subtle way, and
     my experience there at its very inception was unique.”
Eric R. Bentley: His first book, A Century of Hero Worship, came out
     while he was a teacher at BMC. Many books and many plays
     have followed.
Roberta Blair: “After my summer          at BMC, I continued to teach art
      in public schools, doing painting, sculpture, pottery and pho-
      tography. I exhibited pottery and photographs in the Philadel-
      phia Art Alliance, the Philadelphia Print Club, and in various
      college galleries in South Jersey. Since my retirement in 1975,
      I've had a chance to travel extensively.”
Charles Bloomstein:       “Became Chief of Mission for CARE           in Japan,
      Okinawa,   France     and   Algeria.   Left in 1955, and       worked   as
    executive in contemporary design, then in book publishing,
    and from 1963-79 was Executive Vice President of the NY
    Friends Group. Since my retirement in May 1979, have en-
    gaged in unsalaried activities around the world on the nonvio-
    lent improvement of human rights.”
Lyle Bongé: “I am beginning to do honest work as a sculptor, and
    have done legitimate work as a photographer, publishing The
    Sleep of Reason and The Photographs of Lyle Bongé, both by Jargon
    Press. In 1968 I broke the world’s record for sailing single-

330                        Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 359 ---
handed on cross Gulf [of Mexico] passages. Since BMC life has
    become rich, and it seems to be getting richer all the time.”
John Cage: An advance-guard musician and innovator, he has writ-
    ten instrumental,     vocal,     orchestral,   magnetic-tape    and   elec-
    tronic music from 1936 on. He is the author of over ten books
    on a variety of subjects, and the recipient of numerous musical
    awards and commissions. He also founded the New York My-
    cological Society out of his botanical ardor and interest in
    mushrooms.
June (Rice/Lane)      Christensen:     “I became    dance teacher at Santa
    Barbara High School for 29 years, forming a department of Per-
    forming Arts to produce quality theater with high school tal-
    ent. During my last four years I created and directed an alter-
    native school for students who          ‘hated school’; and on retire-
    ment in 1981, began an educational consulting partnership, The
    Human Curriculum, looking at ‘how students learn’ as opposed
    to ‘what they learn.’ I now read, write, travel, teach movement
    for senior citizens, and have two wonderful children.”
Gerda Slavson Cooke: “Stan and I raised four daughters in a house
    we both built and rebuilt. Extra time has been spent house
    painting, gardening, teaching, driving a bus for handicapped
    preschool children. I now grow houseplants, work for a land-
    scaping business, maintain and improve other peoples’ gar-
    dens, which doesn’t seem to leave very much time for my own.
    Our three-year-old grandchild, Betsy, is a joy.”
David H. Corkran: Went on to teach English and then history in
    schools in the Chicago area; received a Newberry Fellowship in
    1962, and grants from the American Philosophical Society in
    1964 and 1966. Besides articles written for professional jour-
    nals, he has authored The Cherokee Frontier: 1740-62 (1962), The
    Creek Frontier:    1540-1783     (1967), The Carolina   Indian Frontier
    (1970), and The Iroquois Frontier: 1550-1783 (1979).
John R. Corkran: “Graduated Wesleyan University in 1958; then
    worked for the U.S. Forest Service as a campground designer
    and supervisor; became first development director for Catlin
    Gabel School, director of development for the Fountain Valley
    and the Bush Schools; received the Robert Bell Crow award in
    1984 for service to Independent            Schools.   Married    to Toni
    (Bender), with children: Carol, Susan, Tim and Laurel.”

Doughten     Cramer:    “Taught   school   in Rochester,   N.Y.,   for four

      years, then drafted, spending three years in England during

Judith Davidoff: “I have stayed in music, performing and teaching.

      I teach and perform at Sarah Lawrence College. I direct the
      New York Consort of Viols, have continued a lively interest in
      contemporary music and art, and have begun work in ceramics
     as an avocation.”
Fielding Dawson: Since his BMC experience, he has developed pri-
      marily as a novelist and short-story writer. He is the author of
      seventeen published books, and is an artist with several one-
     man shows to his credit; he is best known for his photocollages.
     He is an art and literary critic, teaches in prisons, and is pres-
     ently completing a nonfiction account of a ten-week class he
     led in Sing Sing during the spring of 1988.
Elaine de Kooning (1920-1989): In 1943 married Willem de Koon-
     ing, who taught painting at BMC in the summer of 1948. An
     artist, teacher, writer, with many academic appointments,
     Elaine de Kooning has innumerable gallery and museum exhi-
     bitions to her credit. Her work is displayed and owned by
     numerous museums and private collections throughout the
     United States.
Theodore Dreier: For a number of years Ted has been very active in
      the Transcendental Meditation movement, and is on the faculty
    (emeritus) of its university in Iowa. He wrote: “Any extra effort
    I can spend . . . I want to give primarily to helping Maharishi
    ... by developing the potential inner silence and peace latent
    in every individual. . . . It really means that everyone of us has
    to learn how to be an artist.”
Giddy Erwin Dyer: Guests of Mildred and John Adams at BMC,
    Giddy and Bob have raised two children and have seven
    grandchildren. Giddy has been active in Illinois politics, serv-

ing on the Du Page County Board, and in 1968 elected to the II-
    linois General Assembly for six two-year terms; she remains
    active in community work.
Nancy Farrell: “I teach English to native speakers of other lan-
    guages, and draw on French and German literature and con-
    versation, which harvests J. A. Rice’s Plato I class. Drawing,
    painting, and Werklehre      propelled me toward 16-mm film
    (M.A. at Univ. of So. Calif.) and work in film information, re-

    York University.”
Mary Fitton Fiore: “Grew up in Ohio with good parents; attended
    Radcliffe College for a while, then joined Women’s Army
    Corps, World War II. BMC           1949-56, first as a student, then as a
    faculty wife married to Joseph Fiore, artist. Two grown chil-

F. A. Foster: “Graduated      BMC      in 1948, did graduate work at Co-

Helen   Frankenthaler:     After Helen       left BMC,     she married   Robert
    Motherwell, the painter, and taught painting herself at many
    schools and colleges, notably Yale, Princeton, and Hunter Col-
    lege. She is internationally recognized and exhibited, and has
    won numerous awards and honors, here and abroad. Her work
    is exhibited   in the MMA,          MOMA,        and   in other museums
    throughout the United States.
Paul Goodman (1911-1972): A well-known author and social critic,
    his books cover the areas of community planning, literary criti-
    cism, gestalt therapy, education, language theory, and social
    criticism. He taught for brief periods at many colleges. He
    wrote The Quiet House at BMC, based on a meditation cabin in
     the woods, built to commemorate Mark Dreier, the young son
     of a faculty member, killed in an accident. This is its first print-
     ing.
Lorrie Goulet: “Married 38 years to sculptor José de Creeft, whom I
    met at BMC; raised one daughter, Maria, who is a professional

--- Page 362 ---
painter.    Became a professional sculptor, carving over 400
      pieces in wood and stone; had my 18th one-woman show in
      1988; have enjoyably taught sculpture for the past 32 years, and

Francine du Plessix Gray: After BMC, Francine continued serving
      as a reporter for United Press (1952-54), was book editor for
      Art in America (1964-66), taught and lectured at CCNY,                        Yale,
      and Princeton, and is the author of Divine Disobedience (1970),
      Hawaii     (1972),    Lovers   and    Tyrants    (1976),    World   without    End
      (1981), October Blood (1985), and Adam and Eve & the City (1987).
Jesse Dawes        Green:     “Went    on    to Reed     College     (B.A.), Berkeley
