      Conrad, Whitman, Williams, and others, and two books of the
    writings of the American anthropologist Frank Hamilton
    Cushing, who lived among the Zuni Indians of New Mexico in
    the 1880s.”
Mary Gregory: Molly has continued to teach by working, making
      custom     furniture, doing millwork, remodeling, and building.
      She has run her own woodworking shop from 1954 to 1988,
      also teaching at Concord Academy in Massachusetts. “I have
      managed to really make most of what I have designed: church
      furnishings, a gazebo, dovecotes, carvings, barns, and finally
      houses.”
A. G.: “Munching for some 50 years on my share of the loaves and
      fishes passed out at BMC, it would seem that this was the meal
      for my lifetime. It tastes just as delicious now as ever!”
Hannelore Hahn: She enjoys a fulfilling engagement with writing,
    business, and the arts. She is director of the International
    Women’s Writing Guild, a wide network for the empowerment
    of women through writing. She has published Take a Giant Step
    and On the Way to Feed the Swans, a first volume of an autobio-
    graphical trilogy. She graduated from the University of South-
    ern California and holds an honorary doctorate from Skidmore
    College.
James Hall (1916-1982): Taking degrees from UCLA and the Uni-



334                          Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 363 ---
versity of Wisconsin, he spent most of his life teaching political
    science and English at Windsor Mt. School in Lenox, Massa-
    chusetts. He was a writer and educator. His wife, Frances, is a
    published poet. One of their sons, Parnell, is an author; the
    other, Terrence, a musician.
Mildred (Adams) Harding: “After BMC, John and I taught eight
    years in the Middle East, then both went on teaching at the
    University of Florida until 1967, the year John died. I taught
    many more years, remarried, kept on writing, and now am
    immersed in a biography of Pearl Craigie [who wrote under
    the pseudonym “John Oliver Hobbes”]. Both children have
    flourished: Branwen, an archaeologist; Brayton,      a seaman and
    flyer.”
Helen Hellman: “I spent two summers at BMC with Robert Hell-
    man, at the end of its glory days in 1955-56. Then I embarked
    on obtaining a belated B.A. and Ph.D. in modern French litera-
    ture and drama,    and to a university teaching career in Phi-
    ladephia and Pittsburgh, Pa., Delaware, Iowa, and Brooklyn,
    N.Y. This came to a halt in 1973, when I began training in psy-
    choanalysis. After many years I became a psychoanalyst.”
Robert Hellman (1919-1984): Teaching in the 1950s, then working
    in theater in Philadelphia in the 1960s, his first play, Kling, was
    produced, as was Open Shut, at the Guthrie Theater in Minnea-
    polis in the 1970s. Remarrying and moving to Denmark, he and
    his wife, Margaret, had a daughter, now       twelve. Robert was
    close to Charles and Connie Olson. Helen Hellman, his first
    wife, holds an unpublished manuscript of his verse, deserving
    a wider audience.
Fannie Hillsmith: After teaching at BMC, Fannie continued her ca-
    reer as a painter. She has had over twenty one-woman shows,
    exhibited in over fifty group shows; her work is held by at least
    fourteen museums in the United States. She has received many
    gallery exhibition awards, is a fellow of the Academy of Arts
    and Letters, and is presently affiliated with the Graham Gallery
     in New York City.
Elizabeth Jennerjahn: “After dancing, I made wall hangings, collage
     tapestry, then hand-embroidered wool fleece appliqué. My in-
    volvement in Waldorf education drew me into this vital ap-



Contributors                              .                        335

--- Page 364 ---
proach to art. I paint now, attempting to use all of my
      influences and experiences for the greater glory of God. We
      have the joy of our two grown children, and live and work in
      dry red rock Sedona, Ariz., and the moist green Adirondacks.”
Warren “Pete” Jennerjahn: “After BMC, spent a year in Europe,
    painting, traveling; then five years in Manhattan, where son
    Hans was born (1954), teaching at Cooper Union and Hunter
      College.   Five years at Westbury,     L.I., where     daughter,     Ann,
      was born (1958), and continued teaching at Adelphi Univ. from
      1954-87. Early retirement. Now living in Sedona, Ariz., as well
    as the Adirondacks.”
Margaret Kennard Johnson: Since BMC, she has been continuously
    involved in making and teaching art. Her special interest in
    Japanese contemporary printmakers and their techniques has
    burgeoned in the publication of books, articles, and lectures.
      Her own work is exhibited and collected both here and abroad,
     while she very actively keeps on making and showing her own
     new work.
Irwin Kremen: Krem teaches psychology at Duke, where in 1966 he
    also began making collages, paintings, assemblages. In 1978 a
    Smithsonian museum organized the first exhibits of his col-
    lages, which subsequently have been widely shown at other
      museums     and   university   galleries.   Barbara,   his   wife,   is a
    writer; their son, Adam, is a graduate student in psychology at
    Berkeley; daughter, Claire, a conservation biologist, holds a
    doctorate from Duke.
Leo Krikorian: He continues to paint, living and working in Paris.
    He exhibits in galleries and museums both in Europe and the
    United States. His work combines the illusion of spatiality
    within a two-dimensional plane, exploring the interplay of illu-
      sion and surface. His work grows out of the Cubist tradition,
    and in particular, Bill Max.
Mervin Lane: “All the areas I was involved in at the College have
    burgeoned. I’ve acted in plays, am a writer, poet, musician, and
    teacher. The adobe and stone house I built thirty-seven years
    ago I live in with my wife, Peg, a botanist and singer. We enjoy
    nature and music together, and have seven grandchildren.”
Angelica (Bodky) Lee: “Am currently teaching sculpture at DeCor-



336                     Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 365 ---
dova    Museum   School,   Lincoln,   Mass.;   have   had   two   one-
    woman    shows; play chamber music (flautist), and am doing
    research in historic flutes. Husband, Roger, is also involved
    with music, though an engineer. We have two children, four
     grandchildren.
Albert William Levi (1911-1988): An educator and writer in the
     fields of philosophy, sociology, and aesthetics; at Washington
    University since 1952, and the David May Distinguished Pro-
    fessor in the Humanities from 1966 to 1979. He has published
    over ten major works in the above fields and was commis-
    sioned to do a book on art history, art criticism, aesthetics, and
     art education, which was completed before he died.
Gretel J. Lowinsky: “I worked for 18 years with pre-school children
     and their parents, went to school at night to gain a graduate
     degree. Since retiring in 1965, I’ve been working as a family
     therapist in a private school, teaching the violin and viola pri-
     vately, and am a member of several professional orchestras
     and chamber groups. I have had four children. Encouragement
     in music was rooted in the Black Mountain years.”
Anne G. Mangold (1910-1989): From BMC Fred and Anne retired
     to Gilbertsville, Anne’s family seat since 1787. They devoted
    themselves to village affairs, liberal causes, the arts, and local
    and family history. After Fred’s death (early 1970s), Anne
    maintained   these interests, followed    national politics avidly,
    and continued to generously attend to the needs of the village,
    its historic buildings, and its library.
Jane Slater Marquis: After graduating from BMC in 1945, Jane
    taught art in various schools from 1948 to 1975, including
    graphic design at the University of Oregon. Stained-glass
    maker with work in private residences and churches, she has
    major windows in California at St. Matthews Parish, Pacific
    Palisades; All Souls Episcopal Church, Berkeley; and Congre-
    gation Rodef Sholem in San Raphael. She has been married to
    Lucian Marquis since 1947—two children.
George Martin (1893-1973): Born in Cologne, Germany; a pianist,
    graduating from the Cologne Conservatory of Music, immi-
    grated to the United States in 1934; lived in Staten Island, New
    York, where he taught piano both privately and at Wagner Col-



Contributors                              .                           337

--- Page 366 ---
lege. Traveled extensively, moving in prominent literary and
      artistic circles, particularly the avant-garde of the 1920s. Spent
      one summer at BMC (1945).
Greg Masurovsky:      “Studied art in NYC, where I met my wife, also
      a painter. We went to Paris in May 1954 and stayed on doing
      our art. Hard time, but a rich life. Shirley died in 1980, and I
      continued working in Paris, raising our son, who now lives in
      the States with a wife and a child. He is a translator, which is
      what he does best, being born in Paris to American parents.”
William McGee:      “I completed   graduate work at Indiana    Univ. in
      1953, and began a college teaching career at the Univ. of Cin-
      cinnati, then Brown Univ., then Hunter College. In New York, I
      exhibited with the Grace Borgenicht Gallery, and through it my
      work began to be acquired by collectors and museums in Balti-
      more,   Dallas, London   (the Tate Gallery), and Chicago.     I am
      now affiliated with the Leslie Cecil Gallery in Manhattan.”
Mary Miller: Married Matt Miller, with whom        she raised two chil-
      dren and built a house in Topanga, California. She made a
      Folkways record (“Mickey Miller Sings American Folksongs”)
      with Bess Lomax     Hawes    in 1959. Divorced   in 1968, she re-
      ceived an M.A. that same year in psychology and started the
      Topanga Center for Human Development, which operated for
      thirteen years. She now counsels in private practice, and has
      begun writing a book on the people in Topanga.
1S: Nakata: “Married Alexa McLane, had four children: U.S. Postal
      Service 1950-84; since 1987 involved in take-out cuisine, a fam-
      ily enterprise. Interested in writing stories, enjoying good food
      and wine since coming to California in 1969. Have tried to be
      creative and constructive in work and play to make life more
      rewarding and interesting.”
Kenneth Noland: Taught at the Institute of Contemporary Arts
      (1950-52), and at Catholic University (1951-60). His work has
      had notable representation and recognition in the United States
      and abroad, and his paintings are in major museums and col-
      lections throughout the world. He continues to be an active
      painter and “colorist,” working out of his studios in South Sa-
      lem, New York, and near Santa Barbara, California.
Andy Oates: “After graduating from BMC, went to live in Boston
      with the Williamses, Nick Cernovich,      and Stan Vanderbeek;


338                     Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 367 ---
worked in a photo lab, then Goodspeed’s Rare Books, then
    Brattle Theatre, a repertory company in Cambridge. Opened a
    gallery. Came to Nantucket for a summer and have been here
    for 30 years. Co-owner of Nantucket Looms: still weaving and
    taking photos.”
Herbert Oppenheimer:    “After BMC, did army service (1943-46)
    and then political work for Henry Wallace (1947-48); studied
    sculpture and then architecture at Columbia (1950-54). Started
    my own architectural office in 1960, which is still operating.
    Married Judith Jordan in 1947, and have three children: Linda,
    37, Alissa, 29, and Catherine, 25.”
Sheila Oline Marbain Ravin: “I have a silk-screen printing studio in
    New   York City, where I have been working with artists since
    the 1950s. I also do ceramic sculpture and play recorder. I have
    a 32-year-old son, and am married       to Irving Ravin, who is a
    music and volleyball enthusiast. In Dec. 1989, I had a 25-year
    retrospective show of my collaborative prints at Rutgers Zim-
    merli Art Museum.”
Harold Bradford Raymond: “I graduated BMC as one of the
    school’s rare history majors in 1942; then four years in the U.S.
    Army Engineers. Graduate student at Harvard, from which I
    received my Ph.D. in 1952..I have taught History since that
    time on the university level. Married Dorothy MacLean, a fel-
    low educator and environmentalist. Our son, Paul, is a college
    professor. Since 1987 I teach half-time, but have no desire to
    retire from anything.”
John J. Reiss: “Since BMC have been a free-lance designer, in and
    out of advertising. One of my three children’s books, Colors, is
    in its eleventh printing; other books are Numbers and Shapes.
    I’ve done some mountain climbing in the Canadian Rockies,
    and in my semi-retirement I am working on another children’s
   book.”
Ann Craig Rice: Having raised three children with husband Frank
    A. Rice, she later studied for teacher certification at the Univer-
    sity of Virginia Extension, and has taught handicapped chil-
    dren, English in an alternative high school, and English as a
    second language. She lives in Arlington, Virginia, with her hus-
    band.
Frank A. Rice: Went on to become a specialist in Arabic language

Contributors                                                       339

--- Page 368 ---
and linguistics with the Arabian American Oil Company, the
      Foreign Service Institute of the Department of State, and the
      Center for Applied Linguistics, Washington, D.C. He is retired
      and lives in Arlington, Virginia, with his wife, the former Ann
    Craig Sutton.
John Andrew Rice (1888-1968): He was the principal founder of
      Black Mountain College, and author of I Came Out of the Eight-
      eenth Century (Harper, 1942), Local Color (Dell, 1955), and ar-
      ticles and short stories in Harper’s, The New Yorker, Collier's and
      other magazines. (For a more complete biography, see the in-
      troduction to his Black Mountain memoirs.)
William Craig Rice: Born in Washington, D.C., in 1955, writes fre-
      quently on literary, political, and environmental subjects. He
      has taught at Temple University and the University of Pennsyl-
      vania, and is currently a graduate student at the University of
      Michigan.
M. C. Richards: A poet, potter, teacher, and interdisciplinary enthu-
      siast and practitioner, she has produced five books as well as
      articles, monographs, and pamphlets that deal with the rela-
      tion of the creative process in its vital human and educational
      connection to the whole person. When she is not moving about
      lecturing and teaching, she works and lives most of the year at
      Camphill Village in Kimberton, Pennsylvania. Her pottery,
      writing, and teaching were honored in Carmel, California, in
      1986, and at the Arts Festival in San Francisco, 1989.
Sue Spayth Riley: “Married BMC graduate, Jerry Wolpert, who
    died in 1949 in Buffalo, where he was teaching at the Univer-
    sity. Have done writing and newspaper work during WWIL
      and after. Raised two boys; remarried in 1958, had another boy;
    returned to school, earning a B.A. and M.A. in early childhood
    education, and have been teaching and writing in that field
    ever since.”
Dick Roberts: After teaching school on a Blackfoot reservation in
    Montana, Dick operated a small business for fifteen years, then
    became a remedial reading teacher on a Northern Cheyenne
    reservation. He has taught elementary school in Montana for
    ten years, retiring in 1979 to build a log home in Bitteroot
    Mountains, where he and Janet live. Now writing environ-
    mental and nature verse, with some small acceptance.

340                    Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 369 ---
Janet Heling Roberts: She has been a rural school teacher, Director
    of reading clinics and Head Start programs on Indian reserva-
    tions, Regional Training Officer for Head Start at the Univer-
    sity of Montana, Director of Family and Child Services in Mis-
    soula;   served   as a consultant   to state   and   Indian   colleges,
    schools, welfare programs, and has coauthored for teachers
    and parents of young children 793 Ways to Help Children Learn
    and Grow.
Ron Robertson: Ron is a painter, printmaker and assemblage artist,
    and teacher of graphic processes. He studied with the six fore-
    most printmakers of Japan, which culminated in the publica-
    tion of Contemporary Printmaking in Japan (New York: Crown
    Publishers). He has taught in the art departments at the Uni-
    versity of California, the Santa Barbara Art Institute, and at the
    Santa Barbara City College. His work is exhibited in both the
    United States and Japan.
Howard Rondthaler: “In 1949 Manvel Schauffler bet me two quarts
    of beer I would not move to Oregon. I took him up on it,
    moved, got a job with the Forest Service, graduated from Reed
    College, and retired from the Forest Service after 35 years. Life
    has been very, very good to me. Friends I met at BMC are still
    friends, and we still get together.”
May Sarton: “In my ten days at BMC I learned a lot, argued a lot,
    dissented about some things, and wrote a long poem to try to
    understand what my dissent was all about. Since then I have
    published forty-five books. My early tour of American colleges
    (1940-41), of which BMC     was one, was seminal. Now I am 77
    and still look back on those ten days at Black Mountain as a
    rare experience.”
Manvel Schauffler: Manvel has been associated as a teacher and
    Headmaster of the Catlin Gabel School in Portland for thirty
    years. In 1980 he returned to the classroom and now teaches at
    the Bush School in Seattle, where he is also Dean of the Faculty.
    The school enables him to travel, giving workshops for teach-
    ers and trustees. He and Verna have three grown children, two
    of whom are teachers, and one who is a news and anchor man.
Zoya Sandomirsky Slive: “At the University of Chicago I received
    an M.A.    in Sociology, followed by marriage, three children,
    and the latest move to Cambridge, Mass., where my husband

Contributors                                                           34]

--- Page 370 ---
teaches Art History at Harvard. Received a Ph.D. in Counsel-
     ing Psychology from Boston Univ., and established my private
     practice. BMC helped nourish my artistic and socio-political
     interests, which I’ve spent a lifetime integrating.”
Allan Sly: After various appointments in music, the Slys moved to
     the Boston area. Allan served for sixteen years as a musical di-
      rector at a Unitarian church and eighteen years at MIT. He was
      a proofreader for the Christian Science Monitor for twenty-two
      years. His retirement has been active. At eighty-one, he listens
      to recorded books as his sight declines. He has two active
     daughters.
Yella Pessl Sobotka: “After teaching at BMC, I taught at Columbia
      Univ., the Mozarteum in Salzburg, and at Eaglebrook School in
      Deerfield, Mass. Since my retirement in 1968, I concertize fre-
    quently with various musician friends and performers.”
Dick Spahn: “I’m retired from a career which has been devoted to
    supporting psychiatric patients, juvenile offenders, and other
    victims and casualties. I’ve begun to write as an effort to dis-
    cover if my lifetime accumulation of beliefs are justified,
    specifically trying to connect the psychological experiences of
    the infant to the way adults, especially in groups, solve the
    wrong problems and avoid solving the right ones.”
Morton    Steinau:   “Married   BMC   student   Barbara   Hill; magazine
      work for Time and Life (1943-53); sold life insurance (1955-80).
      From 1960-80s Barbara and | organized and participated in
      many antiwar rallies. We continue regular vigils in No. Bran-
      ford, Ct., protesting Trident submarine launchings at nearby
      Groton, Ct.”
Knute Stiles: “An Alaskan fisherman in the summers from ’46 to
    ‘54, studied painting in NYC, graduated the Calif. School of
      Fine Arts in ’53; have exhibited my work        here and abroad,
      written reviews for Artforum and Art in America, designed thea-
      ter sets, illustrated books, taught painting and drawing for six
      years, and now live in Bisbee, Az.”
Ruth Lyford Sussler: “Since BMC, have tried to balance the pleas-
    ures and demands of family, work, and friends, with ongoing
    delights in work/study visits to Japan, teaching art to school
    children, living with my husband and four children in New
    London, Ct., and doing landscape and still-life painting.”

342                     Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds

--- Page 371 ---
Evelyn Oxenberg Swann: “I married Jim Swann in 1948, and have
    four adult offspring; received my degree in Education in 1971
    while organizing alternative schools for five years. Have be-
    come involved in studying traditional blues and jazz piano;
    have led improvisational drama and antinuke workshops, and
    recently have started cartooning.”
Marian (Nacke) Teeter: “I raised two sons and worked in a variety
    of fields, the last being school psychology. Main interests now
    are sharing music and books with friends, and activities for
    peace and other human needs. All the hills and valleys of BMC
    terrain are very vivid: the wonderful people, my brother
    Frank’s    short life there, and   Rice, that bold   and   innovative
    spirit.”
Phyllis Josephs Thomas: “During the War years, took night classes
    at Temple Univ.; became editor for a medical house; married,
    came west to Calif., raised two sons; became exec. sec’y in an
    advertising agency, an editorial ass’t for graphic arts trade
    journals, a circulation   librarian, then a successful     real estate
    sales person. Now 7 years in Oregon, I’m an energetic swim-
    mer, but take things easier in this Northwest location.”
Jim Tite: At the end of his career as a public-relations publicist, Jim
    discovered the bucolic Arkansas Ozarks, where he lives with
    his wife, Joey, in a geodesic domehouse overlooking Beaver
    Lake. They design and make wood goods, which they offer at
    craft fairs throughout Arkansas, which Jim depicts as “the State
    that one must honor for its complacence in anonymity.”
John Urbain: “After BMC, married Elaine Schmitt, did art directing
    for 25 years at Philip Morris, had two children, went to Paris
    again under the GI Bill, returned to Tarrytown, N.Y., for 20
    years, then moved to Manhattan, where I continue painting,
    and writing some poetry in workshops at NYU under Ander
    Williams Packard.”
Esteban Vicente: His wife, Harriet, writes, “He has just had a joy-
    ous show of paintings and collages of his 85th year. He believes
    in doing the work, surviving with grace and in health, and
    keeping his moral attitudes in place.”
Harry Weitzer: “When the Oregon community split up, I married
    Chrissie Schauffler. We had five children and a cabinet making
    business—all, including the marriage, are still extant. I have

Contributors                                                          343

--- Page 372 ---
been a woodworker all this time, though recent effort has been
      on the business side. I hope to get back to the creative end be-
      fore long, as that is where my heart lies.”
Norman     B. Weston:   “Nan    and I were   married   in 1935 while   at
      BMC. Graduated Harvard in 1940 with MBA, and joined the
      National Bank of Detroit as investment analyst. Served in U.S.
      Navy from 1942-46. Have four children. Between us, Nan and I
      have served on over 20 eleemosynary boards. Retired from
      Bank of Detroit in 1979 as Vice Chairman of the Board.”
Judd Woldin: “Studied with Ernst Krenek in New Mexico, and af-
      ter my first commission   to write for a B’way musical in 1960,
      have been happily composing ever since: Raisin, King of Schnor-
      rers, Lorenzo, Goodbye and Good Luck, and Little Ham are some of
      the shows for which I wrote the music. Have four great chil-
      dren, and live ‘with an angel’ in Manhattan.”
Hilda Morley Wolpe: She has taught literature in universities both
    here and abroad, and is a practicing poet, with published work
    in numerous journals over the past twenty years. She has three
    collections of verse; has been the recipient of a Guggenheim
    Fellowship, a Rockefeller residence in Bellagio, the Capricorn
    Award for two of her books, and was made a Fellow in Poetry
    in 1989 by the New York Foundation of the Arts.
Patsy Lynch Wood: “After graduating, became involved in early
    music; married LaNoue Davenport, brought up two sons in the
    Paul Williams’s community at Stony Point, N.Y. Moved west,
    where I developed new interests in Jungian psychology and
    women’s issues. My second husband was an artist and teacher.
    I obtained certificates in gerontology and music therapy, which
      have enabled further teaching of music, art, and life enrich-
      ment classes for Adult Education in Santa Barbara, Calif. In my
      spare time I make collages, whose processes seem a reflection
      of the way I’ve lived my life over the years.”

Black Mountain College: Sprouted Seeds was
designed by Dariel Mayer and composed at the
University of Tennessee Press on the Apple
Macintosh IIcx with Microsoft Word and Aldus
PageMaker. Linotronic camera pages were generated
by AM/PM, Inc. The book is set in Palatino with
Avant Garde used for display and printed on 50-lb.
Glatfelter Natural. Manufactured in the United States
of America by BookCrafters, Inc.

the eyes of its students and faculty.
Comprising ninety essays, poems, and
plays—as well as reproductions of artwork
by former students—this collection shows
the effects of the Black Mountain experience
over the past fifty years on the contributors’
evolving conceptions of creativity, learning,
and living.

The Editor: Mervin Lane teaches literature
and writing at Santa Barbara City College.
He has published Going to Town and The
Houston Passage and has written three
collections of poetry.




Jacket illustration: Kenneth Noland, Half, 1959.
Acrylic on canvas, 68-5/8 x 68-5/8 inches.
Collection of The Museum of Fine Arts, Houston.
